\chapter{Proximity Service}

In this chapter we design a proximity service.

\begin{itemize}
\item
A proximity service is used to discover nearby places such as restaurants, hotels, theaters, museums, etc., and is a core component that powers features like finding the best restaurants nearby on Yelp or finding k-nearest gas stations on Google Maps.
\item
Figure 1.1. shows the user interface via which you can search for nearby restaurans on Yelp [1]
\item
Note the map tiles used in this book are from Stamen Design [2] anbd data are from OpenStreetMap [3]
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{nearby_search_on_yelp.jpg}
    \caption{Nearby search on yelp}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Step 1 - Understand the Problem and Establish Design Scope}

Yelp supports many features and it is not feasible to design all of them in an interview seesion, so it's important to narrow down the scope by asking questions

The interactions between the candidate and interviewer could look like this:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Candidate: Can a user specify the search radius? If there are not enough businesses within the search raidus, does the system expand the search?
\item
Interviewer: That's a great question. Let's assume we only care about businesses within a specified radius. If time allows, we can then discuss how to expand the search if there are not enough binesses within the radius
\item
Candidate: Can a user change the search radius on the UI?
\item
Interviewer: Yes, we have the following options: 0.5km (0.31 mile), 1km (0.62 mile), 2km (1.24 mile), 5km (3.1 mile), and 20 km (12.42 mile)
\item
Candidate: How does business information get added, deleted, or updated? Do we need to reflect these operations in real-time?
\item
Interviewer: Business owners can add, delete or update a business. Assume we have a business agreement upgfront that newly added/updated businesses will be effective the next day.
\item
Candidate: A user might be moving while using the app/website, so the search results could be slightly different after a while. Do we need to refresh the page to keep the results up to date?
\item
Interviewer: Let's assume a user's moving speed is slow and we don't need to constantly refresh the page
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Functional requirements}

Based on this conversation, we focus on 3 key features:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Return all businesses based on a user's location (latitidue and longitude pair) and radius
\item
Business owners can add, delete, or update a business but this information doesn't need to be reflected in real time
\item
Customers can view detailed information about a business
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Non-functional requirements}

From the business requirements, we can infer a list of non-functional requirements. You should also check these with the interviewer.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Low latency: Users should be able to see nearby businesses quickly
\item
Data privacy: Location info is sensitive data

\begin{itemize}
\item
When we design a location-based service (LBS), we should always take user privary into consideration
\item
We need to compy with data privacy laws like General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) [4] and California Consumer Pirvacy Act (CCPA) [5], etc.
\item
High availability and scalability requirements: We should ensure our system can handle the spike in traffic during peak hours in densely populated areas
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}


\subsection{Back-of-the-envelope-estimation}

Let's take a look at some back-of-the-envelope-estimation

\begin{itemize}
\item
Let's take look at some back-of-the-envolpe calculations to determine the potential scale and challegnes our solution will need to address
\item
Assume we have 100 million daily active users and 200 million businesses
\end{itemize}

\begin{defbox}{Calculate QPS}
\begin{itemize}
\item
Seconds in a day = $24 \times 60 \times 60 \times = 86400$ seconds. We can round it up to \( 10^5 \) for easier calculation
\item
\(10^5\) is used in this book for easier calculation and is used through out this book to represent seconds in a day
\item
Assume a user makes 5 search queries per day
\item
Search QPS = \(\frac{100 \text{ million} \times 5}{10^5} = 5000 \)
\end{itemize}
\end{defbox}

\begin{table}[htpb]
\centering
\caption{Table 1.1: Request Parameters}
\label{tab:label}
\begin{tabular}{|c|c|c|}
\hline
Field        & Description                      & Type \\
\hline
Latitude     & Latitude of a given location     & decimal \\
\hline
Longitude    & Longitude of a given location    & decimal \\
\hline
Radius       & Optional. Default is 5000 meters & int     \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\begin{lstlisting}
  {
    "total": 18,
    "businesses": [{business object}]
  }
\end{lstlisting}

The business object contains everything needed to render the search result page, but we may still need additional attributes such as pictures, reviews, star rating, etc., to render the business detail page.

Therefore, when a user cliks on the business detail page, a new endpoint call to fetch the detailed information of a business is usually required.

\subsection{APIs for business}

The APIs related to a business object are shown in table below.

\begin{table}[htpb]
\caption{Table 1.2: APIs for a business}
\centering
\begin{tabular}{|c|c|}
\hline
\textbf{API} & \textbf{Detail} \\
\hline
GET /v1/businesses:id   & Return detailed information about a business \\
\hline
POST /v1/businesses     & Add a business \\
\hline
PUT /v1/businesses/:id  & Update details of a business \\
\hline
DELETE /v1/businesses/:id  & DELETE a business \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

If you are interested in real-world for place/business search, two examples are Google Places API [7] and Yelp business endpoints [8]

\subsection{Data Model}

In this section, we discuss the read/write ratio and the schema design. The scalability of the database is covered in deep dive.

\subsection{Read/Write Ratio}

Read volume is high because the following two features are very commonly used.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Search for nearby businesses
\item
View the detailed information of a business
\end{itemize}

On the other hand, the write volume is low because adding, removing, and eidting business info are infrequent operations.

For a read-heavy system, a relational databse such as MySQL can be a good fit. Let's take a closer look at the schema design.

\subsection{Data Schema}

The key database tables are the business table and the geopation (geo) index table.

\subsection{Business Table}

The business table contains detailed information about a business. It is shown in Table 1.3 and the primary key is \lstinline|business_id|.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{|c c|}
\hline
\textbf{business} & \\
\hline
business\_id & PK \\
\hline
address & \\
\hline
city & \\
\hline
state & \\
\hline
country & \\
\hline
latitude & \\
\hline
longtitude & \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{Geo index table}

A geo index table is used for the efficient processing of spatial operation. Since this table requires some knowledge about geohash, we will discuss it in the ``Scale this database'' section on page 24.

\subsection{High-level design}

The high level design diagram is shown in Figure 1.2. The system comprises two parts: location-based service (LBS) and business-related service. Let's take a look at each component of the system.

\subsection{Location-based service (LBS)}

The LBS service is the core part of the system which finds nearby buisnesses for a given radius and location.

The LBS has the following characteristics:

\begin{itemize}
\item
It is a read-heavy service with no write requests
\item
QPS is high, espcially during peak hours in dense areas
\item
This service is stateless so it's easy to scale horizontally
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Business service}

Business service mainly deals with two types of requests:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Business owners create, update, or delete businesses. Those requests are mainly write operations, and the QPS is not high
\item
Customers view detailed information about a business. QPS is high during peak hours
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Database cluster}

\begin{itemize}
\item
The database cluster can use the primary-secondary setup
In this setup, the primary database handles all the write operations, and multiple replicas are used for read operations
\item
Data is saved to the primary database first and then replicated to replicas
\item
Due to the replication delay, there might be some discrepancy between data read by the the LBS and the data written to the primary database
\item
This inconsistency is usually not an issue because business information doesn't need to be updated in real-time
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Scalability of bsuiness service and LBS}

Both the bisness service and LBS are stateless services, so it's easy to autmatically add more servers to accommodate peak traffic (e.g. mealtime) and remove servers during off-peak hours (e.g. sleep time)

If the system operates on the cloud, we can set up different regions and availability zones to further improve availability [9]. We discuss this more in deep dive.

\subsection{Algorithsm to fetch nearby businesses}

\begin{itemize}
\item
In real life, companies might use existing geospatial databases such as Geohash in Redis [10] or Postgres with PostGIS extension [11]
\item
You are not expected to know the internals of those geospatial databases during an interview
iiIt's better to demonstrate your problem-solving skills and technical knowledg  by explaining how the geospatial index works, rather than to simply throw out database names
\item
THe next step is to explore different options for fetching nearby business
\item
We will list a few options, go over the though process, and discuss trade-offs
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Option 1: Two-dimensional search}

The most intuitive but naive way to get nearby businesses is to draw a circle with the predefined radius and find all the businesses within the circle as shown in Figure 1.3.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{two_dimensional_search}
    \caption{Figure 1.3: Two dimensional search}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}


This process can be tranlsated into the following pseudo SQL query:

\begin{lstlisting}
SELECT business.id, latitude, longitude,
FROM business
WHERE (latitude BETWEEN [:my_lat])- radius AND {:my_lat} + radius)
AND
  (longitude BETWEEN {:my_long} - radius AND {:my_long} + radius)
\end{lstlisting}

\begin{itemize}
\item
This query is not efficient because we need to scan the whole table
\item
What if we build indexes on longitude and latitude columns?
\item
Would this improve the efficiency? The answer is not by much
\item
The problem is that we have two-dimensional data and the dataset returned from each dimension could still be huge
\item
For example, as shown in Figure 1.4, we can retrieve dataset 1 and dataset 2, thanks to indexes on longitude and latitude columns
\item
But to fetch businesses within the radius, we need to perform an intersect operation on those two datasets
\item
This is not efficient because each dataset contains lots of data
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{intersect_two_datasets}
    \caption{Figure 1.4: Intersect Two Datasets}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The problem with the previous approach is that the database index can only improve search speed in one dimension

\begin{itemize}
\item
So naturally, the follow-up question is, can we map two-dimensional data to one dimension?
\item
The answer is yes
\item
Before we dive into the answers, let's take a look at different types of indexing methods
\item
In a broad sense, there are two types of geospatial indexing approaches, as shown in Figure 1.5
\item
The highlighted ones are the algorithms we discuss indetail because they are commonly used in the industry
\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Hash: even grid, geohash, cartesian tiers [12], etc.
\item
Tree: quadtree, Google S2, RTree [13], etc
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{different_types_of_geospatial_indexes}
    \caption{Figure 1.5: Different types of geospatial indexes}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Even though, the udnelying implmentations of those approaches are different, the high level idea is the same, that is to divide the map into smaller areas a build indexes for fast search.

Among those, geohash, quadtree, and Google G2 are most widely used in real-world applications

Let's take a look at them one by one.

\subsection{Option 2: Evenly Divided Grid}

One simpple approach is to evenly divide the world into small grids (Figure 1.6) This way one grid could have multiple businesses, and each business on the map belongs to one grid.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{global_map}
    \caption{Figure 1.6: Global map (source: 14)}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
This approach works to some extent, but it has one major issue: teh distribution of businesses is not even
\item
There could be lots of bsuinesses in downtown New york, while other grids in deserts or coceans have no business at all
\item
By dividing the world into even grids, for dense areas and large grids in sparse areas
\item
Another potential challnege is to find neighboring grids of a fixed grid
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{divide_grid}
    \caption{Figure 1.8: Divide grid}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Repeat this subdivision until the grid size is within the precision desired. Geohash usually uses base32 representation [15]

Let's look at two examples: 9q8qzn.



\begin{itemize}
\item
geo has of the Google headquarter (length = 6): \\
\lstinline|1001 10110 01001 10000 11011 11010| (base 32 in binary) $\rightarrow$ \\
\textbf{9q9hvu} (base 32)
\item
geohash of the Facebook headquarter (length = 6): \\
\lstinline|1001 10110 01001 10001 10000 10111| (base 32 in binary) $\rightarrow$ \\
\textbf{9q9jhr} (base 32)
\end{itemize}

Geohash has 12 precisions (also called levels as shown in Table 1.4.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The precision factor determines the size of the grid
\item
We are only interested in geohashes with lengths between 4 and 6
\item
This is because when it's longer than 5, the grid size is too small, while if it is smaller than 4, the grid size is too large (see Table 1.4)
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{geohash_length_to_grid_size_mapping}
    \caption{Table 1.4: Geohash length to grid size mapping (source: [16])}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

How do we choose the right precision?

\begin{itemize}
\item
How do we choose the right precision?
\item
We want to find the minimal geogash length that covers the whole cricle drawn by the user0defined raidus
\item
The corresponding relationship between the radius and the legnth
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{radius_to_geohash_mapping}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

This approach works great most of the time, but there are some edge cases with how the geohas boundary is
hanlded that we should discuss with the geohash boundary is handled that we should discuss with the interviewer.

\subsection{Boundary issues}

Geohasing guarantees that the longer a shared prefix is between two geohashes the closer they  are

As shown in Figure 1.9, all the grid have a shared prefix:

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{shared_prefix}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Boundary issue 1

\begin{itemize}
\item
However, the reverse is not true: two locations can be very close but have no shared prefix at all
\item
This is because two close locations on either side of the equator or prime meridian belong to different ``halves'' of the world
\item
For example, in France, La Roche-Chalais (geohash: u000) is just 30km from Pomerol (geohash: 0zzz) but their geohashes have no shared prefix at all [17]
\end{itemize}


\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{no_shared_prefix}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Because of this boundary issue, a simple prefix SQL query below would fail to fetch all nearby businesses

\begin{lstlisting}
SELECT * FROM geohash_index WHERE geohash LIKE '9q8zn%'
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Boundary issue 2}

Another boundary issue is that two positions have can have a long shared prefix, but they belong to different geo hashes as shown in Figure 1.11

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{boundary_issue}
    \caption{Figure 1.11: Boundary issue}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

A common solution is to fetch all businesses not only within the current grid but also from its neighbors.

The geohashes of neighbors can be calculated in constant time and more details about this can be found here [17]

\subsection{Not enough businesses}

Now let's tackle the bonus question

\begin{itemize}
\item
What should we do if there are not enough businesses returned from the current grid and all the neighbors combind?
\item
Option 1: only return businesses within the radius. This option is easy to implement, but the drawback is obvious. It doesn't return enough results to satisfy a user's needs
\item
Option 2: increase the search raidus
\item
We can remove the last digit of the geohash and use the new geohash to fetch nearby businesses
\item
If there are not enough bisnesses, we continue to expand the scope by removing another digit
\item
This way, the grid size is gradually expanded until the result is greater than the desired number of results
\item
Figure 1.12 shows the conceptual diagram of the expanding search process
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Option 4: Quadtree}

Another popular solution is quadtree

\begin{itemize}
\item
A quadtree [18] is a data structure that is commonly used to partition a two-dimensional space by recursively subdividing it into four quadarants (grids) until the contents of the grids meet certain criteria
\item
For example, the criterion can be to keep sudividing until the number of businesses in the grid is not more than 100
\item
This number is arbitrary as the actual number can be determined by business needs
\item
Witha  quadtree, we build an in-memory tree structure to answer queries
\item
Note that quadtree is an in-memory data structure and it is not a database solution
\item
It runs on each  LBS server, and the data structure is built at server start-up time
\item
The following figure visualizes the conceptual process of subdividing the world into a quadtree
iiLet's assume the world contains 200m (million) businesses
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{quadtree}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Figure 1.14 explains the quadtree building process in more detail.
\item
The root node represents the whole world map.
\item
The root node is recusively broken down into 4 quadrants until no nodes are left with more than 100 businesses
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{build_quadtree}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The pseudo code for building quadtree is shown below:

\begin{lstlisting}
public void buildQuadtree(TreeNode node) {
  if (countNumberOfBusinessesInCurrentGrid(node) > 100) {
    node.subdivide();
  }
  for (TreeNode child: node.getChildren()) {
    buildQuadTree(child);
  }
}
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{How much memory does it need to store the whole quadtree?}

To answer this question, we need to know what kind of data is stored.

Table 1.6: Leaf Node \\
\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
Name & Size \\
\hline
Top left coordinates and bottom-right coordinates to identify the grid & 32 bytes (8 bytes $\times$ 4) \\
\hline
List of business IDs in the grid & 8 bytes per ID $\times$ 100 (maximal number of businesses allowed in one grid)\\
\hline
Total & 832 bytes\\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 1.7: Internal Node \\
\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
Name & Size \\
\hline
Top left coordinates and bottom-right coordinates to identify the grid & 32 bytes (8 bytes $\times$ 4) \\
\hline
Pointers to 4 children & 32 bytes (8 bytes $\times$ 4)\\
\hline
Total & 64 bytes\\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Even though the tree-building process depends on the number of businesses within a g rid, this number does not need to be store in the quad tree node because it can be inferred from records in the database.

Now that we know the data structure for each node, let's take a look at the memory usage.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Each grid can store a maximal of 100 businesses
\item
Number of leaf noes =~ $\frac{200 \text{ million}}{100} =\sim 2 \text{ million }$
\item
Number of internal notes = $2 \text{ million} \times \frac{1}{3} =\sim 0.67 \text{ million }$. If you do not know why
the number of internal nodes is one-third of the leaf nodes, please read the reference material [19]
\item
Total memory requirement = $2 \text{ million} \times 832 \text{ bytes} + 0.67 \text{ million} \times 64 \text{ bytes} =\sim 1.71 \text{ GB}$
\end{itemize}

In a real interview, we shouldn't need such detailed calculations.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The key takeway here is that the quadtree index doesn't take too much memory and can easily fit in one server.
\item
Does it mean we should use only one server to store the quadtree index?
\item
The answer is no
\item
Depending on the read volume, a single quadtree server might not have neough CPU or network bandwidth to server.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{How long does it take to build the whole quadtree?}

Each leaf node contains approximately 100 business IDs.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The time complexity to build the tree is $\frac{n}{100} \text{log} \frac{n}{100}$, where $n$ is the total number of businesses
\item
It might take a few minutes to build the whole quadtree with 200 million businesses
\end{itemize}

\subsection{How to get nearby businesses with quadtree?}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Rebuild the quaddtree in memory
\item
After the quadtree is built, start search from the root and traverse the tree, until we find the leaf node where the seach origin is
\item
If that leaf node has 100 businesses, return the node
\item
Otherwise, add businesses from its neighbors until enough businesses are returned
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Operational considerations for quadtree}

As mentioned above, it may take a few minutes to build a quadtree with 300 million businesses at the server start0up time.

\begin{itemize}
\item
It is important to consider the operational implications of such a long server start-up time
\item
While the quadtree is being built, the server cannot serve traffic
\item
Therefore, we should roll out a new release of the server incrementally to a small subset of servers at a time
\item
This avoids taking a large swath of the server cluster offline and causes service brownout
\item
Blue/green deployment [20] can also be used, but an entire cluster of new servers fetching 200 million businesses at the same time from the
database service can put a lot of strain on the system
\item
This can be done, but it may complicate the design and you should mention that in the interview
\item
Another operational consideration is how to update the quadtree as businesses are added and removed over time
\item
The easiest approach would be to incrementally reuilb d the quadtree, a small subset of servers at a time, across the entire cluster,
\item
But this would mena some servers would return stale data for a short period of time
\item
However, this is generally an acceptable compromise based on the requirements
\item
This can be further mitigated by setting up a business agreement that newly added / updates businesses will only be effective the next day
\item
This means we can update the cache using a nightly job
\item
One potential problem with this approach is that tons of keys will be invalidated at the same time, causing heavly load on cache servers
\item
It's also possible to update the quadtree o9n the fly as businesses are dadded and removed
\item
This certainly complicates the design, especially if the quadtree data structure could be accesssed by multiple threads
\item
This will require some locking mechanism which could dramatically complicate the quadtree implementation
\end{itemize}

Yext [21] provided an image (Figure 1.15) that shows a constructured quadtree near Denver [21]. We want smaller,  more granular grids for
dense areas and larger grids for sparse areas.

\subsection{Option 5: Google S2}

Google S@ geometry library [22] is another big player in this field.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Similar to Quadtree, it is an in-memory solution
\item
It maps a sphere to a 1D index based on the Hilbert curve (a space-filling curve) [23]
\item
The Hilbert curve has a very importatn property: two poitns that are close to each other on the Hilbert curve are close in 1D space (Figure 1.16)
\item
Search on 1D space is much more efficient than on 2D
\item
Interested readers can play with an online tool [24] for the Hilbert curve
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{hilbert_curve}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

S2 is a complicated library and you are not expected to explain its internals during an interview.

But because it's widley used in companeis such as Google, Tinder, etc., we will briefly cover its advantages.

\begin{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
S2 is great fro geofencing because it can cover arbitrary areas with varying levels (Figure 1.17)
\item
According to Wikipedia, ``A geofence is a vritual perimeter for a real-world geographic area''
\item
``A geo-fence could be dynamically generated - as in a raidus around a point location, or a geo-fence can be a predefined set of boundaries (such as school zones or neighborhood boundaries'' (25)
\item
Geofencing allows us to define parameters that surround the areas of interest and to send notifications to users who are out of the areas.
\item
This can provide richer functionalities than just returning nearby businesses
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Another advantage of S2 is its Region Cover algorithm [26]
\item
Instead of having a fixed level (precision) as in geohash, we can specify min level, max level, and max cells in S2
\item
The result returned by S2 is more granular because the cell sizes are flexible
\item
If you want to learn more take a look at the S2 tool [26]
\end{itemize}

\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{geofence}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Recommendation}

To find nearby businesses efficeintly, we have discussed a few options: geohash, quadtree, and S2

As you can see from Table 1.8, different companies or technologies adpot different options.

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
Geo Index & Companies \\
\hline
geohash & Bing map [27], Redis [10], MongoDB [28], Lyft [29] \\
\hline
quadtree & Yext [21] \\
\hline
Both geohash and quadtree & Elasticsearch [30] \\
\hline
S2 & Google Maps, Tinder [31] \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

During an interview, we suggest choosing \textbf{geohash} or \textbf{quadtree} because S2 is more complicated to explain during an interview.

\subsection{Geohash vs quadtree}

Before we conclude this section, let's do a quick comparison between geohash and quadtree

\subsubsection{Geohash}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Easy to use and implement. No need to build a tree
\item
Supports returning businesses within a specified radius
\item
When the precision (level) of geohash is fixed, the size of the grid is fixed as well. It cannot dynamically adjust the grid size, based on population density
\item
More complex logic is needed to suppport this
\item
Updating the index is easy. For example, to remove a business from the index we jsut ened to remove it from the corresponding row with the same \lstinline|geohash| and \lstinline|business_id|. See Figure 1.18 for a concrete example.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Quadtree}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Slightly harder to implement because it eneds to build a tree
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Supports fetching k-nearest businesses and don't care if businesses are within a specified radius
\item
For example, when you are traveling and your car is low on gas, you just want to find the nearest \lstinline|k| gas stations
\item
These gas stations may not be near you, but the app needs to return the nearest \lstinline|k| results
\item
For this type of query, a quadtree is a good fit because its subdividing process is based on the number \lstinline|k| and it can automatically adjust the query range until it returns \lstinline|k| results
\item
It can dynamically adjust the grid size based on population desnity (see the Denver example in Figure 1.15)
\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
Updating the index is more complicated than geohash
\item
A quadtree is a tree stucture
\item
If a business is removed, we need to traverse from the root to the leaf node, to remove the business
\item
For example, if we want to remove the business with \lstinline|ID = 2|, we have to travel from the root all the way down to the leaf node, as shown in Figure 1.19
\item
Updating the index takes \lstinline|O(log n)|, but the implementaiton is complicated if the sdata structure is accessed by a multi-threaded program, as locking is required
\item
Also, reblaancing the tree can be complicated
\item
Rebalacning is necessary if, for exmaple, a leaf node has no room for a new addition
\item
A possible fix is to over-allocate the ranges
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{update_quadtree}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Step 3: Design Deep Dive}

By now you should have a good picture of what the overall system looks like. Now let's dive deeper into a few areas:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Scale the database
\item
Caching
\item
Region and availability zones
\item
Filter results by time or business type
\item
Final architecture diagram
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Scale the database}

We will discuss how to scale two of the most importatn tables: the business table and the geospatial index table.

\subsection{Business table}

The data for the business table may not all fit in one server, so it is a good candidate for sharding.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The easiest approach is to shared everything by busness ID
\item
This sharding scheme ensures that load is evenly distributed among all the shareds, and operationally it is easy to maintain
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Geospatial index table}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Both geohash and quadtree are widely used
\item
Due to geohas's simplicity, we use it asn an example
\item
There are two wasy to structure the table
\item
\end{itemize}

Option 1: for each geohash key, there is a JSON array of business IDs in a single row

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|geospatial_index| \\
\hline
\lstinline|geohash| \\
\lstinline|list_of_business_ids| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

\vspace{10pt}
\centerline{Table 1.9: \lstinline|list_of_business_ids| is a JSON array}

Option 2: If there are multiple businesses in the same geohash, there will be multiple rows, one for each business.

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|geospatial_index| \\
\hline
\lstinline|geohash| \\
\lstinline|business_id| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

\vspace{10pt}
\centerline{Table 1.10: \lstinline|business_id| is a single ID}

Here are some sample rows for option 2:

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|geohash| & \lstinline|business_id| \\
\hline
\lstinline|32feac| & \lstinline|343| \\
\lstinline|32feac| & \lstinline|347| \\
\lstinline|f31cad| & \lstinline|112| \\
\lstinline|f31cad| & \lstinline|113| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}


\vspace{10pt}
\centerline{Table 1.10: \lstinline|business_id| is a single ID}

\textbf{Recommendation}: we recommend option 2 because of the following reasons:

For option 1:

\begin{itemize}
\item
To update a business, we need to fetch the array of \lstinline|business_ids| and scan the whole array to find the business to update
\item
When inserting a enw business, we have to scan the entire array to make sure there is no duplicate
\item
We also need to lock the row to prevent concurrent updates
\item
There are a lot of edge cases to handle
\end{itemize}

For option 2:

\begin{itemize}
\item
If we have two columns with a compound key of \lstinline|(geohash, business_id)|, the addition and removal of a business are very simple
\item
There would be no need to lock anything
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Scale the geospatial index}

One common mistake about scaling the geosptail index is to quickly jump to a sharding scheme without considering the actual data size of the table.

\begin{itemize}
\item
In our case, the full dataset for the geospatial index table is not large (quadtree index only takes 1.71G memory and storage requirement for geohash index is similar)
\item
The whole geospatial index can easily fit in the working set of a modern database server.
\item
HOwever, depending on the read volume, a single database server mgiht not have enough CPU or network badnwidth to hanlde all read requests
\item
If that is the case, it is necessary to spread the read load among multiple database servers
\item
There are two general approaches for spreading the load of a relational database server
\item
We can add read replicas, or shared the database
\item
Many engineers like to talk about sharding during interviews
\item
However, it might not be a good fit fo rthe geohash table as sharding is complicated
\item
For instance, the sharding logic has to be added to the application layer
\item
Sometimes, sharding is only option
\item
In this case,t hough, everything can fit in the working set of a database server, so there is no strong technical reason to shard the data among multiple servers
\item
A better approach, in this case, is to have a series of read replicas to help with the read load
\item
This method is much simpler to develop and maintain
\item
For this reason, scaling the geospatial index table through replicas is recommended
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Caching}

Before introducing a cache layer we ahve to ask ourselves, do we really need a cache layer?

It is not immediately obvious that caching is a solid win:

\begin{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The workload is read-heavy, and the dataset is relatively small
\item
The data could fit in the working set of any modern database server
\item
Therefore, the queries are not I/O bound and they should run almost as fast as an in-memory cache
\end{itemize}

\item
If read performance is a bottleneck, we can add database read replicas to improve the read throughput
\end{itemize}

Be mindful when discussing caching with the interviewer as it will require careful bench marking and cost analysis. If you find out
caching does fit the business requirements then you can proceed with discussions about caching strategy.

\subsection{Cache key}

The most straightforward cache key choic is the location coordinates (latitude and longitude) of the user.

However, this choice has a few issues:

\begin{itemize}
\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
Location coordinates returned from mobile phones are not accurate as they are just the best estimation [32]
\item
Even if you don't move, the results might be slightly different each time you fetch coordinates on your phone
\end{itemize}

\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
A user can move from one location to another, causing location coordinates to change slightly
\item
For most applications, this change is not meaningful
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

Therefore, location coordinates are not a good cache key. Ideally, small changes in location should still map to the same cache key.

The geohash/quadtree solution mentioned earlier handles this problem well because all businesses within a grid map to the same geohash.

\subsection{Types of data to cache}

As shown in Table 1.12, there are two types of data that can be cached to improve the overall performance of the system.

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
Key & Value \\
\hline
geohash & List of business IDs in the grid \\
\hline
\lstinline|business_ids| & Business object \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

\subsection{List of business IDs in a grid}

Since business data is relatively stable, we precompute the list of buisness Ids for a given geohash and store it in a key-value store such as Redis.

Let's take a look at a concrete example of getting nearby businesses with caching enabled.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
Get the list of business IDs for a given geohash.

\begin{lstlisting}
SELECT business_id FROM geohash_index WHERE geohash LIKE `{:geohash}%`
\end{lstlisting}

\item

\begin{lstlisting}
public List<String> getNearbyBusinessIds(String geohash) {
  String cacheKey = hash(geohash);
  List<string> listOfBusinessIds = Redis.get(cacheKey);
  if (listOfBusinessIds == null) {
    listOfBusinessIds = Run the select SQL query above;
    Cache.set(cacheKey, ListOfBsuinessIds, "Id");
  }
}
\end{lstlisting}
\end{enumerate}

When a new business is added, edited, or deleted, the database is updated and the cache invalidated.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Since the volume of those operations is relatively small and no locking mechanism is needed for the geohash approach, update operations
are easy to deal with.
\item
According to the requirements, a user can choose the following 4 radii on the client: 500m, 1km, 2km, and 5km.
\item
Those radii are mapped to geohash lengths of 4, 5, 5, and 6 respectively
\item
To quickly fetch nearby businesses for different radii, we cache data in Redis on all three precision (\lstinline|geohash_4|, \lstinline|geohash_5| and \lstinline|geohash_6|)
\end{itemize}

As mentioned earlier, we have 200 million businesses and each business belongs to 1 grid in a given precision.

Therefor the total memory required is:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Storage for Redis values: $8 \text{ bytes} \times 200 \text{ million} \times 3 \text{ precisions} = \sim 5 \text{ GB }$
\item
Storage for Redis keys: negligible
\item
Total memory required: $\sim 5 \text{ GB } $
\item
We can get away with one modern Redis server from the memory usage perspective but to ensure high availability and reduce cross continent latency, we deploy the Redis cluster across the globe.
\item
Given the estimated data size, we can have the same copy of cache data deployed globally. We call this
\item
We call this Redis cache ``Geohash'' in our final architecture deployment (Figure 1.21)
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Business data needed to render pages on the client}

This type of data is quiet straightforward to cache.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The key is the \lstinline|business_id| and the value is the business object which contains the business name, address, image URLs, etc
\item
We call this Redis cache ``Business info'' in our final architecture diagram (Figure 1.21)
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Region and availability zones}

We deploy a location-based service to multiple regions and availability zones as shown in Figure 1.20.

This has a few advantages:

\begin{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Makes users phyysically ``closer'' to the system
\item
Users from the US West are connected to the data centers in that region, and users from Europe are connected with data centers in Europe
\end{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Gives us the flexibility to spread the traffic evenly across the population
\item
Some regions such as Japan and Korea have high population densities
\item
It might be wise to put them in separate regions, or even deploy location-based services in multiple availability zones to spread the load
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Privacy laws: Some countries may require user data to be used and stored locally in this case, we could set up a region in that country and employ DNS routing to restrict all requests from the country to only that region.
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{deploy_lbs_closer_to_the_user}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}


\subsection{Follow-up question: filter results by time or business type}

The interviewer might ask a follow-up question: how to return buisiness that are open now, or only return businesses that are restaurants?

\begin{itemize}
\item
\textbf{Candidate}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
When the world is divided into small grids with geohash or quadtree, the number of businesses returned from the search result is relatively small
\item
Therefore, it is acceptable to return business IDs first, hydrate business object, and filter them based on opening time or business type
\item
This solution assuems opening time and business type are stored in the business table
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Final design diagram}

Putting everything together, we come up with the following diagram.

\subsection{Get nearby businesses}

\begin{enumerate}
\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
You try to find restaurants within 5000 meters on Yelp
\item
The client sends the user location (latitude = 37.776720, longitude = -122.419730) and radius (500m) to the load balancer
\end{itemize}
\item
The load balancer forward the request to the LBS
\item
Based on the user location and radius info, the LBS find the geohash length that matches the search. By checkint Table 1.5, 500m map to geohash length = 6
\item
LBS calculate neighroing geohashes and adds them to the list. The result looks like this

\begin{lstlisting}
list_of_geohashes = [my_geohash, neighbor1_geohash, neighbor2_geohash, .... , neighbor8_geohash]
\end{lstlisting}

\item
For each geohash in \lstinline|list_of_geohashes|, LBS calls the ``Geohash'' Redis server to fetch corresponding business IDs. Calls to fetch business IDs for each geohash can be made in parallel to reduce latency.

\item
Based on the list of business IDs returned, LBS fetches fully hydrated business in formation from the ``Business info'' Redis server, then calculates distances between a user and businesses, and returns the result to the client
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{View, update, add or delete a business}

All business related APIs are separated from the LBS.

\begin{itemize}
\item
To view the detailed information about a business, the business service first checks if the data is stored in the ``Business info'' Redis cache
\item
If it is, cached data cached data will be returned to the client
\item
If not, data is fetched from the database lcluster and then stored in the Redis cache, allow subsequent requests to get results from the cache directly
\item
Since we have an upfront bsuienss agreement that newly added updates businesses will be effective the enxt day, cached business data is updated by a nightly job
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 4 - Wrap Up}

In this chapter, we have presented the design for proximity service. The system is a typical LBS that lverages geospatial indexing.

We discussed several indexing options:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Two-dimesnional search
\item
Evenly divided grid
\item
Geohash
\item
Quadtree
\item
Google S2
\end{itemize}

Geohash, quadtree, and S2 are widely used by different tech companies.

\begin{itemize}
\item
We choose geohash as an example to show how a geospatial index works
\item
In the deep dive, we discuused why caching is effective in reducing the latency, what should be cached and how to use cache to retrieve
nearby businesses fast.
\item
We also discussed how to scale the database with replication and sharding
\item
We then looked at deploying LBS in different regions and availability zones to improve availability, to make users physicallly closer to the servers  and to comply better with local privacy laws
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{proximity_service}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\chapter{Nearby friends}

In this chapter, we design a scalable dbackend system for a new mobile app feature called ``Nearby friends''.

\begin{itemize}
\item
For an op-in user who grants permission to access their location, the mobile lcient presents a list of friends who are geographically nearby
\item
If you are looking for a real-world example, please refer to this article [1] about a similar feature in the Facebook app.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{facebook_nearby_friends}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

If you read Chapter 1 Proximity Service, you may wonder why we need a separate chapter for designing ``nearby friends'' since it looks similar to proximity services.

\begin{itemize}
\item
If you think carefully though, you will find major differences
\item
In proximity services, the addresses for businesses are static as their locations do not change, while in ``nearby friends'', data is more dynamic because user locations change frequently
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 1: Understand the Problem and Establish Design Scope}

Any backend system at the Facebook scale is complicated. Before starting with the design, we need to ask clarification questions to narrow down the scope.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
Candidate: How geographically close is considered to be ``nearby''?

Interviewer: 5 miles. This number should be configurable.
\item
Candidate: Can I assume the distance is calculated as the straight-line distance between tow users? In real life, there could be, for example, a river in between the users, resulting a longer travel distance.

Interviewer: Yes, that's a reasonable assumption.
\item
Candidate: Do we need to store location history?

Interviewer: Yes, location history can be valuable for different purposes such as machine learning.
\item
Candidate: Could we assume if a friend is inactive for more than 10 minutes, that friend will disappear from the nearby friend list? Or should we dispaly the last known location?

Interviewer: We can assume inactive friends will no longer be shown

\item
Candidate: Do we need to worry about privacy and data laws such as GDPR or CCPA?
Interviewer: Good question. For simplicity, we don't worry about it for now.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Functional requirements}

\begin{itemize}
\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
Users should be able to see nearby friends on their mobile apps
\item
Each entry in the nearby friend list has a distance and a timestamp incidcating when the distance was last updated
\end{itemize}
\item
Nearby friend lists should be updated every few seconds
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Non-functional requirements}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Low latency: It's important to receive location updates from friends without too much delay
\item
Reliability: teh system needs to be reliable overall, but occasional data point loss is acceptable
\item
Eventual consistency: The location data store doesn't need strong consistency. A few second delay in receiving location data in
different replicas is acceptable
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Back of the envelope estimation}

Let's do a back of the envelope estimation to determine the potential scale and challeges  our solution will need to address. Some constrains and assumptions are listed below:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Nearby friends are defined as friends who location are within a 5-mile radius
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The location refresh interval is 30 seconds
\item
The reason for this is that human walking speed is slow (average 3-4 miles per hour)
\item
The distance traveled in 40 seconds does not make a significatn difference on the ``nearby friends'' feature
\end{itemize}
\item
On average 1000 million users use the ``nearby friends'' feature every day
\item
Assume the number of concurrent users is 10\% of DAU (Daily Active Users) so the number of concurrent users is 10 million
\item
On average, a users has 400 friends. Assume all of them use the ``nearby friends'' feature
\item
The app displays 20 nearby friends per page and may load more nearby friends upon request
\end{itemize}


\underline{Calculate QPS}

\begin{itemize}
\item
100 million DAU
\item
Concurrent users: $10\% \times 100 \text{ million} = 10 \text{ million }$
\item
Users report their locations every 30 seconds
\item
Location update QPS = $\frac{10 \text{ million}}{30} =~ 334,000$
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 2: Propose High-level Design and Get Buy-in}

In this section, we will discuss the following:

\begin{itemize}
\item
High-level design
\item
API design
\item
Data model
\end{itemize}

In other chapters, we usually API design and data model before the high-level design.

\begin{itemize}
\item
However, for this problem, the commucation protocol between client and server might not be a straightforward HTTP protocol, as we
need to push location data to all friends
\item
Without understanding the high-level design, it's adifficult to know what the APIs look like
\item
Therefore, we discuss the high-level design first
\end{itemize}

\subsection{High-level design}

At a high level, this problem calls for a design with efficient message passign.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Conceptually, a user would like to receive location updates from every active friend nearby
\item
It could in theory be done puerly peer-to-peer, that is, a user could maintain a persistent connection to every other active friend in the vicinity (Figure 2.2)
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{peer_to_peer}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

This solution is not practical for a mobile device with sometimes faky connections and a tight power consumption budge, but the idea
sheds some light on the general design direction.

A more practical design would have a shared backend and look like this:


\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{shared_backend}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

What are responsibilities of the backend in Figure 2.3?

\begin{itemize}
\item
Receive location updates from all active users
\item
For each location update, find all the active friends who should receive it and forward it to those users' devices
\item
If the distance between two users is over a certain threshold, do not forward it to the recipient's device
\end{itemize}

This sounds pretty simple. What is the issue? Well, to do this at scale is not easy.

\begin{itemize}
\item
We have 10 million active users
\item
With each user updating the location information every 30 seconds, there are 334k updates per seoncd
\item
If on average each user has 100 friends, and we fruther assume that roughly $10\%$ of those friends are online, and nearby, every second
the backend forwards $334K \times 400 \times 10\% = 14$ location updates per second.
\item
That is a lot of updates to forward
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Proposed design}

We will first come up with a high-level design for the backend at a lower scale.

Later in the deep dive section, we will optimize the design for scale.

Figure 3.4 shows the basic design that should satisfy the functional requirements.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{high_level_design}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Load balancer}

The load balancer sits in front of the RESTful API servers and the stateful, bi-directional WebSocket servers. It distributes
traffic across those servers to spread out load evenly.

\subsection{RESTful API Servers}

\begin{itemize}
\item
This is a cluster of stateless HTTP servers that handles the typical request response traffic
\item
The API request flow is high lighted in Figure 2.5:
\item
This API layer handles auxiliary tasks like adding removing friends, updateing user profiles, etc
\item
These are very common and we will not go into more detail.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{restful_api_request_flow}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}


\subsection{Websocket servers}

This is a cluster of stateful servers tha handles the near real-time update of friends locations.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Each client maintains one persistent WebSocket connection to one of these servers
\item
when there is a location update from a friend who is within the search radius the update is sent on this connection to the client
\item
Another major responsibility of tht WebSocket servers is to handle client initialization for the ``nearby friends'' feature
\item
It seeds the mobile client with the locations of all nearby online friends
\item
We will discuss how this is done in more detail later
\item
Note ``WebSocket connection'' and ``WebSocket connection handler'' are interchangeable in this chapter
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Redis location cache}

Redis isused to store the most recent location data for each active user.

\begin{itemize}
\item
There is a Time to Live (TTL) set on each entry in the cache
\item
When the TTL expires, the user is no longer active and the location data is expunged from the cache
\item
Every update refreshes the TTL Other KV stores that support TTL could also be used
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{redis_pub_sub}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

In this design, location  updates received via the WebSocket server are published to the user's own channel in the Redis pub/sub server.

\begin{itemize}
\item
A dedeicated WebSocket connection handler for each active friend subscribes to the channel
\item
When there is a location update the WebSocket handler function gets invoked, and for each active friend, the function recomputes the distance
\item
If the new distance is within the search raidus, the new location and timestamp are sent via the WebSocket connection to the friend's client
\item
Other message buses with lightweight channels could also be used
\item
Now that we understand what each component does, let's examine what happens when a user's location changes form the system's perspective
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Periodic location update}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{periodic_location_update}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}


The mobile client sends periodic location updates over the persistent WebSocket connection. The flown is shown in Figure 2.7.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
The mobile client sends a location update to the load balancer
\item
The load balancer forwards the location update to the persistent connection on the WebSocket server for that client
\item
The WebSocket server saves the location data to the locatio nhistory database
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The WebSocket server update the new location in the location cache
\item
The update refreshes the TTL
\item
The WebSocket server also saves the new location in a variable is the user's WebSocket connection hanlder for subsequent distance
calculations
\end{itemize}
\item
The WebSocket server publishes the new location to the user's channel in the Redis Pub/Sub server. Steps 3 to 5 can be executed in parallel.
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
When Redis Pub/Sub receives a location update on a channel, it broadcasts the update to all the subscribes (WebSocket connection handlers)
\item
In this case, the subscribers are all the online friends of the user sending the update
\item
For each subscriber (i.e., for each of th euser's friends), its WebSocket connection handler would receive the user location update
\item
On receiving the message, the WebSocket server, on which connection hanlder lives, computes the distance between the user sending the new location (the location data is in the message) and the subscriber (the location data is stored in a variable within the WebSocket
connection handler for the subscriber)
\end{itemize}
\item
This step is not drawn on the diagram. If the sdistance does not execced the search radius, the new location and the last updated timestamp are sent to the subscriber's client. Otherwise, the update is dropped.
\end{enumerate}

Since understanind this flow is extremely important,  let's examine it again with a concrete example, as shown in Figure 2.8. Before we start, let's make a few assumptions.

\begin{itemize}
\item
User 1's friends: User 2, User 3, and User 4
\item
User 5's friends: User 4 and User 6
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{send_location_update_to_friends}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
When User 1's location changes, their location update is sent to the WebSocker server which holds User 1's connection
\item
The location is published to User 1's channel in Redis Pub/Sub server
\item
Redis Pub/Sub server brodcast the location update too all subscribers. In this case, subscribers are WebSocket connection handlers (User 1's friends)
\item
If the distance between the user sending the location (User 1) and the subscriber (User 2) doesn't exceed the search radius, the new location is sent to the client (User 2)
\end{itemize}

This compuation is repeated for every subscriber to the channel.

Since there are 100 friends on average, and we sassume that $10\%$ of those friends are online and nearby, there are about 40 location
updates to forward fo reach user's location update.

\subsection{API design}

Now that we have created a high-level design, let's list APIs needed.

\textbf{Websocket}: Users send and receive location updates through the WebSocket protocol. At the minimum, we need the follow APIs.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
\textbf{Periodic location update}

Request: Client sends latitude, longitude, and timestamp

Response: Nothing

\item
\textbf{Client receives location updates}

Data sent: Friend location data and timestamp

\item
\textbf{WebSocket initialization}

Request: Client sends latitude, longitude, and timestamp

Response: Client receives friends' location data
\item
\textbf{Subscribe to a new friend}

Request: WebSocket server sends friend ID

Response: Friend's latest latidtude, longitidue, and timestamp
\item
\textbf{Unsubscribe a friend}

Request: WebSocket server sends friend ID

Response: Nothing
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{HTTP requests}: the API servers handle tasks like add/removing friends, updating user profiles, etc. There are very common and we will not go into detail here.

\subsection{Data model}

Another important element to discuss is the data model. We already talked about the User DB in the high-level dsignm, so let's focus on the location cache and location history database.

\subsection{Location cache}

The location cache stores the latest locations of all active users who have had the nearby friends feature turned on. We use Redis for
this cache. The key/value of the cache is shown in Table 2.1.

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
key1 & value \\
\hline
\lstinline|user_id| & (latitude, longitude, timestamp) \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

\subsection{Why don't we use a database to store location data?}

The ``Nearby friends'' feature only cares about the \textbf{current} location of a user.

Therefore, we only need to store one location per user.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Redis is an excellent choice because it provides super-fast read and write operations.
\item
It supports TTL, which we use to auto-purge users from the cache who are no longer active.
\item
The current locations do not need to be durably stored
\item
If the Redis instance goes down, we could replace it with an empty new instance and let the cache be filled as new location updates stream in
\item
The active users could miss location updates from friends for an update cycle or two while the new cache warms
\item
It is an acceptable tradeoff
\item
In the deep dive section, we will dicuss ways to lessen the impact on users when the cache gets replaced
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Location history database}

The location history database stores users' histoical location data and the schema looks like this:

\begin{tabular}{|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|user_id| & \lstinline|latitude| & \lstinline|longitude| & \lstinline|timestamp| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

We need a database that handles the heavy-write workload well and can be horizontally scaled.

\begin{itemize}
\item
We need a database that handles the heavy-write workload well and can be horizontally scaled.
\item
Cassandra is a good candidate
\item
We could also use a relational database
\item
However, with a relational database, the historical  data owuld not fit in a single instance so we need to shard that data
\item
The most basic approach is to shard by user ID
\item
This sharding scheme ensures that load is evenly distributed among all the shards, and operationally, it is easy to maintain
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 3: Design Deep Dive}

The high-level design we created in the previous section works in most cases, but it will likely break at our scale.

In this section, we work together to uncover the bottlenecks as we increase the scale, and along the way work on solutions to
eliminate those bottlenecks.

\subsection{How well does each component scale?}

\subsubsection{API servers}

The methods to scale the RESTful API tiers are well understood. These are statless servers, and there are many ways to auto-scale the
clusters based on CPU usage, load, or I/O. We will not go into detail here.

\subsubsection{WebSocket servers}

For the WebSocket cluster, it is not difficult to auto-scale based on usage.

\begin{itemize}
\item
However, the WebSocket servers are stateful, so care must be taken when removing existing nodes
\item
Before a node can be removed, all existing connections should be allowed to drain
\item
To achieve that, we can mark a node as ``draining'' at the load balancer so that no new WebSocket connections will be routed to the draining server
\item
Once all the existing connections are closeed (or after a reasonably long wait), the server is then removed
\end{itemize}

Release a new version of the application software on a WebSocket server requires the same level of care.

It is worth nothing that effective auto-scaling of stateful servers is the job of a good load balancer.

Most cloud loud balancers handle this job very well.

\subsubsection{Client initialization}

The mobile client on start up establishes a persisten WebSocket connection with one of the WebSocket server instances.
\begin{itemize}
\item
Each connection is long running.
\item
Most modern languages are capable of maintaining many long-running connections with a reasonably small memory footprint
\item
When a WebSocket connection is initialized the client sends the initial location of the user, and the server performs the following tasks in the WebSocket conenction handler.
\end{itemize}

\begin{enumerate}
\item
It updates the users's location in the location cache
\item
It saves the location in a variable of the connection handler for subsequent calculations
\item
It loads all the user's friends from the user database
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
It makes a batched request to the location cache to fetch the locations for all the friends
\item
Note that because we set a TTL on each entry in the location cache to match out inactivity timeout period, if a friend is inactive then their location will not be in the location cache
\end{itemize}
\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
For each location returned by the cache, the server computes the distance between the user and the friend at that location
\item
If the distance is within the search radius, the friend's profile, location, and last updated timestamp are truend over the WebSocket
connection to the client
\end{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
For each friend, the server subscribes to the frien's channel in the Redis Pub/Sub server
\item
We will explain our use of Redis Pub/Sub shortly
\item
Since creating a new channel is cheap, the user subscribes to all active and inactive friends
\item
The inactive friends will take up a small amount of memory on the Redis Pub/Sub server, but they will not consume any CPU or I/O (since they do not publish updates) until they come online
\end{itemize}
\item
It sends the user's current location ot the user's channel in the Redis Pub/Sub server
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{User database}

The user database holds two distinct sets of data: user profiles (user ID, username, profile URL, etc.) and friendships.

\begin{itemize}
\item
These datasets at our design scale will likely not fit in a single relational database instance: The good news is that the data is horizintally scalable by sharding based on user ID
\item
Relational database sharding is a very common technique
\item
As a side note, at the scale we are designing for, the user and friendhsip datasets will likely be managed by a dedicated team and be
available via an internal API.
\item
In this scenario the WebSocket servers will use the internal API instead of querying the database directly to fetch user and friendship-related data
\item
Whether accessing via API or direct database queries, it does not make much difference in terms of functionality or performance
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Location cache}

We chooose Redis to cache the most recent locations of all active users.

\begin{itemize}
\item
As mentioned earlier, we also set a TTL on each key.
\item
The TTL is renewed upon every location update
\item
This puts a cap on the maximum amount of memory used
\item
With 10 million active users at peak, and with each location taking no more than 100 bytes, a single modern Redis server with many GBs
of memory should be able to easily hold the location information for all users.
\item
However, with 10 million active users roughly updateing every 30 seconds, the Redis server will have to handle 334k updates per second
\item
That is likely a little too high, even for a modern high-end server
\item
Lucily, this cache data is easy to shared
\item
The location data for each user is independent, and we can evenly spread the laod among several Redis servers by sharding the location data based on user ID
\item
To improve availability, we could replicate the location data on each shared to a standby node
\item
If the primary node goes down, the standby could be quickly promoted to minimize downtime
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Redis Pub/Sub server}

The Pub/Sub server is used as a routing layer to direct messages (location updates) from one user to all the online friends.

\begin{itemize}
\item
As mentioned earlier, we choose Redis Pub/Sub ecause it is very lightweight to create new channels.
\item
A new channel is created when someone subscribes to it
\item
If a message is published to a channel that has no subscribes, the message is dropped, placing very little load on the server
\item
When a channel is created, Redis uses a small amount of memory to maintain a hash table and a linked list [3] to track the subscribers
\item
If there is no update on a channel when a user is offline, no CPU cycles are used after a channel is created
\end{itemize}

We take advantage of this oin our design in the following ways:

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
We assign a unique channel to every user who uses the ``nearby friends'' feature
\item
A user would, upon app initialization, subscribe to each friend's channel, whether the friend is online or not
\item
This simplifies the design since the backend does not need to handle subscribing to a frien's channel when the friend becomes active, or handling, unsubscribing when the friend becomes inactive
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The tradoff is that the design would use more mmory
\item
As we will see later, memory use is unlikely to be the bottleneck
\item
Trading higher memory use for a simpler architecture is worth it in this case
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{How many Redis Pub/Sub servers do we need?}

Let's do some math on memory and CPU usage.

\subsubsection{Memory usage}

Assuming a channel is allocated for each user who uses the nearby firneds feature, we need 100 million channels ($1 \text{ billion} \times 10\%$)

\begin{itemize}
\item
Assuming that on average a user has 100 active friends using this feature (this includes friends who are bnearyby, or not), and it takes about 20 bytes of pointers in the internal hash table and linked list to track across each subscriber, it will need about 200GB ($100 \text{ million} \times 20 \text{ bytes} \times 100 \text{ friends} / 10^{9} = 200 \text{ GB}$) to hold all the channels
\end{itemize}

\subsection{CPU usage}

As previously calculated, the Pub/Sub server pushes about 14 million updates per second to subscribers.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Even though it is not easy to esimate with any accuracy how many messages a modern Redis server could push a second without actual benchmarking, it is safe to assume that a single Redis server will not be able to handle that load
\item
Let's pick a conservative number and assume that a modern server with a gigabit netowrk could handle about 100000 subscriber pushes per second
\item
Given how small our location update messages are, this number is likely to be conservative
\item
Using this conservative estimate, we will need to distribute the load among 14 million / 100000 = 140 Redis servers.
\item
Again, this number is liekly too conservative, and the actual number of servers could be much lower
\end{itemize}

From the math, we conclude that:

\begin{itemize}
\item
The bottleneck of Redis Pub/Sub server is the CPU usage, not the memory usage
\item
To support our scale, we need a distributed Redis Pub/Sub cluster
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Distributed Redis Pub/Sub server cluster}

How do we distribute the channels to hundres of Redis servers?

\begin{itemize}
\item
The good news is that the channels are independent of each other
\item
This makes it releatively easy to spread the channels among multiple Pub/Sub servers by sharding, based on the publisher's user ID
\item
Practically speaking though, with hundreds of Pub/Sub servers, we should go into a bit more detail on how this is done so that operationally it is somehwat manageable, as servers inevitably go down from the time to time
\item
Here, we introducer a service discovery component to our design
\item
There are many service discovery packages available, with etcd [4] and ZooKeeper [5] among the most popular ones
\end{itemize}

Our need for the service discover component is very basic

We need these two features:

\begin{enumerate}
\item

The ability to keep a list of servers in the service discover component, and a simple UI or API to update it. Fundamentally, service discovery is a small key-value store for holding configuration data. Using Figure 2.9 as an example, the key and value for the hash ring could look like this

\begin{lstlisting}
Key: /config/pub_sub_ring
Value: ["p_1", "p_2", "p_3", "p_4"]
\end{lstlisting}

\item
The ability for clients (in this case, the WebSocket servers) to subscribe to any updates to the ``Value'' (Redis Pub/Sub servers)
\end{enumerate}

Under the ``Key'' mentioned in point 1, we store  ahash ring of all the active Redis Pub/Sub servers in the service discovery component (see the consistent hashing chapter in Volume 1 of the System Design Interview book or [6] on details of a hash ring)

The hash ring is used by publishers and subscribers of the Redis Pub/Sub servers to determine the Pub/Sub server to talk to for each channel.

For example, channel 2 lives in redis Pub/Suv server 1 in Figure 2.9.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{consistent_hashing}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Figure 2.10 shows what happens when a WebSocket server publishes a location update to a user's channel.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_out_the_correct_redis_server}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}


\begin{enumerate}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The WebSocket server conults the hash ring to determine the Redis Pub/Sub server to write to
\item
The source of truth is stored in service discover, but for efficiency, a copy of the hash ring could be cached on each WebSocket server
\item
The WebSocket server subscribes to any updates on the hash ring to keep its local in memory copy up to date
\end{itemize}

\item
WebSocket server publishes the location update to the user's channel on that Redis Pub/Sub server.
\end{enumerate}

Subscribing to a channel for location updates users the same mechanism.

\subsection{Scaling considerations for redis Pub/Sub servers}

How should we scale the Redis Pub/Sub server cluster?

\begin{itemize}
\item
Should we scale it up and down daily, based on traffic patterns?
\item
This  is a very common practice for stateless servers because it is low risk and saves costs
\item
To answer these questions, let's examine some of the properties of the Redis Pub/Sub server cluster
\end{itemize}

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The messages sent on a Pub/Sub channel are not persisted in memory or on disk
\item
They are not sent to all subscribes of the channel and removed immediately after
\item
If there are no subscribers, the messages are just dropped
\item
In this sense, the data going through the Pub/Sub channel is stateless
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
However, there are indeed states stored in the Pub/Sub servers for the channels
\item
Specifically, the subscriber list for each channel is a key piece fo the states tracked by the Pub/Sub servers
\item
If a channel is moved, which ould happen when the channel's Pub/Sub server is replaced, or if a new server is added or an old server removed on the hash ring, then every subscriber to the moved channel must known about it, so they could unsubscribe from the channel on the old server and resubscribe to the replacement channel on the new server
\item
In this sense, a Pub/Sub server is stateful, and coordination with all subscribers to the server mus be orchestrated to mimimize service interruptions
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

For these reasons, we should treat the Redis Pub/Sub cluster more like a stateful cluster, similar to how we would handle a storage cluster.

\begin{itemize}
\item
With stateful clusters, scaling up or down has some operational overhead and risks, so it should be done with careful planning
\item
The cluster is normally over-provisioned to make sure it can handle daily peak traffic with some comfortable headroom to avoid unnecessary resizing of the cluster
\end{itemize}

When we inevitably have to scale, be mindful of these potential issues:

\begin{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
When we resize a cluster, many channels wil be moved to different servers on the hash ring
\item
Whe the service discovery component notifies all the WebSocket servers of the hash ring update, there will be a ton of resubscription requests
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
During these mass resubscription events, some location updates might be missed by the clients
\item
Although occasional misses are acceptable for our design, we should minimized the occurences
\end{itemize}
\item
Because of the potential interruptions, resizing should be done when usage is at it slowest in the day
\end{itemize}

How is resizing actually done? It is quit simple. Follow these steps:
\begin{itemize}
\item
Determine the new ring size, and if scaling up, provision enough new servers
\item
Update the keys of the hash ring with the new content
\item
Monitor your dashboard. There should be some spike in CPU usage in the WebSocket cluster.
\end{itemize}

Using the hash ring from Figure 2.9 above, if we were to add 2 new nodes, say, \lstinline|p_5|, and \lstinline|p_6|, the hash
ring would be updated like this:

\begin{tabular}{|p{8cm}|}
\hline
Old: \lstinline|["p_1", "p_2", "p_3", "p_4"]| \\
New: \lstinline|["p_1", "p_2", "p_3", "p_4", "p_5", "p_6"]| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

\subsection{Operational condierstaions for Redis Pub/Sub servers}

The operational ris of replacing an existing Reid Pub/Sub server is much, much lower.

It does not cause a large number of channels to be moved.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Only the channels on the server being replaced will need to be hanlded
\item
This is good because servers inevitably go down and need to be replaced regularly
\item
When a Pub/Sub server goes down, the mointoring software should alert the on-call operator
\item
Precisely how the monitoring software monitors the health of a Pub/Sub server is bound the scope of this chapter, so it is not covered
\item
The on -call operator updates the hash ring key in service discover to replace the dead node with a fresh standby node
\item
The WebSocket servers are notified about the update and each one then notififes its connection handlers to re-subscribe to the channels on the new Pub/Sub server
\item
Each WebSocket handler keeps a list of all channels it ahs subscribved to and upong receign the notification from ther server, it checks each channel against the hash ring to determine if a channel need to be re-subscribed on a new server
\end{itemize}

Using the hash ring from Figure 2.9 above, if \lstinline|p_1| went down, and we replace it with \lstinline|p1_new|, the hash ring
would be updated like so:

\begin{tabular}{|p{8cm}|}
\hline
Old:  \lstinline|"p_1", "p_2", "p_3", "p_4"| \\
New:  \lstinline|"p_1_new", "p_2", "p_3", "p_4"| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{replace_pub_sub_serverer}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Adding/removing friends}

What should the client do when the user removed a friend?

\begin{itemize}
\item
When a new friend is added, the client's Websocket connection handler on the server needs to be notified so it can subscribe to the new friend's Pub/Sub channel
\item
Since the ``nearby friends'' feature is within the ecosystem of a larger app, we can assume that the ``nearby feature'' could register a callback on the mobile client whenver a new friend is added
\item
The callback, upon invocation, sends a message to the WebSocket server to subscribe to the new frien's Pub/Sub channel
\item
The WebSocket server also returns a message containing the new friend's latest location and timestamp, if they are active
\item
Likewise, the client could reggister a callback in the application wehenver a friend is removed
\item
The callback would send a message to the WebSocket server to unsubscribe from the friend's Pub/Sub channel
\item
This subscribe/unsubscribe callback could also be used whever a riend has opted in or out of the location update
\end{itemize}

\subsection{users with many friends}

It is worth discussing whether a user with many friends could cause performance hotspots in our design.

\begin{itemize}
\item
We assume ehre that there is a hard cap on the number fo friends.
\item
Facebook has a cap of 5000 friends for example
\item
Friendships are bi-directional
\item
We are not talking about a follower model in which a celebrity could have millions of followers
\item
In a scenario with thousands fo friends, the Pub/Sub subscribers will be scattered among the many WebSocket servers in the cluster
\item
The update load would be spread among them and it's unlikely to cause any hotspots
\item
The user would place a bit more load on the Pub/Sub server where their channel lives
\item
