Since there are over 100 Pub/Sub servers, these ``whale'' users would be spread out among the Pub/Sub servers and the incremental
load should not overwhelm any single one
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Nearby random person}

You might call this section an extra credit, as it's not in the initial functional requirements.

\begin{itemize}
\item
What if the interviewer want sto update the dseign to show random people who opted-in to location-sharing?
\item
One way to do this while leveraging our design is to add a pool of Pub/Sub channels by geohash
\item
(See Chapter 1 Proximity Service for details on geohash)
\item
As shown in Figure 2.12, an area is divided into four geohash grids and a channel is created for each grid
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{redis_pub_sub_channels}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Anyone within the grid subscribes to the same channel. Let's take grid 9q8znd for example as shown in Figure 2.13.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{publish_location_update_to_random_nearby_person}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Here, when user 2 updates their location, the WebSocket connection handler computers the uer's geohash ID and sends the location to the channel for that geohash
iiAnyone nearby who subscribes to the channel (exclude the sender) will receive a location update message
\end{itemize}

To handle people who are close to the border of a geohash grid, every client coudl subscribe to the geohash the user is in and the eight surrounding geohash grids. An example with all 9 geohash grids highlighted is shown in Figure 2.14.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{nine_geohash_grids}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Alternative to Redis Pub/Sub}

Is there any good alternative to using Reid Pub/Sub as the routing layer?

\begin{itemize}
\item
The answer is a resounding yes
\item
Erlang [7] is a great solution for this particular problem
\item
We would argue that Erlang is a better solution than the Redis Pub/Sub proposed above
\item
However, Erlang is quite a niche, and hiring good Erlang programmers is hard
\item
But if your team has Erlang expertise, this is a great option
\item
So, why Erlang?
\item
Erlang is a general programming language and runtime environment built for hihgly distributed and concurrent applications
\item
When we say Erlang here, we specifically talk about the Erlang ecosystem itself
\item
This includes the language component (Erlang or Elixir [8]) and the runtime environment and libraries (the Erlang virtual machine called BEAM [9] and the Erlang runtime libraries called OTP [10])
\item
The power of Erlang lies in its lightweight processes
\item
An Erlang process is an entity running on the BEAM VM
\item
It is several orders of magnitude cheaper to create than a Linux process
\item
A minimal Erlang process takes about 400 bytes, and we can have millions of these processes on a single modern server
\item
If there is no work to do in an Erlang process, it just siets there without using any CPU cycles at all
\item
In other words, it is extremely cheap to model each of the 10 million active users in our design as an individual Erlang process
\item
Erlang is also very easy to distribute among many Erlang servers
\item
The operational overhead is very low, and there are great tools to support debugging live production issues, safely
\item
The deployment tools are also very strong
\item
How would we use Erlang in our design?
\item
We would implement the WebSocket service in Erlang, and also replace the entire dcluster of Reis Pub/Sub with a distributed Erlang application
\item
In this application, each user is modeled as an Erlang process
\item
the user process would receive updates from the WebSocket server when a user's location is updated by the client
\item
The user process also subscribes to updates from the Erlang processes of the user's friends
\item
Subscription is native in Erlang/OTP and it's easy to build
\item
This forms a mesh of connections that would efficiently route location updates from one user to many friends
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 4: Wrap Up}

In this chapter, we presented a design that supports a nearby friends feature

Conceptually, we want to design a system that can efficiently pass location updates from one user to their friends.

Some of the core components include

\begin{itemize}
\item
WebSocket: real-time communication between clients and the server
\item
Redis: fast read and write of location data
\item
Redis Pub/Sub: routing layer to direction location updates from one user to all the online friends
\end{itemize}

We first came up with a high-level design at a lower scale and then discussed channlegfes that arise as the scale increases. We explored how to scale the following:

\begin{itemize}
\item
RESTful API servers
\item
WebSocket servers
\item
Data layer
\item
Redis Pub/Sub servers
\item
Alternative to Redis Pub/Sub
\end{itemize}

We discussed potential bottlnecks when a user has many friends and we proposed a design for the ``nearby random person'' feature.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{chapter2_summary}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}


\chapter{Google Maps}

In this chapter, we design it simple version of Google Maps.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Before we proceed to the system design, let's learn a bit about Google Maps
\item
Google started Porject Google Maps in 2005 and developed a web mapping service
\item
It provides many services such as satellite imagery, street maps, real-time traiffic conditions and route planning [1]
\item
Google Maps helps users find directions and navigate to their desitnation
\item
As of March 2021, Google Maps had one billion daily active users, 99\% coverage of the world, and 25 million updates daily of accurate and real-time location infformation [2]
\item
Given the enormous complexity of Google Maps, it is important to nail down which features our version of it supports
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 1: Understand the Problem and Establish Design Scope}

The interaction between the interviewer and the candidate could look like this:

\begin{enumerate}
\item
\textbf{Candidate}: How many daily active users are we expecting?\\
\textbf{Interviewer}: I billion DAU

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Which features should we focus on? Direction, navigation, and estimated time of arrival?\\
\textbf{Interviewer}: Let's focus on location update, navigation ETA, and map rendering

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: How large is the road data? Can we assume we have access to it?\\
\textbf{Interviewer}: Great questions. Yes, let's assume we obtained the raod data from different sources. It is terabytes (TBs) of raw data

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Should our system take traffic conditions into consideration?\\
\textbf{Interviewer}: Yes, traffic conditions are very importatant for accurate time estimation.

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: How about different travel modes such as driving, walking, bus, etc?\\
\textbf{Interviewer}: We should be able to support different travel modes.

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Should it support multi-stop directions?\\
\textbf{Interviewer}: It is good to allow a user to definte multiple stops, but let's not focus on it.

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: How about business places and photos? How amny photos are we expecting? \\
\textbf{Interviewer}: I am happy you asked and considered these. We do not need to design those.
\end{enumerate}

In the rest of this chapter, wefocus on three key features. The main devices that we need to support are mobile phones.

\begin{itemize}
\item
User location update
\item
Navigation service, including ETA service
\item
Map rendering
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Non-functional requirements and constraints}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Accuracy: Users shoudl not be give the wrong directions
\item
Smooth navigation: On the client-side, users should experience very smooth map rendering
\item
Data and battery usage: The client should use a little data band battery as possible. This is very important for mobile devices.
\item
General availability and scalability requirements
\end{itemize}

Before jumping into the design, we will briefly introduce some basic concepts and terminologies that are helpful in designing Google Maps.

\subsection{Map 101}

\subsubsection{Positioning system}

The world is a sphere that rotates on its axis. At the very top, there is the north pole, and the very bottom is the south pole.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{latitude_and_longitude}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Lat (latitude): denotes how far north or south we are
Long (Longitude): denotes how far east or west we are

\subsection{Going from 3D to 2D}

\begin{itemize}
\item
The process of translating the points from a 3D globe to a 2D plane is called ``Map Projection''
\item
There are different ways to do map projection, and each comes with its own strenghts and limitations
\item
Almost all of them distort the actual geometry
\item
Below we can see more examples
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{map_projections}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Google Maps selected a modified version of Mercator projection called Web Mercator.

For more details on positioning systems and porjections, please refer to [3].

\subsection{Geocoding}

Geocoding is the process fo converting addresses to geographic coordinates.

\begin{itemize}
\item
For instance, ``1600 Amphitheatre Parkway, Mountain View, CA'' is geocoded to a ltitude/longitude pair of (latitidue, 37.423021, longitude -122.083739)
\item
In other direction, the conversion from the latitude/longitude pair to the actual human readable address is called reverse geo coding
\item
On eway to geocode is interpolation [8]
\item
This method lverages the data from different sources such as geographic information systems (GIS) where the street network is mapped to the geographic coordinate space
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Geohashing}

Geohasing is an ecoding system that encodesa geographic area into a short string of letters and digits

\begin{itemize}
\item
At its core, it depicts the eart as a flattened surface and recursively divides the grids into sub-grids, which can be square or rectangular
\item
We represent each grid with a string of numbers between 0 to 3 that are created recursively
\item
``et's assume the initial flattened surface is of size $20,000 \text{ km } \times 10,000 \text{ km }$
\item
After the first division, we would have 4 grids of size $10,000 \text{ km } \times 5,000 \text{ km }$
\item
We represent them as 00, 01, 10, and 11 as shown in Figure 3.3
\item
We further divide each grid into 4 grids and use the same naming strategy
\item
Each sub-grid is now of size $5000 \text{ km } \times 2500 \text{ km }$
\item
We recursively divide the grids until each grid reachces a certain size threshold
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{geohashing}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Geohashing has many uses. In our design, we use geohasing for map tiling. For more details on geohashing and its benefits, please refer to [9]

\subsection{Map rendering}

\begin{itemize}
\item
We won't go into a lot of detail about map rendering here, but it is worth mentioning the basics
\item
One foundation concept to map rendering is tiling,
\item
Instead of rendering the entire map as one large custom image, the world is broken up into smaller tiles
\item
The client only downloads the relevant tiles for the area the user is in and stitches them togher like a mosaic for display.
\end{itemize}

There are distinct sets of tiles at different zoom levels.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The client choooses the set of tiles appropritate for the zoom level of the map viewer on the client
\item
This provides the right level of map details without consuming execess bandwidth
\item
To illustate with an extreme example, when the client is zoomed all the way out to show the entire world, we don't want to have to download hundreds of thousands of tiles for a very high zoom level
\item
All the details would go to waste
\item
Instead, the client would download one tile at the lowest zoom level, which represents the entire world with a single $256 \times 256$ pixel image
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Road data processing}

Most routing algorithms are variations of Dijkstra's or A* pathfinding algorithms.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The exact algorithm choice is a complex topic and we won't go into much detail in this chapter
\item
What is important to note is that all these algorithms operate on a graph data structure where intersections are nodes and roads are
edges of the graph
\end{itemize}

See Figure 3.4 for an example:

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{map_as_a_graph}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The pathfinding performance for most of these algorithsm is extreneley sensitive to the size of the graph.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Representing the entire world of road networks as a single graph would consume too much memory and is liekly too large for any of these algorithms to run efficiently
\item
The graph needs to be broken up into manageable units for these algorithsm to work at our design scale
\item
One way to break up road networks around the world is very similar to the tiling concept we discussed for map rendering
\item
By employing a similar subdivision technique as geohasing, we divide the world into small grids
\item
For each grid, we convert the raods whithin the grid into a small graph data structure that consists of the nodes (intersections) and
edges (roads) inside the geographical area covered by the grid
\item
We call these grids routing  tiles
\item
Each routing title holds rreferences to all the other tiles it connects to
\item
This is how the routing algorithms can stitch together a bigger road graph as it traverses these interconnected routing tiles
\item
By breaking up raod networks into routing tiles that can ve loaded on demand, the routing algorithms can significantly reduce memory consumption and improve pathfinding performance by only consuming a small subset of the routing tiles at a time, and only loading additional tiles as needed
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{routing_tiles}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{defbox}{Reminder}
\begin{itemize}
\item
In Figure 3.5, we call these grids routing tiles
\item
Routing tiles are similar to map tiles in that both a re grids covering certain geographical areas
\item
map tiles are PNG images, while routing tiles are binary files of road data for the area covered by the tiles
\end{itemize}
\end{defbox}

\subsection{Hierarchical routing tiles}

efficient navigation routing also requires having road data at the right level of detail.

\begin{itemize}
\item
For example, for cross country routing, it would be slow to run the routing algorithm against a highly detailed set of street-level routing tiles
\item
The graph stitched from these detailed routing tiles would liekly be too large and cosume too much memory
\item
There are typically three sets of routing tiles with different levels of detail
\item
At the most detailed level, the routing tiles are small and contain only local roads
\item
At the next level, the tiles are bigger and contain only arterial roads connectiong distrcits toeghter
\item
At the lowerest level of detail, the tiels cover large areas and contain only major highways connecting cities and states together
\item
At each level, there could be edges connecting to tiles at a different zoom level
\item
For example, for a freeway entrance from local street A to freeway F, there would be a reference from the node (street A) in the samll tile to the node (freeway F) in the big tile
\item
See figure 3.6 for an example of routing tiles of varying sizes
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{routing_tiles_of_varying_sizes}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Back-of-the-envelope estimation}

Now that we understand the basics, let's do a back-of-the-envelope esitmation

Since the focus of the design is mobile, data usage and battery consumption are two important factos to consider.

Before we dive into the estimation, here are some imperial/metric conversions for reference.

\begin{itemize}
\item
1 foot = 0.3048 meters
\item
1 kilometer (km) = 0.6214 miles
\item
1 km = 1000 meters
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Storage usage}

We need to store three types of data.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Map of the world: A detailed calculation is shown below
\item
Metadata: Given that the metadata for each map tile could be begligible in size, we can skip the metadata in our computation
\item
Road info: The interviewer told us there are TBs of road data from external source. We transform this dataset into routing tiles, which are also likely to be terabytes in size.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Map of the world}

We discussed the concept of map tiling in the ``Map 101'' section on page 60.

\begin{itemize}
\item
There are many sets of map tiles, with one at each zoom level
\item
To get an idea of the stroage requirement for the entire collection of map tile images, it would be informative to estimate the
size of the largest tile set at the highest zoom level first
\item
At zoom level 21, there are about 4.3 million tiles (Table 3.1)
\item
Let's assume that each tile is a $256 \times 256$ pixel compressed PNG image, with the image use of about 100KB.
\item
The entire set at the highest zoom level would need about 4.4 trillion $\times$ 100KB = 440 PB.
\end{itemize}

In table 3.1, we show the progression of the tile counts at every zoom level.

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
Zoom & Number of Tiles \\
\hline
0 & 1 \\
\hline
1 & 4 \\
\hline
2 & 16 \\
\hline
3 & 64 \\
\hline
4 & 256 \\
\hline
5 & 1024\\
\hline
6 & 4096 \\
\hline
7 & 16384 \\
\hline
8 & 65536 \\
\hline
9 & 262144 \\
\hline
10 & 1048576 \\
\hline
11 & 4194304 \\
\hline
12 & 16777216 \\
\hline
13 & 67108864 \\
\hline
14 & 268435456 \\
\hline
15 & 1073741824 \\
\hline
16 & 4294967296 \\
\hline
17 & 17179869184 \\
\hline
18 & 68719476736 \\
\hline
19 & 274877906944 \\
\hline
20 & 1099511627776 \\
\hline
21 & 4398046511104 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

However, keep in mind that about 90\% of the world's surface is natural and mostly inhabited  areas like oceans, deserts, lakes and mountains.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Since these areas are highly compressible as images, we could conservatively reduce the storage estimate by 80 $\sim$ 90\%.
\item
That would reduce the storage size to a range of 44 to 88PB. Let's pick a simple roud number of 50PB.
\item
Next, let's estimate how much storage each subsequent lower zoom level would take.
\item
At each lower zoom level, the number of tiles for both north-south and east-west directions drops by half
\item
This results in a total reducation of the number of tiles by 4x, which drops the storage size for the zoom level by 4x.
\item
With the storage size reduced by 4x at each lower zoom level, the math for the total size is a series: $50 + \frac{50}{4} + \frac{50}{16} + \frac{50}{64} + \cdots =\sim 67$ PB.
\item
This is just a rough estimate
\item
It is good enough to know that we need roughly about 100PB to store all the map tiles at varying levels of detail.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Server throughput}

To estimate the server throughput, let's review the types of requests we need to support.

\begin{itemize}
\item
There are two main types of requests
\item
The first is navigation requests
\item
These are sent by the clients to initiate a navigation session
\item
The second is location update requests
\item
These are sent by the client as the user moves aorund druing a navigation session
\item
The location data is used by down stream services in many different ways
\item
For example, location data is one of the inputs for live traffic data
\item
We will cover the use cases of location data in the design deep dive section
\item
Now we can analyze the server throughput for navigation requests
\item
Let's assume we have 1 billion DAU, and each user on average uses navigation for a total of 35 minutes per week
\item
this translates to 35 billion minutes per week or 5 billion minutes per day
\item
One simple approach would be to send GPS coordinates every second, which results in 300 billion (5 billion minutes $\times$ 60) requests per day or 3 million QPS ($\frac{300 \text{ billion requests}}{10^5}$ = 3 million)
\item
However, the client may not need to send a GPS update every second
\item
we can batch these on the client and send them at a much lower frequency (for example, every 15 seconds or 30 seconds) to reduce the write QPS
\item
The actual frequency  could depend on factors such as how fast the user moves
\item
If they are stuck in traffic, a client can slow down the GPS updates
\item
In our design, we assume GPS updates are batched and then sent to the server every 15 seconds
\item
With this batched approach, the QPS is reduced to 200000 ($\frac{3 \text{ million}}{15}$)
\item
Assume peak QPS is five times average. Peak QPS for location updates = 200000 $\times$ 5 = 1 million
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Setp2- Popose High-level Design and Get Buy-In}

Now that we have more knowledge about Google Maps, we are ready to propose a high level design (Figure 3.7)

\subsubsection{High-level design}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_37_high_level_design}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The high-level design supports three features. Let's take a look at them one by one.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Location service
\item
Navigation service
\item
Map rendering
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Location Service}

The location service is responsible for recording a uer's location update. The architecture is shown in Figure 3.8.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_38_location_service}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The basic design calls for the clients to send location updates every $t$ seconds, where $f$ is a configurable interval.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The periodic updates have several benefits
\item
First, we can lerage the streams of location data to imporve our system over time
\item
We can use the data to monitor live traffic, etetect new or closed roads, and analyze user behavior to enable personalization, for example.
\item
second, we can leverage the location data in near real-time to provide more accurate ETA estimates to the users and to reourte around traffic, if necessary.
\item
But do we really need to send every location update to the server immediately?
\item
The answer is probably no
\item
Location history can be buffered on the client and sent in batch to the server at a much lower frequency
\item
For example, as shown in Figure 3.9, the location updates are recorded  every second, but area only sent to the server as part of a batch ervery 15 second
\item
This significantly reduces the total update traffic sent by all the clients
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_39_batch_requests}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

For a system like Google Maps, even when location updates are batched, the write volume is still very high.

\begin{itemize}
\item
We need a database that is optimzied for high write colume and is highly scalable, such as Cassandra
\item
We may also need to log location data using a stream processing engine such as Kafka for further processing
\item
We discuss this in detail in the deep dive section
\item
What communication protocol might be a good fit here?
\end{itemize}

HTTP with the keep-alive operation [10] is a good choice because it is very efficient.

The HHTP request might look like this:

\begin{lstlisting}
POST /v1/location
Parameters
locs: JSON encoded array of (latitude, longitude, timestamp) tuples
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Navigation service}

This component is responsible for dfinding a reasonably fast route from point A to point B.

\begin{itemize}
\item
We can tolerate a little bit of latency
\item
The calculated route does not have to be the fastest, but accuracy is critical
\item
As shown in Figure 3.8, the user sends an HTTP request to the navigation service through a load balancer
\end{itemize}

The request includes origin and estimation as the parameters. The API might look like this.

\begin{lstlisting}
{
  'distance': {'text':'0.2 mi', 'value': 259},
  'duration': {'text': '1 min', 'value': 83},
  'end_location': {'lat': 37.4038943, 'lng': -121.9410454},
  'html_instructions': 'Head <b>northeast</b> on <b>Brandon St</b> toward <b>Lumin Way</b><div style="font-size:0.9em">Restricted usage road</div>',
  'polyline': {'points': '_fhcFjbhgVuAwDsCal'},
  'start_location': {'lat': 37.4027165, 'lng': -121.9435809},
  'geocoded_waypoints': [
    {
      "geocoder_status" : "OK",
      "partial_match" : true,
      "place_id" : "ChIJwZNMti1fawwRO2aVVVX2yKg",
      "types" : [ "locality", "political" ]
    },
    {
      "geocoder_status" : "OK",
      "partial_match" : true,
      "place_id" : "ChIJ3aPgQGtXawwRLYeiBMUi7bM",
      "types" : [ "locality", "political" ]
    }
  ],
  'travel_mode': 'DRIVING'
}
\end{lstlisting}

Please refer to [11] for more details on Google Map's offical APIs.

So far we have not taken reroute and traffic hanges into consideration. Those problems are tackled by the Adaptive ETA service in the deep dive section.

\subsection{Map rendering}

As we discussed in the back-of-the-envelope estimation, the entire collection of map tiles at various zoom levels is about a hundred
petabytes in size.

It is not practical to hold the entire dataset on the client

The map tiles must be fetch on-demand from the server based on the client's location and the zoom level of the client viewport.

Whwne should the client fetch new map tiles from the server? Here are some scenarios:

\begin{itemize}
\item
The user is zooming and panning the map viewpoint on the client to explore these surroundings
\item
During navigation, the user moves out of the current amp tile into a nearby tile.
\end{itemize}

We are dealing with a lot of data. Let's see hwo we could serve these map tiles from the server efficiently.

\subsection{Option 1}

The server builds the map tiles on the fly, based on the client location and zoom level of the client viewport.

Considering that there is an infinite number of location and zoom level combinations, generating map tiles dynamically has a few severe disadvantages:

\begin{itemize}
\item
It puts a huge load on the server cluster to generate every map tile dynamically
\item
Since the map tiles are dynamically generated, it is hard to take advantage of caching
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Option 2}

Another option is to server a pre-generated set of map tiles at each zoom level.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The map tiles are static, with each tile convering a fixed rectangular grid using a subdivision schema like geohashing
\item
Each tile is therefore represented by its geohash
\item
In other words, there is a unique geohash associated with each grid
\item
When a client needs a map tile, it first determines the map tile collection to use based on its zoom level
\item
It then computes the map tile URL by converting its location to the geohash at the appropriate zoom level
\item
These static, pre-generated images are served by a CDN as shown in Figure 3.10.
\end{itemize}


\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_310_pre_generated_images}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

In the diagram above, the milbe user makes an HTTP request to fetch a tile from the CDN.

\begin{itemize}
\item
If the CDN has not yet served that specific tile before, it fetches a copy from the origin server, caches it locally, and returns it to the user
\item
On subsequent reequests, even if those requests are from different users, the CDN returns a cached copy without contacting the origin server
\item
This approach is more scalable and performant becaues the map tiles are served from the nearest point of presence (POP to the client as hsown in Figure 3.11)
iiThe static nature of the map tiles makes them highly cacheable.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{without_cdn_vs_with_cdn}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

It is important to keep mobile data useage low.

Let's calculate the amount of data the client needs to load during a typical navigation session.

Note the following calculations don't take client-side acching into consideration.

Since the routes a user takes could be similar each day, the data usage is likely to be a lot lower with client-side caching.

\begin{defbox}{Data usage}
\begin{itemize}
\item
Let's assume a user moves at 30 km/h, and at a zoom level where each image covers a block of 200m $\times$ 200m (a block can be represented by a 256-pixel by 256-pixel image and the average image size is 100KB)
\item
For an area of 1 km $\times$ 1 km, we need 25 images or 2.5MB (25 $\times$ 100 KB) of data
\item
Therefore, if the pseed is 30 km/h, we need 75MB  (30 $\times$ 2.5MB) of data per hour or 1.25MB of data per minute
\end{itemize}
\end{defbox}

Next, we estimate the CDN data usage. At our scale, the cost is an important factor to consider.

\begin{defbox}{Traffic through CDN}
\begin{itemize}
\item
As metnioned earlier, we serve 5 billion minutes of navigation per day
\item
This translates to 5 billion $\times$ 1.25MB = 6.25 million MB per day
\item
Hence, we serve 625000MB $\frac{6.25 \text{ million}}{10^5 \text{ seconds in a day}}$ of map data per second
\item
With a CDN, these map images are going to be served from the POPs all over the world.
\item
Let's assume there are 200 POPs
\item
Each POP would only need to esrve a few hundred MBs ($\frac{62500}{200}$) per second
\end{itemize}
\end{defbox}

There is one final detail in the map rendering design we have only briefly touched on.

\begin{itemize}
\item
How does the client know which URLs to use to fetch the map tiles from the CDN?
\item
Keep in mind that we are using option 2 as discussed above
\item
With that option, the map tiles are static and pre-generated based on fixed sets of grids, with each set representing a discrete
zoom level
\item
Since we encode the grids in geohash, and there is one unique geohash per grid, computationally it is very efficient to go from the client's location (in latitude and longitude) and zoom level to the geohash, for the map tile
\item
This calculation can be done on the client and we can fetch any static image tile from the CDN
\item
For example, the URL for the image tile of Google headquarter could look like this: \lstinline|https://cdn.map-provider.com/tiles/9q9hvu.png|
\end{itemize}

Refer to Chapter 1 Proximity Service on page 10 for a more detailed discussion of geohash encoding

Calculating geohash on the client should work well.

\begin{itemize}
\item
However, keep in mind that this algorithm is hardcoded in all the clients on all different platforms
\item
Shipping changes to mobile apps is a time-consuming and somehwat risky process
\item
We have to be sure that geohashing is the method we plan to use long-term to ecndoe the collection of map tiles and that is unlikely to change
\item
If we need to switch to another encoding method for some reason, ti will take a lot of effort and the risk is not low
\item
Here is another option worth considering
\item
Instead of using a hardcoded client-side algorithm to convert a ltatude/longitude (lat/lng) pair and zoom level to a tile URL, we could introduce a service as an intermeidary whose job is to construct the tile URLs based ont he same inputs mentioned above
\item
This is a very simple service
\item
The added operational flexibility might be worth it
\item
This is a very intresting tradeoff discussion we could have with the interviewer
\item
The alternative map rednering flow is whon in Figure 3.12
\item
When a user moves to a new location or to a new zoom level, the map tile service determines which tiles are needed and translates that information into a set of tile URLs to retrieve.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_3_12_map_rendering}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
A mobile user calls the map tile service to fetch the tile URLs. The request is sent to the load balancer
\item
The load balancer forwards the request to the map tile service
\item
The map tile service takes the client's location and zoom level as inputs and returns 9 URLs of the tiles to the client. These tiles include the tile to render and the eight surrounding tiles
\item
The mobile client downloads the tiles from the CDN
\end{itemize}

We will go into more detail on the precomputed map tiles in the design deep dive section.

\subsection{Step 3: Design Deep Dive}

In this section, we first have a discussion about the data mode. Then we talk about location service, navigation service, and map rendering in more detail.

\subsubsection{Data Model}

We are dealing with four types of data: routing tiles, user location data, geo coding data, and precomputed map tiles of the wordl.

\subsubsection{Routing tiles}

As mentioned previously, the initial raod dataset is obtained from different sources and authorities.

\begin{itemize}
\item
It contains terabytes of data
\item
The dataset is improved over time by the location data the application continously collects from ther users as they use the application
\item
This dataset contains a large number of raods and associated metadata such as names, county, longitude, and latitude
\item
This data is not organized as graph data structures and is not usable by most routing algorithms
\item
We run a periodic offline processing pipline, called routing tile processing service, to transform this dataset into the routing tiles we introduced
\item
The service runs periodically to capture new changes to the road data
\item
The output of the routing tile processing service is routing tiles
\item
There are three sets of these tiles at different resolutions, as described in the ``Map 101'' section on page 60
\item
Each tile contains a list of graph nodes and edges representing the intersections and raods within the area covered by the tile
\item
It also contains references to other tiles its raods connect to
\item
These tiles together form an interconnected network of roads that the routing algorithms can consume incrementally
\item
Where should the routing tile processing service store these tiles?
\item
Most graph data is represented as adjancency lists [12] in memory
\item
There are too many tiles to keep the entire set of adjancency lists in memory
\item
We could store the nodes and edges as rows in a database, but we would only be using the database as storage, and it seems an expensive way to store bits of data
\item
We also don't need any database features for routing tiles
\item
the more efficient way to store these tiles is in object storage lik S3 and cache it aggressively on the routing service that uses those tiles
\item
There are many high-performance packages we could use to serialize the adjancency lists to a binary file
\item
We could organize thes4e tiles by their geohashes in object storage
\item
This provides a fast lookup mechanism to locate a tile by lat/lng pair
\item
We discuss how the shortest path service uses these routing tiles shortly
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{User location data}

User location data is valuable

\begin{itemize}
\item
We use it to update our road data and routing tiles
\item
We also use it to build a database of live and historical traffic data
\item
This location data is also consumed by multiple data stream processing services to update the map data
\item
FOr user location data, we need a database that can handle the write-heavy workload well and can be horizontally scaled
\item
Cassandra could be a good candidate
\end{itemize}

Here is what a single row could like

\begin{tabular}{|p{3cm}|p{3cm}|p{3cm}|p{3cm}|p{3cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|user_id| & \lstinline|timestamp| & \lstinline|user_mode| & \lstinline|driving_mode| & \lstinline|location| \\
\hline
101 & 1635740977 & active & driving & (20, 30.5) \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 3.2: Location table

\subsubsection{Geocoding database}

\begin{itemize}
\item
This databse stores places and their corresponding lat/lng pair
\item
We can use a key-value database such as Redis for fast reads, since we have frequent reads and infrequent writes
\item
We use it convert an origin or desitnation to a lat/lng pair before passing it to the route planner service
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Precomputed images of the world map}

\begin{itemize}
\item
When a device asks for a map of a particular area, we need to get enarby roads and compute an image that represents that area with all the roads and related details.
\item
These computations would be heavy and redundant, so it could be helpful to compute them once and then cache the images.
\item
We precompute images at different zoom levels and store them ona CDN, which is backed by cloud storage such as Amazon S3.
\end{itemize}

Here is an example of such an image:

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_3_13_precomputed_tiles}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Services}

NOw that we have discussed the data model, let's take a close look at some of the most important services: location service, map rendering service, and navigation service.

\subsubsection{Location service}

\begin{itemize}
\item
In the high level design, we discussed how location service works.
\item
In this section, we focus on the database design for this service and also how user location is used in detail
\item
In Figure 3.14, the key-value store is used to store user location data
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_3_14_user_location_database}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Given the fact we have 1 million location updates every second, we need to have a database that supports fast writes.

\begin{itemize}
\item
A NoSQL key-value databse or column-oriented database would be a good choice here
\item
In addition, a uer's location is continously changing and becomes stale as soon as a new update arrives
\item
Therefore, we can prioritize availability over consistency
\item
The CAP theorem [13] states that we would choose two attributes among consistency, availability, and partition tolerance
\item
Given our constraints, we would go with availability and partition tolerance
\item
One database that is a good fit is Cassandra
\item
It can handle our scale with a strong availability guarantee
\item
THe key is the combination of (\lstinline|user_id|, \lstinline|timestamp|) and the value is a lat/lng pair.
\item
In this setup, \lstinline|user_id| is the primary key and \lstinline{timestamp} is the clustering key
\item
The advantage of using \lstinline|user_id| as the partition key is that we can quickly read the latest position of specific user
\item
All the data with the same partition key are stored together, sorted by \lstinline|timestamp|
\item
With this arrangement, the retrieval of the location data for a specific user within a time range is very efficient
\end{itemize}

Below is an example of what the table may look like.

\begin{tabular}{|p{2.2cm}|p{2.2cm}|p{2.2cm}|p{2.2cm}|p{2.2cm}|p{2.4cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|key (user_id)| & \lstinline|timestamp| & \lstinline|lat| & \lstinline|long| & \lstinline|user_mode| & \lstinline|navigation_mode| \\
\hline
51 & 132053000 & 21.9 & 89.8 & active & driving \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

\subsection{How do we use the user location data?}

User location data is essential.

\begin{itemize}
\item
It supports many use cases
\item
We use the data to detect new and recently closed roads
\item
We use it as one of the inputs to improve th accuracy of our map over time
\item
It is also a n input for live traffic data
\item
To support these use cases, in addition to writing currnt user locations in our database, we log this information int a message queue,
such as Kafka
\item
Kafka is a unified low-latency, high-throughput data streaming platform designed for real-time data feeds.
\item
Figure 3.15 shows  how Kafka is used in the improved design
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_3_15_location_data_is_used_by_other_services}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Other services consume the location data stream form Kafka for various use cases
\begin{itemize}
\item
For instance, the live traffic service digests the output stream and updates the live traffic database
\item
The routing tile processing service improves the map of the world by detecting new or closed roads and updating the affected routing tiles in object storage
\item
Other services can also tap into the stream for different purposes
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Rendering map}

In this section, we dive deep into prcomputed map tiles and amp rednering optimization. THey are primary inspired by the work of Google Design [3].

\subsubsection{Precomputed tiles}

As mentioned previously, there are different sets of precomputed map tiles at various distinct zoom levels to provide the approrpiate level of map detail to the user, based on the client's viewport size and zoom level
\begin{itemize}
\item
Google Maps users 21 zoom levels (Table 3.1)
\item
THis is what we use, as well
\item
Level 0 is the most zoom-out level
\item
The entire map is represented by a single tile of size $256 \times 256$ pixels
\item
WIth each increment of the zoom level, the number of map tiles doubles in both north-south and east-west directions, while each tile
stays at $256 \times 256$ pixel
\item
As shown in Figure 3.16, at zoom level 1, there are $2 \times 2$ tiles, with a total combined resolution of $512 \times 512$ pixels
\item
At zoom level 2, there are $4 \times 4$ tiles, with a total combined resolution of $1024 \times 1024$ pixels
\item
With each increment, the entire set of tiles has $4\times$ as many pixels as the previous level.
\item
THe increased pixel count provides an increasing level of detail to the user
\item
This allows the client to render the map at the best granularities depending on the clinet's zoom level, without consuming excessive bandwidth to download tiles in excessive detail
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_3_16_zoom_levels}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Optimization: Use vectors}

With the development and implemtnation of WebGL, one potential imporvement is to change the design from sending the images over the network, to sending the vector information (paths and polygons) instead.

The client draws the paths and polygons from the vector information.

\begin{itemize}
\item
One obvious advantage of vector tiles is that vector data compresses much better than images do.
\item
The bandwidth saving is substantial
\item
A less obvious benefit is that vector tiles provide a much better zooming experience
\item
With rasterized images, as the client zooms in from one level to another, everything gets stretched and looks pixelated
\item
The visual effect is pretty jarring
\item
With vectorized images the client can scale each element appropriately, providing a much smoother zooming experience
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Navigation service}

Next, let's deep dive into the navigation service. This service is responsible for finding the fastest routes. The design diagram is shown in Figure 3.17.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_3_17_navigation_service}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Geocoding service}

\begin{itemize}
\item
First, we need to have a service to resulve an address to a location of a lattidude and longitude pair
\item
An address could be in different formats for example, it could be the name of a place or a textual address
\end{itemize}

Here is an example request and response from Google's geocaching API:

\textbf{Request:}

\begin{lstlisting}
https://maps.googleapis.com/maps/api/geocode/json?address=1600+Amphitheatre+Parkway,+Mountain+View,+CA
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{JSON response:}

\begin{lstlisting}
{
  "results" : [
    {
      "formatted_address" : "1600 Amphitheatre Parkway, Mountain View, CA 94043, USA",
      "geometry" : {
        "location" : {
          "lat" : 37.4224764,
          "lng" : -122.0842499
        },
        "location_type" : "ROOFTOP",
        "viewport" : {
          "northeast" : {
            "lat" : 37.4238253802915,
            "lng" : -122.0829009197085
          },
          "southwest" : {
            "lat" : 37.4211274197085,
            "lng" : -122.0855988802915
          }
        }
      },
      "place_id" : "ChIJ2eUgeAK6j4ARbn5u_wAGqWA",
      "plus_code" : {
        "compound_code" : "CWC8+W5 Mountain View, California, United States",
        "global_code" : "849VCWC8+W5"
      },
      "types" : [ "street_address" ]
    }
  ],
  "status" : "OK"
}
\end{lstlisting}

The navigation service calls this service to geocode the origin and the destination before passing the latitude/longitude pairs downstream to find the routes.

\subsection{Route planner service}

This services computes a suggested rout that is optimzied for tavel time according to current traffic and road conditions. It interacts
with serveral services which are discsused next.

\subsection{Sourtest-path service}

THe shortest-path service receives the origiin and the desintation in lat/lng pairs and returns the top-k shortest paths without considering traffic or current conditions

\begin{itemize}
\item
THis computation only depends on the structure of the reaods
\item
Here, caching the routes could be beneficial because the graph rarely changes
\item
\end{itemize}

The shoretest-path services runs a variabtion of A* pathfind algorithsm aginst the routing tiles in object storage.

Here is an overview:

\begin{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The algorithm receives the origin and desitnation in lat/lng pairs
\item
The lat/lng pairs are converted to geohashes which are then used to load the start and end-points of routing tiles
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The algorithm starts from the origin routing tile, travsers the graph data structure, and hydrates additinal neighoboring tiles from object storage (or its local cache if it has loaded it before) as it expands the search area
\item
It's worth nothign that there are connections from one level of tile to another covering the same area
\item
This is how the algorithm could ``enter'' the bigger tiles containing only highways, for example
\item
THe algorithm continues to expand its search by hudrating more neighboring tiles (or tiles at differentesolutions) as needed until a set of best routes is found
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

Figure 3.18 (based on [14]) gives a conceptual overview of the tiles used in the graph traversal.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_3_18_graph_traversal}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{ETA service}



\begin{itemize}
\item
Once the route planner receives a lit of possible shortest paths, it caals the ETA service for each possible route and gets a time estimate.
\item
For this, the ETA services uses machine learning to predict the ETAs based onthe current traffic and historical data.
\item
One of the challenges here is that we not only need to have real-time traffic data bu also to predict how the traffic will look like in 10 or 20 minutes
\item
These kinds of challenges need to be addressed at an aglrithmic level and will not be discussed in this section if you are interested, refer to [15] and [16]
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Ranker service}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Finally, after the route pallner obtains the ETA predictions, it passes this info to the ranker to apply possible filters
as defined by the user.
\item
Some example filters include operations to avoid toll roads or to avoid freeways
\item
The ranker service then ranks the possible routes from fastest to slowest and returns top-k results to the navigation service
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Updater dservices}

These services tap into the Kafka location update stream and asynchronously update some of the important databases to keep them up-to-date.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The traffic database and the routing tiles are some examples
\item
The routing tile processing service is responsible for transforming the road dataset with newly found roads and road closures into a continously updated set of routing tiles
\item
This helps the shortest path service to be more accurate
\item
THe traffic update service extracts traffic conditions from the streams of locaiton updates sent by the active users
\item
This insight is fed into the live traffic database
\item
This enables the ETA service to provide more accurate estimates
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Improvement: adaptive ETA and rerouting}

The current design does not support adaptive ETA and rerouting. To address this, the server needs to keep track of all the active navigating users and update them on ETA continously, whever traffic conditions change.

Here we need to answer a few important questions:

\begin{itemize}
\item
How do we track actively navigating users?
\item
HOw do we stores the data, so that we can efficiently locate the users affected by traffic changes among millions of navigation routes?
\end{itemize}

Let's start with a naive solution. In Figure 3.19, \lstinline|user_1|'s navigation route is represented by routing tiles \lstinline|r_1|, \lstinline|r_2|, \lstinline|r_3|, ..., \lstinline|r_7|.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_3_19_navigation_route}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The database stores actively navigating users and routes information which might look like this:

The database stores actively navigating users and routes information which might look like this:

\begin{lstlisting}
user_1: r_1, r_2, r_3, ..., r_k
user_2: r_4, r_6, r_9, ..., r_n
user_2: r_2, r_8, r_9, ..., r_m
...
user_n  r_2, r_10, r_21, ..., r_l
\end{lstlisting}

Let's say there is a traffic incident in routing tile 2 (\lstinline|r_2|). To figure out which users are affected, we scan
through each row and check if routing tile 2 is in our list of routing tiles (see example below),

\begin{lstlisting}
user_1: r_1, r_2, r_3, ..., r_k
user_2: r_4, r_6, r_9, ..., r_n
user_3: r_2, r_8, r_9, ..., r_m
\end{lstlisting}

Assume the number of rows in the table is \lstinline|n| and the average length of the navigation route is \lstinline|m|,

The time complexity to find all users affected by the traffic change is \lstinline|O(n x m)|.

Can we make this process faster?

Let's explore a different approach

For each actively navigating user, we keep the current routing tile, the routing tile at the next resolution level that contains it, and recursively find the routing tile at the next resolution level until we find the user's destination in the
tile as well (Figure 3.20)

By doing this, we can get a row of the database table like this.

\begin{lstlisting}
user_1, r_1, super(r_1), super(super(r_1))
\end{lstlisting}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_3_20_building_tiles}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

To find out if a user is affected by the traffic change, we need only check if a routing tile is inside the last routing tile of a row in the databse.

\begin{itemize}
\item
If not, the user is not impacted. If it is, the user is affected.
\item
BY doing this, we can quickly filter out many users
\item
This approach doesn't specify what happens when traffic clears
\item
For example, if routing tile 2 clears sand users can go back to the old route, how do users know reoruting is available
\item
One idea is to keep track of all possible routes for navigating user, recalculate the ETAs regularly and notify the user if a new route with a shorter ETA is found.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Delivery protocols}

It is a reality that during navigation, route conditions can change and the server needs a reliable way to push data to mobile clients.

For delivery protocol from the server to the client, our options include mobile push notification, long polling, WebSocket, and Server-Sent Events (SSE).

\begin{itemize}
\item
Mobile push notification is not a great option because the apyload size is very limited (4096 bytes for iOS) and it doesn't support web applications
\item
WebSocket is generally considered to be a better option than long polling because it has a very light footprint on servers
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Since we have rules out the mobile push notification and long polling, the choice mainly between WebSocket, and Server-Sent (SSE)
\item
Even though both can work, we lean towards webSocket because it supports bi-directional communication and features such as last-minute delivery might require bi-directional real-time communication
\end{itemize}

\end{itemize}

For more details about ETA and rerouting, please refer to [15].

Now we have every piece of the design together. Please see the update design in Figure 3.21.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_3_21_final_design}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

IN this chapter, we designed a simplified Google Maps application with key features sush as location update, ETAs, route planning and map rendering.

\begin{itemize}
\item
If you are interested in expanding the system, one potential improvement would be to provide multi-stop navigation capability for enterprise cusomters
\item
For example, for a given set of destinations, we have to find the optimal order in which to visit them all and provide proper navigation, based on live traffic conditions
\item
This could be helpful for deliver services such as DoorDash, Uber, Lyft, etc.
\end{itemize}

\chapter*{Chapter 4}

In this chapter, we explore a popular question in system design itnerviews: design a distributed message queue.

\begin{itemize}
\item
In modern architecture, systems are broke up into small and independent building blocks with well-defined interfaces between them.
\item
Message queues provide communication and coordination for those building blocks.
\end{itemize}

What benefits do message queues bring?

\begin{itemize}
\item
Decoupling. Message queues eliminate the tight coupling between components so they can be updated independently.
\item
Improved scalability. We can scal producers and consumers independelty based on traffic load. For example, during peak hours, more consumers can be added to handle the increased traffic
\item
Increased availability. If one part of the system goes offline, the other components can continue to interact with the queue
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Better performance. Message queues make asynchronous communication easy.
\item
Producers can add messages to a queue without waiting for the response and consumers consume messages whenever they are available
\item
They don't need to wait for each other
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

Figure 4.1 shows some of the most popular distributed message queues on the market.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_1}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Message queues vs event streaming platforms}

Strictly speaking, Apache Kafka and Pulsar are not message queues as they are event streaming platforms.

However, there is a convergence of features that starts to blur the distinction between message queues (RocketMQ, ActiveMQ, RabbitMQ, ZeroMQ, etc.) and event streaming platforms (Kafka, Pulsar).

\begin{itemize}
\item
For example, RabbitMQ, which is a typical message queue, added an optional streams to allow repeated message consumption and long message retention, and its implementation uses an appened-only log, much like an event streaming platform would
\item
Apache Pulsar is primarily a Kafa competitor but it is also flexible and performant enough to be used as a typical distributed message queue
\end{itemize}

In this chapter, we will design a distributed message queue with \textbf{additional features, such as long data retention, repeated consumption of messages, etc.,} that are typically only available on event streaming platforms.

These additional features make the design more complicated.

Throughout the chapter, we will highlight places where the design could be simplified if the focus of your interview centers around the more traditional distributed message queues.

\subsection{Step 1: Understand the Problem and Establish Design Scope}

In a nutshell, the basic functionality of a message queue is striaghtforward: producers send messages toa queue, and consumers consume messages from it.

Beyond this basic functionality, there are other considerations including performance, message delivery semantics, data detention, etc.

THe following set of questions will help clarify requirements and narrow down the scope.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
\textbf{Candidate}: What's the format and average size of messages? Is it text only? Is multimedia allowed?

\textbf{Interviewer}: Text messages only. Messages are generally measured in the range of kilobytes (KBs)
\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Can messages be repreatedly consumed?

\textbf{Interviewer}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Yes, messages can be repeatedly consumed by different consumers.
\item
Note that is an added feature.
\item
A traditional distributed message queue does not retain a message once it has been successfully delivered to a consumer
\item
Therefore, a message cannot be repeatedly consumed in a traditional message queue
\end{itemize}

\item

\textbf{Candidate}: Are messages consumed in the same order they were produced?

\textbf{Interviewer}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Yes, messages should be consumed in the same order they were produced
\item
Note that this is an added feature
\item
A traditional distributed message queue does not usually guarantee delivery orders
\end{itemize}
\item

\textbf{Candidate}: Does data need to be persisted and what is the data retention?

\textbf{Interviewer}: Yes, let's assume data retention is two weeks. This is an added feature. A traditional distributed message queue does not retain messages.

\item

\textbf{Candidate}: How many producers and consumers are we going to support?

\textbf{Interviewer}: The more the better

\item

\textbf{Candidate}: What's the data delivery semantic we need to support? FOr example, at most-once, at-least-once, and exactly once.

\textbf{Interviewer}: We definitely want to support at-least-once. Ideally, we should support all of them and make them configurable

\item

\textbf{Candidate}: What's the target throughput and end-to-end latency?

\textbf{Interviewer}: It should support high throughput for use cases like log aggregation. I should also support low latency delivery for more traditional message queue use cases.
\end{enumerate}

With the above conversation, let's assume we have the following functional requirements:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Producers send messages to a message queue
\item
Consumers consume messages from a message queue
\item
Messages can be consumed repeatedly or only once
\item
Historical data can be truncated
\item
Message size is in the kilobyte range
\item
Ability to deliver messages to consumers in the order they were added to the queue
\item
Data delivery semantics (at-least once, at-most once, or exactly once) can be configured by users
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Non-functional requirements}

\begin{itemize}
\item
High throughput or low latency, configurable based on use cases
\item
Scalable. The system should be distributed in nature, It should be able to support a sudden surge in message volume
\item
Persistent and durable. Data should be persisted on disk and replicated across multiple nodes
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Adjustments for traditional message queues}

Traditional message queues like RabbitMQ do not have as strong a retention requirement as event streaming platforms.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Traditional queues retain messages in memory just long enough for them to be consumed
\item
THey provide on-disk overflow capacity [1] which is several orders of magnitude smaller than the capacity required for event streaming platforms
\item
Traditional message queues do not typically maintain message ordering
\item
The messages can be consumed in a different order than they were produced
\item
These differences greatly simplify the design which we will discuss where appropriate
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Step 2: Propose High-level Design and Get Buy-in}

First, let's discuss the basic functionalities of a message queue.

Figure 4.2 shows the key components of a message queue and the simplified interactions between these components.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_2}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Producer send messages to a message queue
\item
Consumer subscribes to a queue and consumes the subscribed messages
\item
Message queue is a service in the middle that decouples the producers from the consumers, allowing each of them to operate and scale independently
\item
Both producer and consumer are clients in the client/server model, while the message queue is the server. THe clients and servers communicate over the network
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Messaging models}

The most popular messaging models are point-to-point model, its capabilities map more naturally to the publish-subscribe model.

\subsubsection{Publish-subscribe}

First, let's introduce a new concept, the topic.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Topics are the categoriesused to organize messages
\item
Each topic has a name that is unique across the entire message queue service
\item
Messages are sent to and read from a specific topic
\item
IN the publish-subscribe model, a message is sent to a topic and received by the consumers subscribing to this topic
\item
As shown in Figure 4.4, message A is consumed by both consumer 1 and consumer 2
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_4}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Our distributed message queue supports both models.
\item
THe publish-subscribe model is implemented by \textbf{topics}, and the point-to-point model can be simulated by the concept of the \textbf{consumer group}, which will be introduced in the consumer group section
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Topics, partitions, and brokers}

As mentioend earlier, messages are persisted by topics. What if the data volume in a topic is too large for a single server to handle?

One approach to solve this problem is called \textbf{partition (sharding)}

\begin{itemize}
\item
As Figure 4.5 shows, we deivid a topic into partitions and deliver messages evenly across partitions
\item
Think of a partition as a msall subset of the messages for a topic
\item
Partitions are evenly distributed across the servers in the message queue cluster
\item
These servers that hold partitions are called \textbf{brokers}
\item
The distribution of partitions among brokers is the key element to support high scalability
\item
We can scale the topic capacity by expanding the number of partitions
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_5}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Each topic partition operates in the form of a queue with the FIFO (first in, first out) mechanism

\begin{itemize}
\item
Thie means we can keep the order of messages inside a partition
\item
The position of a message in the partition is called an \textbf{offset}
\item
When a message is sent by a producer, it is actually sent to one of the partitions for the topic
\item
Each message has an optional message key (for example, a user's ID), and all messages for the same message key are sent to the same partition
\item
If the message key is absent, the message is randonly sent to one of the partitions
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_5}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Each topic partition operates in the form of a queue with the FIFO (first in, first out) mechanism.

\begin{itemize}
\item
This means we can keep the order of messages inside a parittion
\item
THe position of a message in the partition is called an \textbf{offset}
\item
When a message is sent by a producer, it is actually sent to one of the parittions for the topic
\item
Each message has an optional message key (for example, a user's ID), and all messages for the same message key are sent to the same partition
\item
If the message keykey is absent, the message is randly sent to one of the partitions
\end{itemize}

When a consumer subscribes to a topic, it pulls data from one or more of these partitions.

The consumers form a \textbf{consumer group} in a topic.

The message queue cluster with brokers and partitions is represented in Figure 4.6.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_6}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Consumer group}

As mentioned earlier, we need to support both point-to-point and subscribe-publish models.

\textbf{A consumer group} is a set of consumers, working together to consume messages from topics.

Consumers can be organized into groups. Each consumer group can subscribe to multipel topics and maintain its own consuming offsets.

For example, we can group consumers by use cases, one group for billing and the other for accounting.

The instances in the same group can consume traffic in parallel, as in Figure 4.7.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Consumer group 1 subscribes to topic A
\item
Consumer group 2 subscribes to both topics A and B
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Topic A is subscribed by both consumer groups-1 and group-2, which means the same message is consumed by multiple consumers
\item
This pattern supports the subscribe/publish model
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_7}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

However, there is one problem. Reading data in paralllel improves the thorughput, but the consumption order of messages in the same parittion cannot be guaranteed.

\begin{itemize}
\item
For example, if Consumer-1 and Consumer-2 both read from Partition-1, we will not be able to guarantee the message consumption order in Partition-1
\item
THe good news is we can fix this by adding a constraint, that a single a single partition can only be consumed by one consumer in the same group
\item
If the number of consumers of a group is larger than the number of partitions of a topic, some consumers will not get data from this topic
\item
For example, in FIgure 4.7, Consumer-3 in Consumer group-2 cannot consume message from topic B because it is consumed by Consumer-4 in the same consumer group, already
\item
With this constraint, if we put all consumers in the same consumer gorup, then messages in the same partition are consumed by only one consumer, which is equivalent to the point-to-point model
\item
Since a partition is the smallest storage unit, we can allocate enough parititions in advance to avoid the need to dynamically increase the number of partitions
\item
To handle high scale, we just need to add consumers
\end{itemize}

\subsection{High-level architecture}

Figure 4.8 shows the updated high-level design.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_8}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Clients
\begin{itemize}
\item
Producer: pushes messages to specific topics
\item
Consumer group: subscribes to topics and consumes messages
\end{itemize}

Core service and storage

\begin{itemize}
\item
Broker: holds multiple partitions. A partition holds a subset of messages for a topic
\item
Storage:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Data storage: messages are persisted in data storage in partitions
\item
State storage: consumer states are managed by state storage
\item
Metadata storage: configuration and properties of topics are persisted in metadata
\end{itemize}
\item
Coordination service:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Service discover: which brokers are alive
\item
Leader elelection: one of the brokers is selected as the active controller THere is only on e active controller in the cluster. THe active contrller is reponsible for assigning partitions
\item
Apache ZooKeeper [2] or etcd [3] are commonly used to elect a controller
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 3: Design Deep Dive}

To achieve high throughput while satisfying the high data retention requirement, we made three important design choices, which we explain in detail now.

\begin{itemize}
\item

\item
We chose an on-disk data structure that takes advantage of the great sequential acccess performance of rotational disks and the aggressive disk caching stregy of modern operating systems.
\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
We dicussed the message data structure to allow a message to be passed from the producer to the queue and finally to the consumer, with no modifications
\item
This minimizes the need for copying which is very expensive in a high volume and high traffic system
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
We designed the system to favor batching
\item
Small I/O is an enemy of high throughput
\item
SO, wherever possible, our deisng encourages batching
\item
The producers send messages in batches
\item
The message queue persists messages in even larger bathces
\item
The consumers fetch messages in batches when possible too
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Data storage}

Now let's explore the options to persist messages in more detail, In order to find the best choice, let's consider the trafffic pattern of a message queue.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Write-heavy, read-heavy
\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
No update or delete operations
\item
As a side note, a traditional message queue does not persist messages unless the queue falls behind, in which case there will be ``delete'' operations when the queue cathces up
\item
What we are talking about here is the persistence of a data streaming platform
\end{itemize}
\item
Predominantly sequential read/write access.
\end{itemize}

Option 1: Database

The first option is to use a database.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Relational database: create a topic table and write messages to the table as rows
\item
NoSQL database: create a collection as a topic and write messages as documents
\end{itemize}

Databases can handle the storage requirement, but they are not ideal because it is hard to design a database that supports both write-heavy and read-heavy access patterns at a large scale.

The database solution does not fit our specific data usage patterns very well.

This means a database is not the best choice and could become a bottleneck of the system.


Option 2: Write-ahead log (WAL)

The second option is write-ahead log (WAL).

\begin{itemize}
\item
WAL is just a plain file where new entries are appended to an append-only log
iiWAL  is used in many systems, such as the redo log in MySQL [4] and the WAL in ZooKeeper
\item
We recommend persisting messages as WAL log files on disk
\item
WAL has a pure sequential read/write access pattern
\item
The disk performance of sequential access is very good [5]
\item
Also, rotational disks have large capacity ant they are pretty affordable
\item
As shown in Figure 4.9, a new message is appended to the tail of a partition, witha monotonically increasing offset
\item
THe easiest option is to use the line number of the log file as the offset
\item
However, a file cannot grow infinitely, so it is a good idea to divide it into segments
\item
With segments, new messages are appended only to the active segment file
\item
When the active segment reahces a certain size, a new active segment is created to receive new messages, and the currently active segment becomes inactive, like the rest of the non-active segments
\item
Non-active segments only serve read requests
\item
Old non-active-segment files can bvbe truncated if they exceeed the retention or capacity limit
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_9}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Segment files of the same parittion are oganized in a folder named \lstinline|Parittion-{:partition_id}|. The structure is shown in Figure 4.10.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_10}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{A note on disk performance}

To meet the high data retention requirement, our design relies heavily on disk drives to hold a large amount of data.

\begin{itemize}
\item
There is a common misconception that rotational disks are slow, but this is really only the case for random access
\item
FOr our workload, as long as we deisgn our on-disk data structure to take advantage of the sequential access pattern, the moden disk drives in a RAID configuration (i.e. with disks striped together for higher performance) could comfortably achieve several hundred MB/sec of read and write speed
\item
This is more than enough for our needs, and the coststructure is favorable
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Message data structure}

The data structure of a message is key to high throughput.

\begin{itemize}
\item
It defines the contract between the producers, message queue, and consumers
\item
Our design achieves high performance by eliminating unnecessary data copying while the messages are in transit from the producers to the queue and finally to the consumers
\item
If any parts of the system disagree on this contract, messages will need to be mutated which invles expensive copying. It could seriously hurt the performance of the system.
\end{itemize}

Below is a sample schema of the mssage data structure

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
\textbf{Field Name} & \textbf{Data Type} \\
\hline
\lstinline|key| & \lstinline|byte[]| \\
\hline
\lstinline|value| & \lstinline|byte[]| \\
\hline
\lstinline|topic| & \lstinline|string| \\
\hline
\lstinline|partition| & \lstinline|integer| \\
\hline
\lstinline|offset| & \lstinline|long| \\
\hline
\lstinline|timestamp| & \lstinline|long| \\
\hline
\lstinline|size| & \lstinline|integer| \\
\hline
\lstinline|crc| & \lstinline|integer| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

\subsection{Message key}

The key of the message is used to determine the partition of the message.

\begin{itemize}
\item
If the key is not defined, the partition is randomly chosen
\item
Otherwise, the partition is chosen by \lstinline|hash(key) % numPartitions|
\item
If we need more flexilibility, the producer can define its own mapping algorithm to choose partitions
\item
Please note that the key is not equivalent to the partition number
\item
The key can be a string or a number
\item
It usually carries some business infoormation
\item
The partition number is a concept in the message queue, which should not be explicitly exposed to clients
\item
The key can be a string or a number
\item
It usually carries some buisness information
\item
The partition number is a concept in the message queue, which should not be explicitly exposed  to clients
\item
With a proper mapping algorithmm, if the number of partitions changes, messages can still be evenly sent to all the partitions
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Message value}

The message value is the payload of a message. It can be plain text or a compressed binary block.

\begin{defbox}{Reminder}
\begin{itemize}
\item
The key and value of a message are different from the key-value pair in a key-value (KV) store
\item
In the KV store, keys are unique, and we can find the value by key
\item
In a message, keys do not need to be unique
\item
Sometimes they are not even mandatory, and we don't need to find a value by key
\end{itemize}
\end{defbox}

\subsubsection{Other fields of a message}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Topic: the name of the topic that the message belongs to
\item
Partition: the ID of the partition that the message blongs to
\item
Offset: the position of the message in the partition. We can find a message via the combination of three fields: topic, partition, offset
\item
Timestamp: the timestamp of when this message is stored
\item
Size: the size of this message
\item
CRC: Cyclic redundancy check (CRC) is used to ensure the integrity of raw data
\end{itemize}

To support additional features, some optinal fields can be added on demand. For example, messages can be filtered by tags, if tags are part of the optional fields.

\subsection{Batching}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Batching is pervasive in this design
\item
We batch messages in the producer, the consumer, and the message queue itself
\item
Batching is critical to the performance of the system
\item
In this section, we focus primarily on batching in the message queue
\item
We discuss batching for producer and consumer in more detail, shortly
\end{itemize}

Batching is critical to imporving performance because:

\begin{itemize}
\item
It allows the operating system to group emssages together in a single network request and amortizes the cost of expensive network round trips
\item
The broker writes messages to the append logs in large chunks, which leads to larger lbocks of sequential writes and larger contiguous blocks of disk cache, maintained by the oeprating system
\item
Both lead to much greater sequential disk access throughput
\end{itemize}

There is a tradeoff between throughput and latency.

\begin{itemize}
\item
If the system is deployed as a traditional message queue where latncy might be more important, the system could be tuned to use a smaller batch size
\item
DIsk performance will suffer a little bit in this use case
\item
If tuned for throughput, there might need to be a higher number of  parititions per topic, to make up for the slower sequential disk write throughput
\item
SO far, we've covered the main disk storage subsystem and its associated on-disk data structure
\item
NOw, let's switch gears and discuss the producer and consumer flows
\item
There we will come back and finish teh deep dive into the rest of the message queue
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Producer flow}

If a producer wants to send messages to a partition, which broker should it connect to?

\begin{itemize}
\item
The first option is to introduce a routing layer.
\item
ALl messages sent to the routing layer are routed to the ``correct'' broker
\item
If the brokers are replicated, the ``correct'' broker is the leader replica. We will cover replication later
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_11}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

A shown in Figure 4.11, the producer tries to send messages to Partition-1 of Topic-A.
