\begin{enumerate}
\item
THe producer sends messages ot the routing layer
\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
The routing layer reads the replicat distribution  from the metadata storage and cahces it locally. When a message arrives, it routes the message to the leader replica of Partition-1, which is stored in Broker-1.
\item
The distribution of replicas for each parittion is called a replca distribution plan
\end{itemize}

\item
The leader replicae receives the emssage and follower replicas pull data from the leader
\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
When ``enough'' replicas have synchronized the message, the leader commits the data (persisted on disk), which means the data can be consumed
\item
Then it responds to the producer
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

You might be wonderingy why we need both leader and follower replicas.

The reason is fault tolerance. We dive deep into this process in the ``In sync replicas'' section on page 113.

This approach works, but it has a few drawbacks.

\begin{itemize}
\item
A new routing layer means additional network latency caused by overhead and additional network hops
\item
Request batching is one of the big drivers of efficiency. This design doesn't take that into consideration.
\end{itemize}

Figure 4.12 shows the improved design.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_12}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The routing layer is wrapped into the producer and a buffer component is added ot the producer. Both can be installed in the producer as part of the producer client library.

This change brings several benefits:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Fewer network hops mean lower latency
\item
Producers can have their own logic to determine which parittion the message should be sent to
\item
Batching buffers messages in memory and sends out larger bathces in a single request. This increases throughput.
\end{itemize}

THe choice of the batch size si a classic trade off between throughput and latency (Figure 4.13).

\begin{itemize}
\item
With a large batch size, the throughput increases but latncy is higher, due to a longer wait time to accumulate the batch
\item
With a small batch size, requests are sent sooner so the latency is lower, but throughput suffers
\item
Producers can tune the bgatch size based on use cases.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_13}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Consumer flow}

The consumer specifies its offset in a partition and receives back a chunk of events beginning from that position. An example is shown in Figure 4.14.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_14}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Consumer flow}

The consumer specifics its offset in a parittion and receives back a chunk of events

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_14}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Push vs Pull}

AN importatn question to answer is wehetare brokers should push data to consumers, or if consumers should pull data from the brokers.

\subsubsection{Push model}

Pros:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Low latency. The broker can push messages ot the consumer immediately upon receiving them
\end{itemize}

Cons:

\begin{itemize}
\item
If the rate of consumption falls below the rate of production, consumers could be overwhelmed
\item
It is difficult with consumers with diverse processing power because the brokers control the rate at which data is transferred
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Pull model}

Pros:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Consumers control the consumption rate. We can have on set of consumers process messages in real-time and another set of consumers process messages in batch mode
\item
If the rate of consumption falls below the rate of production, we can scale out the consumers, or simply catch up when it can
\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
The pull model is more suitable for batch processing
\item
eeIN the push model, the broker has no knowledge of whether consumers will be able to prcess messages immediately
\item
If the broker sends one message at a atime to the consumer and the consumer is backed up, new messages wil end up waiting in the buffer
\item
A pull model pulls all available messages after the consumer's current position in the log (or up to the configurable max size)
\item
It is suitable for aggressive batching of data.
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

Cons:

\begin{itemize}
\item
When there is no message in the broker, a consumer might still keep pulling data, wasting resources, to overcome this issue, many message queues support long polling mode, which allows pulls to wait a specified amount of time for new messages[6].
\end{itemize}

Based on these considerations, most message queues choose the pull model.

Figure 4.15 shows the workflow of the consumer pull model.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_15}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
A new consumer wants to join group-1 and subscribves to Topic-A
\item
It finds the corresponding broker node by hasing the group name
\item
By doing so, all the consumers in the same gorup connect to the same broker, which is also called the coordinator of this consumer group
\item
Despite the naming similarity, the consumer group coordinator is different from the coordination service mention in Figure 4.8
\item
This coordinator coordinates the consumer gorup, while the coordination service mentioned earlier coordinates the broker cluster
\end{itemize}
\item
The coordinator confirms that the consumer has joined the group and assign Partition-2 to the consumer. THere are different parittion assignment strategies including roudn-boring, range, etc. [7]
\item
Consumer processes messages and commits the offset to the broker. The order of data processing and offset committing affects the message delivery semantics, which wil be discussed shortly.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{COnsumer rebalancing}

Consumer rebalancing decides which consumer is responsible for which subset of partitions.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The process could occur when a consumer joins, when a consuer leaves, when a consumer crashes, or when parittions are adjusted.
\item
WHen consumer rebalancing occurs, the coordinator plays an important role
\item
Let's first take a look at what a coordinator is
\item
THe coordinator is one of the brokers responsible for communicating with consumers to achieve consumer rebalancing
\item
THe coordinator receives heartbeat from consumers and manages their offset on the partitions
\item
Let's use an example to undertsnad how the coordinator and the consumers work together
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_16}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
A shown in FIgure 4.16, each consumer belongs to a group
\item
It finds the dedicated coordinator by hashing the group name
\item
All consumers from the same group are connected to the same coordinator
\end{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The coordinator maintains a joined consumer list
\item
When the list changes, the coordinator elects a new leader of the group
\end{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
As the new leader of the consumer group, it generates a new parittion dispatch plan and reports it back to the coordinator
\item
The coordinator will broadcast the plan to the other consumers in the group
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

IN a distributed system, consumers might encounter all sorts of issues including network issues, crashes, restarts, etc.

From the coordinator's perspective, they will no longer have heartbeats.

WHen this happens, the coordinator will trigger a rebalance process to re-dispatch the partitions as illustrated in Figure 4.17.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_17}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Let's simulate a few rebalance scenarios. Assume there are 2 consumers in the group, and 4 partitions in the subscribed topic.

Figure 4.18 shows the flow when a new Consumer B joins the group.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_18}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{enumerate}
\item
Initially, only Consumer A is in group. It consumes all the partitions and keep the heartbeat with the coordinator
\item
Consumer B sends a request to join the groupe
\item
The coordinator knows it's time to rebalance, so it notifies al the consumers in the group in a passive way. When Consumer A's heartbeat is received by the coordinator, it asks Consumer A to rejoin the group.
\item
Once all the consumers have rejoined the group, the coordinator chooses one of them as the leader and informs all the consumers about the election result
\item
The leader consumer generates the paritition dispatch plan and sends it ot the coordinator. Follower consumers ask the coordinator about the partition dispatch plan
\item
Consumers start consuming messages from newly assigned partitions
\end{enumerate}

Figure 4.19 shows the flow when an existing Consumer A leaves the group.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_19}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Consumer A and B are in the same consumer group
\item
Consumer A needs to be shutdown, so it requests to leave the group
\item
The coordinator knows it's time to rebalance. When Conusmer B's heartbeat is received by the coordinator, it asks Consumer B to rejoin the group
\item
The remaining steps are the same as the ones shown in Figure 4.18
\end{itemize}

Figure 4.20 shows the flow when an existing Consumer A crashes.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Consumer A and B are in the same consumer group
\item
Consumer A needs to be shutdown, so it requests to leave the group
\item
The coordinator knows it's time to rebalance. When Consumer B's heartbeat is received by the coordinator, it asks Consumer B to rejoin the group
\item
The remaining steps are the same as the ones shown in Figure 4.18
\end{itemize}

Figure 4.20 shows the flow when an existing Consumer A crashes.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_20}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Consumer A and B keep heartbeats with the coordinator
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Consumer A crashes, so there is no heartbeat sent from Consumer A to the coordinator
\item
Since the coordinator, doesn't get any heartbeat signal within a specified amount of time from Consumer A, it marks the consumer as dead
\end{itemize}

\item
The coordinator triggers the rebalance process
\item
The following steps are the same as the ones in the previous scenario
\end{itemize}

Now that we finished the detour on producer and consumer flows, let's come back and finish the deep dive on the rest of the message queue broker.

\subsection{State storage}

In the message queue broker, the state storage stores:

\begin{itemize}
\item
The mapping between partitions and consumers
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The last consumed offsets of consumer groups for each partition
\item
As shown in Figure 4.21, the last consumed offset for consumer group-1 is 6 and the offset for consumer group-2 is 13
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_21}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

FOr example, as shown in Figure 4.21, a consumer in group-1 consumes messages from the partition in sequence and commits the consumed offset 6.

\begin{itemize}
\item
This means all the messages before and at offset 6 are already consumed
\item
If the consumer crashes, another new consumer in the same group will resume consumption by readin the last consumed offset from the state storage.
\end{itemize}

The data access patterns for consumer states are:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Frequent read and write operations but the volume is not high
\item
Data is updated frequently and is rarely deleted
\item
Random read and write operations
\item
Data consistency is important
\end{itemize}

Lots of storage solutions can be used for storing the consumer state data.

Considering the data consistency and fast read/write requirements, a KV store like ZooKeeper is a great choice.

Kafka has moved the offset storage from ZooKepper to Kafka brokers.

Interested readers can read the reference material [8] to learn more.

\subsection{Metadata storage}

The metadata storage stores the configuration and properties of topics, including a number of partitions, retention period, and distribution of replicas.

Meta data does not change frequently and the data volume is small, but it has a high consistency requirement.

ZooKeeper is a good choice for storing metadata.

\subsection{ZooKeeper}

By reading previous sections, you probably have already sensed that ZooKeeper tis very helpful for designing a distributed message queue.

\begin{itemize}
\item
If you are not familiar with it, ZooKeeper is an essential service for distributed systems offering a hierarchical key-value store
\item
It is commonly used to provide a distributed configuratino service, synchronization service, and naming registry [2]
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_22}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Let's briefly go over the change.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Metadata and state storage are moved to ZooKeeper
\item
The broker now only needs to maintain the data storage for messages
\item
ZooKeeper helps with the leader election of the broker cluster
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Replication}

In distributed systems, hardware issues are common and cannot be ignored.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Data gets lost when a disk is damamaged or fails permanently
\item
Replication is the classic solution to achieve high availability
\item
As in Figure 4.23, each partition has 3 replicas, distributed across different broker nodes
\item
For each partition, the highlighted replicas are the leaders and the others are followers
\item
Producers only send messazges to the leader replica
\item
The follower replicas keep pulling new messages from the leader
\item
Once messages are synchronized to enough replicas, the leader returns an acknowledgment to the producer
\item
We will go into detail about how to definee ``enough'' in the In-sync Replicas section on page 113
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_23}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The distribution of replicas for each partition is called a replica
distribution plan.

For example, the replica distribution plan in Figure 4.23 can be described as:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Partition-1 of Topic-A: 3 replicas, leader in Broker-1, folowers in Broker-2 and 3;
\item
Partition-2 of Topic-A: 3 replicas, leader in Broker-2, followers in Broker-3 and 4
\item
Partition-1 of Topic-B: 3 replicas, leader in Broker-3, followers in Broker-4 and 1
\end{itemize}

Who makes the replica distribution plan?

\begin{itemize}
\item
It works as follows; with the help fo the coordination service, one of the broker nodes is elected as the leader
\item
It generates the replica distribution plan and persists the plan in metadata storage
\item
All the brokers now can work according to the plan
\item
If you are interested in knowing more about replicatons, check out ``Chapter 5. Replication'' of the book ``Design Data-Intensive Applications'' [9]
\end{itemize}

\subsection{In-sync replicas}

We mentioned that messages are persisted in multiple partitions to avoid single node failure, and each partition has multiple replicas.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Messages are only written to the leader, and followers ysnchronize data from the leader
\item
One problem we need to solve is keeping them in sync
\item
In-sync replicas (ISR) refer to replicas that are ``in-sync'' with the leader
\item
The definition of ``in-sync'' depends on the topic configuration
\item
For example, if the value of replica.lag.max.messages is 4, it means that as long as the follower is behind the leader by no more than 3 messages, it will not be removed from ISR [10]
\item
The leader is an ISR by default
\end{itemize}

Let's use an example as shown in Figure 4.24 to sshows how ISR works

\begin{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The committed offset in the leader replica is 13
\item
Two new messages are written to the leader, but not committed yet
\item
Committed offset means that all messages before and at this offset are already synchronized to all the replicas in ISR
\end{itemize}
\item
Replica-2 and replica-3 have fully caught up with the leader, so they are in ISR and can fetch new messages
\item
Replica-4 did not fully catch up with the leader within the configured lag time, so it is not in ISR. When it catches up again, it can be added to ISR
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_24}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Why do we need ISR?

\begin{itemize}
\item
The reason is that ISR reflects the trade-off between performance and durability
\item
If producers don't want to lsoe any messages, the safest way to do that is to ensure all replicas are already in sync before sneding an acknoewledgment
\item
But a slow replica will cause the whole partition to become slow or unavailable
\item
Now that we've discussed ISR, let's take a look at acknowledgment settings
\item
Producers can choose to receive acknowledgments until the $k$ number of ISRs has received the messager, where $k$ is configurable
\end{itemize}

\subsection{ACK=all}

Figure 4.25 illustrates the case with ACK=all.

With ACK=all, the producer gets an ACK when all ISRs have received the message.

This means it takes a longer time to send a emssage because we need to wait fo rthe slowerest ISR, but it gives the strongest message durability.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_25}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{ACK=1}

With ACK=1, the producer receives an ACK once the leader persists the message.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The latency is improved by not waiting for data synchronization
\item
If the elader fails immediately after a message is acknowledged but before it is replicaed by follower nodes, then the message is lost
\item
THis setting is suitable for low latency systems where occasional data loss is acceptable
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_26}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{ACK=0}

The producer keeps sending messages to the leader without waiting for any acknoledgment, and it never retires.

This method provides the lowest latency at the cost of potential message loss.

This setting might be food for usecases like collecting metrics or loggin data since sdata volume is high and occasional data loss is acceptable.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_27}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Configurable ACK allows us to trade durability for performance.

Now let's look at the consumer side. The easiest setup is to let consuemrs connect to a leader replica to consume messages.

You might be wondering if the leader replica would be overwhelmed by this design and why messages are not read from ISRs. The reasons are:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Design and operational simplicity
\item
Since messages in one partition are dispatched to only one consumer within a consumer group, this limits the number of connections to the leader replica
\item
The number of connections to the leader replicas is usually not large as long as a topic is not super hot
\item
If a topic is hot, we can scale by expanding the number of partitions and consumers
\end{itemize}

In some scenarios, reading from the leader replica might not be the best option

\begin{itemize}
\item
For example, if a consumer is located in a different data center from the leader replica, the read performance suffers
\item
In this case, it is wrothwhile to enable consumers to read from the closest ISRs
\item
Interested readers can checkout the reference material about this [11]
\item
ISR is very important
\item
How does it determine if a replica is ISR or not?
\item
Usually, the leader for every partition tracks the ISR list by computing the lag of every replca from itself.
\item
If you are interested in detailed algoriths, you can find the implementations in reference materials [12] [13]
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Scalability}

By now we have made great process designing the distributed message queue system.

In the next step, let's evaluate the scalability of different system components:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Producers
\item
Consumers
\item
Brokers
\item
Partitions
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Producers}

The producer is conceptually much simpler than the consumer because it doesn't need group coordination.

The scalability of producers can easily be achieved by adding or removing producer instances

\subsection{COnsumer}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Consumer groups are isolated from each other, so it is easy to add or remove a consumer gorup
\item
INside a consumer group, the rebalancing mechanism helps to handle the cases where a consumer gets added ore removed, or when it crashes
\item
With consumer groups and the rebalance mechanism, the scalability and fault tolerance of consumers can be achieved
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Broker}

Before discussing scalability on the broker side, let's first consider the failure recover of brokers


\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_28}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Let's use an example in Figure 4.28 to explain how failure recovery works.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
Assume there are 4 brokers and the partition (replica) distribution plan is shown below:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Partition-1 of topic A: replicas in Broker-1 (leader), 2, and 3
\item
Partition-2 of topic A: replicas in Broker-2 (leader), 3, and 4
\item
Partition-1 of topic B: replicas in Broker-3 (leader), 4, and 1
\end{itemize}
\item
Broker-3 crashes, which means all the partitions on the node are lost.

The partition distribution plan is changed to

\begin{itemize}
\item
Partition-1 of topic A: replicas in Broker-1 (leader) and 2
\item
Partition-2 of topic A: replicas in Broker-2 (leader) and 4
\item
Partition-1 of topic B: replicas in Broker-4 and 1
\end{itemize}

\item
The broker controller detects Broker-3 is down and generates a new partition distribution plan for the remaining broker nodes:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Partition-1 of topic A: replicas in Broker-1 (leader), 2, and 4 (new)
\item
Partition-2 of topic A: replicas in Broker-2 (leader), 4 and 1 (new)
\item
Partition-1 of topic B: replicas in Broker-4 (leader), 1, and 2 (new)
\end{itemize}
\item
The new replicas work as followers and catch up with the leader
\end{enumerate}

To make the broker fault-tolerant, here are additional considerations:

\begin{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The minimum number of ISRs specifies how many replicas the producer must receive before a message is considered to be succesfully committed
\item
The higher the number, the safer
\item
But on the other hand, we need to balance latency and safety
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
If all replicas of a partition are in the same broker node, then we cannot tolerate the failure of this node
\item
It is also a waste of resources to replicate data in the same node
\item
Therefore, replicas should not be in the same node
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
If all the replicas of a partition crash, the data for that partition is lost forvever
\item
When choosing the number of replicas and replica locations, there's a trade-off between data safety, resource cost, and latency
\item
It is safer to distribute replicas across data centers, but this will incur much more latency and cost, to synchronize data between replicas
\item
As a workaround, data mirroring can help to copy data across data centers, but this is out of scope
\item
The reference material [14] covers this topic
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

Now let's get back to discussing the scalability of brokers.

THe simplest solution would be to redistribute the replicas when broker nodes are added or removed.

However, there is a better approach. The broker controller can temporarily allow more replcas in the system than the number of replicas in the config file.

WHen the newly added broker catches up, we can remove the ones that are no longer needed.

Let's use an example as shown in Figure 4.29 to understand the approach.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_29}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
The initial setup: 3 brokers, 2 partitions, and 3 replicas for each partition
\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
New Broker-4 is added.
\item
Assume the broker controller changes the replica distribution of Partition-2 to the broker (2, 3, 4)
\item
The new replica in Broker-4 starts to copy data from leader Broker-2
\item
Now the number of replicas for Partition-2 is temporarily more than 3
\item
After the replica in BRoker-4 cathces up, the redundant partition in Broker-1 is gracefully removed
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

By following this process, data loss while adding brokers can be avoided. A similar process can be applied to remove brokers safely.

\subsection{Partition}

For various operational reasons, such as scaling the topic, throughput tuning, balancing avaiability / throughput, etc., we may change the number of partitions.

\begin{itemize}
\item
When the number of partitions changes, the producer will be notified after it communicates with any broker, and the consumer will trigger consumer rebalancing
\item
Therefore, it is safe for both the producer and consumer
\end{itemize}

Now let's consider the data storage layer when the number of partitions changes.

As in Figure 4.30, we have added a partition to the topic.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_30}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Persisted messages are still in the old partitions, so there's no data migration
\item
After the new partition (partition-3) is added, new messages wil be persisted in all 3 partitions
\end{itemize}

So it is straightforward to scale the topic by increasing partitions.

\subsection{Decrease the number of partitions}

Decreasing partitions is more complicated, as illustrated in Figure 4.31.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_31}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Partition-3 is decommiseioned so new messages are only received by the remaining partitions (partition-1 and partition-2)
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
THe decommissioned partition (partition-3) cannot be removed immediately because date might be currently consumed by consumers for a certain amount of time
\item
Only after the configured retention period passes, data can be truncated and storage space is freed up
\item
Reducing partitions is not a shortcut to reclaiming data space
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
During this transactional period (while partition-3 is decommisioned), producers only send messages to the remaining 2 partitions, but consumers can still consume from all 3 partitions
\item
After the retention period of the decommisioned partition expires, consumer groups need rebalancing
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Data delivery semantics}

As the name suggests, at-most once means a message will be delivered not more than once. Messages may be lost but are not redelivered.

This is how at-most once delivery works at the high level.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The producer sends a message a asynchronously to a topic without waiting for an acknowledgment (ACK=0). If message delivery fails, there is no retry.
\item
\begin{itemize}
\item

Consumer fetches the message and commits the offset before the data is processed.
\item
 If the consumer crashes just after offset commit, the message will not be reconsumed.
\item
On the other hand, if a consumer processes the message but fails to commit the offset to the broker, the message will be re-consumed when the consumer restarts, resulting in duplicates
\end{itemize}

\item
A message might be delivered more than once to the brokers and consumers
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_33}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Use cases: With at-least once, messages won't be lost but the same message might be delivered multiple times.

\begin{itemize}
\item
While not ideal from a user perspective, at-least once delivery semantics are usually good enough for use cases where data duplication is not a big issue or deduplication is possible on the consumer side
\item
For example, with a unique key in each message, a message can be rejected when writing duplicate data to the database.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Exactly Once}

Exactly once is the most difficult delivery semantic to implement. It is friendly to users, but it has a high cost for the system's performance and complexity.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_34}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Use cases: Financial-related use cases (payment, trading, accounting, etc.).

Exactly once is especially important when duplication is not acceptable and the downstream service or third party doesn't support idempotency.

\subsubsection{Advanced features}

In this section, we talk briefly about some advanced features, such as message filtering, delayed messages, and scheduled messages.

\subsubsection{Message filtering}

A topic is a logical abstraction that contains messages of the same type.

\begin{itemize}
\item
However, some consumer groups may only want to consume messages of certain subtypes
\item
For examples, that the ordering system sends all the activites about the order to a topic, but the payment system only cares about messages related to checkout and refund.
\item
One option is to build a dedicated topic for the payment system and another topic for the ordering system
\end{itemize}

This method is simple, but it might raise some concerns.

\begin{itemize}
\item
What if other systems ask for different subtypes of messages? Do we need to build dedicated topics for every single consumer request?
\item
It is a waste of resources to save the same messages on different topics
\item
The producer needs to change every time a new consumer requirement comes, as the producer and consumer are now tightly coupled
\end{itemize}

Therefore, we need to resolve this requirement using a different approach.

Luckily, message filtering comes to the rescue.

A naive solution for message filtering is that consumer fetches the full set of messages and filters out unnecessary messages during processing time.

\begin{itemize}
\item
This approach is flexible but introduces unnecessary traffic that will affect system performance
\item
A better solution is to filter messages on the broker side so that consumers wil only get messages they care about
\item
Implementing this requires some careful consideration
\item
If data filtering requires data decryption or deserialization, it will degrade the performance of the brokers
\item
Additionally, if messages contain sensitive data, they should not be readable in the message queue
\item
Therefore, the filtering logic in the broker should not extract the message payload
\item
It is better to put data used for filtering into the metadata of a emssage, which can be efficiently read by the broker
\item
For example, we can attach a tag to each message
\item
With a message tag, a broker can filter messages in that dimension
\item
If more tags are attached the messages can be filtered in multiple dimensions
\item
Therefore, a list of tags can support most of the filtering requirements
\item
To support more complex logic such as mathematical formulae, the broker will need a grammar parser or a script executor, which might be too heavyweight for the message queue
\item
With tags attached to each message, a consumer can subscribe to messages based on the specfied tag, as shown in Figure 4.35
\item
Interested readers can refer to the reference material [15]
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_35}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Delayed messages \& scheduled messages}

Sometimes you want to delay the delivery of messages to consumer for a specified period of time.

\begin{itemize}
\item
For example, an order should be closed if not paid within 30 minutes after the order is created
\item
A dealyed verification message (check if the apyment is completed) is sent immediately but is delivered to the consumer 30 minutes later
\item
When the consumer receives the message, it checks the payment status
\item
If the payment is not completed, the order will be closed
\item
Otherwise, the message will be ignored
\item
Different from sending instant messages, we can send delayed messages to temporary storage on the broker side instead of to the topics immediately, and then deliver them to the topics when time's up
\item
The high-level design for this is shown in Figure 4.36
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_4_36}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Core components of the system include the temporary storage and the timing function.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The temporary storage can be one or more special message topics
\item
The timing function is out of scope, but here are 2 popular solutions:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Dedicated delay queues with predefined delay levels [16]
\item
For example, RocketMQ doesn't support delayed messages with arbitrary messages with arbitrary time precision, but delayed messages with specific levels are supported
\item
Message delay levels are 1s, 5s, 10s, 30s, 1m, 2m, 3m, 4m, 6m, 8m, 9m, 10m, 20m, 30m, 1h, and 2h
\item
Hierarchical time wheel
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

A scheduled message means a message should be delivered ot the consumer at the scheduled time. The overall design is very similar to delayed messages.

\subsection{Step 4: Wrap Up}

In this chapter, we have presented the design of a distrubted message queue with some advanced features commonly found in data streaming platforms.

If there is extra time at the end of the interview, here are some additional talking points:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Protocol: it defines rules, syntax, and APIs on how to exchange information and transfer data between different nodes. In a distributed message queue, the protocol should be able to:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Cover all the activities such as production, consumption, heartbeat, etc
\item
Effectively transport data with large volumes
\item
Verify the integrity and correctness of the data
\end{itemize}
\item
Retry consumption

\begin{itemize}
\item
If some messages cnnot be consumed successfully, we need to retry the operation
\item
In order not to block incoming messages, how can we retry the operation after a certain time period?
\item
One idea is to send failed messages to a dedicated retry topic, so they can be consumed later
\end{itemize}

\item

Historical data archive

\begin{itemize}
\item
Assume there is a time-based or capacity-based log retrention mechanism
\item
If a consumer needs to replay some historical messages that are already truncated, how can we do it?
\item
One possible solution is to use storage systems with large capacities, such as HDFS [20] or object storage, to store historical data
\end{itemize}

\end{itemize}

Some popular protocols include Advanced Message Queuing Protocol (AMQP) [18] and Kafka protocol [19].

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{chapter_4_summary}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\chapter*{Chapter 5}

In this chapter, we explore the design of a scalable metrics monitoring and altering system

\begin{itemize}
\item
A well-designed monitoring and alerting system plays a key role in providing clear visibility into the health of the infrastructure to ensure high availability and reliability
\item
Figure 5.1 shows some of the most popular metrics monitoring and altering services in the marketplace
\item
In this chapter, we design a similar service that can be used internally by a large company
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_1}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Step 1: Understand the Problem and Establish Design Scope}

\begin{itemize}
\item
A metrics monitorining and altering system can mean many different things to different companies, so it is essnetial to nail down the exact requirements first with the itnerviewer
\item
For example, you do not want to design a system that focuses on logs such as web server errror or access logs if the interviewer has only infrastructure metrics in mind
\end{itemize}

Let's first fully understand the problem and establish the scope of the design before diving into the details.


\begin{enumerate}
\item
\textbf{Candidate}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Who are we building the system for?
\item
Are we building an in-house system for a large corporation like Facebook or Google, or are we designing a SaaS service like Datadog [1], Splunk [2], etc?
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Interviewer}:
That's a great question. We are building it for internal use only.

\item
\textbf{Candidate}:
Which metrics do we want to collect?

\textbf{Interviewer}:
\begin{itemize}
\item
We want to collect operational system metrics
\item
These can be low-level usage data of the operating system, such as CPU load, memory usage, and disk space consumption
\item
They can also be high-level concepts such as requests per second of a service or the running server count of a web pool
\item
Business metrics are not in the scope of this design
\end{itemize}


\item
\textbf{Candidate}:
What is the scale of the infrastructure we are monitoring with this system?

\textbf{Interviewer}:
100 million daily active users, 1,000 server pools, and 100 machines per pool
\item
\textbf{Candidate}: How long should we keep the data?
\textbf{Interviewer}: Let's assume we want 1 year retention

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: May we reduce the resolution of the metrics data for long-term storage?
\textbf{Interviewer}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
That's a great question.
\item
We would like to be able to keep newly received data for 7 days. After 7 days, you may roll them up to a 1 minute resolution for 30 days.
\item
After 30 days, you may further roll them up at a 1 hour resolution
\end{itemize}

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: What are the supported alert channels?
\textbf{Interviewer}: Email, phone, PageDuty [3], or webhooks (HTTP endpoint)

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Do we need to collect logs, such as error log or access log?
\textbf{Interviewer}: No

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Do we need to support distributed system tracing?
\textbf{Interviewer}: No.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{High-level requirements ans assumptions}

Now you have finished gathering requirements from ther interviewer and have a clear scope of the dersign. The requirements are:

\begin{itemize}
\item
The infrastructure being monitored is large-scale.

\begin{itemize}
\item
100 million dialy active users
\item
Assume we have 1,000 server pools, 100 machines per pool, 100 metrics per machine $\Rightarrow$ $\sim$ 10 million metrics
\item
1 year data retention
\item
Data retention policy: raw form for 7 days, 1 minute resolution for 30 days
\end{itemize}

\item
A variety of metrics can be monitored, for example:

\begin{itemize}
\item
CPU Usage
\item
Request count
\item
Memory usage
\item
Message count in message queues
\end{itemize}

\end{itemize}

\subsection{Non-functional requirements}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Scalability. The system should be scalable to accommodate growing metrics and alert volume
\item
Low latency. The system needs to have low query latency for dashboards and alerts
\item
Reliability. The system should be highly reliable to avoid missing critical alters
\item
Flexibility. Technology keeps chaning, so the pipeline should be flexible enough to easily integrate new technologies in the future.
\end{itemize}

Which requirements are out of scope?

\begin{itemize}
\item
Log monitoring. The Elasticsearch, Logstash, Kibana (ELK) stack is very popular for collecting and monitoring logs [4]
\item
Distributed system tracing [5] [6]. Distributed tracing refers to a tracing solution that tracks service requests as they flow through distributed systems. It collects data as requests go from one service to another
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 2: Propose High-level Design and Get Buy-in}

In this section, we discuss some fundamental sof building the system, the data model, and the high-level design.

\subsection{Fundamentals}

A metrics monitoring and alterting system generally contains five components, as illustrated in Figure 5.2.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Data collection: collect metric data from different sources
\item
Data transmission: transfer data from sources to the metrics monitoring system
\item
Data storage: organize and store incoming data
\item
Alerting: analyze incoming data, detect anomalies, and generate alerts. The system must be able to send alerts to different communication channels
\item
Visualization: present data in graphs, charts, etc. Engineers are better at identifying patterns, trends, or problems when data is presented visually, so we need visualization functionality
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_2}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Data model}

Metrics data is usually recorded as a time series that contains a set of values with their associated timestamps. The series itself can be uniquely identified by its name, and operationaly by a set of labels.

Example 1:

What is the CPU load on production server instance \lstinline|i631| at \lstinline|20:00|.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_3}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The data point highlighted in Figure 5.3 can be represented by Table 5.1.

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|metric_name| & \lstinline|cpu.load| \\
\hline
\lstinline|labels| & \lstinline|host:i631,env:prod| \\
\hline
\lstinline|timestamp| & \lstinline|1613707265| \\
\hline
\lstinline|value| & \lstinline|0.29| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 5.1: The data point reprsented by a table

In this example, the time series is represented by the metric name, the labels (\lstinline|host:i631, env:prod|), and a single point value at a specific time.

\subsubsection{Example 2:}

What is the average CPU load across all web servers in the us-west region for the last 10 minutes?

Conceptually, we would pull up something like this from storage where the metric name is \lstinline|CPU.load| and the region label is \lstinline|us-west|:

\begin{lstlisting}
CPU.load host=webserver01,region=us-west 1613707265 50
CPU.load host=webserver01,region=us-west 1613707265 62
CPU.load host=webserver01,region=us-west 1613707265 43
CPU.load host=webserver01,region=us-west 1613707265 53

CPU.load host=webserver01,region=us-west 1613707265 76
CPU.load host=webserver01,region=us-west 1613707265 83
\end{lstlisting}

The average CPU load could be computed by averaging the values at the end of each line.

The format of the lines in the above example is called the line protocol.

It is a common input format for many monitoring software in the market.

Prometheus [7] and OpenTSDB [8] are two examples.

Every time series consists of the following [9]:

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
\textbf{Name} & \textbf{Type} \\
\hline
metric name & String \\
\hline
A set of tags/labels & List of \lstinline|<key:value>| pairs \\
\hline
An array of values and their timestamps & An array of \lstinline|<value, timestamp>| pairs\\
\hline
\end{tabular}

\subsection{Data access pattern}

In Figure 5.4, each label on the y-axis represents a time series (uniquely identified by the names and labels) while the x-axis represents time.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_4}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The write load is heavy.

\begin{itemize}
\item
As you can see, there can be many time-series data points written at any moment
\item
As we mentioned in the ``High-level requirements'' section on page 132, about 10 million operational metrics are written per day, and many metrics are collected at high frequeucny, so the traffic is undoubtedly write-heavy
\item
At the same time, the read load is spiky
\item
Both visualizatoin and alterting services send queries to the database, and depending on the access patterns of the graphs and alterts the read volume could be bursty
\item
In other words, the system is under constant heavy write load, while the read load is spiky
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Data storage system}

The data storage system is the heart of the design.

\begin{itemize}
\item
It's not recommended to build your own storage system or use a general-purpose storage system (for example, MySQL [10]) for this job.
\item
A general-purpose database, in theory, could support time-series data, but it would require expert-level tuning to make it work at our scale
\item
Specifically, a relational database is not optimized for operations you would commonly perform against time-series data
\item
For example, computing the moving average in a rolling time window requires complicated SQL that is difficult to read (there is an example of this in the deep dive section)
\item
Besides, to support tagging/labeling data, we need to add an index for each tag
\item
Moreover, a general-purpose relational database does not perform well under constant heavy write load
\item
At our scale, we would need to expend significant effort in tuning the database, and even then, it might not perform well
\item
How about NoSQL?
\item
In theory, a few NoSQL databases on the market could handle time-series data effectively
\item
For example, Cassandra and BigTable [11] can both be used for time series data
\item
However, this would require deep knowledge of the internal workings of each NoSQL to deivse a scalable schema for effectively storing and querying time-series data
\item
With industrial-scale time-series databases readily available, using a general-purpose NoSQL database is not appealing
\end{itemize}

There are many storage systems available that are optimized for time-series data.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The optimization lets us use far fewer servers to handle the same volume of data
\item
Many of these databases also have custom query interfaces specially designed for the analysis of time-series data that are much easier to use than SQL
\item
Some even provide features to manage data retention and data aggregation
\item
Here are a few exaqmples of time-series databases
\end{itemize}

OpenTSDB is a distributed time-series databse, but since it is based on Hadoop and HBase, running a Hadoop/HBase cluster adds complexity

\begin{itemize}
\item
Twitter uses MetricsDB [12] and Amazon offers Timestream as a time-series database [13]
\item
According to DB-engines [14], the two most popular time-series databases are InfluxDB [15] and Prometheus, which are designed to store large volumes of time-series data and quckly perform real-time analysis on that data
\item
Both of them primarily rely on an in-memory cache and on-disk storage
\item
And they both handle durability and performance quite well
\item
As shown in Figure 5.5, an InfluxDB with 8 cores and 32GB RAM can handle over 250,000 writes per second
\end{itemize}

\begin{tabular}{|p{1.5cm}|p{1.5cm}|p{1.5cm}|p{1.5cm}|p{1.5cm}|p{1.5cm}|}
\hline
vCPU or CPU & RAM & I0PS & Writes per second & Queries per second & Unique series \\
\hline
2-4 cores & 2-4 GB  & 500 & $<$ 5000 & $<$ 5 & $<$ 100000\\
\hline
4-6 cores & 8-32 GB  & 500-1000 & $<$ 250000 & $<$ 25 & $<$ 1000000\\
\hline
8+ cores & 32+ GB  & 1000+ & $>$ 250000 & $>$ 25 & $>$ 1000000\\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Figure 5.5: InfluxDB benchmarking

Since a time-series databse is a specialized database, you are not expected to understand the internals in an interview unless you explicitly mentioned it in your resume.

\begin{itemize}
\item
For the purpose of an interview, it's important to undertsand the metrics data are time-series in nature and we can select time-series databases such as INfluxDB for stoage to store them
\item
Another feature of a strong time-series database is efficient aggregation and analysis of a large amount of time-series data by labels, also known as tags in some databses
\item
For example, InfluxDB builds indexes on labels to facilitate the fast lookup of time-series by labels [15].
\item
It provides clear best-practice guidelines on how to use labels, without overloading the database
\item
The key is to make sure each label is of low cardinality (having a small set of possible values).
\item
This feature is critical for visuzliation, and it would take a lot of effort to build this with a general-purpose database
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{High-Level Design}

The high-level design diagram is shown in Figure 5.6

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_6}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
\textbf{Metrics source}: This can be application servers, SQL databases, message queues, etc.
\item
\textbf{Metrics collector}. It gathers metrics data and writes data into the time-series database
\item
\textbf{Time-series database}.

\begin{itemize}
\item
This stores metrics data as time series
\item
It usually provides a custom query interface for analyzing and summarizing a large amount of time-series data
\item
It maintains indexes on labels to facilitate the fast lookup of time-series data by labels
\end{itemize}
\item
\textbf{Alerting systems}

This sends alert notifications to various alerting desitnations
\item
\textbf{Visualization system}.

This shows metrics in the form of various graphs/charts.

\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 3: Design Deep Dive}

In a system design interview, candidates are expected to dive deep into a few key compones or flows. In this section, we investigate the following topics in detail:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Metrics collection
\item
Scaling the metrics transmission pipeline
\item
Query service
\item
Storage layer
\item
Alerting system
\item
Visualization system
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Metrics collection}

For metrics collection like counters or CPU usage, occasional data loss is not the end of the world.

It's acceptable for clients to fire and forget.

Now let's take a look at the metrics collection flow, This part of the system is inside the dashed box (Figure 5.7).

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_7}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Pull vs push models}

There are two ways metrics data can be collected, pull or push. It is a routine debate as to which one is better and there is no clear answer. Let's take a close look.

\subsection{Pull model}

Figure 5.8 shows data collection with a pull model over HTTP. We have deicated metric collectors which pull metrics values from the running applications periodically.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_8}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

In this approach, the metrics collector needs to know the complete list of services endpoints to pull data from.

\begin{itemize}
\item
One naive approach is to use a file to hold DNS/UP information for every services endpoint on the ``metric collector'' servers
\item
While the idea is simple, this approach is hard to maintain in a large-scale environment where servers are added or removed frequently, and we want to ensure that metric collectors don't miss out on collecting metrics from any new servers
\item
The good news is that we have a reliable, scalable, and maintainable solution available through Service Discovery, provided by etcd [16], ZooKeeper [17], etc., wherein services register thei availability and the metrics collector can be notified by the Service Discovery component whever the list of service endpoints changes
\item
Service discovery contains configuration rules about when and where to collect metrics as shown in Figure 5.9.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_9}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Figure 5.10 explains the pull model in detail.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_10}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
The metrics collector fetches fongiruation metadata of service endpoints from Service Discovery. Metadata include pulling interval, IP addresses, timeout, and retry parameters, etc.
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The metrics colelctor pulls metrics data via a pre-defined HTTP endpoint (for example, \lstinline|/metrics|).
\item
 To expose the endpoint,a client library usually needs to be added to the service.
\item
In figure 5.10, the service is Web Servers.
\end{itemize}

\item
Optionally, the metrics collector registers a change event notification with Service Discovery to receive an update whenever the service enpoints change. Alternatively, the metrics collector can poll for endpoint changes periodically.
\end{itemize}

At our scale, a single metrics collector will not be able to handle thousands of servers

\begin{itemize}
\item
We must use a pool of metrics collectors to handle the demand
\item
ONe common problem when there are multiple collectors is that multiple instances might try to pull data from the same resource and produce duplicate data
\item
There must exist some coordination scheme among the instances to avoid this
\item
One potential approach is to designate each collector to a range in a consistent hash ring, and then map every single server being monitored by its unique name in the hash ring
\item
This ensures one metrcs source server is handled by one collector only
\item
Let's take a look at an example
\item
As shown in Figure 5.11, there are four collectors and six metrics source servers
\item
Each collector is responsible for collectin metrics from a distinct set of servers
\item
Collector 2 is responsible for collecting metrics from Server 1 and Server 5
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_11}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Push model}

As shown in Figure 5.12, in a push model various metrics sources, such as web servers, database servers, etc, directly send metrics to the metrics collector.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_12}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

In a push model, a collection agent is commonly installed on every server being monitored.

\begin{itemize}
\item
A collection agent is a piece of long-running software that colelcts metrics from the services running on the server and pushes those metrics periodically to the metrics collector
\item
The collection agent may also aggregate metrics (especifally a simple counter) locally, before sending them to metric collectors
\item
Aggregation is an effective way to reduce the volume of data sent to the metrics collector
\item
If the push traffic is high and the metrics collector rejects the push with an error, the agent could keep a small buffer of data locally (possibly by storign them locally on disk), and resend them later
\item
However, if the servers are in auto-scaling roup where they are rotated our frequently, then holding data locally (even temporarily) might result in data loss when the metrics collector falls behind
\item
To prevent the metrics collector from falling behind in a push model, the metrics collector should be in an auto-scaling cluster with a load balancer in front of it (Figure 5.13)
\item
The cluster should scale up and down based on the CPU load of the metric collector servers
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_13}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Pull or push?}

So, which one is the better choice for us? Justlike many things in life. Both sides have widely adopted real-world use cases.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Examples of pull architectures include Prometheus
\item
Examples of push architectures include Amazon CloudWatch and Graphite [19]
\end{itemize}

Knowing the advantages and disadvantages of each approach is more important than picking a winner during an interview. Table 5.3 compares the proces and cons of push and pull architectures [20] [21] [22] [23].

Pull vs Push.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
\textbf{\underline{Easy Debugging}}

Pull

The metrics endpoint on application servers used for pulling
metrics can be used to view metrics at any time.

You can even do this on your laptop.

\textbf{Pull wins}

Push

N/A
\item

\textbf{\underline{Health check}}

Pull

If an application servser doesn't respond to the pull, you can quickly figure out if an application servser is down.

\textbf{Pull wins}

Push

If the metrics collector doesn't receive metrics, the problem mgiht be caused by network issues.

\item

\textbf{\underline{Short-lived jobs}}

Pull: N/A

Push

Some of the batch job smight be short-lived and don't last long enough to be pulled. \textbf{Push wins}. This can be fixed by introducing push gateways fo rthe pull model [24]

\item

Firewall or complicated network setups

Pull

Having servers pulling metrics requires all metric endpoints to be reachable. This is potentially problematic in multiple data center setups. It might require a more elaborate network infrastructure.


Push

If the metrics collector is set up with a load balancer and an auto-scaling group, it is possible to receive data from anywhere.

\textbf{Push wins}
\item

\textbf{\underline{Performance}}

Pull: Pull methods typically use TCP.

Push:
Push methods typically use UDP. This means the push method provides lower-latency transports of metrics. The counterargument here is that the effort of establishing a TCP connection is small compared to sending the metrics payload

\item
\textbf{\underline{Data authenticity}}

Push:

Application servers to collect metrics from are defined in config files in advance.

Metrics gathered from those servers are guaranteed to be authentic.

Pull:

Any kind of client can push metrics to the metrics collector

This can be fixed by whitelisting servers from which to accept metrics, or by requiring authentication.
\end{enumerate}

As mentioned above, pull vs push is a routine debate topic and there is no clear answer.

A large organization probably needs to support both,e speically with the popularity of serverless [25] these days.

There might not be a way to install an agent from which to push data in the first place.

\subsubsection{Scale the metrics transmission pipeline}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_14}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Let's zoom in on the metrics collector and time-series databases.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Whether you use a push or pull model, the metrics collector is a cluster of servers, and the cluster receives enormous amounts of data
\item
For either push or pull, the metrics collector cluster is setup for auto-scaling to ensure that there are an adequate number of collector instance to handle the demand
\item
However, there is a risk of data loss if the time-series database is unavailable
\item
To mitigate this proble, we introduce a queueing component as shown in Figure 5.15
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_15}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

In this design, the metrics collector sends metrics data to queuing systems like Kafka.

THe consumers or streaming processing services such as Apache Storm, Flink, and SPark, process and push data to the time-series database.

This approach has several advantages:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Kafka is used as a hihgly reliable and scalable distributed messaging platform
\item
It decouples the data collection and data processing services from each other
\item
It can easily prevent data loss when the database is unavailable, by retaining the data in Kafka
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_16}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Alternative to Kafka}

Maintaining a production-scale Kafka system is no msall undertaking.

\begin{itemize}
\item
You might get push back from the interviewer about this
\item
There are large-scale monitoring ingestion system sin use without using an intermediate queue
\item
Facebook's Gorilla [26] in-memory time-series database is a prime example; itis designed to reamin hgihly avilable for writes, even when there is a partial network failture
\item
It could be argued that ushc a design is as reliable as having an intermediate queue like Kafka
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Where aggregations can happen}

Metrics can be aggregated in different places; in the collection agent (on the client-side), the ingestion pipeline (before writing to storage), and the query side (after writing to storage)

Let's take a closer look at each of them.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
\textbf{Collection agent}:

The collection agent installed on the client-side only supports simple aggregation logic. For example, aggregate a counter every minute before it is sent to the metrics collector

\item

\textbf{Ingestion Pipeline}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
To aggregate data before writing to the storage, we usually need stream processing engines such as FLink
\item
The write volume will be significantly reduced since only the calculated result is written to the database
\item
However, handling late-arriving events could be a challenge and another downside is that we lose data precision and some flexibility because we no longer store the raw data
\end{itemize}
\item
\textbf{Query side}:

Raw data can be aggregated over a given time period at query time

There is no data loss with this approach, but the query speed might be slower because the query result is computed at query time and is run against the whole dataset.

\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Query service}

The query service comprises a cluster of query servers, which access the time-series databases and handle requrest from the visualizatoin or altering system

\begin{itemize}
\item
Having a dedicated set of query servers decouples time-series databases from the clients (visualziation and alerting systems)
\item
And this gives us the flexibility to change the time-series database or the visualization and altering systems, whenever needed
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Cache Layer}

To reduce the load of the time-series database and make query service more performance cache servers are added to store query results, as shown in Figure 5.17.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_17}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}


\subsubsection{The case against query service}

There might not be a pressing need to introduce oour own abstraction (a query service) because most industrical-scale visual and altering systems have powerful plugs to interface with well-known time-series databases on the market.

And with a well-chosen time-series database, there is no need to add our own caching, either.

\subsubsection{Time-series database query language}

Most popular metrics monitoring systems like Prometheus and InfluxDB don't use SQL and have their own query languages.

ONe major reason for this is that it is hard to build SQL queries to query time-series data.

For example, as metnioned here [27], computing an exponential moving average might look like this in SQL:

\begin{lstlisting}
select id,
       temp,
       avg(temp) over (partition by group_nr order by time_read)
       as rolling_avg
from (
    select id,
           temp,
           time_read,
           interval_group,
           id - row_number() over (partition by interval_group
                                    order by time_read) as group_nr
    from (
        select id,
               time_read,
               "epoch"::timestamp + "900 seconds"::interval *
                   (extract(epoch from time_read)::int4 / 900) as interval_group,
               temp
        from readings
    ) t1
) t2
order by time_read;
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Equivalent query in Flux}

\begin{lstlisting}
from(db:"telegraf")
    |> range(start:-1h)
    |> filter(fn: (r) => r._measurement == "foo")
    |> exponentialMovingAverage(size:-10s)
\end{lstlisting}


\subsection{Storage layer}

Now let's dive into the storage layer

\subsection{Choose a time-series database carefully}

\begin{itemize}
\item
According to a research paper published by Facebook [26], at least 85\% of all queries to the operational data store were for data collected in the past 26 hours.
\item
If we use a time-series database that harnesses this property, it could have a significant impact on overall system performance
\item
If you are interested in the design o fthe storage engine, please refer to the design document of the InfluxDB storage engine [28]
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Space optimization}

As explained in high-level requirements, the amount of metric data to store is enourmous. Here are a few strategies for tackling this.

\subsection{Data encoding and compression}

Data encoding and compression can significantly reduce the size of data. Those features are usually built into a good time-series database. Here is a simple example.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_18}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

As you can see in the image above, 1610087371 and 1610087381 differ by only 10 seconds, which takes only 4 bits to represent, instead of the full timestamp of 32 bits

So, rather than storing absolute values, the delta of the values can be stored along with one base value like: 1610087371, 10, 10, 9, 11.

\subsection{Downsampling}

Downsampling is the process of converting high-resolution data to low-resolution to reduce overall disk usage.

Since our data retention is 1 year, we can downsample old data.

For example, we can let engineers and data scientists define rules for different metrics.

Here is an example:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Retention: 7 days, no sampling
\item
Retention: 30 days, downsample to 1 minute resolution
\item
Retention: 1 year, downsample to 1 hour resolution
\end{itemize}

Let's take a look at another concrete example. It aggregates 10-second resolution data to 30-second resolution data.

\begin{tabular}{|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|}
\hline
metric & timestamp & hostname & \lstinline|metric_value| \\
\hline
cpu & 2021-10-24T19:00:00Z & host-a & 10 \\
\hline
cpu & 2021-10-24T19:00:10Z & host-a & 16 \\
\hline
cpu & 2021-10-24T19:00:20Z & host-a & 20 \\
\hline
cpu & 2021-10-24T19:00:30Z & host-a & 30 \\
\hline
cpu & 2021-10-24T19:00:40Z & host-a & 20 \\
\hline
cpu & 2021-10-24T19:00:50Z & host-a & 30 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Rollup from 10 second resolution data to 30 second resolution data

\begin{tabular}{|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|}
\hline
metric & timestamp & hostname & \lstinline|metric_value| \\
\hline
cpu & 2021-10-24T19:00:00Z & host-a & 10 \\
\hline
cpu & 2021-10-24T19:00:30Z & host-a & 25 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 5.5: 30-second resolution data

\subsubsection{Cold storage}

Cold storage is the storage of inactive data that is rarely used. The financial cost for cold storage is much lower.

In a nutshell, we should probably use third-party visualization and alerting systems, instead of building our own.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_19}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The alert flow works as follows:

1. Load config files to cache servers. Rules are defined as config files on the disk. YAML [29] is a commonly used format to define rules.

Here is an example of alert rules:

- name: \lstinline{instance_down}

rules:
Alert for any instance that is unreachable for >5 minutes
-alert: instance down
  expr: up == 0
  for: 5m
  label:
  severity: page

2. The alert manager fetches lart configs from the cache

3. Based on config rules the alert manager calls the query service at a predefied interval.

If the value violates the threshold, an alert event is created.

The alert amanager is responsible for the follow:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Filter, merge, and dedupe alerts. Here is an example of merging alerts that are trigger within one instace within a short amount of time (instance 1) (Figure 5.20)
\item
Access control. To avoid human error and keep the system secure, it is essential to restrict access to certain alert management operations to authorized individuals only.
\item
Retry. The alert manager checks alert states and ensures a notification is sent at least once
\end{itemize}

4. The alert sotre is a key-value database, such as Cassandra, that keeps the state (inactive, pending, firing, resolved) of all alerts. It ensures a notificiation is sent at least once

5. Eligible alerts are inserted into Kafka

6. Alert consumers pull alert events from Kafka.

7. Alert consuemrs process lart events from Kafka and send notifications

\subsection{Alerting system - building vs buy}

There are mny industrial-scale alerting system savailable off-the-shelf, and most provide tight integration with the popular time-series databases.

Many of these alerting systems integrate well with existing notification channels, such as email and PagerDuty.

In the real world, it is a tough call to justify building your own alert system.

In interview settings, especially for a senior position, be ready to justify your decision.

\subsection{Visualization system}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Visualization is built on top of the data layer
\item
Metrics can be shown on the metrics dashboard over various time scales and alerts can be shown on the alerts dashboard
\item
Figure 5.21 shows a dashboard that displays some o fthe metrics like the current server requests, memory/CPU utilization, page load time, traffic, and login information. [30]
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_21}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

A high quality visualization system is hard to build. The argument for using an off-the-shelf system is very strong. For example, Grafana can be a very good system for this purpose.

It integrates well with many popular time-series databases which you can buy.

\subsection{Step 4: Wrap Up}

In this chapter, we presented the design for a metrics monitoring and alerting system.

At a high level, we talked about data collection, time-series database, alerts, and visualization.

Then we went in-depth into some of the most important techniques/components

\begin{itemize}
\item
Push vs pull model for collecting metrics data
\item
Utilize Kafka to scale the system
\item
Choose the right time-series database
\item
Use downsampling to reduce data size
\item
Build vs buy options for alerting and visualization systems
\end{itemize}

We went through a few iterations to refine the design, and our final design looks like this:

    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_5_22}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\chapter*{Chapter 6: Ad Click Event Aggregation}

With the rise of Facebook, YouTube, TikTok, and the online media economy, digital advertising is taking an ever-bigger share of the total advertising spending.

\begin{itemize}
\item
As a result, tracking ad click events is very important
\item
In this chapter, we explore how to design an ad click event aggregation system at Facebook or Google scale
\item
Before we dive into technical design, let's learn about the core concepts of online advertising to better understand this topic
\item
One core benefit of online advertising is its mersurability, as quantified by real-time data
\item
Digital advertising has a core process called Real-Time Bidding (RTB), in which digital advertising  inventory is bought and sold
\item
Figure 6.1 shows how the online advertising process works
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_1}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}


The speed of the RTB process is important as it usually occurs in less than a second.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Data accuracy is also very important
\item
Ad click event aggregation plays a critical role in measuring the effectiveness of online advertising, which essnetially impacts how much money advertisers pay
\item
Based on the click aggregation results, campaign managers can control the budget or adjust bidding strategies, such as changing targeted audience groups, keywords, etc.
\item
The key metrics used in online advertising, including click-through rate (CTR) [1] and conversion rate (CVR) [2], depend an aggregated ad click data
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 1: Understand the Problem and Establish Design Scope}

The following set of questions helps to clairfy requirements and narrow down the scope.

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\textbf{Candidate}: What is the format of the input data?

\textbf{Interviewer}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
It's a log file located in different servers and the latest click events are appended to the end of the log file
\item
The event has the following attributes: \lstinline|ad_id|, \lstinline|click_timestamp|, \lstinline|user_id|, \lstinline|ip| and \lstinline|country|
\end{itemize}

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: What's the data volume?

\textbf{Interviewer}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
1 billion ad clicks per day and 2 million ads in total
\item
The number of ad click events grows 30\% year-over-year
\end{itemize}

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: WHat are some of the most important queries to support?

\textbf{Interviewer}:

The system  needs to support the following 3 queries:
\begin{itemize}
\item
Return the number of click events for a particular ad in the last $M$ minutes
\item
Return the top 100 most clicked ads in the past 1 minute. Both parameters should be ocnfigurable. Aggregation occurs every minute.
\item
Support data filtering by \lstinline|ip|, \lstinline|user_id| or \lstinline|country| for the above two queries
\end{itemize}

\item

\textbf{Candidate}: Do we need to worry about edge cases? I can think of the following:

\begin{itemize}
\item
There might be events that arrive later than expected
\item
There might be duplicated events
\item
Different parts of the system might be down at any time, so we need to consider system recovery
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Interviewer}: That's a good list. Yes, take these into consideration.

\item

\textbf{Candidate}: What is the latency requirement?
\textbf{Interviewer}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
A few minutes of end-to-end latency
\item
NOte that latency requirements for RTB and ad click aggregation are very different
\item
While latency for RTB is usually less  than one second due to the responsiveness requirement, a few minutes of latency is acceptable for ad click event aggregation because it is primaryly used for ad billing and reporting
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

With the information gathered above, we have both functional and non-functional requirements.

\subsection{Functional requirements}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Aggregate the number of clicks of \lstinline|ad_id| in the last $M$ minutes
\item
Return the top 100 most clicked \lstinline|ad_id| every minute
\item
Support aggregation filtering by different attributes
\item
Dataset volumeis at Facebook or Google scale (see the back-of-evenlop estimation section for detailed system scale requirements)
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Non-functional requirements}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Correctness fo the aggregation result is important as the data is used for RTB and ads billing
\item
Properly handle delayed or duplicate events
\item
Robustness. THe system should be resilient to partial failures
\item
Latency requirement. End-to-end latency shouldbe a few minutes, at most.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Back-of-the-envelope estimation}

Let's do an estimation to understand the scale of the system and the potential challenges we will need to address

\begin{itemize}
\item
1 billion DAU (Daily Active Users)
\item
Assume on average each user clicks 1 ad per day. That's 1 billion ad click events per day
\item
Ad click QPS $\frac{10^{9} \text{ events}}{10^{5} \text{ seconds in a day}} = 10,000$
\item
Assume peack ad click QPS is 5 times the average number. Peak QPS = 50,000 QPS
\item
Assume a single ad blick event occupies 0.1KB storage. Daily storage requirement is: \lstinline|0.1 KB x 1 billion = 100GB|. THe monlty storage requirement is about 3TB.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 2: Propose High-level Design and Get Buy-in}

In this section, we discuss query API design, data model, and high-level design

\subsubsection{Query API design}

The purpose of the API design is to have an agreement between the client and the server.

\begin{itemize}
\item
In a consumer app, a client is usually the end-user who uses the product
\item
In our case, however, a client is the dashboard user (data scientist, product manager, advertiser, etc.) who runs queries against the aggreation service
\end{itemize}

Let's review the functional requirements os we can better design the APIs.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Aggregate the number of clicks of \lstinline|ad_id| in the last $M$ minutes
\item
Return the top N most clicked \lstinline|ad_ids| in the last \lstinline|M| minut
\item
Support aggregation filtering by different attributes
\end{itemize}

We only need to APIs to support those three use cases because filtering (the last requirement) can be supported by adding query parameters ot the requests.

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
API & Detail \\
\hline
\lstinline|GET /v1/ads/{:ad_id}/aggregate_count| & Return aggregated event count for a give \lstinline|ad_id| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

filter & An identifer for different filtering strategies. For example, filter = 001 filters out non-US clicks & long \\

\end{tabular}

\textbf{Field} & \textbf{Description} & \textbf{Type} \\
\hline
ad\_id & The identifier of the ad & string \\
\hline

\hline
\end{tabular}

API 2: Return top $N$ most clicked \lstinline|ad_ids| in the last \lstinline|M| minutes

There are two types of data in the system: raw data and aggregated data

\subsection{Comparison}

The comparison between storing raw data and aggregated data is shown below.

\begin{tabular}{|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|}
\hline
 & Raw data only & Aggregated data only \\
\hline
 Pros &
\begin{itemize}
\item full data set
