\item support data filter and recalculation
\end{itemize} &
\begin{itemize}
\item
Smaller data set
\item
Fast query
\end{itemize} \\
\hline
Cons
&
\begin{itemize}
\item
Huge data storage
\item
Slow query
\end{itemize}
&
\begin{itemize}
\item
Data loss. This is derived data.
For example, 10 entries might be aggregated to 1 entry
\end{itemize}
\\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 6.12: Raw data vs aggregated data

Should we store raw data or aggregated data? Our recommendation is to store both. Let's take a look at why.

\begin{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
It's a good idea to keep the raw data
\item
If something goes wrong, we could use the raw data fordebugging
\item
If the aggregated data is corrupted due to a bad bug, we can recalculate the aggregated data from the raw data, after the bug is fixed
\end{itemize}

\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
Aggregated data should be stored as well
\item
The data size of the raw data is huge
\item
The large size makes querying raw data directly very ineffcieint
\item
To mitigate this problem, we run read queries on aggregated data
\end{itemize}

\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
Raw data served as backup data
\item
We usually don't need to query raw data unless recalculation is needed
\item
Old raw data could be moved to cold storage to reduce costs
\end{itemize}
\item
Aggregated data serves as active data. It is tune for query performance.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Choose the right database}

When it comes to choosing the right databsae, we4e need to evaluate the following:

\begin{itemize}
\item
What does the data look like? Is the data relational? Is it a document or a blob?
\item
Is the workflow read-heavy, write-heavy, or both?
\item
Is transaction support needed?
\item
Do the queries rely on many online analytical processing (OLAP) functions [3] like SUM, COUNT?
\end{itemize}

Let's examine the raw data first.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Even though we don't need to query the raw dat aduring normal operations, it is useful for data scientists or machine learning engineers to study user response prediction, behavioral targeting, relevance feedback, etc. [4].
\item
A shown in the back of the envelope estimation, the average write QPS is 10,000 and the peak QPS can be 50,000, so the sytem is write-heavy
\item
On the read side, raw data is used as backup and a source for recalculation, so in theory, the read volume is low
\item
Relational databases can do the job, but scaling the write can be challenging
\item
NoSQL databases like Cassandra and InfluxDB are more suitable because they are optimized for write and time-range queries
\item
Another option is to store the data in Amazon S3 wusing one of the column datta formats like ORC [5], Parquet [6], or AVRO [7]
\item
We could put a cap on the size of each file (say 10GB) and the stream processor responsible for writing the raw data could handle the file rotation when the szie cap is reached
\item
Since this setup may be unfamiliar for many in this design we use Cassandra as an example
\item
FOr aggregated data, it is time-series in nature and the worflow is btoh read and write heavy
\item
This is because, for each ad, we need to query the database every minute to diplay the aggregation count fo rcustomers
\item
This feature is useful for au-refreshing the dashboard or tiggering alters in a timely manner
\item
Since there are two million ads in total,  the workflow is read-heavy
\item
Data is aggregated and written every minute by the aggregation service, so it's write-heavy as well
\item
We could use the same type of database to store both raw data and aggregated data.
\item
NO we have discussed query API design and data model, let's put together the high-level design
\end{itemize}

\subsection{High-level design}

In real-time big data [8] processing, data usually flows into and out of the processing system as unboudned data streams.

The aggregation service works in the same way; the input is the raw data (unbounded data streams), and the output is the aggregated results (see Figure 6.2).

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_2}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Figure 6.2: Aggregation Workflow

\subsection{Asynchronous processing}

The design we currently have is sunchronous.

\begin{itemize}
\item
This is not good because the capacity of producers and consumers is not always equal
\item
Consider the following case; if there is a sudden increase in traffic and the number of events produced is far beyond what consumers can handle, consumers might get out-of-memory errors or experience an unexpected shotdown
\item
If one component in the syncronous link is down, the whole system stops working.
\item
A common solution is to adpt a message queue (Kafka) to decouple producers and consumers
\item
This makes the whole process asynchronous and producers/consumers can be scaled independently
\item
Putting everything we have discussed together, we come up with the high-level design as shown in Figure 6.3
\item
Log watcher, aggregation service, and database are decoupled by two message queues
\item
The database writer polls data form the message queue, transforms the data into the database format, and writes it to the database
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_3}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

What is stored in the first message queue? It contains ad click event data as shown in Table 6.13.

\begin{tabular}{|p{2cm}|p{3cm}|p{2cm}|p{2cm}|p{2cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|ad_id| & \lstinline|click_timestamp| & \lstinline|user_id| & \lstinline|ip| & \lstinline|country| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

What is stored in the second message queue? THe second message queue contains two-types of data:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Ad click counts aggregated at per-minute granularity

\begin{tabular}{|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|ad_id| & \lstinline|click_minute| & \lstinline|count| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 6.14: Data in the second message queue

\item
Top N most clicked ads aggregated at per-minute granularity


\begin{tabular}{|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|update_time_minute| & \lstinline|most_clicked_ads| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{itemize}

You might be wonderin why we don't write the aggregated results to the database directly. THe short answer is that we need the second message queue lik Kafka to achieve end-to-end exactly once semantics (atomic commit) [9].

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_4}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Next, let's dig into the details of the aggregation service

\subsection{Aggregation service}

The MapReduce framework is a good option to aggregate ad click evets
\begin{itemize}
\item
The directed acyclic graph (DAG) is a good model for it [10].
\item
The key to the DAG model is to break down the system into small computing units, like the MAP/Aggregate/Reduce nodes, as shown in Figure 6.5
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_5}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Each node is responsible for one single task, and it sends the processing result to its downstream nodes.

\subsection{Map node}

A Map node reads data from a data source, and then filters, and transforms the data.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_6}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

YOU might be wondering why nwe need the Map node.

\begin{itemize}
\item
An alternative option is to set up Kafka parittions or tags and let the aggregate nodes subscribe to Kfa direclty
\item
This works, but the input data may need to be cleaned or normalized , and these operations can be done by the Map node
\item
Another reason is that we may not have control over how data is produced and therefor events with ethe same \lstinline|ad_id| might land in different Kafka partitions
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Aggregate node}

An aggregate node counts ad click events by \lstinline|ad_id| in memory every minute

In the MapReduce paradigm, the AAggregate node is part of the Reduce.

So the map aggregate reduce process really means map-reduce-reduce.

\subsection{Reduce node}

\begin{itemize}
\item
A Reduce node reduces aggregated results from all ``Aggregate'' nodes to the final result
\item
For example, as shown in Figure 6.7, there are three aggregation nodes and each contains the top 3 most clicked ads within the node
\item
The Reduce node reduces the total number of most clicked ads to 3
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_7}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The DAG model represents the well-known MapReduce paradigm,

\begin{itemize}
\item
It is designed to take big data and use paralllel distributed computing to turn big data into little or regular-sized data
\item
In the DAG model, intermediate data can be stored in memory and fiferent nodes communicate with each other through either TCP (nodes running in different processes) or shared memory (nodes running in different threads)
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Main use cases}

Now that we understand how MapReduce works at the high level, let's take a look at how it can be utlized to support the main use cases:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Aggregate the number of clicks \lstinline|ad_id| in the last \lstinline|M| mins
\item
Return the top \lstinline|N| most clicked \lstinline|ad_ids| in the last \lstinline|M| minutes
\item
Data filtering
\end{itemize}


\subsection{Use case 1: aggregate the number of clicks}

As shown in FIgure 6.8, input events are partitioned by \lstinline|ad_id (ad_d % 3)| in Map nodes and are then aggregated by Aggregation nodes.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_8}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Use case 2: return top N most clicked ads}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Figure 6.9 shows a simplified design of getting the top 3 most clicked ads, which can be exteneded to top N.
\item
Input events are mapped using \lstinline|ad_id| and each Aggregate node maintains a heap data structure to get the top 3 ads within the node efficiently.
\item
In the last step, the Reduce node reduces 9 ads (top 3 from each aggregate node) to the top 3 most clicked ads every minute
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_9}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Use case 3: data filtering}

\begin{itemize}
\item
To support data filtering like ``show me the aggregated click count for ad001 within the USA only'', we can pre-define filtering criteria and aggregate based on them.

For example, the aggregation results look like this for \lstinline|ad001| and \lstinline|ad002|.
\end{itemize}

\begin{tabular}{|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|p{2cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|ad_id| & \lstinline|click_minute| & \lstinline|country| & \lstinline|count| \\
\hline
ad001 & 20210101001 & USA & 100 \\
\hline
ad001 & 20210101001 & GPB & 200 \\
\hline
ad001 & 20210101001 & others & 3000 \\
\hline
ad002 & 20210101001 & USA & 10 \\
\hline
ad002 & 20210101001 & GPB & 25 \\
\hline
ad002 & 20210101001 & others & 12 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 6.16: Aggregation results (filter by country)

This technique is called the star schema [11], which is widely used in data warehouses. THe filtering fields are called dimensions.

This approach has the following benefits:

\begin{itemize}
\item
It is simple to understand and build
\item
The current aggregation service can be reused to create more dimensions in the star schema. No additional component is needed.
\item
Accessing data based on filtering criteria is fast because the result is pre-calculated
\end{itemize}

A limitation with this approach is that it creates many more buckets and records, especially when we have a lot of filtering criteria.

\subsection{Step 3: Design Deep Dive}

In this section, we will dive deep in the following:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Steaming vs batching
\item
Time and aggregation window
\item
Delivery guarantees
\item
Scale the system
\item
Data monitoring and correctness
\item
Final design diagram
\item
Fault tolerance
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Streaming vs batching}

The high-level architecture we proposed in Fgiure 6.3 is a type fo stream processing syste. Table 6.17 shows the comparison of three types of systems [12]:

\begin{tabular}{|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|}
\hline
       & Services (online system) & Batch system (offline system) & Streaming system (near real-time system) \\
\hline
\textbf{Responsiveness} & Respond to the client quickly & No response to the client needed & NO response to the client needed\\
\hline
\textbf{Input} & User requests & Bounded input with finite size. A large amount of data & Input has no boundary (infinite streams)\\
\hline
\textbf{Output} & Responses to clients & Materialized views, aggregated metrics, etc. & Materialized views, aggregated metrics, etc.\\
\hline
\textbf{Performance measurement} & Availability. latency & Throughput & Throughput, latency\\
\hline
\textbf{Example} & Online shopping & MapReduce & Flink [13]\\
\hline
\end{tabular}

In our design, both stream processing and batch procesing are used.

\begin{itemize}
\item
We utilized stream processing to process data as it arrives and generates aggregated results in a near real-time fashin
\item
We utilized batch processing fo r hisotrical data backup
\item
For a system that contains two processing paths (batch and streaming) simultaenously,  this achitecture is called lambda [14]
\item
A disadvantage of lambda architecture is that you have two processing paths, meaning there are two codebases to maintian
\item
Kappa architecture [15], which combines the batch and streaming in one processing path, solves the problem
\item
The key idea is to handle both real-time data processing and continuous data reprocessing using a single stream processing engine
\item
Figure 6.10 shows a comparison of lambda and kappa architecture
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_10}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Our high-level design uses Kappa architecture, where the reprocessing of historical data also goes thorugh the real-time aggregation service. Se the ``Data recalculation'' section below for details.

\subsection{Data recalculation}

Sometimes we have to recalculate the aggregated data, also called historical data replay.

For example, if we discover a major bug in the aggregation service, we would need to recalculate the aggregated data from raw data starting at the point where the bug was introduced.

Figure 6.11 shows the data recalculation flow:

\begin{itemize}
\item
The recalculation service retrieves data from raw data storage. This is a batched jbo.
\item
Retrieved data is sent to a dedicated aggregation service so that the real-time processing is not impacted by historical data replay
\item
Aggregated results are sent to the second message queue, then updated in the aggregation database
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_11}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The recalculation process reuses the data aggregation service but uses a different data source (the raw data).

\subsection{Time}

We need a timestamp to perform aggregation. The timestamp can be generated in two different places:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Event time: when an ad click happens
\item
Processing time: referes to the system time of the aggregation server that processes the click event
\end{itemize}

Due to network delays and asynchronous environments (data go through a message queue), the gap between event time and processing time can be large. As shown in Figure 6.12, event 1 arrives at the aggregation service very late (5 hours later).

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_12}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
If event time is used for aggregation, we have to deal with delayed events
\item
If processing time is used for aggregation, the aggregation result may not be accurate
\item
There is no perfect solution, so we need to consider the trade-offs
\end{itemize}

\begin{tabular}{|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|}
\hline
 & \textbf{Pros} & \textbf{Cons} \\
\hline
 Event time & Aggregation results are more accurate because the client knows exactly when an ad is clicked & It depends on the timestamp generated on the client-side. Clients might have the wrong time, or the timestamp might be generated by malicious users \\
\hline
 Procesing time & Server timestamp is more reliable & The timestamp is not accurate if an event reahces the system at a much later time\\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 6.18: Event time vs processing time


Since data accuracy is very important, we recommend using event time for aggreation.

\begin{itemize}
\item
How do we properly process delayed events in this case?
\item
A technique called ``watermark'' is commonhly ustilized to handle slightly delayed events
\item
In figure 6.13, ad click events are aggregated in the one-minute tubling window (see the ``Aggregation window'' section on page 177 for more details)
\item
If event time is used to decide whether the event is in the window, window 1 misses event 2, and window 3 misses event 5 because they arrive slightly later than the end of their aggregation windows.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_13}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

One way to mitigate this problem is to use ``watermark'' (the extended rectangles in Figure 6.14), which is regarded as an extension of an aggregation window.

\begin{itemize}
\item
This improves the accuracy of the aggregation result.
\item
By extending an extra 15 second (adjustable) aggregation window, window 1 is able to include event 2, and window 3 is able to include event 5.
\item
The value set for the watermark depends on the business requirement
\item
A long watermark could catch events that arrive very late,m but it adds more latency to the system
\item
A short watermark means data is less accurate, but it adds latency to the system
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_14}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Notice that the watermark technique does not handle events that have long delays.

\begin{itemize}
\item
We can argue that it is not worth the return on investment (ROI) to have a complicated esign for low probability events
\item
We can always correct the tiny bit of inaccuracy with end-of-day reconciliation (see ``Reconciciliation'' section on page 189)
\item
One trade-off to consider is that using water mark improves data accuracy but increases overall latency, due to extended wait time
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Aggregation window}

According to the ``Designing data-intesive applications'' book by Martin Kleppmann [16], there are four types of window functions: tumbling window (also called fixed window), hopping window, sliding window, and session window.

\begin{itemize}
\item
We will discuss the tubling window and sliding window as they are most relevant to our system.
\item
In the tumnbling window (highlighted in Figure 6.15), time is partitioned into same-length, non-overlapping chunks.
\item
The tumbling window is a good fit for aggregating ad click events every minute (use case 1).
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_15}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
In the sliding window (highlighted in FIgure 6.16), events are grouped within a window that slides across the data stream, according to a specified interval
\item
A sliding window can be an overlapping one
\item
This is a good strategy to satisfy our second use case; to get the top \lstinline|N| most clicked ads during the last \lstinline|M| minutes.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_16}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Delivery guarantees}

Since the aggregation result is utilized for billing, data accuracy, and completeness are very important.

The system needs to be able to answer questions such as:

\begin{itemize}
\item
How to avoid processing duplicate events?
\item
How to ensure all events are processed?
\end{itemize}

Message queues such as Kafka usually provide three delivery semantics: at-most once, at-least once, exactly.

\subsection{Which delivery method should we choose?}

In most circumstances, at-least once processing is good enough if a small percentage of duplicates are acceptable.

\begin{itemize}
\item
However, this is not the case for our system
\item
Differences of a few percent in data points could result in discrepencies of millions of dollars
\item
Therefore, we recommend exactly-once delivery for the system
\item
If you are interested in learning more about a real-life ad aggregation system, take a look at how Yemp implements it [17]
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Data deduplication}

One of the most common data quality issues is duplicated data. Duplicated data can come from a wide range of sources and in this section, we discuss two common sources.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Client side

\begin{itemize}
\item
For example, a client might resend the same event multiple times
\item
Duplicated events sent with malicious intent are best hanlded by add fraud/risk control components
\item
If this is of interest, please refer to the reference material [18]
\end{itemize}

\item
Server outage

\begin{itemize}
\item
If an aggregation service node goes down in the middle of aggregation and the upstream service hasn't yet received an acknowledgment, the same events might be sent and aggregated again
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

Figure 6.17 shows how the aggregation service node (Aggregator) outage introduces duplicate data. The Aggregator managers the status of data consumption by storing the offset in upstream Kafka.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_17}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
If step 6 fails, perhaps due to Aggregator outage, events from 100 to 110 are already sent to the downstream, but the new offset 110 is not persisted in upstream Kakfa.
\item
In this case, a new Aggregator would consume again from offset 100, even if those events are already processed, causing duplicate data
\item
The most straightforward data solution (Figure 6.18) is to use external file storage, such as HDFS or S3, to record the offset
\item
However, this solution has issues as well.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_18}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

In step 3, the aggregator will process events from offset 100 to 110, only if the last offset stored in external storage is 100.

\begin{itemize}
\item
If the offset stoored in the storage is 110, the aggregator ignores events before offset 110
\item
But this design has a major problem: the offset is saved to HDFS or S3 (step 3.2) before the aggregation result is sent downstream
\item
If step 4 fails due to Aggregator outage, events from 100 to 110 wil never be processed ba newly brought up aggregator node, since the offset stored in external storage is 110
\item
To avoid data loss, we need to save the offset once we get an acknowledgment back from downstream
\item
The updated design is shown in Figure 6.19
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_19}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

In this design, if the Aggregator is down before step 5.1 is executed, events from 100 to 110 will be sent downstream again.

\begin{itemize}
\item
TO achieve exactly once processing, we need to put operations between step 4 to step 6 in one distributed transaction
\item
A distributed transaction is a transaction that works across sveveral nodes
\item
If an of the operations fails, the whole transaction is rolled back
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_20}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

As you can see it's not easy to dedupe data in large-scale systems. How to achieve exactly-once processing is an advanced topic. If you are interested in the details, please refer to reference material [9].

\subsection{Scale the system}

From the back-of-the-envelope estimation, we know the business grows 30\% per year, which doubles traffic every 3 years.

How do we handle this growth? Let's take a look.

Our system consits of three independent components: message queue, aggregation service, and database. Since these components are decoupled, we can scale each one idependently.

\subsection{Scale the message queue}

We have already sicussed how to scale the message queue extensively in the ``Distributed Message Queue'' chapter, so we'll only briefly touch on a few points.

\textbf{Producers}: We don't limit the number of producer instances, so the scalability orf producers can be easily achieved.

\textbf{Consumers}: Inside a consumer group, the rebalancing mechanism helps to scale the consumers by adding or removing nodes. As shown in FIgure 6.21, by adding two more consumers, each consumer only processes events form one partition.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_21}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

When there are hundreds of Kafka consumers in the system, consumer rebalance can be quite slow and could take a few minutes or even more.

Therefore, if more consumers need to be added, try to do it during off-peak hours to minimize impact.

\subsubsection{Brokers}

\begin{itemize}
\item
\textbf{Hashing key}

\begin{itemize}
\item

Using \lstinline|ad_id| as hashing key for Kafka parttion to store
events from the same \lstinline|ad_id| in the same Kafka partition.
\item
In this case an aggregation service can subscribe to all events of the same \lstinline|ad_id| from one single partition.
\end{itemize}

\item
\textbf{The number of partitions}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
If the number of partitions changes, events of the same \lstinline|ad_id| might be mapped to a different partition
\item
Therefore it's recommended to pre-allocate enough partitions in advance, to avoid dynamically incre4asing the number of partitions in production.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Topic physical sharding}

\begin{itemize}
\item
One single topic is not enough. We can split the data by geography (\lstinline|topic_north_america, topic_europe, topic_asia|, etc.) or by business type (\lstinline|topic_web_ads, topic_mobile_ads|, etc.)
\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Pros: slicing data to different topics can help increase the system throughput. WIth fewer consumers for a single topic, the time to rebalance consumer groups is reduced
\item
Cons: It introduces extra complexitiy and increases maintenance costs.
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Scale the aggregation service}

In the high-level design, we talked about the aggregation service being a map/reduce operation. Figure 6.22 shows how thigns are wired together.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_22}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

If you are interested in the details, please refer to reference material [19].

Aggregation service is horizontally scalable by adding or removing nodes.

Here is an interesting question; how do we increase the throughput of the aggregation service?

There are two options.

Option 1: allocate events with different \lstinline|ad_ids| to different threads, as shown in Figure 6.23.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_23}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}


Option 2: Deploy aggregation service nodes on resource provides like Apache Hadoop YARN [20]. YOu can think of this approach as utilizing multi-processing.

Option 1 is easier to implement and doesn't depend on resource providers. In reality, however, option 2 is more widely used because we can scale the system by adding more computing resources.

\subsection{Scale the database}

Cassandra natively supports horizontal scaling in a way similar to consistent hashing.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_24}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Data is evenly distributed to every node with a proper replication factor.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Each node saves its own part of the ring based on a hashed value and also saves copies from other virtual nodes
\item
If we add a new node to the cluster, it automatically rebalances the virtual nodes among all nodes.
\item
If we add a new node to the cluster, it autmatically rebalances the virtual nodes among all nodes
\item
No manual resharding is required
\item
See Cassandra's official documentation for more details [21]
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Hotspot issue}

A shard or service that receives much more data than the others is called a hotspot

\begin{itemize}
\item
This occurs because major compnaies have advertising budgeets in the millions of dollars and their ads are clicked more often
\item
Since events are partitioned by \lstinline|ad_id|, some aggreation service nodes might receive man more ad click events than others, potentially causing server overload
\end{itemize}

This problem can be mitigated by allocating more aggregation nodes to process popular ads. Let's take a look at an example as shown in Figure 6.25. Assume each aggregation node can handle only 100 events.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
Since there are 300 events in the aggregatino node (beyond the capacity of a node can handle), it applies for extra resources throught he resource mananger
\item
The resource mamanger allocates more resources (for example, add two more aggregation nodes) so the original aggregation node isn't overloaded
\item
The original aggregation node split events into 3 groups and each aggregation node handles 100 events
\item
The result is written back to the original aggregate node.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_25}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

There are more sophisticated ways to handle this problem, such as Global-Local Aggregation or Split Distinct Aggregation. For more information, please refer to [22].

\subsubsection{Fault tolerance}

Let's discuss the fault tolerance of the aggregation service.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Since aggregation happens in memory, when an aggregation node goes down, the aggregated result is lost as well
\item
We can rebuild the count by replaying events from upstream Kafka brokers
\item
Replaying data from the beginning of Kafka is slow
\item
A good practice is to save the ``system status'' like upstream offset to a snapshot and reover from the last saved status
\item
In our design, the ``system status'' is more than just the upstream offset because we need to store data like top \lstinline|N| most cliekd ads in the past \lstinline|M| minutes.
\end{itemize}

Figure 6.26 shows a simple example of what the data looks like in a snapshot.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_26}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

With a snapshot, the failover process of the aggregation service is quite simple.

\begin{itemize}
\item
If the aggregation service node fails, we bring up a new node and recover data form the latest snapshot (Figure 6.27)
\item
If there are new events that arrive after the last snapshot was taken, the new aggregation node will pull those data from the Kafka broker for replay
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_27}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Data monitoring and correctness}

As mentioned earlier, aggregation results can be used for RTB and billing purposes. It's critical to monitor the system's health and to ensure correctness.

\subsubsection{Continouous monitoring}

Here are some metrics we might want to monitor:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Latency.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Since latency can be introduced at each stage, it's invaluable to track timestamps as events flow through different parts of the system
\item
The differences between those timestamps can be exposed as latency metrics
\end{itemize}

\item
Message queue size.

\begin{itemize}
\item
If there is a sudden increase in queue size, we may need to add more aggregation nodes
\item
Notice that Kafka is a message queue implemented as a distributed commit log, so we need to monitor the records-lag metrics instead
\item
System resources on aggregation nodes: CPU, disk, JVM, etc.
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}


\subsection{Reconciliaton}

Reconciliation means comparing different sets of data in order to ensure data integrity.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Unlike reconciliation in the baking industry, where you can compare your records with the bank's records, teh result of ad click aggregation has no thiird-party result to reconcile with.
\item
WHat we can do is sort the ad click events by event time in every partition at the end of the day, by using a batch job and reconciling witht the real-time aggregation result.
\item
If we have higher accuracy requirements, we can use a smaller aggregation window; for example, one hour
\item
Please note, no matter which aggregation window is used, the result from the batch job might not match exactly with the real-time aggregation result, since some events might arrive late (see ``Time'' section on page 175)
\end{itemize}

Figure 6.28 shows the final design diagram with reconciliation support.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_28}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Alternative design}

In a generalist system design interview, you are not execpted to know the internals of different pieces of specialized software used in a big data pipeline.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Explaining your though process and discussing trade-offs is very important, which is why we propose a generic solution
\item
Another option is to store ad lick data in Hive, with an ElasticSearch layer built for faster queries
\item
Aggregation is usually done in OLAP databases such as CLickHOuse [23] or Druid [24]. Figure 6.29 shows the architecture.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_6_29}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

For more detail on this, please refer to reference material [25].

\subsection{Step 4: Wrap Up}

In this cahpter, we went through the process of desining an ad click event aggregation system at the scale of Facebook or Google. We covered:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Data model and API design
\item
Use MapReduce paradigm to aggregate ad lick events
\item
Scale the message queue, aggregation service, and database
\item
Mitigate hotspot issue
\item
Monitor the system continuously
\item
Use reconciliation to ensure correctness
\item
Fault tolerance
\end{itemize}

The ad click event aggregation system is a typical big data processing system.

\begin{itemize}
\item
It will be easier to undertsna dnd esign if you have prior knowledge or experience with industr standard solutions such as Apache Kafka, Apache Flink, or Apache Spark
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{chapter_6_summary}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\chapter*{Chapter 7: Hotel Reservation System}

In this chapter, we design a hotel reservation system for a hotel chain such as Marriott International.

THe design and techniques used in this chapter are also applicable to other popular booking-related interview topics.


\begin{itemize}
\item
Design Airbnb
\item
Design a flight reservation system
\item
Design a movie ticket boooking system
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 1: Understand the Problem and Establish DEsign Scope}

The hotel reservation system is complicated and its component vary based on business use cases. Before diving into the design, we should ask the itnerviewer clarification questions to narrow down the scope.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
\textbf{Candidate}: WHat is the scale of the system?

\textbf{Interviewer}: Let's assume we are building a website for a hotel chain that has 5000 hotels and 1 million rooms in total.

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Do customers pay when they make reservations or when they arrive at the hotel?

\textbf{Interviewer}: For simplicity, they pay in full when they make reservations.
\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Do customers book hotel rooms through the hotel's website only? Do we need to support other reservation options such as phone calls?

\textbf{Interviewer}: Let's assume people could book a hotel room through the hotel website or app?

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Can customers cancel their reservations?

\textbf{Interviewer}: Yes.

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Are there any other thigns we need to consider?

\textbf{Interviewer}:
\begin{itemize}
\item
Yes, we allow 10\% overbooking
\item
In case you do not know, overbooking means the hotel will sell more rooms than they actually have
\item
Hotels do this in anticipation that some customers will cancel their reservations
\end{itemize}

\item

\textbf{Candidate}: SInce we have limited time, I assume the hotel room search is not in scope. We focus in the following features.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Show the hotel-related page
\item
Show the hotel room-related detail page
\item
Reserve a room
\item
Admin panel to add/remove/update hotel or room infor
\item
Support the overbooking feature.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Interviewer}: Sounds good.

\item
\textbf{Interviewer}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
One more thign, hotel prices change dynamically
\item
The price of a hotel room depends on how full the hotel is expected to be on a given day
\item
For this interview, we can assume the price could be different each day
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Candidate}: I'll keep this in mind
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Non-functional requirements}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Support high concurrency. During peak season or big events, some popular books may have a lot of customers trying to book the same room
\item
Moderate latency. It's ideal to have a fast response time when a user makes the reservation, but it's acceptable if the system takes a few seconds to process a reservation request.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Back-of-the-envelope estimation}

\begin{itemize}
\item
5000 hotels and 1 million hotels in total
\item
Assume 70\% of the rooms are occupied and the average stay duration is 3 days
\item
Estimated daily reservations: $\frac{1 \text{ million} \times 0.7}{3}$ = 233,333 (rounding up to $\sim$ 240,000)
\item
Rservations per second = $\frac{240000}{10^{5} \text{ seconds in a day}} = \sim 3$. As we can see, the average reservation transaction per second (TPS) is not high.
\end{itemize}

Next, let's do a rough calculation of the QPS of all pages in the system. There are three steps in a typical customer flow:

\begin{enumerate}
\item
View hotel/room detail page. Users browse this page (query).
\item
View the booking page. Users can confirm the booking details, such as dates, number of guests, payment information before booking (query)
\item
Reserve a room. Users click on the ``book'' button to book the room and the room is reserved (transaction)
\end{enumerate}

Let's assume around 10\% of the users reach the next step and 90\% of users drop off the flow before reaching the final step.

\begin{itemize}
\item
We can also assume that no prefetching feature (prefetching the content before the user reachces the next step is implemented figure 7.1 shows a rough esimation of waht the QPS looks like for different steps)
\item
We know the final reservation TPS is 3 so we can work backward along the funnel
\item
The QPS of the order confirmation page is 30 and the QPS for the detail page is 300
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_1}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Step 2: Propose High-level Design and Get Buy-in}

IN this section, we'll discuss:

\begin{itemize}
\item
API design
\item
Data models
\item
High-level design
\end{itemize}

\subsection{API design}

We explore the API design for the hotel reservation system. The most important APIs are listed below using RESTful conventions.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Note that this chapter focuses on the design of a hotel reservation system.
\item
For a complete hotel website, the design needs to provide intuitive features for customers to search for rooms based on a large array of criteria
\item
The APIs for these search features, while important are not technically challenging
\item
They are out of scope for this chapter
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Hotel-related APIs}

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
API & Detail \\
\hline
GET \lstinline|/v1/hotels/ID| & Get detailed information about a hotel \\
\hline
POST \lstinline|/v1/hotels| & Add a new hotel. This API is only available to hotel staff\\
\hline
PUT \lstinline|/v1/hotels/ID| & Update hotel information. This API is only available to hotel staff\\
\hline
DELETE \lstinline|/v1/hotels/ID| & Delete a hotel. This API is only available to hotel staff\\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 7.1: Hotel-related APIs

\subsection{Room-related APIs}

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
API & Detail \\
\hline
GET \lstinline|/v1/hotels/ID| & Get detailed information about a room \\
\hline
POST \lstinline|/v1/hotels/ID/rooms| & Add a room. This API is only available to hotel staff\\
\hline
PUT \lstinline|/v1/hotels/ID/rooms/ID| & Update room information. This API is only available to hotel staff\\
\hline
DELETE \lstinline|/v1/hotels/ID/rooms/ID| & Delete a room. This API is only available to hotel staff\\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 7.2: Hotel-related APIs

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
API & Detail \\
\hline
GET \lstinline|/v1/reservations| & Get the reservation history of the logged-in user \\
\hline
GET \lstinline|/v1/reservations/ID| & Get detailed information about a reservation\\
\hline
POST \lstinline|/v1/reservations| & Make a new reservation\\
\hline
DELETE \lstinline|/v1/reservations/ID| & Cancel a reservation\\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 7.3: Reservation-related APIs

Making a new reservation  is a very important feature. THre request parameters of making a new reservation (\lstinline|POST /v1/reservations|) could look like this.

\begin{lstlisting}
{
  "startDate": "2021-04-28",
  "endDate": "2021-04-30",
  "hotelID": "245",
  "roomID": "U12354673389",
  "reservationID": "13422445"
}
\end{lstlisting}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Please note \lstinline|reservationID| is used as the idempotency key to prevent double booking
\item
Double booking means multiple reservations are made for the same room on the same day
\item
The details are explained in the ``Concurrency issue'' section on page 206
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Data model}

Before we decide which database to use, let's take a close look at the data access patterns.

For the hotel reservation system, we need to support the following queries.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
Query 1: View detailed information about a hotel
\item
Query 2: Find available types of rooms given a date range
\item
Query 3: Record a reservation
\item
Query 4: Look up a reservation or past history of reservations
\end{enumerate}

From the back-of-the-envelope estimation, we know the scale of the system is not large, but we need to prepare for traffic surges during big events.

With these requirements in mind, we choose a relational database because:

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
A relational database works well with read-heavy and write less frequently workflow
\item
This is because the number of users who visti the hotel webiste/apps is a few orders of magnitude higher than those who actually make reservations
\item
NoSQL databses are generally optimized for writes and the relational databse works well enough for read-heavy workflow
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
A relational database provides ACID (atomicity, consistency, isolation, durability) guarantees
\item
ACID properties are important for a reservation system
\item
With those properties, it's not easy to prevent problems such as negative balance, double charge, double reservations, etc.
\item
ACID properties make application code a lot simpler and make the whole system easier to reason about that
\item
A relational databse usually provides these guarantees
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
A relational database can easily model the data
\item
The structure of the business data is very clear and the relationship between different entities (\lstinline|hotel|, \lstinline|room|, \lstinline|room_type|, etc) is stable
\item
This kind of data model is easily modeled by a relational database
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

Now that we have chosen the relational database as our data store, let's explore the schema design. Figure 7.2 shows a striaghtforward schema design and it is the most natural way for many candidates to model the hotel reservation system.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_2}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Most attributes are self-explanatory and we will only explain the \lstinline|status| field in the reservation table. The \lstinline|status| field can be in one fo these states: \lstinline|pending|, \lstinline|paid|, \lstinline|refunded|, \lstinline|canceled|, \lstinline|rejected|.

The state machine is shown in Figure 7.3.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_3}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

This schema design has a major issue.

\begin{itemize}
\item
This data model works for compnaies like Airbnb as \lstinline|room_id| (might be called \lstinline|listing_id|) is given when users make reservations
\item
However, this isn't the case for hotels
\item
A user actually reservers a \textbf{a type of room} in a given hotel instread of a specific room
\item
For instance, a room type can be a standard room, king-size room, queen-size room with two queen beds, etc.
\item
Room numbers are given when the guest checks in and not at the time of the reservation
\item
We need to update our data model to reflect this new requirement.
\item
See ``Improved data model'' in the deep dive section
\end{itemize}

\subsection{High-level design}

We use the microservice architecture for this hotel reservation system.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Over the past few years, microservice architecture has gained great popularity
\item
Companies that use microservice include Amazon, Netflix, Uber, Airbnb, Twitter, etc.
\item
If you want to learn more about the benefits of a microservice architecture, you can checkout some good resources
\end{itemize}

Our design is modeled with the microservice architecture and the high-level design diagram is shown in Figure 7.4.


\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_4}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

We will briefly go over each component of the system from top to bottom:

\begin{itemize}
\item
User: a user books a hotel room on their mobile phone or computer
\item
Admin (hotel staff): authorized hotel staff perform administrative operations such as refunding a customer, canceling a reservation, updating room information, etc.
\item
CDN (content delivery network): for better load time, CDN is used to cache all static assets, including javascript bundles, images, videos, HTML, etc.
\item
Public API Gateway:

\begin{itemize}
\item
this is a fully managed service that supports rate limiting, authentication, etc.
\item
The API gateway is configured to direct requests to specific services based on the endpoints
\item
For example, requests to load the hotel homepage are directed to the hotel service and requests to book a hotel room are routed to the reservation service
\end{itemize}
\item
Internal APIs: those APIs are only available for authorized hotel staff. They are accessible through internal software or websites. They are usually further protected by a VPN (virtual private network)
\item
Hotel Service: this provides detailed information on hotels and rooms. Hotel and room data are generally static, so can be easily cached
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Rate service: this provides room rates for different future dates
\item
An interesting fact about the hotel industry is that the price of a room depends on how full the hotel is expected to be for a given day
\end{itemize}

\item
Reservation Service:

\begin{itemize}
\item
receives reservation requests and reserves the hotel rooms
\item
This service also tracks rooom inventory as rooms are reserved or reservations are canceled
\end{itemize}

\item
Payment Service: executes payment from a customer and updates the reservation status to \lstinline|paid| once a payment trasacation succeeds, or \lstinline|rejected| if the transaction fails

\item
Hotel Management Service:

\begin{itemize}
\item
only available to authorized hotel staff
\item
Hotel staff are eligible to use the follow features: view the record of an upcoming reservation, reserve a room for a customer, cancel a reservation, etc.
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

For clarity, Figure 7.4 omits many arrows of interactions between microservices.

\begin{itemize}
\item
FOr example, as shown in Figure 7.5, there should be an arrow between Reservation service and Rate service
\item
Rservation service queries Rate service for room rates
\item
THis is used to compute the total room charge for a reservation
\item
ANother example is that there should be many arrows connecting the Hotel Management Service with most o fthe other services
\item
When an admin makes changes via Hotel Management Service, the requests are forwarded to the actual service woning the data, to handle the changes
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_5}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

For production systems, inter-service communication often eomploys a modern and high-performance rmote produce call (RPC) framework like gPRC.

There are many benefits to using such frameworks.

To learn more about gPRC in particular, check out [3].

\subsection{Step 3: Design Deep Dive}

Now we've talked about the high-level design, let's go deeper into the following:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Improved data model
\item
Concurrency issues
\item
Scaling the system
\item
Resolving data inconsistency in the microservice architecture
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Improved data model}

As mentioned in the high-level design, when we reserve a hotel room, we actually reserve a type of room, as opposed to a specific room.

What do we need to change about the API and schema to accommodate this?

For the reservat  on API, \lstinline|roomID| is replaced by \lstinline|roomTypeID| in the request parameter.

THe API to make a sreservation looks like this:

\lstinline|POST /v1/reservations|

Request parameters
\begin{lstlisting}
{
  "startDate": "2021-04-28",
  "endDate": "2021-04-30",
  "hotelID": "245",
  "roomTypeId": "12354673389",
  "reservationID": "13422445",
}
\end{lstlisting}

The updated schema is shown in Figure 7.6.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_6}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

We'll briefly go over some of the most important tables.

\textbf{room}: contains information regarding a room.


\lstinline{room_type_rate}: stores price data for a specifc room type, for future dates

\textbf{reservation}: records guest reservation data

\lstinline{room_type_inventory}:  stores inventory data about hotel rooms. This table is very important for the reservation system, so let's take a close look at each column.

\begin{itemize}
\item
\lstinline|hotel_id|: ID of the hotel
\item
\lstinline|room_type_id|: ID of a room type
\item
\lstinline|date|: a single data
\item
\lstinline|total_inventory|: the total number of rooms minus those that are teporarily takes off the inventory. SOMe rooms might be taken off from the market for maintenance
\item
\lstinline|total_reserved|: the total number of rooms booked for the specified \lstinline|hotel_id|, \lstinline|room_type_id|, and \lstinline|date|
\end{itemize}

There are other ways to design the \lstinline|room_type_inventory| table, but having one row per data makes mananging reservations within a data range and queries easy.

\begin{itemize}
\item
As shown in FIgure 7.6, (\lstinline|hotel_id|, \lstinline|room_type_id|, \lstinline|data|) is the composite primary key
\item
The rows of the table are pre-populated by querying the inveotry data across all future dates within 2 years
\item
We have a scheduled daily job that pre-populates inventory data when the dates advance further
\item
Now that we've finalized the scema design, let's do some estimation about the storage volume
\item
As mentioned in the back-of-the-envelope estimation, we have 5000 hotels
\item
Assume each hotel has 20 types of rooms
\item
That's $5000 \text{ hotels} \times 20 \text{ types of rooms} \times 2 \text{ years} \times 365  \text{ days} = 73 \text{ million rows}$
\item
73 million is not a lot of data and a single database is enough to store the data
\item
However, a single server means a single point of failure
\item
To acheive high availability, we could set up database replications across multiple regions or availability zones.
\end{itemize}

Table 7.4 shows the sample data of the \lstinline|room_type_inventory| table.

\begin{tabular}{|p{2cm}|p{3cm}|p{2.2cm}|p{3cm}|p{3cm}|}
\hline
\textbf{hotel\_id} & \textbf{room\_type\_id} & \textbf{date} & \textbf{total\_inventory} & \textbf{total\_reserved} \\
\hline
211 & 1001 & 2021-06-01 & 100 & 80 \\
\hline
211 & 1001 & 2021-06-02 & 100 & 82 \\
\hline
211 & 1001 & 2021-06-03 & 100 & 86 \\
\hline
211 & 1001 & ... & ... & \\
\hline
211 & 1001 & 2023-05-31 & 100 & 0 \\
\hline
211 & 1002 & 2023-06-01 & 100 & 0 \\
\hline
2210 & 101 & 2021-06-01 & 30 & 23 \\
\hline
2210 & 101 & 2021-06-02 & 30 & 25 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 7.4: Sample data of the \lstinline|room_type_inventory| table

The \lstinline|room_type_inventory| table is utilized to check if a customer can reserve a specific type of room or not. THe input and ouput for a reservation might look like this:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Input: \lstinline|startData| (2021-07-01), \lstinline|endDate| (2021-07-03), \lstinline|roomTypeId|, \lstinline|hotelId|, \lstinline|numberOfRoomsToReserve|
\item
Output: \lstinline|True| if the specified type of room has inventory and users can book it. Otherwise, it returns \lstinline|False|
\end{itemize}

From the SQL perspective, it contains the following two steps:

\begin{enumerate}
\item
Select rows within a data range

\begin{lstlisting}
SELECT data, total_inventory, total_reserved
FROM room_type_inveotry
WHERE room_type_id = ${roomTypeId} and hotel_id = ${hotelId}
AND data between ${startDate}$ and ${endDate}
\end{lstlisting}

This query returns data like this:

\begin{tabular}{|p{3cm}|p{3cm}|p{2cm}|}
\hline
date & total\_inventory & total\_reserved \\
\hline
2021-07-01 & 100 & 97 \\
\hline
2021-07-02 & 100 & 96 \\
\hline
2021-07-03 & 100 & 95 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 7.5: Hotel inventory
\item
For each entry, the application checks the condition below:

\begin{lstlisting}
if ((total_reserved + ${numberOfRoomsToReserve}) <= total_inventory)
\end{lstlisting}
\end{enumerate}

If the condition returns \lstinline|True| for all entires, it means there are enough rooms for each data within the data range.

ONe of the requirements is to support 10\% overbooking. With the new schema, it is easy to implement:

\begin{lstlisting}
if ((total_reserved + ${numberOfRoomsToReserve} <= 100% * total_inventory))
\end{lstlisting}

At this point, the interviewer might ask a follow-up question: ``if the reservation data is too large for a single database, what would you do?''

There are a few strategies:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Store only current and future reservation data. Reservation history is not frequently accessed. SO they can be archived and some can even be moved to cold storage.
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Database sharding

\begin{itemize}
\item
The most frequent queries include making a reservation or looking up a reservation by name
\item
In both queries include making a reservation or looking up a reservation by name
\item
In both queries, we need to choose the hotel first, meaning \lstinline|hotel_id| is a good sharding key
\item
The data can be sharded by \lstinline|hash(hotel_id) % number_of_servers|
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Concurrency Issues}

Another important problem to look at is double boooking. We need to solve two problems:

\begin{enumerate}
\item
The same user clicks on the book button multiple times
\item
Multiple users try to book the same room at the same time
\end{enumerate}

Let's take a look at the first scenario. As shown in FIgure 7.7, two reservations are made.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_7}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

There are two common approahces to solve this problem:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Client-side implementation
\begin{itemize}
\item
A client can gray out, hide or disable the ``submit'' button once a request is sent
\item
This should prevent the double-clicking issue most of the time
\item
However, this approach is not very reliable
\item
For example, users can disable javascript, thereby bypassing the client check
\end{itemize}

\item
Idempotent APIs

\begin{itemize}
\item
Add an idempotency key in the reservation API request
\item
An API call is idempotent if it produces the same result no matter how many times it is called
\item
Figure 7.8 shows how to use the idempotency key \lstinline|reservation_id| to avoid the double-reservation issue. The detailed steps are explained below.
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_8}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{enumerate}
\item
Generate a reservation order

\begin{itemize}
\item
After a customer enters detailed information about the reservation (room type, check-in date, check-out date, etc) and clicks the ``continue'' button, a reservation order is generated by the reservation service
\end{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The system generates a reservation order for the customer to review
\item
The unique \lstinline|reservation_id| is generated by a globally unique ID generator and returned as part of the API response
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_9}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\item[3a.]

\begin{itemize}
\item
Submit reservation 1

\begin{itemize}
\item
The \lstinline|reservation_id| is included as part of the request
\item
It is the primary key of the reservation table (Figure 7.6)
\item
Please note that the idempotency key doesn't have to be the \lstinline|reservation_id|
\item
We choose \lstinline|reservation_id| because it already exists and works well for our design
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\item[3b.]

\begin{itemize}
\item
If a user clicks the ``Complete my booking'' button a second time, reservation 2 is submitted
\item
Because \lstinline|reservation_id| is the primary key of the reservation table, we can rely on the unique constraint of the key to ensure no double reservation happens
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

Figure 8.10 explains why double reservation can be avoided.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_10}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Scenario 2: what happens if multiple users book the same type of room at the same time when there is only one room left? Let's consider the scenario as shown in Figure 7.11.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_11}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Let's assume the database isolation level is not serializable [5]
\item
User 1 and User 2 try to book the same type of room at the same time, but there is only 1 room left
\item
Let's calll User 1's execution transaction 1 and User 2's execution transact  on 2
\item
At this time there are 100 rooms in the hotel and 99 of them are reserved
\end{itemize}

\item
Transaction 2 checks if there are enough rooms left by checking if (\lstinline|total_reserved + rooms_to_book| $\leq$ \lstinline|total_inventory|). Since there is 1 more room left, it returns \lstinline|True|.

\item
Transaction 1 checks it there are enough roooms by checking if (\lstinline|total_reserved + rooms_to_book| $\leq$ \lstinline|total_inventory|.) Since there is 1 more room left, it returns \lstinline|True| as well.

\item
Transaction 1 reserves the room and updates the inventory: \lstinline|reserved_room| becomes 100.
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Then transaction 2 reserves the room
\item
The \textbf{isolation} property in ACID means database transactions must complete their tasks independently from other transactions
\item
So data changes made by transaction 1 are not visible to transaction 2 until transaction 1 is completed (committed)
\item
So transaction 2 still sees \lstinline|total_reserved| as 99 and reserves ithe room by updating the inventory: \lstinline|reserved_room| becomes 100.
\item
This result in the system allowing both users to book a room, but there is only 1 room left
\end{itemize}

\item
Transaction 1 succesfully commits the change
\item
Transaction 2 successfully commits the change
\end{enumerate}

The solution to this problem generally requires some form of locking mechanism. We explore the following techniques:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Pessimistic locking
\item
Optimistic locking
\item
Database constraints
\end{itemize}

Before jumping into a fix, let's take a look at the SQL pseudo-code utlized to reserve a room. The SQL has two parts:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Check room inventory
\item
Reserve a room
\end{itemize}

\begin{lstlisting}
# step1: check room inventory

SELECT date, total_inventory, total_reserved
FROM room_type_inventory
WHERE room_type_id = ${roomTypeId} AND hotel_id = ${hotelId}
AND data between ${startDate} and ${endDate}

$ For every entry returned from step 1
if ((total_reserved + ${numberOfRoomsToReserve}) > 110% * total_inventory) {
  Rollback
}

$ step 2: reserve rooms
UPDATE room_type_inventory
SET total_reserved = total_reserved + ${numberOfRoomsToReserve}
WHERE room_type_id = ${roomTypeId}
AND data between ${startDate} and ${endDate}

Commit
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Option 1: Pessimistic locking}

The pessimistic locking [6], also called psessimistic concurrency control, prevents simulateneous updates by placing a lock on a record as soon as one user starts to update it

\begin{itemize}
\item
Other users who attempt to update the recaord have to wait until the first user has released the lock (committed the changes)
\item
For MySQL, the ``\lstinline{SELECT ... FOR UPDATE}''tement works by locking the row retruend by a selection query
\item
Let's assume a transcation is started by ``transaction 1''.

\item
Other transactions have to wait for transaction 1 to finish before beginning another transaction.
\end{itemize}

A detailed explanation is shown in Figure 7.12.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_12}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_12}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Figure 7.12: Pessimistic locking

In Figure 7.12, the ``\lstinline|SELECT ... FOR UPDATE|'' statement of transaction 2 waits for transaction 1 to finish because 1 locks the rows.

After transaction 1 finishes, \lstinline|total_reserved| becomes 100, which means there is no room for user 2 to book.

\textbf{Pros:}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Prevents applications from updating data that is being or has been changed
\item
It is easy to implement and it avoids conflict by serializing updates. Pessimistic locking is useful when data contention is heavy.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Cons:}
\begin{itemize}
\item
Deadlocks may occur when multiple resrouces are locked. Writing deadlock-free applicaton code could be challenging
\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
This approach is not scalable

\begin{itemize}
\item
If a transaction is locked for too long, other transactions cannot access the resource
\item
This has a significant impact on database performance, especially when transactions are long-lived or involve a lot of entities
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

Due to these limitations, we do not recommend pessimistic locking for the reservation system.

\subsection{Option 2: Optimistic locking}

Optimistic locking [7]m, also referred to as optimistic concurrency control, allows multipel concurrent users to attemp to update the same resource.

\begin{itemize}
\item
There are two common ways to implement optimistic lcoking: version number and timestamp
\item
Version number is generally considered to be a better option because the server clock can be inaccurate over time
\item
We explain how optimistic locking works with version number
\end{itemize}

Figure 7.13 shows a successful case and a failure case.

\begin{itemize}
\item
A new column called \lstinline|version| is added to the database table
\item
Before a user modifies a database row, the application reads the version number of the row
\item
When the user updates the row, the application increases the version number by 1 and writes it back to the database
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
A database validation check is put in place; the next version number should exceed the current version number by 1.
\item
The transaction aborts if the validation fails and the user tries again from step 2
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

Optimisitc locking is usually faster than pessimistic locking because we do not lock the database.

\begin{itemize}
\item
However, the performance of optimistic locking drops dramatically when concurrency is high
\item
To understand why, consider the case when many clients try to reserve a hotel room at the same time
\item
Because there is no limit on how many lcients can read the available room count, all of them read back the same available room count and the current version number
\item
When different clients make reservations and write back the results to the database, only one of them will succeed, and the reset of the clients receive a version check failure message
\item
These clients have to retry
\item
In the subsequent round of retries, there is only one successful client again, and the reset have to retry
\item
ALthough the end result is cocrect, repeated retries cause a very unpleasant user experience
\end{itemize}

Pros:

\begin{itemize}
\item
It prevents applications from editing stale data
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
We don't need to lock the database reousrce
\item
THere's actually no locking from the database point of view
\item
It's entirely up to the application to handle the logic with the version number
\item
Optimistic locking is generally used when the data contention is low. When conflicts are rare, transactions can complete wihtout the expense of managing locks
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

Cons:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Perofmrance is poor when data contention is heavy
\end{itemize}

Optimisitc locking is a good option for a hotel reservation system since the QPS for reservations is usually not high.

\subsection{Option 3 : Database constraints}

This approach is very similar to optimistic locking.

Let's explore how it works. In the \lstinline|room_type_inventory| table, add the following constraint:

\begin{lstlisting}
CONSTRAINT `check_room_count` CHECK((`total_inveotry - total_reserved`) >= 0)
\end{lstlisting}

Using the same example as shown in FIgure 7.14, when user 2 tries to reserve a room, \lstinline|total_reserved| becomces 101, which violates the \lstinline|total_inventory| (100) - \lstinline|total_reserved| (101) $\geq$ 0 constraint. The transaction is then rolled back.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_14}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\textbf{Pros}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Easy to implement
\item
It works well when data contention is minimal
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Cons}

\begin{itemize}
\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
Similar to optimistic lockign, when data contention is ehavy, it can result in a high volume of failures
\item
Users could see there are rooms available, but when they try to book one, they get the ``no rooms available'' response
\item
The experience can be frustraing to users
\end{itemize}
\item

The database constraints cannot be version-controlled easily like the application code
\item
Not all databases support constraints . It might cause problems when we migrate from one database solution to another
\end{itemize}

Since this approach is easy to implement and the data contention for a hotel reservation is usually not high (low QPS), it is another good option for the hotel reservation system.

\subsection{Scalability}

Usually, the load of the hotel reservation system is not high
\begin{itemize}
\item
However, the interviewer might have a follow-up question: ``what if the hotel reservation system is used not jsut for ahotel chain but for a popular travel site such as booking.com or expedia.com?''
\item
In this case, the QPS could be 1000 times higher.
\item
When the system load is high, we need to udnerstand what might become the bottleneck
\item
All our services are stateless, so they can be easily expanded by adding more servers
\item
THe database, however, contains all the states and cannot be scaled up by simply adding more databases. Let's explore how to scale the database
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Database sharding}

One way to scale the database is to apply database sharding.

\begin{itemize}
\item
THe idea is to split the data into multiple databases so that each of them only contains a portion of data
\item
When we shard a databse, we need to consider how to distribute the data
\item
As we see from the data model section, most queries need to filter by \lstinline|hotel_id|
\item
So a natural conclusion is we shard data by \lstinline|hotel_id|
\item
IN Figure 7.15, the load is spread among 16 shards
\item
Assume the QPS is 30,000
\item
After database sharding, each shard handles $\frac{30,000}{16}$ = 1,875 QPS, which is within a single MySQL server's load capacity
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_15}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Caching}

The hotel inveotry data has an interestin characteristic; only current and future hotel inveotry data are menaingful because  customers can only book rooms in the near future.

So for the storage choice, ideally we want to have a time-to-live (TTL) mechanism to expire old data automatically.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Historical data can be queried on a different database
\item
Redis is a good choice because TTL and Least Recently Used (LRU) cache eviction policy help us make optimal use of memory
\item
If the loading speed and database scalbility become an issue (for instance, we are designing at booking.com or Expedia's scale), we can add a cache layer on top of the database and move the check room inventory and reserve room logic to the cache layer, as shown in Figure.16
\item
In this design, only a small percentage of the requests hit the inventory databse as most ineligible requests are blocked by the invetory cache
\item
One thing worth mentioning is that even when there is enough inventory shown in Redis, we still need to recheck the inveotry at the database side as a precaution
\item
The database is the source of truth for the inventory data
\end{itemize}


\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_16}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Let's first go over each component in this system.

\textbf{Reservation service}: supports the following inventory management APIs:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Query the number of available rooms for a given hotel, room type, and date range
\item
Reserve a room by executin \lstinline|total_reserved + 1|
\item
Update inveotry when a user cancels a reservation
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Inventory Cache}: all invetory management query operations are moved to the inventory cache (Redis) and we need to pre-populate inveotry data to the cache.

The cache is a key-value store with the following structure:

\begin{lstlisting}
key: hotel-D_roomTypeID_{date}
value: the number of available rooms for the given hotel ID,
room type ID and date.
\end{lstlisting}

FOr a hotel reservation syste, the volume of read operations (check room inventory) is an order of magnitude higher than write operations.

Most of the read operations are answered by the cache.

\textbf{Inventory DB}: stores invetory data as the source of truth.

\subsection{New challenges posed by the cache}

Adding a cahce layer significantly increases the system scalability and throughput, but it also introduces a new challenge: how to maintain data consistency between the database and the cache.

When a user boooks a room, two operations are executed in the happy path:

\begin{enumerate}
\item

Query room invetory to find out if there are enough rooms left. THe query runs on the Inventory cache.

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Update inventory data
\item
The inventory DB is updated first
\item
The change is tehn propagated to the cache asynchronously
\item
This asynchrnous cache update could be invoked by the application code, which updates the invetory cache after data is saved to the database
\item
It could also be propagated using change data capture (CDC) [8]
\item
CDC is a mechnaism that reads data changes fomr the database and applies the changes to another data system
\item
One common solution is Debezium [9]
\item
It uses a source connector to read changes from a database and applies them to cache solutions such as Redis [10]
\end{itemize}


Because the inveotry data is updated on the database first, there is a possibility that the cache does not reflect the latest invetory data.

\begin{itemize}
\item
For example, the cache may report there is still an empty room when the database says there is no room left or vice versa
\item
If you think carefully, you find that the inconsistentcy between invoetyr cache and database actually  does not matter, as long as the databse does the final inventory validation check
\end{itemize}

\item
Let's take a look an example.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Let's say the cache says there is still an emtpy room, but the database says no
\item
IN this case, when the user queries the room inventory, they find there is still room available, so they try to reserve it
\item
When the request reaches the inveotry databse, the database does the validation and finds that there is no room left
\item
In this case, the client receives an error, indicating someone else just booked the last room before them
\item
WHen a user refreshes the website, they probably see there i sno room left because the databse has synchronized invetory data to the cache, before they click the refresh button
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Pros}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Reduced database load. Since read queries are answered by the cache layer, database load is significnatly reduced
\item
High performance: Read quries are very fast because reuslts are fetched from memory.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Cons}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Maintaining data consistency between the database and cache is hard. We need to think carefully about how this incosistency affects user experience.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Data inconsistency among services}

In a traditional monlithic architecture [11], a shared relational database is used to ensure data consitency.

\begin{itemize}
\item
In our microservice design, we chose a hybrid approach by having Reservation SErvice handle both reservation and invetory APIs so that the invotry and reservation database tables are stored in the same relational databse
\item
As explained in the ``Concurrency issues'' section on page 206, this arrangement allows us to leverage the ACID  properties of the relational databse to elegantly handle many concurrency issues that arise during the reservation flow.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_17}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

This pure design introduces many data consistency issues.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Since this is the first time we cover microservices, let's explain how and why it happens
\item
To make it easier to understand, only two services are used in this discussion
\item
In the real world, there could be hundereds of microservices within a company
\item
In a monolithic architecture, as shown in Figure 7.18, different operations can be wrapped in a single transaction to ensure ACID properties
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_18}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

However, in a microservice architecture, each service has its own database.

\begin{itemize}
\item
ONe logically atomic operation can span multiple services
\item
THis means we cannot use a single transaction to ensure data consistency
\item
As shown in Figure 7.19, if the update operations fails in the reservation database, we need to roll back the reserved room count in the inveotry database
\item
Generally, there is only one happy path, but many failure cases that could cause data inconsistency.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_7_19}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

To address the data inconsistency, here is high-level summary of industry-proven techniques. If you want to read the details, please refere to the reference materials.

\begin{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Two-phase commit (2PC) [12], 2PC is a databse protocol used to guarantee atomic transaction commit across multiple nodes, i.e., either all nodes succeeded or all nodes failed
\item
Because 2PC is a blocking protocol, a single nod efailure blocks the progress until the node has recovered. It's not performant.
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Saga.

\begin{itemize}
\item
A Saga is a sequence of local transactions
\item
Each transaction updates and publishes a message to trigger the next transaction step

\item
If a step fails, the saga executes compenstating transactions to undo the changes that were made by prceding transactions [13]
\item
2PC works as a single commit to perform ACID transactions while Saga consists of multiple steps and relies on eventual consistency
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\end{itemize}

It is worth nothing that addressing data inconsistency between microservices requires some complicated  mechanisms that greatly incease the complexity of the overall design.

\begin{itemize}
\item
