It is up to you as an architect to decide if the added complexity is worth it
\item
For this proble, we decided that it was not worth it and so went with the more pragmatic approach of storing reservation and ivnetory data under the same relational database
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 4: Wrap Up}

In this chapter, we presented a design for a hotel reservation system.

We started by gathering requirements and calculating a back-of-the-envelope estimation to understand the scale

\begin{itemize}
\item
In the high level design , we presente d the API design, the first draft of the data model, and the system architecture diagram
\item
In the deep dive, we explored alternative database schema design as we realized reservations should be made at the room type level, as opposed to specific rooms
\end{itemize}

We discussed race conditions in depth and proposed a few potential solutions:

\begin{itemize}
\item
pessimistic locking
\item
optimistic locking
\item
database constraints
\end{itemize}

We then discussed different approaches to scale the system, includign databse sharding and using Redis cache.

Lastly, we wen through data consistency issues in microservice architecture and briefly went through a few solutions.

\chapter*{Chapter 8}

In this chapter, we design a large-scale email service, such as Gmail, Outlook, or Yahoo Mail.

The growth of the internet has led to an explosing in the volume of emails.

In 2020, Gmail had over 1.8 billion active users and Outlook had over 400 million useres worldwide [1] [2].

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_8_1}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Step 1: Understand the Problem and Establish Design Scope}

Over the years, email services have changed significantly in complexity and scale.

A modern email service is a complex system with many features.

There is no way we can design a real-world system in 45 minutes.

So before juping into the design we definitely want to ask clarifying questions to narrow down the scope.

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\textbf{Candidate:} How many people use the product?

\textbf{Interviewer:} One billion Users.

\item

\textbf{Candidate:} I think the following features are important.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Authentication
\item
Send and receive emails
\item
Fetch all emails
\item
Filter emails by read and unread status
\item
Search emails by subject, sender, and body
\item
Anti-spam and anti-virus
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Interviewer}: That's a good idea. We don't need to worry about authenticaion. Let's focus on the other features you mentioned.

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: How do users connect with mail servers?

\textbf{Interviewer}:
\begin{itemize}
\item
Traditionally, users connect with mail servfers through native clients that use SMTP, POP, IMAP, and vendor-specific protocols.
\item
These protocols are legacy to some extent, yet still very popular
\item
For this interview, let's assume HTTP is used for client and server communication
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Candidate}: Can emails have attachments?

\textbf{Interviewer}: Yes.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Non-functional requirements}

Next, let's go over the most important non-functional requirements.

\begin{itemize}
\item
\textbf{Reliability.} We should not lose email data.

\item
\textbf{Availability.} Email  and user data should be automatically replicated across multiple nodes to ensure availability. Besides, the system should continue to function despite partial system failures.

\item
\textbf{Scalability.} As the number of users grows, the system should be able to handle the increasing number of users and emails. The performance of this system should no degrade with more users or emails.

\item
\textbf{Flexibility and extensibility.}

\begin{itemize}
\item
A flexible/extensible system allows us to add new features or improve performance easily by adding new components
\item
Traditional email protocols such as POPI and IMAP have very limited functionality (more on this in high-level design)
\item
Therefore, we amy need custom protocols to satisy the flexibility and extensibility requirements
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Back-of-the-envelope-estimation}

Let's do a back-of-the-evenolpe calculation to determine the scale and to disccover some challenges our solution will need to address. By design, emails are storage heavy applications.

\begin{itemize}
\item
1 billion users
\item
Assume the average number of emails a person sends per day is 10. QPS for sending emails = \[\frac{10^{9}}{10^{5}} = 100,000 \]
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Assume the average number of emails a person receives in a day is 40 [3] and the average size of email metadata is 50KB
\item
Metadata refers to everything related to an email, exlcuding attachment files
\end{itemize}

\item
Assume metadata is stored in a database. Storage requirement for maintaining metadata in 1 year: \[ 1 \text{ billion users} \times 40 \text{ emails/day} \times 365 \text{ days} \times 50 \text{ KB} = 730 \text{ PB}\]
\item
Assume 20\% of emails contain an attachment and the average attachment size is 500 KB.
\item
Storage for attachments in 1 year is: \[ 1\text{ billion users} \times 40 \text{ emails/day} \times 365 \text{ days} \times 20\% \times 500 \text{ KB} = 1,460 \text{ PB}\]
\end{itemize}

From this back-of-the-envelope calculation, it's clear we would deal with a lot of data. SO it's likely that we need a distributed database solution.

\subsection{Step 2: Propose High-level Design and Get Buy-in}

In this section, we first discuss some bascis about email servers and how email servers evolve over time. Then we look at the high-level design of distributed email servers.

The content is structured as follows:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Email knowledge 1010
\item
Traditional mail servers
\item
Distributed mail servers
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Emails protocols}

\begin{itemize}
\item
\textbf{SMTP}: SImple Mail Transfer Protocol (SMTP) is the standard protocol for \textbf{sending} emails from one mail server to another.

To most popular protocols for \textbf{retrieving} emails are known as Post Office Portocol (POP) and the Internet Mail Access Portocol (IMAP)

\item
\textbf{POP}

\begin{itemize}
\item
This is a standard mail protocol to receive and download emails from a remote mail server to a local email client
\item
Once emails are downloaded to your computer or phone, they are deleted from the email server, which means you can only access emails on one computer or phone
\item
The details of POP are covered in RFC 1939 [4]
\item
POP requires mail clients to download the entire email
\item
This can take a long time if an email contains a large attachment.
\end{itemize}
\item
\textbf{IMAP}

\begin{itemize}
\item
This is also a standard mail protocol for receiving emails for a local email client
\item
When you read an email, you are connected to an external mail server, and data is transferred to your local device, IMAP only downloads a message when you click it, and emails are not deleted from mail servers, meaning thtat you can access emails from multiple devices
\item
IMAP is the most widely used protocol for individual email accounts
\item
It works well when the connection is slow because only the email header information is downloaded until opened
\end{itemize}

\item
\textbf{HTTPS}: is not technically a mail protocol, but it can be used to access your mailbox, particularly for web-based email FOr example, it's common for Microsoft Outlook to talk to mobile devices over HTTPS, on a custom-made portocol called ActiveSync [5].
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Domain name service (DNS)}

A DNS server is used to look up the mail exchanger record (MX record) for the recipient's domain. If you run DNS lookup for gmsil.com from the command line, you may get MX records as shon in Figure 8.2.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_8_2}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The priority numbers indicate preferences, where the mail server with a lower priority number is more preferred.

\begin{itemize}
\item
In Figure 8.2, \lstinline|gmail-smtp-in.l.google.com| is used first (priority 5)
\item
A sending mail server will attempt to connect and send messages to this mail server first
\item
If the connection fails, the sending mail server will attempt to connect to the mail server with the next lowest priorty, which is \lstinline|alt1.gmail-smtp-in.l.google.com| (priorty 10).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Attachment}

An email attachment is sent along with an email message, commonly with Base64 encoding [6].

\begin{itemize}
\item
There is usually a size limit for an email attachment
\item
FOr example. Outloook and Gmail limit the size of attachments to 20MB and 25MB respectively as of JUne 2021
\item
This number is highly configurable and varies from idividual to coporate accounts
\item
Multi-purpose Internet Mail Extension (MIKE) [7] is a specification that allows the attachment to be sent over the internet
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Traditional mail servers}

Before we deive into distributed mail servers, let's dig a little bit through the history and see how traditional mail servers work, as doing so provides good lessons about how to scale an email server system.

You can consider a traditional mail server as a systems that works when there are limited email users, usually on a single server.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_8_3}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The process consists of 4 steps:

\begin{itemize}
\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
Alice logs in to her Outlook client, composes an email, and preses the ``send'' button.
\item
The email is sent to the Outlook mail server.
\item
The communication protocol betwee the Outlook client and the mail server is SMTP
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Outlook mail server queries the DNS (not shown in the diagram) to fin dthe address of the recipient's SMTP server
\item
IN this case, it is Gmail's SMTP server
\item
Next, it transfers the email to the Gmail mail server
\item
The communication portocol between the mail servers is SMTP
\end{itemize}

\item
The Gmail server stores the email and makes it available to Bob, the recipient
\item
Gmail client fetches new emails through the IMAP/POP server when Bob logs in to Gmail.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Storage}

In a traditional mail server, emails were stored in local file directories and each email was stored in a separate file with a unique name.

Easch user maintained a user directory to store configuration data and mailboxes

Maildir was a popular way to store email messages on the mail server (Figure 8.4).

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_8_4}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

File directories worked well when the user base was small, but it was challenging to retrieve and backup billions of emails.

\begin{itemize}
\item
As the email volume grew and the file structure became more complex, disk I/O because a bottleneck
\item
The local directoreis also don't satisfy our high availability and reliability requirements
\item
The disk can be damanged and servers can go down
\item
We need a more reliable distributed storage layer
\item
Email functionality has come a long way since it was invented in the 1960s, from tet-based format to rich features such as multimedia, threading [8], searhc labels, and more
\item
But email portocols (POP, IMAP, and SMTP) were invented a long time ago and they were not designed to support these new features, nor were they scalable to support billions of users
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Distributed mail esrvers}

Distributed mail servers are designed to support modern use cases and solve the problems of scale and resiliency. This sect on covers email APIs, distributed email servers, architecture, email sending, and email receiving flows.

\subsection{Email APIs}

Email APIs each mean very different things for different mail clients, or at different stages of an email's life cycle. FOr example:

\begin{itemize}
\item
SMTP/POP/IMAP APIs for native mobile clients
\item
SMTP communications between sender and receiver mail servers
\item
RESTful API over HTTP for full-featured and itneractive we-based email applications
\end{itemize}

DUe to the length limitations of this book, we cover only some of the most important API s for web mail. A common way for webmail to communicate is through the HTTP protocol.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
\textbf{Endpoint: POST /v1/messages}

Sends a message to the recipients in the TO, Cc, and Bcc headers
\item
\textbf{Endpoint: GET /v1/folders}

Returns all folders of an email account.

\textbf{Response}:

A message's object.

\begin{lstlisting}
{
  user_id: string                   // Reference to the account owner
  from: name: string, email: string // <name, email> pair of the sender
  to: [name: string, email: string] // <A list of <name, email> paris
  subject: string                   // Subject of an email
  body: string                      // Message body
  is_read: boolean                  // Indicate if amessage is read or not.
}
\end{lstlisting}


\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Distributed mail server architecture}

While it is easy to set up an email server tha naldes a small number of users, it is difficult to scale byound one server.

\begin{itemize}
\item
This is mainly because traditional email servers were designed to work with a single server only
\item
Synchronizaing data across servers can be difficult, and keeping emails from being misclassified as span by recipients' mail servers is very challenging
\item
In this section, we explore how to leverage cloud technologies to make it easier
\item
The high-level design is shown in Figure 8.5
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_8_5}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Let us take a close look at each component.

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\textbf{Webmail.}: Users use web browsers to receive and send emails

\item
\textbf{Web servers.}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Web servers are public-facing request/response services, used to manage features such as login, singup, user profile, etc.
\item
In our design, all email API reuests, such as sending ean email, loading mail folders, loading all mails in a folder, etc.., go through web servers
\end{itemize}

\item

\textbf{Real-time servers.}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Real-time servers are responsible for pushing new email updates to clients in real-time.
\item
Real-time servers are stateful servers because they need to maintina persisten connections
\item
TO support real-time communication, we have a few options, such as long polling and WebSocket
\item
WebSocket is a more elegant solution but one drawback of it is browser compatibility
\item
A possible solution is to establish a WebSocket connection whenever possible and to use long-ppolling as a fallback
\item
Here is an example of a real-world mail server (Apache James [10]) that implements the JSON Meta APplication Protocol (JMAP) subprotocol over WebSocket [11]
\end{itemize}

\item
\textbf{Metadata databse.}

This database stores mail metadata including mail subject, body, from user, to users, etc. We discuss the databse choice in the deep dive section
\item
\textbf{Attachment store.}

\begin{itemize}
\item
We choose object stores such as Amazon Simple Storage Service (S3) as the attachment store.
\item
S3 is a scalable storage infrastructure that's suitable for sotring large files such as images, videos, files,etc.
\item
Attachments can take up to 25 MB in size
\item
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

NoSQL column-family databases like Cassandra might not be a good fit for the following two reasons:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Even though Cassandra supports blobl data type and its maximum theorectical size for a blob is 2GB, the practical imit is less than 1MB [12].
\item
Another problem with putting attachments in Cassandra is that we can't use a row cache as attachments take too much memory space
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Distributed cache.}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Since the most recent emails are repeatedly loaded by a client, caching recent emails in memory significantly improves the load time
\item
We can use Redis here because it offers rich features such as lists and it is easy to scale.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Search store.}

\begin{itemize}
\item
The search store is a distributed document store
\item
It uses a data structure called inverted inex [13] that supports very fast full-text searches
\item
We will discuss this in more detail in the deep dive section
\end{itemize}

Now that we have discussed some of the most important components to build distributed mail servers, let's assemble together two main workflows.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Email sending flow
\item
Email receiving flow
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Email sending flow}

The email sending flow is shown in Figure 8.6.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_8_6}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{enumerate}
\item
A user writes an email on webmail and presses the send button.
\item
The load balancer makes sure it doesn't exceed the rate limit and routes traffic to web servers.
\item
Web servers are responsible for:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Basic email validation. Each incoming email is checked against pre-defined rules such as email size limit
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Checking if the domian of the recipient's email address is the same as the sender
\item
If it is the same, the web server ensures the email data is spam and virus free
\item
If so, email data is inserted into the sender's ``Sent Folder'' and recipient's ``Inbox Folder''
\item
The recipeint can fetch the email directly via the RESTful API
\item
There is no need to go to step 4
\end{itemize}

\item

Message queries

\begin{itemize}
\item[4.1]

\begin{itemize}
\item
If basic email validation succeeds, the email data is passed to the outgoing queue
\item
If the attachment is too large to fit in the queue, we could store the attachment in the object store and save the object reference in the queued message
\end{itemize}

\item[4.2]
If basic email validation fails, the email is put in the error queue
\end{itemize}

\item
SMPTP outgoing workers pull messages from the outgoing queue and make sure emails are spam and virus free.
\item
The outgoing email is stored in the ``Sent Folder'' of the storage layer
\item
SMTP outgoing workers send the email to the recipient mail server.
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

Each message in the outgoing queue contains all the metadata required to create an email

\begin{itemize}
\item
A distributed message queue is a critical component that allows aysnchronous mail porcessing
\item
By decoupling SMTP outgoing workers from the webservers, we can scale SMTP outoing workers independently
\end{itemize}

We monitor the size of the outgoing queue very closely. If there are many emails stuc in the queue, we need to analyze the cuase of the issue. Here are some possibilities:

\begin{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The recipient's mail server is unavailable.
\item
In this case, we need to retry sending the email at a later time.
\item
Exponential backoff [14] might be a good retry strategy
\end{itemize}

\item
Not enough consumers to send emails. In this case, we may need more consumers to reduce the processing time.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Email receiving flow}

The following diagram demonstrates the email receiving flow.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_8_7}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Incoming emails arrive at the SMTP load balancer
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The load balancer distributes traffic among SMTP servers
\item
Email acceptance policy can be configured and applied at the SMTP-connection level
\item
For example, invalid emails are bounced to avoid unnecessary email processing
\end{itemize}

\item
If the attachment of an email is too large to put into the queue, we can put it into the attachment store (S3)
\item
Emails are put in the incoming email queue. The queue decouples mail processing workers from SMTP servers so they can be scaled independently MOreover, the queue serves as a buffer in case the email volume surges
\item
Mail processing workers are responsible for a lot of tasks, including filtering out spam mails, stopping viruses, etc. THe following steps assume an email passed the validation.
\item
The email is stored in the mail storage, cache, and object data store
\item
If the receiver is currently online, the email is pusehd to real-time servers
\item
Real-time servers are WebSocket servers that allow clients to receive new emails in real-time
\item
For offline users, emails are stored in the storage layer
\item
Web servers pull new emails from storage layer and return them to the client
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 3: Design Deep Dive}

Now that we have talked about all the parts of the email server, let's go deeper into some key components and examine how to cale the system

\begin{itemize}
\item
Metadata database
\item
Search
\item
Deliverability
\item
Scalability
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Metadata database}

In this section, we discuss the characteristics of email metadata, choosing the right database, data model, and conversation threads (bonus point).

\subsubsection{Characteristics of email metadata}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Email headers usually small and frequently acccessed
\item
Email body sizes can range from small to big but are infrequently accessed. You normally only read an email. You normally only read an email once.
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Most of the mail operations, such as fetching mails, marking an email as read, and searching are isolated to an individual user
\item
In other words, mails owned by a user are only accessible by that user and all the mail operations
\end{itemize}

\item
Data recency impacts data usage. User usually only read the most recent emails.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Choosing the right database}

At Gmail or Outlook scale, the database system is usually custom-made to redunce input/output operations per second (IOPS) [16], as this can easily become a major constraint in the system.

Choosing the right database is not easy.

It is helpful to consider all the options we have on the table before deciding the most suitable one.

\begin{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Relational database.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The main motivation behind this is to search though emails efficiently
\item
We can build indexes for email header and body
\item
With indexes, simple search queries are fast
\item
However, relational datbases are typically opitmized for small chunks fo dataentires and are not dieal for large ones
\item
A typical email is usually larger than a few KB and can easily be over 100KB when HTML is involved
\item
You might argue that the BLOB data type is designed to support large data entires
\item
However, search quieries over unstructured LBOB data type are not efficient
\item
So relational datbases such as MySQL, or PostgreSQL are not good fits
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Distributed object storage
\item
Another potential solution is to store raw emails in cloud storage such as Amzon S3,  which can be a good option for backup storage, but it's hard to efficiently support features such as marking emails as read, searching emails based on keywords, threading emails, etc.
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
NoSQL datbases
\item
Google Bigtable is used by Gmail, so it's definitely a viable solution
\item
However, Bigtable is not open sourced and how email search is implemented remains a mystery
\item
Cassandra might be a good option as well, but we haven't seen any large meail providers use it yet
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

Based on the above analysis, very few existing solutions seem to fit our needs perfectly. Large email service providers tend to build their own highly customized databsaes.

However, in an interview setting, we won't have time to design a new distributed database,  though it's important to explain the folllowing characteristics that the databse should have.

\begin{itemize}
\item
A single column can be a single-digit of MB.
\item
Strong data consistency
\item
Designed to reduce disk I/O
\item
It should be highly available and fault-tolerant
\item
It should be easy to create incremental backups
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Data Model}

One way to store the data is to use \lstinline|user_id| as a parittion key so data for one user is stored on a single shard.

\begin{itemize}
\item
One limitation of this data model is that messages are not shared among multiple users
\item
Since this is not a requirement for us in this interview, it's not something we need to worry about
\item
Now let us define the tables
\item
The primary key contains two components, the partition key, and the clustering key
\item
Partition key: reponsible for distributing data across nodes. As a general rule, we want to srpread the data evenly
\item
Clustering key: responsible for sorting data within a partition
\end{itemize}

At a high level, an email service needs to support the following queries at the data layer:

\begin{itemize}
\item
The first query is to get all folders for a user
\item
The second query is to display all emails for a specific folder
\item
THe third query is to create/delete/get a specific email
\item
The fourth query is to fetch all read or unread emails
\item
Bonus point: get conversation threads
\end{itemize}

Let's take a look at them one by one.

\subsection{Query 1: get all folders for a user.}

As shown in Table 8.1, \lstinline|user_id|, is the partition key, so folders owned by the smae user are located in one partition

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
K & Partition Key \\
C $\uparrow$ & Clustering Key (ascending) Key \\
C $\downarrow$ $\mathbf{\genfrac{}{}{0pt}{}{\uparrow}{\downarrow}}$ & Clustering Key (descending) Key \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

\subsection{Query 2: display all emails for a specific folder.}

When a user loads their inbox, emails are usually sorted by timestamp, showing the most recent ones at the top.

In order to store all emails for the same folder in one partition, componsite partition key \lstinline|<user_id, folder_id>| is used.

Another column to note is \lstinline|email_id|

Its data type is \lstinline|TIMEUUID| [17], and it is the clustering key used to sort emails in chronological order.

\lstinline|emails_by_folder|

\begin{tabular}{|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|user_id| & \lstinline|UUID| & \lstinline|K| \\
\lstinline|folder_id| & \lstinline|UUID| & \lstinline|K| \\
\lstinline|email_id| & \lstinline|TIMEUUID| & \lstinline|C| $\downarrow$ \\
from & TEXT &  \\
subject & TEXT &  \\
preview & TEXT &  \\
\lstinline|is_read| & BOOLEAN &  \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

\subsection{Query 4: create/delete/get an email}

\begin{tabular}{|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|user_id| & \lstinline|UUID| & \lstinline|K| \\
\lstinline|email_id| & \lstinline|TIMEUUID| & \lstinline|C| $\downarrow$ \\
from & TEXT & \\
to & LIST$<$TEXT$>$ &  \\
subject & TEXT &  \\
body & TEXT &  \\
attachments & LIST$<$filename|size$>$ &  \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 8.3: Emails by user

\subsection{Query 4: fetch all read or unread emails}

If our domain model was for a relational databse, the query to fetch all read emails would look like this:

\begin{lstlisting}
SELECT * FROM emails_by_folder
WHERE user_id = <user_id and folder_id = <folder_id> and
  is_read = true
ORDER BY email_id;
\end{lstlisting}

THe query to fetch all unread emails woud look very similar. We just need to change \lstinline|is_read = true| to \lstinline|is_read = false| in the above query.

Our data model, however, is design for NoSQL.

\begin{itemize}
\item
A NoSQL databse normally only supports queries on parittion and cluster keys
\item
Since \lstinline|is_read| inthe \lstinline|emails_by_folder| table is neither of those, most NOSQL datbases will reject this query.
\item
One way to get around this limitation is to fetch the entire folder for a user and perform the filtering in the application
\item
This could work for a small email service, but at our design scale, this does not work well.
\end{itemize}

This problem is commonly solved with denormalization in NoSQL. To support the read/unread queries,
we denormalize the \lstinline|emails_by_folder| data into two tables as shown in Table 8.4.

\begin{itemize}
\item
\lstinline|read_emails|: it stores all emails that are in read status
\item
\lstinline|unread_emails|: it stores all emails that are in unread status.
\end{itemize}

To mark an \lstinline|UNREAD| email as \lstinline|READ|, the email is deleted from \lstinline|unread_emails| and then inserted to \lstinline|read_emails|

To fetch all unread emails for a specific folder, we can run a query like this:

Table 8.4: Read and unread emails

\begin{lstlisting}
SELECT * FROM UNREAD_EMAILS
WHERE user_id = <user_id> and folder_id = <folder_id>
ORDER BY email_id;
\end{lstlisting}

\lstinline|read_emails|

\begin{tabular}{|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|user_id| & UUID & K \\
\lstinline|folder_id| & UUID & K \\
\lstinline|email_id| & TIMEUUID & C $\downarrow$ \\
from & TEXT & \\
subject & TEXT & \\
preview & TEXT & \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

\lstinline|unread_emails|

\begin{tabular}{|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|user_id| & UUID & K \\
\lstinline|folder_id| & UUID & K \\
\lstinline|email_id| & TIMEUUID & C $\downarrow$ \\
from & TEXT & \\
subject & TEXT & \\
preview & TEXT & \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Denormalization as shown above is a common practice. It amesk the applicatoin code more complicated and harder to maintain, but it imporves the read performance of these queries at scale.

\subsubsection{Bonus point: converstaion threads}

Threads are a feature support by many email clients.

\begin{itemize}
\item
It groups email replies with their original emssage [8].
\item
This allows users to retrieve all emails associated with one conversation
\item
Traditionally, a thread is implemented using algorithms such as JWZ algorithm [18]
\item
We will not go into detail about the algorithm, but just epxlina the core idea behind it
\item
\end{itemize}

An email header generally contains the following three fields:

\begin{lstlisting}
{
    "headers": {
        "Message-Id": "<7BA04B2A-430C-4D12-8B57-862103C34581@gmail.com>",
        "In-Reply-To": "<CAEWTXuPfN=LzECjDJtgY9Vu03kgFvJnJUSHTt6TW@gmail.com>",
        "References": ["<7BA04B2A-430C-4D12-8B57-862103C34581@gmail.com>"]
    }
}
\end{lstlisting}

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|Message-Id| & The value of a message ID. It is generated by a client while sending a message. \\
\hline
\lstinline|In-Reply-To| & The parent Message-Id to which the message replies \\
\hline
\lstinline|References| & A list of message IDs related to a thread\\
\hline
\end{tabular}

With these fields, an email client can reconstruct mail conversations from messages, if all messages in the reply chain are preloaded.

\subsubsection{Consistency trade-off}

Distributed databases that reply on replication for high availability must make a fundamnetal trade-off between consistency and availability.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Correctness is very important for email system, so by design, we want to have single rpimary for any given mailbox
\item
In the event of a failover, the mailbox isn't accessible by clients,  so their sysnc/update operation is paused until failover ends
\item
It trades availability in favor of consistency
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Email deliverability}

It is easy to setup a mail server and start sending emails

\begin{itemize}
\item
The hard part is to get emails actually delivered to a user's inbox
\item
I an email ends up in the spam folder, it means there is a very high chance a receipient won't read it
\item
Email span is a huge issue
\item
According to research done by Statista [19], more than 50\% of all emails sent are spam
\item
If we set up a new mail server, most likely our emails will end up in the spam folder because a new email server has no reputation
\item
There are couple of factors to consider to improve email deliverability
\end{itemize}

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Dedicated IPs
\item
It is recommended to have dedicated IP addresses for sending emails
\item
Email providers are less likely to accept emails from new IP addresses that have no history
\end{itemize}

\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
Classify emails
\item
Send different categories of emails from different IP addresses
\item
FOr example, you may want to avoid sending marketing and other important emails from the same servers because it might make ISPs mark all emails as promotional
\end{itemize}

\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
Email sender reputation
\item
Warm up new email server IP addresses slowly to build a good reputation, so big providers such as Office365, Gmail, Yahoo Mail, etc. are less likely to put our emails in the spam folder
\item
According to Amazon Simple Email Service [20], it takes about 2 to 6 weeks to warmp up a new IP address
\end{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Ban spammers quickly
\item
Spammers should be banned quickly before they have a significant impact on the server's reputation.
\end{itemize}

\item

\textbf{Feedback processing}: It's very important to setup feedback loops with ISPs so we can keep the complaint rate low and ban spam accounts quickly. If an email fails to deliver or a user complains, one of the following outcomes occurs:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Hard bounce. This means an email is rejected by ISP because the recipient's email address is inavlid
\item
Soft bounce. A soft bounce indicates an email failed to deliver due to temporary conditions, such as ISPs being too busy.
\item
Complaint. This means a receipient clicks the ``report spam'' button
\end{itemize}

Figure 8.8 shows the process of collecting and processing bounces/complaints.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_8_8}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Email authentication
\item
According to the 2018 data breach investigation report provided by Verizon, phishing and pretexting represent 93\% of breaches [21]
\item
Some of the common techniques to combat phishing are: Sender Policy Framework (SPF) [22], DomainKeys Identified Mail (DKIM) [23], and Domain-based Message Authenication, Reporting and Conformance (DMARC) [24]
\item
Figure 8.9 shows an example header of a Gmail message
\item
As you can see, the sender \lstinline|@info6.citi.com| is authenticaed by SPF, DKIM, and DMARC
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}
\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_8_9}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

You don't need to remember all those terms. The important thing to keep in mind is that getting emails to work as inteded is hard. It requires not only domain knowledge, but good relationships with ISPs.

\subsection{Search}

Basic mail search refers to searching for emails that contain any of the entered keywords in the subject or body.

\begin{itemize}
\item
More advanced features include filtering by ``From'', ``Subject'', ``Unread'', or other attributes
\item
On one hand, whever an email is sent, received or deleted, we need to perform reindexing
\item
On the other hand, a search query is only run when a user presses th search button
\item
This means the search feature in email systems has a lot more writes than reads
\item
By comparison with Google search, email search ahs quite different characteristics, as shown in Table 8.6
\end{itemize}

\begin{tabular}{|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|}
\hline
& Scope & Sorting & Accuracy \\
\hline
Google search & The whole internet & Sort by relevance & Indexing generally takes time, so some items may not show in the search immediately \\
\hline
Email search & User's own email box & Sort by attirbutes such as time, has attachment, data within, is unread, etc. & Indexing should be near real-time, and the result has to be accurate \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 8.6: Google search vs email search

To support search functionality, we compare two approaches: Elasticsearch nad navtive search embedded in the datastore.

\subsection{Option 1: Elasticsearch}

The high-level design for email search using Elastic search is shown in FIgure 8.10. Becuase queries are mostly performed on the user's own email server, we can group underlying documents to the same node using \lstinline|user_id| as the primary key.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_8_10}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

When a user clicks the search button, the user waits until the search response is received.

\begin{itemize}
\item
A search request is synchronous
\item
Wehn events sucha s ``send email'', ``receive email'' or ``delete email'' are tiggered, nothing related to search needs to be returned to the client
\item
Reindexing is needed and it can be done with offline jobs
\item
Kafka is used in the design to decouple services that rigger reindexing, from services that actually perform reindexing
\item
Elastic search is the most ppoular search-engine databse as of JUne 2021 [25] and it supports full-text search of meails very well
\item
One challenge of adding Elasticsearch is to keep our primary email store in sync with it
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Option 2: Custom search solution}

Large-scale email providers usually develop their own custom search engines to meet their specific requirements.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Designing an email search engine is a very complicated task and is out of the scope of this chapter
\item
Here we only briefly touch on the disk I/O bottleneck, a primary challenge we will face for a custom search engine
\item
As shown in the back-of-the-envelope calculation, the size of the metadata and attacfhments added dialy is at the petabyte (PB) level
\item
Meanwhile, an email account can easily have over half a million emails
\item
The main bottleneck of the index server is usually disk I/O.
\end{itemize}

Since the process of building the idnex is write-heavy, a good stragey might be to use Log-Structured Merge-Tree (LSM) [26] to structure th idnex data on disk (Figure 8.11).

\begin{itemize}
\item
The write path is optimized by only performign sequential writes
\item
LSM trees are the core data structure behind databses such as Bigtable, Cassandra, and RocksDB
\item
When a new email arrives, it is first added to level 0o in-memory cache , and when data size inmoemory reached the predefined threshold, data is merge ot the next level
\item
Another reason to use LSM is to seperate data that change frequently from those that don't
\item
For example, email dataq usually doens't change, but folder infomration tends to change more often due to different filter rules
\item
In this case we can separate them into two different sections, so that if a request is related to a folder change, we change only the rolder and leave the email data alone
\item
If you are interested in reading more about email search, it is highly recommended you take a look at how search works in Microsoft Exchange servers [27]
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_8_11}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Each approach has pros and cons:

\begin{tabular}{|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|Feature| & \lstinline|Elasticsearch| & \lstinline|Custom search engine| \\
\hline
Scalability & Scalable to some extent & Easier to scale as we can optimize the system for the email use case \\
\hline
System complexity & Need to maintian two different system: datastore and Elasticsearch & One system \\
\hline
Data consistency & Two copies of data. One in the metadata dastore, and the other in Elasticsearch. Data consistency is hard to maintain & A single copy of data in the metadata datastore \\
\hline
Data loss possible & NO. Can rebuild the ELasticsearch index from the primary storage, in case of failure & A single copy of data in the metadata datastore \\
\hline
Development effort & Easy to integrate. To support large scale email search, a dedicate Elasticsearch team might be needed & Significant engineering effort is needed to develop a custom email search engine \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 8.7: Elastic search vs custom search engine

A general rule of thumb is that for a smaller scale email system, Elasticsearch is a good option as it's easy to integrate and doesn't require significant engineering effort.

\begin{itemize}
\item
For a larger scale, Elasticsearch might work, but we may need a dedicated team to develop and maintain the email search infrastructure.
\item
To support an email system at Gmail or OUtlook scale, it might be a good idea to have a native search embedded in the database as opposed to the separate indexing approach
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Scalability and availability}

Since data access patterns of individual users are independent of one another, we expect most components in the system are horizontally scalable.

\begin{itemize}
\item
For better availability, data is replicated across multiple data centers.
\item
Users communicate with a mail server that is physically closer to them in the network topology
\item
During a network partition, users can access messages from other data centers (Figure 8.12)
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_8_12}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Step 4: Wrap Up}

In this chapter, we have presented a design for building large-scale email servers.

\begin{itemize}
\item
We start by gathering requirements and doing some back-of-the-envelope calculations to get a good idea of the scale
\item
In the high-level design, we discussed how traditional email servers were designed and why they cannot satisfy modern use cases
\item
We also discussed email APIs and high-level designs for sending and receiving flows
\item
Finally, we devied deep into metadata database design, email deliverability, search, and scalability
\end{itemize}

If there is extra time at the end of the interview, here are a few additional talking points:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Fault tolerance. Many parts of the system can fail, and you can talk about how to handle node failures, network issues, event delays, etc.
\item
Compliance.
\begin{itemize}
\item
Eamil service works all around the world and there are legal regulations to comply with
\item
For instance, we need to handle and store personally identifiable information (PII) from Europe in a way that complies with General Data Portection Regulation (GDPR) [28]
\item
Legal intercept is another typical feature in this area [29]
\end{itemize}

\item
Security. Email security is important because emails contain sensitive information.

Gmail provides safety features such as phishing protections, safe browsing, proactive alerts, account safety, confidential mode, and email encryption [30]

\item
Optimizations

\begin{itemize}
\item
Sometimes, the same email is sent to multiple reciepients, and the same email attachment is stored several times in the object store (S3)
\item
One optimization we could do is to check the existence of the attachment in storage, before performing the expensive save operation.
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{chapter_8_summary}
    \caption{}

\chapter*{Chapter 9: S3-like Object Storage}

In this chapter, we design an object storage service similar to Amazon Simple Storage Service (S3). S3 is a service offered by Amazon Web Services (AWS) that provides object storage through a RESTful API-based interface.

Here are some facts about AWS S3:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Launched in June 2006
\item
S3 added versioning, bucket policy, and multipart upload upload in 2010
\item
S3 added server-side encryption, multi-object delete, and object expiration in 2011
\item
Amazon reported 2 trillion objects stored in S3 by 2013
\item
Life cycle policy, event notification, and cross-region replication support were introduced in 2014 and 2015
\item
Amazon reported over 100 trillion objects stored in S3 by 2021
\end{itemize}

Before we dig into object storage, let's first review storage systems in general and efine some terminologies.

\subsection{Storage System 101}

At a high-level, storage systems fall into three broad categorties:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Block storage
\item
File storage
\item
Object storage
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Block storage}

Block storage came first, in the 1960s.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Common storage devices like hard disk drives (HDD) and soli-state drives (SSD) that are physically  attached to servers are all considered as block storage
\item
Block storage presents the raw blocks to the server as a volume
\item
This is the most flexible and versatile form of storage
\item
The server can format the raw blocks and use them as a file system, or it can hand control of those blocks to an application
\item
Block storage presents the raw blocks to the server as a volume
\item
This is the most flexible and versatile form of storage
\item
The server can format the raw blocks and use them as a file system, or it can hand control of those blocks to an application
\item
Some applications like a database or a virtual machine engine manage these blocks directly in order to squeeze every drop of performance out of them
\item
Block storage is not limited to physically attached storage
\item
Block storage could be connected to a server over a high-speed network or over industry-standard connectivity protocols like Fibre Channel (FC) [1] and iSCSI [2]
\item
Conceptually, the network-attached bloock storage still presents raw blocks
\item
TO the servers, it works the same as physically attached block storage
\end{itemize}

\subsection{File storage}

File storage is built on top of block storage.

\begin{itemize}
\item
It provides a higher-level abstraction to make it seasier to handle files and directories
\item
Data is torage as files under a hierarchical directory structure
\item
File storage is the most common general-purpose storage solution
\item
File storage could be made accessible by a large number of servers using common file-level network protocols like SMB/CIFS [3] and NFS [4]
\item
The servers accessing file storage do not need to deal with the complexity of managing the blocks, formatting volume, etc.
\item
The simplicity of file storage makes it a great solution for sharing a large number of files and folders within an organization
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Object storage}

Object storage is new.

\begin{itemize}
\item
It makes a very deliverate trade off to sacrifice performance for high durability, vast scale, and low cost
\item
It targets relatively ``cold'' data, and is mainly used for achival and backup
\item
Object storage stores all data as objects in a flat structure
\item
There is non hierarchical directory structure
\item
Data access is nromally provided via a RESTful API
\item
It is relatively slow compared to other storage types
\item
Most public cloud service providers have an object storage offereing, such as AWS S3, Google object storage, and Azure blob storage
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Comparison}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_1}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Table 9.1 compares block storage, file storage, and object storage.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{|l|X|X|X|}
\hline
 & \textbf{Block storage} & \textbf{File storage} & \textbf{Object storage} \\
\hline
Mutable Content & Y & Y & N (object versioning is supported, in-place update is not) \\
\hline
Cost & High & Medium to high & Low \\
\hline
Performance & Medium to high, very high & Medium to high & Low to medium \\
\hline
Consistency & Strong consistency & Strong consistency & Strong consistency [5] \\
\hline
Data access & SAS [6]/iSCSI/FC & Standard file access, CIFS/SMB, and NFS & RESTful API \\
\hline
Scalability & Medium scalability & High scalability & Vast scalability \\
\hline
Good for & Virtual machines (VM), high-performance applications like database & General-purpose file system access & Binary data, unstructured data \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\caption{Storage options}
\label{tab:storage-options}
\end{table}

\newpage
\subsection{Terminology}

To design S3-like object storage, we need to understand some core object storage concepts first. This section provides an overview of the terms that apply to object storage.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
\textbf{Bucket}: A logical container for objects. The bucket name is globally unique. To upload data to S3, we must first create a bucket
\item
\textbf{Object}.

\begin{itemize}
\item
An object is an individuall piece of data we store in a bucket
\item
It contains object data (also called payload) and metadata
\item
Object data can be any sequence of bytes we want to store
\item
The metadata is a set of name-value pairs that describe the object
\end{itemize}

\item
\textbf{Versioning}

\begin{itemize}
\item
A feature that keeps multiple variants of an object in the same bucket
\item
It is enabled at bucket-level
\item
This feature enables users to recover events that are deleted or overwritten by accident
\end{itemize}

\item
\textbf{Uniform Resource Identifier (URI)}.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The object storage provides RESTful APIs to access its reousrces, namely, buckets and objects
\item
Each resource is uniquely identified by its URL
\end{itemize}

\item
\subsection{SErvice-level agreement (SLA)}.

A service-level agreement is a contract between a esrvice provider and a client.

For example, the Amazon S3 standard-Infrequent Access storage class provides the following SLA [7]:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Designed for durability of 99.999999999\% of objects across multiple Availability Zones
\item
Data is resilient in the event of one entire Availability Zone destruction
\item
Designed for 99.9\% availability
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Step 1: Understand the Problem and Establish Design Scope}

The following questions help to clarify the requirements and narrow down the scope.

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\textbf{Candidate}: Which features should be included in the design?
\textbf{Interviewer} We would like you to design an S3-like object storage system with the following functionalities:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Bucket creation
\item
Object uploading and downloading
\item
Object versioning
\item
Listing objects in a bucket. It's similar to the \lstinline|aws S3 ls| command [8]
\end{itemize}

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: What is the typical data size?
\textbf{Interviewer}: We need to store both massive objects (a few GBs or more) and a large number of small objects (tens of KBs,) efficiently

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: How much data do we need to store in one year?
\textbf{Interviewer}: 100 petabytes (PB)

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Cn we assume data durability is 6 nines (99.9999\%) and service availability is 4 nines (99.99\%)
\textbf{Interviewer}: Yes, that sounds reasonable.

\subsection{Non-functional requirements}

\begin{itemize}
\item
100PB of data
\item
Data durability is 6 nines
\item
Service availability is 4 nines
\item
Storage efficiency. Reduce storage costs while maintaining a high degree or reliability and performance
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Back-of-the-envelope estimation}

Object storage is likely to have bottlnecks in either disk capacity or disk IO per second (IOPS). Let's take a look.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Disk capacity. Let's assume objects follow the distribution listed below.

\begin{itemize}
\item
20\% of all objects are small objects (less than 1MB)
\item
60\% of objects are medium-sized objects (1 MB ~ 64MB)
\item
20\% are large objects (larger than 64MB)
\end{itemize}

\item
IOPS.

Let's assume one hard disk (SATA interface, 7200 rpm) is capable of doing 100 ~ 150 random seeks per second (100 ~ 150 IOPS).
\end{itemize}

With those assumptions, we can estimate the total number of objects the system can persist.

To simplify the calculation, let's use the median size for each object type (0.5MB for small objects, 32MB for medium objects, and 200MB for large objects)

A 40\% storage usage ratio gives us:

\begin{itemize}
\item
100 PB = $100 \times 1000 \times 1000 \times 1000 \text{MB } = 10^{11} \text{ MB}$
\item
$\frac{10^{11} \times 0.4}{(0.2 \times 0.5 \text{ MB} + 0.6 \times 32 \text{ MB} + 0.2 \times 200 \text{ MB})}$ = 0.68 billion objects.
\item
If we assume the metadata of an object is about 1KB in size, we need 0.68TB space to store all metadata information
\end{itemize}

Even though we may not use those numbers, it's good to have a general idea about the scale and constraint of the system.

\subsection{Step 2: Propose High-level Design and Get Buy-in}

Before diving into the design, let's explore a few interesting properties of object storage, as they may influence it.

\subsubsection{Object immutability}

\begin{itemize}
\item
One of the main differences between object storage and the other two types of storage system is that the objects stored in side of object storage are immutable.
\item
We may delete them or replace them entirely  with a new version, but we cannot make incremental changes.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Key-value store}

We could use object URI to retrieve object data (Listing 9.1). The object URI is the key and object data is the value.

\begin{lstlisting}
Request:
GET /bucket1/object1.txt HTTP/1.1

Response:
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Content-Length: 4567

[4567 bytes of object data]
\end{lstlisting}

Listing 9.1: Use object URI to retrieve object data

\subsubsection{WRite once, read many times}

The data access pattern for object data is written once and read many times.

According to the research done by LinkedIn, 95\% of requests are read operations [9].

\subsubsection{Support both small and large objects}. Object size may very and we need to support both.

The design philosophy of objec storage is very similar to that of the UNIX file system.

\begin{itemize}
\item
In UNIX,  when we save a file in the local file system, it does not save the filename and file data toegether.

INstead, the file name is stored in a data structure called ``inode'' [10], and the file data is store din different disk locations
\item
The inode contains a list of file block pointers that point to the disk locations of the file data
\item
When we access a local file, we first fetch the metadata in the inode
\item
We then read the file data by following the file block pointers to the actual disk locations
\item
The object storage works similarly
\item
The inode becomes the mdetdata store that stores all the object metadata
\item
The hard disk becomes the data store that stores the object data
\item
In the UNIX file system, the inode uses the file block pointer to record the location of data on the hard disk
\item
In object storage, the metadata store uses the ID of the object to find the corresponding object data in the data store, via a network request
\item
Figure 9.2 shows the UNIX file system and the object storage.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_2}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Separating metadata and object data simplifies the design

\begin{itemize}
\item
The data store contains immutable data while the metadata store contains mutable data
\item
This separation enables us to implement and optimize these two components independently
\item
Figure 9.3 shows what the bucket and object look like
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_3}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{High-level design}

Figure 9.4 shows the high-level design.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_4}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Let's go over the components one by one.

\textbf{Load balancer}. Distributes RESTful API requests across a number of API servers

\textbf{API service}.
\begin{itemize}
\item
Orchestrates remote procedure calls to the identity an access managment service, metadata service, and storage stores.
\item
This service is stateless so it can be horizontally scaled
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Identity and access management (IAM)}

\begin{itemize}
\item
The central place the handle uathentication, autorization and access control.
\item
Authentication verfies who you are, and authorization validates what operations you could perform based on who you are.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Data store.} Stores and retrieves the actual data. All data-related operations are based on object ID (UUID).

\textbf{Metadata store}. Stores the metadata of the objects.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Note that the metadata and data store share just logical components, and there are different ways to implement them.
\item
For example, in Ceph's Rados Gateway [11], there is no standalone metadata store.
\item
Everything, including the object bucket, is ppersisted as one or multiple Rados objects.
\end{itemize}

Now we have a basic understanding of the high-level design, let's explore some of the most important workflows in object storage.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Uploading an object
\item
Downloading an object
\item
Object versioning and listing objects in a bucket. They will be explained in the ``design deep dive'' section on page 263.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Uploading an object}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_5}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

An object hast to reside in a bucket. In this example, we first create a bucket named \lstinline|bucket-to-share| and then upload a file named \lstinline|script.txt| to the bucket.

Figure 9.5 explains how this flow works in 7 steps.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The client sends an \lstinline|HTTP PUT| request to create a bucket named \lstinline|bucket-to-share|, The request is forwarded to the API service.
\item
The API service calls the IAM to ensure the user is authorized and has \lstinline|WRITE| permission
\item
The API service calls the metadata store to create an entry with the bucket info in the metadata database. Once the entry is created, a success mesage is returned to the client.
\item
After the bucket is created, the client sends an \lstinline|HTTP PUT| request to create an object named \lstinline|script.txt|
\item
The API service verifies the user's identity and ensures the user has \lstinline|WRITE| permission on the bucket
\item
Once validation succeeds, the API service sends the object data in the \lstinline|HTTP PUT| pyalod to the data store. The data store persists the payload as an object and returns the UUID of the object.
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The API service calls the metadata sotre to create a new entry in the metadata database.
\item
 It contains important metadata such as the \lstinline|object_id| (UUID), \lstinline|bucket_id| (which bucket th eobject belongs to), \lstinline|object_name|, etc.
\item
A sample entry is shown in Table 9.2.
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\begin{tabular}{|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|object_name| & \lstinline|object_id| & \lstinline|bucket_id| \\
\hline
\lstinline|script.txt| & \lstinline|239D5866-0052-00F6-014E-C914E61ED42B| & \lstinline|82AA1B2E-F599-2590-B5E4-1F51AAE5F7E4| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 9.2: Sample entry

The API to upload an object could look like this:

\begin{lstlisting}[caption={Uploading an object}, label={lst:uploading-object}]
PUT /bucket-to-share/script.txt HTTP/1.1
Host: foo.s3example.org
Date: Sun, 12 Sept 2021 17:51:00 GMT
Authorization: authorization string
Content-Type: text/plain
Content-Length: 4567
x-amz-meta-author: Alex

[4567 bytes of object data]
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Downloading an object}

A bucket has no directory hierarchy.

However, we can create a logical hierarchy by concatenating the bucket name and the object name to simulate a folder structure.

\begin{itemize}
\item
For example, we name the object \lstinline|bucket-to-share/script.txt| instead of \lstinline|script.txt|
\item
To get an object, we specify the object name in the \lstinline|GET| request
\item
The API to download an object looks like this:

\begin{lstlisting}
GET /bucket-to-share/script.txt HTTP/1.1
Host: foo.s3example.org
Date: Sun, 12 Sept 2021 18:30:01 GMT
Authorization: authorization string
\end{lstlisting}
\end{itemize}

Listing 9.3: Downloading an object

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_6}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

As mentioned earlier, the data store does not store the name of the object and it only supports object operations via \lstinline|object_id| (UUID).

In order to download the object is shown below:

\begin{enumerate}
\item
The client sends an \lstinline|HTTP GET| request to the load balancer: \lstinline|GET /bucket-to-share/script.txt|
\item
The API services queries the IAM to verify that the user has \lstinline|READ| accesss to the bucket
\item
Once validated, the API service fetches the corresponding object's UUID from the metadata store
\item
Next, the API service fetches the object data from the data store by its UUID
\item
The API service returns the object data to the client in HTTP GET response.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Step 3: Design Deep Dive}

In this section, we dive deep into a few areas:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Data store
\item
Metadata data model
\item
Listing object s in a bucket
\item
Object versioning
\item
Optimizing uploads of large files
\item
Garbage collection
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Data store}

Let's take a closer look at the design of the data store.

\begin{itemize}
\item
As discussed previously, the API service handles external requests from users and calls different internal services to fulfill those requests.
\item
To persist or retrieve an object, the API service calls the data store
\item
Figure 9.7 shows the interactiosn between the API service and the datastore for uploading and downloading an object.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_7}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{High-level design for the data store}

The data store has three main components as shown in Figure 9.8.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_8}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Data routing service}

The data routing service provides RESTful or gRPC [12] APIs to access the data node cluster.

It is a stateless service that can scale by adding more servers.

This service has the following responsibilities:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Query the placement service to get the best data node to store data
\item
Read data from data nodes and return it to the API service
\item
Write data to data nodes
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Placement service}

The placement service determines which data nodes (primary and replicas) should be chosen to store an object.

\begin{itemize}
\item
It maintains a virtual cluster map, which provides the physical topology of the cluster
\item
The virtual cluster map contains location information for each data node which the placement service uses to make sure the replicas are physically separated
\item
This separation is key to high durability
\item
See the ``Durability'' section on page 270 for details
\item
An example of the virtual cluster map is shown in Figure 9.9.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_9}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The pacement service continuously monitors all data nodes through heartbeats.

The placement service continulously monitors all data nodes through heartbeats.

\begin{itemize}
\item
If a data node doesn't send a heartbeat within a configurable 15-second grace period, the placement service marks the node as ``down'' in the virtual cluster map.

\item
This is a critical service, so we suggest building a cluster of 5 or 7 placement service nodes with Paxos [13] or Raft [14] consensus protocol
\item
The consensus protocol ensures that as long as more than half of the nodes are healthy, the service as a whole continues to work.
\item
For example, if the placement service cluster has 7 nodes, it can tolerate a 3 node failure
\item
To learn more about consensus protocols, refer to the reference materials [13] [14]
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Data node}

The data node stores the actual object data.

\begin{itemize}
\item
It ensures reliability and durability by replicating data to multiple data nodes, also called a replication group.
\item
Each data node has a data service daemon reunning on it
\item
The data service daemon continuously sends heartbeats ot the placement service.
\end{itemize}

The heartbeat message includes the following essnetial information:

\begin{itemize}
\item
How many disk drives (HDD OR SSD) does the data node manage?
\item
How much data is stored on each drive?
\end{itemize}

WHen the placement service receives the heartbeat for the first time, it assigns an ID for this data node, adds it to the virtual cluster map, and returns the following information:

\begin{itemize}
\item
a unique ID of the data node
\item
the virtual cluster map
\item
where to replace data
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Data persistence flow}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_10}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Now let's take a look at how data is persisted in the data node.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
The API service forwards the object data to the data store.
\item
The data routing service generates a UUID for this object and queries the placement service for the data node to store this object. The placement service checks the virtual cluster map and returns the primary data node
\item
The data routing service sends data directly to the primary data node, together with its UUID
\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
The primary data node saves the data locally and replicates it to two secondary data nodes
\item
The primary node responds to the data routing service when data is successfully replicated to all secondary nodes
\end{itemize}
\item
The UUID of the object (ObjId) is returned to the API service
\end{enumerate}

In step 2, given a UUID for the object as an input, the placement service returns the replication group for the object.

\begin{itemize}
\item
How does the placement service do this?
\item
Keep in mind that this lokup ne4eds to be deterministic, and it must survive the addition or removal of replication groups
\item
COnsisten hashing is a common implementation of such a lookup function
\item
Refer to [15] for more information
\item
In step 4, the primary data node replicates data to all secondary nodes before it returns a response
\item
This makes data strongly consistent among all data nodes
\item
This consistency comes with latency costs because we have to wait until the slowest replica finishes
\item
Figure 9.11 shows the trade-offs between consistency and latency
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_11}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Data is considered as successfully saved after all three nodes store the data. This approach has the best consistency but the highest latency
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Data is considered as successfully saved after the primary and one of the secondaries store the data.
\item
This approach has a medium consistency and medium latency
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Data is considered as successfully save d after the primary persists the data
\item
This approach has the worst consistency but the lowest latency
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

Both 2 and 3 are forms of eventual consistency.

\subsubsection{How data is organized}

Now let's take a look at how each data node manages the data.

\begin{itemize}
\item
A simple solution is to store each object in a stand-alone file
\item
This works, but the performance suffers when there are many small files
\item
Two issues arise when having too many small files on a file system
\item
First, it wastes many data blocks
\item
A file system stores files in discrete disk blocks
\item
Disk blocks have the same size, and the size is fixed when the volume is initialized
\item
The typical block size is around 4KB
\item
For a file smaller than 4KB, it would still consume the entire disk block
\item
If the file system holds a lot of small files, it wastes a lot of disk blocks, with each one only lightly filled with a small file
\end{itemize}

Second, it could exceed the sytem's inode capacity.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The file system stores the location and other information about afile in a special type of block called inode
\item
For most file systems, the number of inodes is fixed when the disk is initialized
\item
With millions of small files, it runs the risk of consuming all inodes
\item
Also, the operating system does not handle a large number of inoes very well, even with aggresive caching of file system metadata
\item
For these reasons, storing small objects as individual files does not work well in practice
\item
TO addresss these issues, we can merge many small objects into a larger file
\item
It works concetually like a write-ahead log (WAL)
\item
WHen we save an object, it is appnded to an existing read-write file
\item
WHen the read-write file reaches its capacity thredhold (usually set to a few FGBs), the read-write file is marked as read-only and a new read-write file is created to receive new objects
\item
Once a file is marked as read-only, it can only serve read requests.
\item
Figure 9.12 explains how this process works
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_12}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Note that write access to the read-write file must be serialized.

\begin{itemize}
\item
As shown in Figure 9.12, objects are stored in order, one after the other, in the read-write file.
\item
To maintain this on-disk layout, multiple cores processing incoming write requests in parallel must take their turns to write to the read-write file
\item
For a modern server with a large number of cores processing many incoming requests in parallel, this seriously restricts write throughput
\item
To fix this, we could provide deicated read-write files, one for each core processing incoming requests
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Object lookup}

With each data file holding many small objects, how does the data node locate an object by UUID? The data node needs the following information:

\begin{itemize}
\item
The data file that contains the objecjt
\item
The straing offset of the object in the data file
\item
The size of the object
\end{itemize}

The database schema to support this lookup is shown in Table 9.3.

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|object_mapping| \\
\hline
\lstinline|object_id| \\
\lstinline|file_name| \\
\lstinline|start_offset| \\
\lstinline|object_size| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table: \lstinline|Object_mapping| table

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{table_9_4}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

We considered two options for storing this mapping: a file-based key-value store such as RocksDB [16] or a relational database.

\begin{itemize}
\item
RocksDB is based on SSTable [17], and it is fast for writes but slower for reads
\item
A relational database usually uses a B+ tree [18] based storage engine, and it is fast for reads but slower for writes
\item
As mnetioned earlier, the data access pattern is write once and read multiple times
\item
Since a relational database provides better read performance, it is a better choice than RocksDB
\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}
\item
How should we deploy this relational database?
\item
At our scale, the data volume for the mapping table is massive
\item
Deploying a single large cluster to support all data nodes could work, but is difficult to manage
\item
Note that this mapping data is isolated within each data node
\item
There is no need to share this across data nodes
\item
To take advantage of this property, we could simply deploy a simple relational database on each data node
\item
SQLite [19] is a good choice here
\item
It is a file-based relational database with a solid reputation
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Updated data persistence flow}

Since we have made quite a few changes to the data node, let's revist how to save a new object in the data nodde (Figure 9.13).

\begin{itemize}
\item
The API service sends a request to save a new object named \lstinline|object 4|.
\item
The data node service appends the object named \lstinline|object 4| at the end of the read-write file named \lstinline|/data/c|
\item
A new record of \lstinline|object 4| is inserted into the \lstinline|object_mapping| table
\item
The data node service returns the UUID to the API service
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_13}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Durability}

Data reliability is extremely important for data storage systems.

\begin{itemize}
\item
How can we create a storage system that offers six nines of durability?
\item
Each failure case has to be carefully considered and data needs to be properly replicated.
\end{itemize}


\subsubsection{Hardware failure and failure domain}

Hard drive failures are inveitable no matter which media we use.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Some storage media mya have btter duarbility than others, but we cannot rely on a single hard drive to achieve our durability objective
\item
A proven way to increase durability is to replicate data to multiple hardd rives, so a single disk failure does not impact the data availability, as a whole
\item
In our design, we replicate data three times
\end{itemize}

Let's assume the spinning hard drive has an annual failure rate of 0.81\% [20].

\begin{itemize}
\item
This number hihgly depends on the model and make.
\item
Making 3 copies of data gives us $1 - 0.0081^{3} = ~ 0.999999$ reliability
\item
This is a very rough estimate
\item
For more sophisticated calculations, please read [20]
\end{itemize}

For a complete durability evalution, we also need to consider the impacts of different failure domains.

\begin{itemize}
\item
A failure domain is a phyical or logical sect   of the environment that is negatively affected when a critical service experiences problems
\item
In a nodern data center, a server i usually put into a rack [21], and the racks are grouped into rows/floors-rooms
\item
Since each rack shares network swithces and power, all the servers in a rack are in a rack-level failure domain
\item
A modern server shares components like the motherboard, processors, power suplly, HDD drives, etc.
\item
The components in a server are in a node-level failure domain
\end{itemize}

Here is a good example of a large-scale failure domain isolation.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Typically, a data centers divide infrastructure that shares nothing into different Availability Zones (AZs)
\item
We replicate our data to different AZs to minimize the failure impact (Figure 9.14)
\item
Note that the choice of failure domain level doesn't directly increase the durability of data, but it will result in better reliability in extreme cases , such as large-scale power outages, cooling system fialures, natural disasters, etc.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_14}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Erasure coding}

Making three full copies of data gives us roughly 6 nines of data durability.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Are there other options to further increase durability?
\item
Yes, erasure coding is one option
\item
Erasure coding [22] deals with data durability differently
\item
It chunks data into smaller pieces (placed on different servers) and creates parities for redundancy
\item
In the event of failures, we can use chunk data and parities to reconstruct the data
\item
Let's take a look at a concrete example (4 + 2 erasure coding) as shown in Figure 9.15
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_15}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Dataq is broken up into four even-sized data chunks d1, d2, d3, and d4
\item
The mathematical formula [23] is used to calculate the partities p1 and p2. To give a much simplified example, $p1 = d1 + 2 \times d2 - d3 + 4 \times d4$ and $p2 = -d1 + 5 \times d2 + d3 - 3 \times d4$ [24]
\item
Data d3 and d4 are lost due to node crashes
\item
The mathematical formula is used to reconstruct lost data d3 and d4, using the known values of d2, d3, p1, and p2
\end{itemize}

Let's take a look at another example as shown in Figure 9.16 to better understand how erasure coding works with failure domains.

\begin{itemize}
\item
An (8+4) erasure coding setup breaks up the original data evenly into 8 chunks and calculates 4 partiies
\item
ALl 12 pieces of data have the same size
\item
All 12 chunks of data are distributed across 12 different failure domains
\item
The mathematics behind erasure coding ensures that the original data can be reconstructed when at most 4 nodes are down
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_16}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Compared to replication where the data router only needs to read data for an object from on healthy node, in easure coding the data router has to read data from at least 8 healhty nodes.

\begin{itemize}
\item
This is an architectural design tradeoff
\item
We use a more complex solution with a slower access speed, in exchange for higher durability and lower storage cost
\item
For object storage where the main cost is storage, this tradeoff might be worth it
\item
How much extra space does erasure coding need?
\item
For every two chunks of data, we need one prarity block, so the storage overhead is 50\% (Figure 9.17)
\item
While in 3-copy replication, the storage overhead is 200\% (Figure 9.17)
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_17}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Does erasure coding increase data durability?

\begin{itemize}
\item
Let's assume a node has a 0.81\% annual failure rate.

\begin{itemize}
\item
According to the calculation done by   Backbalze [20], erasure coding can achieve 11 nines durability
\item
THe calculation requires complicated math
\item
If you're interested, refer to [20] for details
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

Table 9.5 compares the proces and cons of replication and erasure coding.

\begin{tabular}{|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|}
\hline
      & \textbf{Replication} & \textbf{Erasure coding} \\
\hline
Durability & 6 nines of durability (data copied 3 times) & 11 nines of durability (8 + 4 erasure coding) \textbf{Erasure coding wins} \\
\hline
Storage efficiency & 200\% storage overhead & 50\% storage overhead. \textbf{Erasure coding wins} \\
\hline
