Compute resource & No computation. \textbf{Replication wins.}
 & Higher usage of computation resources to calculate parities \\
\hline
Write performance & Replicating data to multiple nodes. No calculation is needed. \textbf{Replication wins.}
 & Increased write latency because we need to calculate parities before data is written to disk\\
\hline
Read performance & In normal operation, reads are served from the same replica. Reads under a failure mode are not impacted because reads can be served from a non-fault replica. \textbf{Replication wins}
 & In normal operation, every read has to come from multiple nodes in the cluster. Reads under a faulire mode are slower because the missing data must be reconstructed first.\\
\hline
\end{tabular}

In summary, replication is widely adopted in latency-sensitive applications while erasure coding is often used to minimize storage cost.

Erasure coding is attractive for its cost efficiency and druability, but it greatly complicates the data node design.

Therefore, for this design, we mainly focus on replication.

\subsection{Correctness verfication}

Erasure coding increases data durability at comparable storage costs.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Now we can move on to solve another hard challenge: data corruption
\item
If a disk fails completely and the failure can be detected, it can be treated as a data node failure
\item
In this case, we can reconstrcut data using erasure coding
\item
However, in-memory data corruption is a regular occurrence in large-scale systems
\item
This problem can be addressed by verifying checksums [25] between process boundaries
\item
A checksum is a small-size block of data that is used to detect data errors
\item
Figure 9.18 illustrates how the checksum is generated
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_18}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

If we know the checksum of the original data, we can compute the checksum of the data after transmission:

\begin{itemize}
\item
If they are different, data is corrupted
\item
If they are the same, there is a very high probability the data is not corrupted. THe probability is not 100\%, but in practice, we could assume they are the same
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_19}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

There are many checksum algorithms, such as MD5 [26], SHA1 [27], HMAC [28], etc.

\begin{itemize}
\item
A good checksum algorithm usually outputs a significantly different value even for a small change made to the input
\item
For this chapter, we choose a simple check sum algorithym such as MD5
\item
On our design, we append the checksum at the end of each object
\item
Before a file is marked as read-only, we added a checksum of the entire file at the end
\item
Figure 9.20 shows the layout
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_20}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

With (8 + 4) erasure coding and checksum verification, this is what happens when we read data:

\begin{enumerate}
\item
Fetch the objecfvt data and the checksum
\item
Compute the checksum against the data received

\begin{itemize}
\item
If the two checksums match, the data is error-free
\item
If the checksums are different, the data is corrupted We will try to recover by reading the data from other failure domains
\end{itemize}
\item
Repeat steps 1 and 2 until all 8 pieces of data are returned. We then reoncstruct the data and send it back to the client.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Metadata data model}

In this section, we first discuss the database schema and then dive into scaling the database.

\subsubsection{Schema}

The database schema needs to support the following 3 queries:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Query 1: Find the object ID by ocject name
\item
Query 2: Insert and dlete an object based on the object name
\item
Query 3: List objects in a bucket sharing the same prefix
\end{itemize}

Figure 9.21 shows the schma design. We need two database tables: \lstinline|bucket| and \lstinline|object|.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_21}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Scale the bucket table}

Since there is usually a limit on the number of buckets a user can create, the size of the \lstinline|bucket| table is small.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Let's assume we have 1 million customers, each customer owns 10 buckets and each record takes 1KB
\item
That means we need 10GB ($1 \text{ million} \times 10 \times 1 \text{ KB}$) of storage space
\item
THe whole table can easily fit in a modern database server
\item
However, a single database server mgiht not have enough CPU or network bandwidth to handle all read requests
\item
If so, we can spread the read load among multiple database replicas
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Scale the object table}

The \lstinline|object| table holds the object metadata.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The dataset at our deisgn scale will liekly not fit in a single databvase isnstance
\item
We can scale the boject table by sharding
\item
One option is to shard by \lstinline|bucket_id| so all the objects under the smae bucket are stored in one shared
\item
This doesn't work because it causes hotpot shards as a bucket might contain billions of objects
\item
Another option is to shard by \lstinline|object_id|
\item
The beneifit of this sharding scheme is that it evenly distributes the load
\item
But we will not be able to execute query 1 and query 2 effficiently because those two queries are based on the URI
\item
We choose to shard by a combination of \lstinline|bucket_name| and \lstinline|object_name|
\item
This is because most of the metadata operations are based on the object URI, for example, finding the object ID by URI or uploading an object via URI
\item
TO evenly distribute the data, we can use the hash of the \lstinline|<bucket name, object_name>|
\item
With this sharding scheme, it is straightforward to support the first two queries, but the last query is less obvious. Let's take a look.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Listing object in a bucket}
The object store arranges files in a flat structure instead of a hierarchy, like in a file system

An object can be accessed using a path in this format, \lstinline|s3://bucket-name/object-name|.

For exmaple, \lstinline|s3://mybucket/abc/d/e/f/file.txt| contains:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Bucket name: \lstinline|mybucket|
\item
Object name: \lstinline|abc/d/e/f/file.txt|
\end{itemize}

To help users organize their objects in a bucket, S3 introduces a concept called `prefixes'.

\begin{itemize}
\item
A prefix iis a string at the beginning of the object name
\item
S3 uses prefixes to organize the data in a way similar to directories
\item
However, prefixes are not directories
\item
Listing a bucket by prefix limits the results to ony those object names that begin with the prefix
\end{itemize}

In the example above with the path \lstinline|s3:bucket//mybucket/abc/d/e/f/file.txt|, the prefix is \lstinline|abc/d/e/f/|

The AWS S3 listing command has 3 typical uses:

\begin{enumerate}
\item
List all buckets owned by a user. The command looks like this:

\lstinline|aws s3 list-buckets|
\item
LIst all objects in  abucket that are at the same level as the specified prefix. The command looks like this:
\lstinline|awes s3 ls s3://mybucket/abc|

In this mode, objects with more slashes in the name after the prefix are rolled up into a common prefix. FOr example, with these objects in the bucket:

\begin{lstlisting}
CA/cities/losangeles.txt
CA/cities/sanfrancisco.txt
NY/cities/ny.txt
federal.txt
\end{lstlisting}

Listing the bucket with the "/" prefix would return these results, with everything under \lstinline|CA/| and \lstinline|NY/| rolled up into them:

\begin{lstlisting}
CA/
NY/
federal.txt
\end{lstlisting}
\item
Recursively list all objects in a bucket that shares the same prefix. THe command looks like this:

\begin{lstlisting}
aws s3 ls s3://mubucket/abc/ --recursive
\end{lstlisting}

Using the same example as above, listing the bucket with the \lstinline|CA/| prefix would return these results:

\begin{lstlisting}
CA/cities/losangeles.txt
CA/cities/sanfrancisco.txt
\end{lstlisting}
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Single database}

Let's first explore how we would support the listing command with a single database. TO list all buckets owned by a user, we run the following query:

\begin{lstlisting}
SELECT * FROM bucket WHERE owner_id={id}
\end{lstlisting}

To list all objects in a bucket that share the smae prefix, we run a query like this:

\begin{lstlisting}
SELECT * FROM object
WHERE bucket_id = "123" AND object_name LIKE `abc/%`
\end{lstlisting}

In this example, we find all objects with \lstinline|bucket_id| equals to 123 that share the prefix \lstinline|abc/|.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Any objects with more slashes in their names after the specified prefix are rolled up in the application code as stated earlier in use case 2.
\item
The same query would support the recursive listing mode, as stated in use case 3 previously
\item
THe application code would list every object sharing the same prefix, without performing any rollups
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Distributed databases}

When the metadata table is sharded, it's difficult to implement the listing function because we don't know which shards contain the data.

THe most obvious solution is to run a search query on all shards and then aggregate the results.

To achieve this, we can do the following

\begin{enumerate}
\item
The metadata service queries every shard by running the following query:

\begin{lstlisting}
SELECT * FROM object
WHERE bucket_id = "123" AND object_name LIKE `a/b/%`
\end{lstlisting}
\item
The metadata esrvice aggregates all objects return from each shard and returns the result to the caller
\end{enumerate}

This solution works, but implementing pagination for this is a bit complicated.

Before explain why, let's review how pagination works for a simple case with a single database

TO return pages of listing with 10 objects for each page, the SELECT query would start with this.

\begin{lstlisting}
SELECT * FROM object
WHERE bucket_id = "123" AND object_name LIKE `a/b/%`
ORDER BY object_name OFFSET 0 LIMIT 10
\end{lstlisting}

The \lstinline|OFFSET| and \lstinline|LIMIT| would restrict the results to the first 10 objects.

\begin{itemize}
\item
In the next call, the user sends the request with a hint to the server, so it knows to construct the query for the second page with an \lstinline|OFFSET| of 10.
\item
This hint is usually done with a curosr that the server returns with each page to the client
\item
The offset information is encoded in the curosr
\item
The client would include the curosr int he request for the next page
\item
The server decodes the curosr and uses the offset information embedded in it to construct the query for the next page
\end{itemize}

To continue with the example above, the query for the second page looks like this:

\begin{lstlisting}
SELECT * FROM metadata
WHERE bucket_id = "123" AND object_name LIKE `a/b/*`
ORDER BY object_anem OFFSET 10 LIMIT 10
\end{lstlisting}

This client-server request loop continues until the server returns a special curosr that marks the end fo the entire listing.

Now let's explore why it's complicated to support pagination for sharded datbases.

Since the objects are distributed across shards, the shards would likely return a varying number of results

Some shards would contain a full page of 10 objects, while others would be partial or empty.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The application code would receive results from every shard, aggregate and sort them, and return only a page of 10 in our example
\item
The objects that don't get included in the current round must cbe considered again for the next round
\item
This means that each shard would likely have a different offset,
\item
The server must track the offsets for all the shards and associate those offsets with the curosr
\item
If there are hundreds of shards, there will be hundreds of offsets to track.
\item
We have a solution that can solve the problem but there are some tradeoffs
\item
Since object storage is tuned for vast scale and high durability, object listing performance is rarely a priority
\item
In fact, all commerical object storage supports object listing with sub-optimal performance.
\item
To take advantage of this, we coulddenormalize the listing data into a separate tablesharded by bucket ID
\item
This table is only used for listing objects
\item
With this setup, even tbuckets with billlions of objects would offer acceptable performance.
\item
This isolates the listing query to a single database which greatly simplifies the implementation
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Object versioning}

Versioning is a feature that keeps multiple versions of an object in a bucket.

\begin{itemize}
\item
With versioning, we can restore object s that are accidentally deleted or overwritten
\item
For example, we may modify a document and save it under the same name, inside the same bucket
\item
Without versioning, the old version of the document metadata is replaced by the new version in the metadata store
\item
The old version of the doucment is marked as deleted, so its storage space will be reclaimed by the garbage collector
\item
With versioning, the object storage keeps all previous versions of the document int he metadata store, and the old versions of the document are never marked as deleted in the object sotre
\item
Figure 9.22 explains how to upload a versioned object
\item
For this to work, we first need to enable versioning on the bucket
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_22}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{enumerate}
\item
The client sends an \lstinline|HTTP PUT| request to upload an object named \lstinline|script.txt|.
\item
The API service verifies the user's identity and ensures that the user has WRITE permission on the bucket
\item
Once verified, the API service uploads the data ot the data store. The data store persists the data as a new object and returns a enw UUID to the API service
\item
The API service calls the metadata store to store the metadata information of this object
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Tos upport versioning, the object table for the metadata store has a column called \lstinline|object_version| that is only used if versioning is enabled
\item
Instead o f overwriting the existing record, a new record is inserted with the same \lstinline|bucket_id| and \lstinline|object_name| as the old record, but with a new \lstinline|object_id| and \lstinline|object_version|
\item
The \lstinline|object_id| is the UUID for the new object retuned in step 3
\item
The \lstinline|object_version| is a \lstinline|TIMEUUID| [29] generated when the new row is inserted
\item
No matter which database we choose for the metadata store, it should be efficinet to look up the current version of an object
\item
The current version has the largest has the largest \lstinline|TIMEUUID| of all the entires with the same \lstinline|object_name|
\item
See Figure 9.23 for an illlustration of how we store versioned metadata
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_23}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

In addition to uploading a versioned object, it can also be deleted. Let's take a look.

When we delete an object, all versions remain in the bucket and we insert a delete marker, as shown in FIgure 9.24.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_24}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

A delete marker is a new version of the object, and it becomes the current version of the object once inserted.

Performing a \lstinline|GET| request when the current version of the object is a delete marker returns a \lstinline|404 Object Not Found| error.

\subsection{Optimizing uploads of large files}

In the back-of-the-envelope esitmation, we estimated that 20\% of the objects are larger tha a few GBs.

\begin{itemize}
\item
It is possible to upload such a large object file direclty, but it could take a logn time
\item
If the network connection fails in the middle of the upload, we have to start over
\item
A better solution is to slice a large object into smaller parts and upload them indepdently
\item
After all the parts are uploaded, the object store re-assmbles the object form the parts. This process is called multipart upload.
\end{itemize}

Figure 9.25 illustrates how multipart upload works:

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_25}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{enumerate}
\item
The client calls the object storage to initaate a multipart upload
\item
The data store retunrs an \lstinline|uploadId|, which uniqely identifies the upload
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The client splits the large file into small objects and starts uploading
\item
Let's assume the size of the file is 1.6GB and the client splits it into 8 parts, so each part is 200MB in size
\item
The client uploads the first part to the data store teogether with the uploadID it received in step 2
\end{itemize}
\item
When a part is uploaded, the data store returns an \lstinline|ETag|, which is essnially the md5 checksum of that part. It is used to verify multipart uploads
\item
After all parts are uploaded, the client sends a complete multiplart upload request, which includes the \lstinline|uploadID|, part numbers, and \lstinline|ETags|
\item
The data store reassembles the object from its parts based on the part number. Since the object is really large, this process may take a few minutes. After reassembly is complete, it returns a success message to the client.
\end{enumerate}

One potential problem with this approach si that old aprts are no longer useful after the object has ben reassembled from them.

To solve this porlbem we can introduce a garbage collection service responsible for freeing up space from parts that are no longer needed.

\subsection{Garbage collection}

Garbage ccollection is the process of autmatically recaliming storage space that is no longer used. There are a few ways that data might become garbage:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Lazy object deletion. An object is marked as deleted at delete time without actually being deleted
\item
Orphaned data. For example, half uploaded data or abondoned multipart uploads
\item
Corruped data. Data that failed the checksum verification.
\end{itemize}

The garbage collector does not remove objects from the data store, right away. Dleted objects will be periodically cleaned up with a compaction metchanism.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The garbage collector is also responsible for reclaming unused space in replicas.
\item
For replication, we delete the object from both primary and backup nodes.
\item
For erasure coding, if we use (8 + 4) setup, we delete the object from all 12 nodes.
\end{itemize}



Figure 9.26 shows an example of how compaction works.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
The garbage collector copies objects from \lstinline|/data/b| to a new file named \lstinline|/data/d|. Note the garbage collector ksips ``Object 2'' and ``Object 5'' because the delete flag is set to true for both of them.
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
After all objects are copied, the garbage collector updates the \lstinline|object_mapping| table
\item
For example, the \lstinline|obj_id| and \lstinline|object_size| fields of ``Object 3'' remain the same, but \lstinline|file_name| and \lstinline|start_offset| are updated to refelect its new location
\item
To ensure data consistency, it's a good idea to wrap the update operations to \lstinline|file_name| and \lstinline|start_offset| in a database transaction
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_9_26}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item
As we can see from FIgure 9.26, the size of the new file after compaction is smaller than the old file.
\item
To avoid creating lot of small files, the garbage collector usually waits until there are a large number of read-only files to compact, and the compaction process appends objects from many read-only files into a few large new files.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 4: Wrap Up}

In this cahpter, we described the high-level design for S3-like object storage. We compared the differences between block storage, file storage, and object storage.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The focus of this interview is on the design of object storage, so we listed how the uploading, downloading, listing objects in a bucket, and versioining of objects are typically done in object storage.
\item
Then we dived deeper into the design
\item
Object storage is composed of a data store and a metadata store
\item
We explained how the data is persisted into the data store and discussed two methods for increasing realiability and ruability: replication and reasure coding
\item
FOr the metadata store, we explained how the multipart upload is executed and how to design the database schema to support typical use cases
\item
Lastly, we explained how to shard the metadata store to support even larger data volume!
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{chapter_9_summary}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\chapter*{Chapter 10: Real-time Gaming Leaderboard}

In this chapter, we are going to walk through the challenge of designing a leaderboard for an online mobile game.

What is a leaderboard?

\begin{itemize}
\item
Leaderboards are common in gaming and elsewhere to show who is leading a particular tournament or compeitition
\item
User are assigned points for completing tasks or challenges, and whoever ahs the most points is at the top of the leaderboard
\end{itemize}

In this chapter, we are going to walk through the challenge of designing a leaderboard for an online mobile game.

What is a leaderboard?

\begin{itemize}
\item
Leaderboards are common in gaming and elsewhere to show who is leading a particular tournament or competition
\item
User are assigned points for completing tasks or challenges, and whoever has the most points is at the top of the leaderboard
\end{itemize}

Figure 10.1 shows an example of a mobile game leaderboard. The leaderboard shows the ranking of the leading competitors and also displays the position of the user on it.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_1}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Step 1: Understand the Problem and Establish Design Scope}

Leaderboards can be pretty straighforward, but there are a number of different matters that can add complexity. We should clarify the requirements.

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\textbf{Candidate}: How is the score calculate for the leaderboard?
\textbf{Interviewer}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
The user gets a point when they win a match
\item
We can go with a simple point system in which each user has a score associated with them
\item
Each time the user wins a match we should add a point to their total score
\end{itemize}

\item

\textbf{Candidate}: Are all players included in the leaderboard?

\textbf{Interviewer}: Yes.

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Is there a time segment associated with the leaderboard?

\textbf{Interviewer}: Each month a new tournament kicks off which starts a new leaderboard.

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Cn we assume we only care about the top 10 users?

\textbf{Interviewer}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
We want to display the top 10 users as well as the position of a specifc user on the leaderboard
\item
If time allows, let's also discuss how to return users who are four places above and below a specific user
\end{itemize}

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: How many users are in a tournament?

\textbf{Interviewer}: Average of 5 million dialy active users (DAU) and 25 million monthly active users (MAU)

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: How many matchecs are plyaed on average during a tournamnet?

\textbf{Interviewer}: In this case, their ranks are the smae. If tiem allows, we can talk about ways to break ties

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Does the learboard need to be real-time?

\textbf{Interviewer}: Yes, we wan to present real-time results, or as close as possible. It is not okay to present a batched history of results.
\end{enumerate}

Now that we've gathered all the requirements, let's list the functional requirements.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Display top 10 players on the leaderboard
\item
Show a user's specific rank
\item
Display players who are four places above and below the desired user (bonus)
\end{itemize}

Other than clarifying functional requirements, it's important to understand non-functional requirements.

\subsubsection{Non-functional requirements}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Real-time update on scores
\item
Score update is reflected on the leaderboard in real-time
\item
General scalability, availability, and reliability requirements
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Back-of-the-envelope estimation}

Let's take a look at some back-of-the-envelope calculations to determine the potential scale and challenges our solution will need to address.

\begin{itemize}
\item
WIth 5 million DAU, if the game had an even distribution of players during a 24-hour period, we would have an average of 50 users per second ($\frac{5,000,000 \text{ DAU}}{10^{5} \text{ seconds}} = ~ 50$)
\item
However, we know that usages most likely aren't evenly distributed, and potentially there are peaks during evenings whe4n many people across different time zones have time to play
\item
To account for this, we could assume that peak load would be 5 times the average
\item
Therefore we'd want to allow for a peak load of 250 users per second
\item
QPS for users scoring a point: if a user plays 10 games per day on average, the QPS for suers socring a point is: $50 \times 10 =~ 500$.
\item
Peak QPS is 5x of the average: 500 $\times 4$ = 2,500
\item
QPS for fetching the top 10 leaderboard: assume a user opens the game once a day and the top 10 leaderboard is loaded only when a user first opens the game
\item
The QPS for this is around 50
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 2: Propose High-levl Design and Get Buy-in}

In this section, we will discuss API design, high-level architecture, and data models.

\subsection{API design}

At a high level, we need the following three APIs:

\textbf{POST /v1/scores}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Update a user's position on the leaderboard when a user wins a game.
\item
The request parameters are listed below.
\item
This should be an internal API that can only be called by the game servers
\item
The client should not be able  to update the leaderboard score directly
\end{itemize}

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|Field| & \lstinline|Description| \\
\hline
\lstinline|user_id| & The user who wins a game \\
\hline
\lstinline|points| & The number of points a user gained by winning a game \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 10.1: Request parameters

Response:

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
\textbf{Name} & \textbf{Description} \\
\hline
\lstinline|200 OK| & Successfully updated a user's score \\
\hline
\lstinline|400 Bad Request| & Failed to update a user's score\\
\hline
\end{tabular}

\textbf{GET /v1/scores}

Fetch the top 10 players from the leaderboard.

Sample response:

\begin{lstlisting}
{
  "data": [
   {
      "user_id": "user_id1",
      "user_name": "alice",
      "rank": 1,
      "score": 976,
   },
  {
    "user_id": "user_id2",
    "user_name": "bob",
    "rank": 2,
    "score": 965
  }
  ]
}
\end{lstlisting}

\lstinline|GET /v1/scores/{:user_id}|

Fetch the rank of a specific user.

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
\textbf{Field} & \textbf{Description} \\
\hline
\lstinline|user_id| & The ID of the user whose rank we would like to fetch \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 10.3: Request parameters

Sample response:

\begin{lstlisting}
{
  "user_info": {
    "user_id": "user5",
    "score": 940,
    "rank": 6
  }
}
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{High-level architecture}

The high-level design diagram is shown in Figure 10.2.

There are two services in this design. The game service allows users to play the game and the leaderboard service creates and displays a leaderboard.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_2}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{enumerate}
\item
When a player wins a game, the client sends a request to the game service
\item
The game service ensures the win is valid and calls the leaderboard service to update the score
\item
The leaderboard service updates the user's score in the leaderboard store
\item
A player makes a call to the leaderboard service direclty to fetch leaderboard data including:

\begin{itemize}
\item
top 10 leaderboard
\item
the rank of the player on the leaderboard
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

Before settling on this design, we considered a few alternatives and decided against them

It might be helpful to go through the thought process of this and to compare different options.

\subsection{Should the client talk to the leaderboard service directly?}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_3}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

In the alternative design, the score is set by the client.

\begin{itemize}
\item
This option is not secure because it is subject to man-in-the-middle attack [1], where players can put in a proxy and change scores at will
\item
Therefore, we need the score to be set on the server-side
\item
NOte that for server authoritative games such as online poker, the client may not need to call the game server explictly to set scores
\item
The game server handles all game logic, and it knows when the game finishes and could set the score wihtout any client intervention
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Do we need a message queue between the game service and the leaderboard service?}


The answer to this question highly depends on how the game scores are used
\begin{itemize}
\item
If the data is used in other places or supports multiple functionalities, then it might make sense to put data in Kafka as shown in Figure 10.4
\item
This way, the same data can be consumed by multiple consumers, such as leaderboard service, analytics service, push notification service, etc.
\item
This is especially true when the game is a turn-bawesd or multi-player game in which we need to notify other players about the score update
\item
As this is not an explicit requirement based on the conversation with the interviewer, we do not use a message queue in our design
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_4}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Data models}

One of the key components in the system is the leaderboard store.

We will discuss three potential solutions: relational database, Redis, and NOSQL (NoSQL solution is explained in deep dive section on page 309).

\subsubsection{Relational database solution}

First, let's take a step back and start with the simplest solution.

\begin{itemize}
\item
What if the scale doesn't matter and we have only a few users?
\item
We would most likely opt to have a simple leaderboard solution using a relational database system (RDS)
\item
Each monthly leaderboard could be represented as a database table containing user id and score columns
\item
When the user wins a match, either award the user 1 point if they are new, or increase their existing score by 1 point
\item
To determine a user's ranking on the leaderboard, we would sort the table by the score in descending order
\item
The details are explained below
\end{itemize}

Leaderboard DB table:

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|user_id| & \lstinline|varchar| \\
\hline
\lstinline|score| & \lstinline|int| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

In reality, the leaderboard table has additional information, such as a \lstinline|game_id|, a timestamp, etc.

\begin{itemize}
\item
However, the underlying logic of how to query and update the leaderboard remains the same
\item
For simplicity, we assume only the current month's leaderboard data is stored in the leaderboard table
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{A user wins a point}:

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_6}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Assume every score update would be an increment of 1. If a user doesn't yet have an entry in the leader board for the month, the first insert would be:

\begin{lstlisting}
INSERT INTO leaderboard (user_id, score) VALUES ('mary1934', 1);
\end{lstlisting}

An update to the user's score would be:

\begin{lstlisting}
UPDATE leaderboard set score=score +1 where user_id='mary1934';
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Find a user's leaderboard position:}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_7}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

To fetch the user rank, we would sort the leaderboard table and rank by the score:
\begin{lstlisting}
SELECT (@rownum := @rownum + 1) AS rank, user_id, score
FROM leaderboard
ORDER BY score DESC;
\end{lstlisting}

The result of the SQL query looks like this:

\begin{tabular}{|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|rank| & \lstinline|user_id| & \lstinline|score| \\
\hline
1 & \lstinline|happy_tomato| & \lstinline|987| \\
\hline
2 & \lstinline|mallow| & \lstinline|902| \\
\hline
3 & \lstinline|smith| & \lstinline|870| \\
\hline
4 & \lstinline|mary1934| & \lstinline|850| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

The solution works when the data set is small, but the qery becomes very slow when there are millions of rows. Let's take a look why.

\begin{itemize}
\item
To figure out the rank of a user, we need to srt every single player into their correct spot on the leaderboard so we can determine exactly what the correct rank is
\item
Rember that there can be duplicate scores as well, so the rank isn't just the position of the user in the list
\item
SQL databases are not performant when we have to process large amounts of continuously changin information
\item
Attempting to do a rank operation over millions of rows is going to take 10s of seconds, which is not acceptable for the desired real-time approach
\item
Since the data is constantly changing, it is also not feasible to consider a cache
\item
A relational database is not designed to handle the high load of read queries this implementation would require
\item
An RDS could be used successfully if done as a batch operation, but that would not align with the rquirement to return a real-time position for the user on the leader board
\end{itemize}


However, this approach doesn't scale well. First finding a user's rank is not performant because it essentially requires a table scan to determine the rank

One optimization we can do is to add an index and limit the number of pages to scan with the \lstinline|LIMIT| clause. The query looks like this:

\begin{lstlisting}
SELECT (@rownum := @rownum + 1) AS rank, user_id, score
FROM leaderboard
ORDER BY score DESC;
LIMIT 10
\end{lstlisting}

Second, this approach doesn't provide a striaghforward solution for determining the rank of a user who is not at the top of the leaderboard.

\subsection{Redis solution}

We want to find a solution that gives us predictable performance even for millions of users and allows us to have easy access to common leaderboard operations, without needing to fall back on complex DB queries.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Redis provides a potential solution to our problem
\item
Redis is an in-memory data store supporting key-value pairs
\item
Since it works in memory, it allows for fast reads and werites
\item
Redis has a specific data type called \textbf{sorted sets} that are idea for solving leaderboard system design problems
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{What are sorted sets?}

A sorted set is a data type similar to a set.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Each member of a sorted set is associated with a score.
\item
THe members of a set must be unique, but scores may repeat
\item
THe score is used to rank the sorted set in ascending order
\item
OUr leaderboard use case maps perfectly to sorted sets
\item
Internally, a sorted set is implemented by two data structures: a hash table and a skip list [2]
\item
The has table maps users to scores and the skip list maps scores to users
\item
In sorted sets, users are sreoted by scores
\item
A good way to understand a sorted set is to picture it as a table with score and member columns as shown in Figure 10.8
\item
The table is sorted by score in descending order
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_8}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

In this chapter, we don't go into the full detail of the sorted set implementation, but we do go over the high-level ideas.

\begin{itemize}
\item
A skip list is a list structure that allows for fast search
\item
It consists of a base sorted linked list and multi-level indexes
\item
Let's take a look at an example
\item
In Figure 10.9, the base list is a sorted singly-linked list
\item
The time complexity of insertion, removal, and search operations is \lstinline|O(n)|
\end{itemize}

How can we make thos operations faster?

\begin{itemize}
\item
ONe idea is to get to the middle quickly,as the binary search algorithm does
\item
TO achieve that, we add a level 1 index that skips every other node, and then a level 2 index that skips every other node of the level 1 indexes
\item
We keep introducing additional levels, with each new level skipping every other nodes of the previous level
\item
We stop this addition when the distance between nodes is $\frac{n}{2} - 1$, where $n$ is the total number of nodes
\item
As shown in Figure 10.9, searching for number 45 is a lot faster when we have multi-level indexes
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_9}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

When the data set is small, the speed improvement using the skip list isn't obvious.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Figure 10.10 shows an example of a skip list with 5 levels of indexes
\item
In the base linked list, it needs to travel 62 nodes to reach the correct node
\item
In the skip list, it only needs to traverse 11 nodes [3]
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_10}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

SOrted sets are more performant than a relational database because each element is automatically positioned in the right order during insert or update, as well as the fact that the complexity of an add or find operation in a sorted set is logarithmic: $O(log(n))$.

In contrast, to calculate the rank of a specific user in a relational database, we need to run nested queries:

\begin{lstlisting}
SELECT *, (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM leaderboard lb2
WHERE lb2.score >= lb1.score) RANK
FROM leaderboard lb1
WHERE lb1.user_id = {:user_id}
\end{lstlisting}

\subsubsection{Implementation using Redis sorted sets}

Now that we know sorted sets are fast, let's take a loook at the Redis operations we will use to build our leaderboard [4] [5] [6] [7]:

\begin{itemize}
\item
\lstinline|ZADD|: insert the user into the set if they don't yet exist. Otherwise, update the score for the user. It takes $O(log(n))$ to execute.
\item
\lstinline|ZINCRBY|: increment the score of the user by the specified increment. If the user doesn't exist in the set, then it assumes the score starts at 0. It takes $O(log(n))$ to execute
\item
\lstinline|ZRANGE/ZREVRANGE|:

\begin{itemize}
\item
fetch a range of users sorted by the score
\item
We can specify the order (range vs revrange), the number of entries, and the position to start from
\item
This takes $O(log(n) + m)$ to execute, where $m$ is the number of entries to fetch (which is usually small in our case) and $n$ is the number of entires in the sorted set
\end{itemize}

\item
\lstinline|ZRANK/ZREVRANK|:

fetch the position of any user sorting in ascending/descending order in logarithmic time
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Workflow with sorted sets}

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
A user scores a point

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_11}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}
\item
Every month we create a new leaderboard sorte set and the previous ones are moved to historical data storage
\item
When a user wins a match, they score 1 point; so we call \lstinline|ZINCRBY| to increment the user's score by 1 in that month's leaderboard, or ad dthe user to the leaderboard set if they weren't already there.
\item
The syntax for \lstinline|ZINCRBY| is:

\begin{lstlisting}
ZINCRBY <key> <increment> <user>
\end{lstlisting}
\item
The following command adds a point to user \lstinline|mary1934| after they win a match.

\begin{lstlisting}
ZINCRBY leaderboard_feb_2021 1 'mary1934'
\end{lstlisting}
\end{itemize}

\item
A user fetches the top 10 global leaderboard

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_12}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

We will call \lstinline|ZREVRANGE| to obtain the members in descending order because we ant the highest scores, and pass the \lstinline|WITHSCORES| attribute to ensure that it also returns the toal score for each user, as well as the set of users with the highest scores.

The following command fetches the top 10 players on the Feb 2021 leaderboard.

\begin{lstlisting}
ZREVRANGE leaderboard_feb_2021 0 9 WITHSCORES \end{lstlisting}

This returns a list like this:

\begin{lstlisting}
[(user2, score2), (user1, score1), (user5, score5)...]
\end{lstlisting}

\item
A user wants to fetch their leaderboard position

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_13}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

TO fetch the position of a user in the leaderboard, we will call \lstinline|ZEVRANK| to retrieve their rank on the leaderboard
\item
Again, we call the rev version of the command because we want to rank scores from high to low.

\begin{lstlisting}
ZREVRANK leaderboard_feb_2021 'mary1934'
\end{lstlisting}
\item
Fetch the relative position in the leaderboard for a user. An example is shown in Figure 10.14.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_14}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

While not an explicit requirement, we can easily fetch the relative position for a user  by leveraing \lstinline|ZREVRANGE| with the number of results above and below the desired player.

For example, if user \lstinline|Mallow007|'s rank is 361 and we want to fetch 4 players above and below them, we would run the following command.

\begin{lstlisting}
ZREVRANGE Leaderboard_feb_2021 357 365
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Storage requirement}

At a minimum, we need to store the user id and score.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The worst-case scenario is that all 25 million montly active users have won at least one game, and they all have entires in the leaderboard for the month
\item
Assuming the id is a 24-character string and the score is a 16-bit integer (or 2 bytes), we need 26 byters of storage per leaderboard entry
\item
Given the worst-case scenario of one leaderboard entry per MAU, we would need $26 \text{ bytes} \times 25 \text{ million} = 650 \text{ million bytes}$ or $\sim 650 MB$ for leaderboard storage in the Redis cache
\item
Even if we double the memory usage to account for the overhead of the skip list and the hash for the sroted set, one modern Redis server is more than enough to hold the data
\end{itemize}

Another related factor to consider is CPU and I/O usage.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Our peak QPS from the back-of-the-envelope estimation is 2500 updates/sec
\item
This is well within the performance envelope of a single Redis server
\item
One concern about the Redis cache is persistence, as a Redis node might fail
\item
Luckily, Redis does support persistence, but restarting a large Redis instance from disk is slow
\item
Usually, Redis is configured with a read replica, and when the main instance fails, the read replica is promoted, and a new read replica is attached
\end{itemize}

Besides, we need to have 2 supporting tables (user and point) in a relational database like MySQL.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The user table would store the user ID and user's display name (in a real-world application, this would contain a lot more data)
\item
The point table would contain the user id, score, and timestamp when they won a game.
\item
This can be leveraged for other game functions such as play hisotry, and can also be used to recreate the redis leaderboard in the event of an infrastructure failure
\item
As a small performance optimization, it may make sense to create an additional cache of the user details, potentially for the top 10 players since they are retrieved most frequently
\item
However, this doesn't amount to a large amount of data
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 3: Design Deep Dive}

Now that we've discussed the high-level design, let's dive into the following:


\begin{itemize}
\item
Whether or not to use a cloud provider

\begin{itemize}
\item
Manage our own services
\item
Leverage cloud service providers like Amazon Web Services (AWS)
\end{itemize}
\item
Scaling Redis
\item
Alternative solution: NoSQL
\item
Other considerations
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{To use a cloud provider or not}

Depending on the existing infrastructure, we generally have two options for deploying our solution. Let's take a look at each of them.

\subsubsection{Manage our own services}

In this approach, we will create a leaderboard sorted set each month to store the leaderboard data for that period.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The sorted set stores member and score information
\item
The rest of the details about the user, such as their name and profile image, are stored in MySQL databases
\item
When fetching the leaderboard, besides the leaderboard data, API servers also query the database to fetch corresponding users' names and profile images to display on the leaderboard
\item
If this becomes too inefficient in the long term, we can leverage a user profile cache to store users' details for the top 10 players
\item
The design is shown in Figure 10.15
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_15}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Build on the cloud}

The second approach is to leverage cloud infrastructures.

\begin{itemize}
\item
In this section, we assume our existing infrastructure is built on AWS and that it's a natural fit to build the leaderboard on the cloud
\item
We will use two major AWS echnologies in this design

\begin{itemize}
\item
Amazon API Gateway and AWS Lambda function [8]
\end{itemize}

\item
The Amazon API gateway provides a way to define the HTTP endpoints of a RESTful API and connect it to any backend services
\item
We use it to connect to our AWS lambda functions
\item
The mapping between Restful APIs and Lambda functions is shown in Figure in Table 10.5.
\end{itemize}

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
\textbf{APIs} & \textbf{Lambda function} \\
\hline
GET \lstinline|/v1/scores| & \lstinline|LeaderboardFetchTop10| \\
\hline
GET \lstinline|/v1/scores/{:user_id}| & \lstinline|LeaderboardFetchPlayerRank| \\
\hline
POST \lstinline|/v1/scores| & \lstinline|LeaderboardUpdateScore| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 10.5: Lambda functions

AWS Lambda is one of the most popular serverless computing platforms.

\begin{itemize}
\item
It allows us to run code without code withotu having to provision or manage the servers ourselves
\item
It runs only when needed and will scale autmatically based on traffic
\item
Serverless is one of the hottest topics in cloud services and is supported by all major cloud service providers
\item
For example, Google Cloud has Google CLoud Functions [9] and Microsoft has named its offering Microsoft Azure Functions [10]
\item
At a high level, our game calls the Amazon API Gateway, which in turn invokes the apprpriate lambda functions return the results back to the API Gateway, and then to the application
\end{itemize}

We can leverage Lambda functions to perform the queries wwe need without having to spin up a server instance.

\begin{itemize}
\item
AWS provides support for Redis clients that can be called from the Lambda function
\item
This also allows for auto-scaling as needed with DAU growth
\item
Design diagram for a user scoring a point and retrieving the leaderboard are shown below:

\textbf{Use case 1: scoring a point}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_16}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\textbf{Use case 2: retrieving leaderboard}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_17}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}
\end{itemize}

Lambdas are great because they are a serverless approach, and the infrastructure wil take care of auto-scaling the function as needed.

\begin{itemize}
\item
This means we don't need to manage the scaling and environment steup and maintenance
\item
Given this, we recommend going witha serverless approach fi we build the game from the ground up
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Scaling Redis}

With 5 million DAU, we can get away with one Redis cache from both a storage and QPS perspective.

\begin{itemize}
\item
However, let's imagine we have 500 million DAU, which is 100 times our original scale
\item
Now our worst-case scenario for the size of the leaderboard goes up to 65GB (650MB $\times$ 100), and our QPS goes up to 250,000 (2,500 $\times$ 100) queries per second
\item
This calls for a sharding solution
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Data sharding}

We consider sharding in one of the following two ways: fixed or hash partitions

\subsubsection{Fixed partition}

One way to understand fixed partitions is to look at the overall range of points on the leaderboard.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Let's say that the number of points won in one month ranges from 1 to 1000, and we break up the data by range
\item
For example, we could have 10 shards and each shard would have a range of 100 scores (For example, $1 \sim 100, 101 \sim 200, 201 \sim 300, \cdot$) as shown in Figure 10.18
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_18}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

For this to work, we want to ensure there is an even distribution of scores across the leaderboard.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Otherwise, we need to adjust the score range in each shared to make sure of a relatively even distribution
\item
In this approach, we shard the data ourselves in the application code
\item
When we are inserting or updating the score for a user, we need to know which shard they are in
\item
We could do this by calculating the user's current score form the MySQL database
\item
This can work, but a more performant option is to create a secondary cache to store the ampping from user ID to score
\item
We need to be careful when a user increases their score and moves between shards
\item
In this case, we need to remove the user from their current shard and move them to the new shard
\end{itemize}

To fetch the top 10 players in the leaderboard, we would fetch the top 10 players from the shard (sorted set) with the highest scores.

\begin{itemize}
\item
In Figure 10.18, the last shard with scores [901, 1000] contains the top 10 players
\item
To fetch the rank of a user, we would need to calculate the rank within their current shard (local rank), as well as the total number of players with higer scores in all of the shards
\item
Note that the total number of players in a shard can be retrieved by running the \lstinline|info keyspace| command in \lstinline|O(1)| [11]
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Hash partition}

A second approach is to use the Redis cluster, which is desirable if the scores are very clustered or clumped.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Redis cluster provides a way to shard data autmatically across multiple Redis nodes
\item
It doesn't use consistent hasing but a different form of sharding, where every key is part of a \lstinline|hash slot|
\item
There are 16384 hash slots [12] and we can compute the hash slot of a given key by doing \lstinline|CRC16(key) % 16384|  [13]
\end{itemize}

This allows us to add and remove nodes in the cluster easily without redistributing all the keys. In Figure 10.19, we have 3 nodes, where:

\begin{itemize}
\item
The first node contains hash slots [0, 5500]
\item
The second node contains hash slots [5501, 11000],
\item
The third node contains hash slots [11001, 16383]
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_19}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

An update would simply change the score of the user in the corresponding shard (determined by \lstinline|CRC16(key) % 16384|)

\begin{itemize}
\item
Retrieving the top 10 players on the leaderboard is more complicated
\item
We need to gather the top 10 players from each shard and have the application sort the data
\item
A concrete example is shown in Figure 10.20
\item
Those queries can be parallelized to reduce latency
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_20}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

This approach has a few limitations:

\begin{itemize}
\item
When we need to return top \lstinline|k| results (where \lstinline|k| is a very large number) on the leaderboard, the latency is high because a lot of entires are returned from each shard and need to be sorted
\item
Latency is high if we have lots of partitions because the query has to wait for the slowest partition
\item
Another issue with this approach is that it doesn't provide a straightforward solution for determining the rank of a specific user
\end{itemize}

Therefore, we lean towards the first proposal, fixed partition

\subsection{Sizing a Redis node}

There are multiple things to consider when sizing the Redis nodes [14].

\begin{itemize}
\item
Write-heavy applications require much more available memory, since we need to be able to accommmodate all of the writes to create the snapshot in case of a failure
\item
To be safe, allocate twice the amount of memory for write-heavy applications
\item
Redis provides a tool caleed Redis-benchmark that allows us to benchmark the performance of the Redis setup, by simulating multiple clients executing multiple queries and returning the number of requests per second for the given hardware
\item
To learn more about Redis-benchmark, see [15]
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Alternative solution: NoSQL}

An alternative solution to consider is NoSQL databases. What kind of NoSQL should we use?

Ideally, we want to choose a NoSQL that has the following properties:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Optimized for writes
\item
Efficiently sort items within the same partition by score
\end{itemize}

NoSQL databases such as Amazon's DynamoDB [16], Cassandra, or MongoDB can be a good fit.

\begin{itemize}
\item
In this chapter, we use DynamoDB as an example
\item
DynamoDB is a fully managed NoSQL database that offers reliable performance and great scalability
\item
To allow efficient access to data with attributes other than the primary key, we can leverage global secondary indexes [17] in DynamoDB
\item
A global secondary index contains a selection of attributes from the parten table, but they are organized using a different primary key
\end{itemize}

Let's take a look at an example.

The updated system diagram is shown in Figure 10.21. Redis and MySQL are replaced with DynamoDB.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_21}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Assume we design the leaderboard for a chess game and our initial table is shown in Figure 10.22.

It is a denormalized view of the leaderboard and user tablers and contains everything needed to render a leaderboard.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_22}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

This table schema works, but it doesn't scale well. As rows are added, we have to scan the entire table to find the top scores.

To avoid a linear scan, we need to add indexes.

Our first attempt is to use \lstinline|game_name#{year-month}| as the partition key and the score as the sort key, as shown in Figure 10.23.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_23}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

This works, but it runs into issues at a high load.

\begin{itemize}
\item
DynamoDB splits data across multiple nodes using consistent hasing
\item
Each item lives in a corresponding node based on its partition key
\item
We want to structure the data so that data is evenly distributed across partitions
\item
In our table design (Figure 10.23), all the data for the most recent month would be stored in one partition and that partition becomes a hot partition
\item
How can we solve this problem?
\item
We can split data into \lstinline|n| partitions and appened a partition number (\lstinline|user_id % number_of_partitions|) to the partition key
\item
THis pattern is called write sharding
\item
Write sharding adds complexity for both read and write operations, so we should consider the trade-offs carefully
\item
THe second question we need to answer is, how many parittions should we have?
\item
It can be based on write volume or DAU
\item
The important thing to remember is that there is a trade-off between load on partitions and read complexity
\item
Because data for the same month is spread evenly across multiple partitions, the load for a single partition is much lighter
\item
However, to read items for a given month, we have to query all the partitions and merge the results, which adds read complexity
\item
The partition key looks omething like this: \lstinline|game_name#{year-month}#p{partition_number}|
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_24}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The global secondary index uses \lstinline|game_name#{year-month}#p{partition_number}| as the partition key and the score as the sort key.

\begin{itemize}
\item
What we end up with are \lstinline|n| partitions that are all sorted within their own partition (locally sorted)
\item
IF we assume we had 3 partitions, then in order to fetch the top 10 leaderboard, we would use the approach called ``scatter-gather'' mentioned earlier
\item
We would fetch the top 10 results in each of the partitions
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_10_25}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

How do we decide on the number of partitions?

\begin{itemize}
\item
This might require some careful bench marking
\item
More partitions decrease the load on each partition but add complexity, as we need to scatter across more partitions to build the final leaderboard
\item
By employing benchmarking, we can see the trade-off more clearly
\end{itemize}

However, similar to the Redis partition solution mentioned earlier, this approach doesn't provide a striaghtforward solution for determining the relative rank of a user.

\begin{itemize}
\item
But it in possible to get the percentile of a user's position, which could be good neough
\item
In real life, telling a player that they are in the top \lstinline|10 ~ 20%| might be better than showing the exact rank at eg. 1,200,001.
\item
Therefore, if the scale is large enough that we neede dto shard, we could assume that the score distributions are roughly the same across all shards
\item
If this assumption is true, we could have a cron job that analyzes the distribution of the score for each shard, and caches that result
\end{itemize}

The result would look something like this:

\begin{align*}
10\text{th percentile} &= \text{ score} < 100 \\
20\text{th percentile} &= \text{ score} < 500 \\
& \vdots \\
90\text{th percentile} &= \text{ score} < 6500
\end{align*}

Then we could quickly return a user's relative ranking (say 90th percentile).

\subsection{Step 4: Wrap Up}

In this chapter, we have created a solution for building a real-time game leaderboard with the scale of millions of DAU.

\begin{itemize}
\item
We explored the straighforward solution of using a MySQL database and rejected that approach because it does not scale to million of users
\item
We then designed the leaderboard using Redis sorted sets
\item
We also looked int scaling the solution to 500 million DAU, by leveraging sharding across different Redis caches
\item
We also proposed an alternative NoSQL solution
\end{itemize}

In the event you have some extra time at the end of the interview, you can cover a few more topics:

\subsubsection{Faster retrieval and breaking tie}

A Redis Hash provides a map between string fields and values. We could leverage a hash for 2 uses cases

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
To store a map of the user id to the user object that we can display on the leaderboard
\item
This allows for faster retrieval than having to go to the database to fetch the user object
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
In the case of two playershaving the same scores, we could rank the users based on who received that score first
\item
When we increment the score of the user, we can also store a map of the user id to the timestamp of the most recently won game
\item
In the case of a tie, the user with the older timestamp ranks higher
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{System failure recovery}

The Redis cluster can potentially experience a large-scale failure.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Given the design above, we could create a script that leverages the fact that the MySQL database records an entry with a timestamp each time a user won a game.
\item
We could iterate through all of the entires for each user, and call \lstinline|ZINCRBY| once per entry, per user
\item
This would allow us to recreate the leaderboard offline if necessary, in case of a large-scale outage.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{chapter_10_summary}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\chapter*{Chapter 11: Payment System}

In this chapter, we design a payment system.

\begin{itemize}
\item
E-commerce has exploded in popularity across the world in recent years
\item
What makes every transaction possible is a payment system running behind the scenes
\item
A reliable, scalable, and flexcible payment system is essential
\item
What is a payment system?
\item
According to Wikipedia, ``a payment system is any system used to settle financial transactions through the transfer of monetary value''
\item
This includes the institutions, instruments, people, rules, procedures, standards, and technologies that make its exchange possible
\item
A payment system is easy to undertsand on the surface but is also intimidating for many developers to work on
\item
A small slip could potentially cause significant revenue loss and destroy credibility among users
\item
But fear not! In this chapter, we demystify payment systems
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 1: Understand the Problem and Establish Design Scope}

A payment system can mean very different things to different people.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Some may think it's a digital wallet like Apple Pay or Google Pay
\item
Others may think it's a backend system that handles payments such as PayPal or Stripe
\item
It is very important to determine the exact requirements at the beginning of the itnerview
\item
These are some questions you can ask the interviewer
\end{itemize}

\begin{enumerate}
\item
\textbf{Candidate}: What kind of payment system are we building?

\textbf{Interviewer}: Assume you are building a payment backend for an e-commerce application like Amazon.com. When a customer places an order on Amazon.com, the payment system handles everything relatedf to money movement.

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: What payment options are supported? Credit cards, PayPal, bank cards, etc.

\textbf{Interviewer}: The payment system should support all of these optoins in real life. However, in this interview, we can use cred card payment as an example

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Do we handle credit card payment processing ourselves?

\textbf{Interviewer}; No, we use third-party payment processors, such as Stripe, Braintree, Square, etc.

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Do we store credit card data in our system?

\textbf{Interviewer}: Due to extremely high security and compliance requirements, we do not store card numbers direclty in our system

\item

\textbf{Candidate}: Is the application global? Do we need to support different currencies and international payments?

\textbf{Interviewer}: Great question. Yes, the application would be global but we assume only one currency is used in this interview.

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: How many payment trnasactions per day?

\textbf{Interviewer}: 1 million transactions per day.

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Do we need to support the pay-out flow, which an e-commerce site like Amazon uses to pay sellers every month?
\item
\textbf{Interviewer}: Yes, we need to support that

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: I think I gathered all the requirements. I there anything else I should pay attention to?

\textbf{Interviewer}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Yes. A payment system interfacts with a lot of internal services (accounting, analytics, etc.) and external services (payment service providers)
\item
When a service fails, we may see inconsistent states among services
\item
Therefore, we need to perform reconciliation and fix any inconsistencies
\item
This is also a requirement
\item
With these questions, we get a clear picture of both the functional and non-functional requirements
\item
In this interview, we focus on designing a payment system that supports the following.
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Functional requirements}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Pay-in flow: payment system receives money from cusomters on behalf of sellers
\item
Pay-out flow: payment system sends money to sellers around the world
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Non-functional requirements}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Reliability and fault tolerance. Failed payments need to be carefully handled
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
A reconciliation process between internal services (payment systems, accounting systems) and external services (payment service providers) is required
\item
The process asynchronously verfies that the payment information across these systems is consistent
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Back-of-the-envelope estimation}

The system needs to process 1 million transactions per day, which is 1,000,000 trnasactions / $10^5$ seconds = 10 transactions per second (TPS).

10 TPS is not a big number for a typical database, which means the focus of this system design interview is on how to correctly handle payment trnasactions, rahter than aiming for high throughput.

\subsection{Step 2: Propose High-level Design and Get Buy-in}

At a high level, the payment flow is broken down in to two steps to reflect how money flows

\begin{itemize}
\item
Pay-in flow
\item
Pay-out flow
\end{itemize}

Take the e-commerce site, Amazon, as an example.

\begin{itemize}
\item
After a buyer places an order, the money flows into Amazon's bank account, Amazon does not own all of the money
\item
THe seller owns a substraintial part of it and Amazon only works as the money customdian for a fee
\item
Later, when the products are delivered and money is released, the balance after fees then flows from Amazon's bank account to the seller's bank account
\item
This is the pay-out flow
\item
The simplified pay-in and pay-out flows are shown in Figure 11.1
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_11_1}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Pay-in flow}

The high-level design diagram for the pay-in flow is shown in Figure 11.2. Let's take a look at each component of the system.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_11_2}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Payment service}

The payment service accepts payment events from users and coordinates the payment process.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The first think it usually does is a reisk check, assessing for compliance with regulartions such as AML/CFT [2], and fo revidence of criminal activity such as money laundering or financing of terrorism.
\item
Usually, the risk check service uses a third-party provider because it is very complicated and highly specialized
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Payment executor}

The payment executor executes a single payment order via a Payment Service Provider (PSP). A payment event may contain several payment orders.

\subsubsection{Payment Service Provider (PSP)}

A PSP moves money from account A to account B. In this simplified example, the PSP moves the money out of the byer's credit card account.

\subsubsection{Card schemas}

Card schemes are the orgnaizations that process credit card operations. Well known card schemes are Visa, MasterCard, Discovery, etc. The card scheme ecosystems is very complex. [3]

\subsubsection{Ledger}

The ledger keeps a financial record of the payment transaction.

\begin{itemize}
\item
For example, when a user pays the seller \$1, we record it as debit \$1 from the user and credit \$1 to the seller
\item
The ledger system is very important in post-payment analysis, such as calculating the total revenue of the e-commerce website or forecastin future revenue
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Wallet}

The wallet keeps the account balance of the merchant. It may also record how much a given user has paid in total.

A shown in Figure 11.2, a typical pay-in flow works like this:

\begin{enumerate}
\item
When a user clicks the ``place order'' button , a payment event is generated and sent to the payment service.

\item
The payment services stores the payment event in the database
\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
Sometimes, a single apyment event may contain several payment orders
\item
For example, you may select products from multiple sellers in a single checkout process
\item
If the e-commerce website splits the checkout into multiple payment orders, the payment service calls the payment executor for each payment order
\end{itemize}
\item
The payment executor stores the payment order in the database
\item
The payment executor calls an external PSP to process the credit card payment
\item
After the payment executor has successfully processed the payment, the payment service updates the wallet to record how much meny a given seller has
\item
The wallet server stores the updated balance information in the database
\item
After wallet service has successfully updated the seller's balance information, the payment service calls the ledger to update it
\item
The ledger service appends the new ledger information to the database
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{API for payment service}

We use the RESTful API design convention for the payment service.

\subsubsection{POST /v1/payments}

This endpoint executes a payment event. As mentioned above, a asingle payment event may contain multiple payment orders. The request parameters are listed below:

\begin{tabular}{|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|Field| & \lstinline|Description| & \lstinline|Type| \\
\hline
\lstinline|buyer_info| & The information of the buyer & json \\
\hline
\lstinline|checkout_id| & A globally unique ID for this checkout & string \\
\hline
\lstinline|credit_card_info| & This could be encrypted credit card information or a payment token. The value is PSP-specific & JSON \\
\hline
\lstinline|payment_orders| & A list of the payment orders & list \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 11.1: API request parameters (execute a payment event)

The \lstinline|payment_orders| look like this:

\begin{tabular}{|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|Field| & \lstinline|Description| & \lstinline|Type|  \\
\hline
\lstinline|seller_account| & Which seller will receive the money & string  \\
\hline
\lstinline|amount| & The transaction amount for the order & string  \\
\hline
\lstinline|currency| & The currency for the order & string (ISO 4217 [4])  \\
\hline
\lstinline|payment_order_id| & A globally unique ID for this payment & string \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 11.2: \lstinline|payment_orders|

NOte that the \lstinline|payment_order_id| is globally unique. When the payment executor sends a payment request  to a third-party PSP, the \lstinline|payment_order_id| is used by the PSP as the deduplication ID, also called the idempotency key.

You may have noticed that the data type of the ``amount'' field is ``string'' rather than ``double''. Double is not a good choice because

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Different protocols, software, and hardware may support different numeric precisions in serialization and deserialization
\item
This difference might cause unintended rounding errors
\end{itemize}
\item

The number could be extremely big (for example, Japan's GDP is around $5 \times 10^{14}$ yen for the calendar year 2020), or extremely small (for example, a satoshi of Bitcoin is $10^{-8}$)
\end{enumerate}

It is recommended to keep numbers in string format during transmission and storage.

They are only parsed to numbers when used for display or calculation.

\subsubsection{GET /v1/payments/{:id}}

This endpoint returns the execution status of a single payment order based on \lstinline|payment_order_id|.

The payment API mentioned above is simlar to the API of some well-known PSPs.

If you are interested in a more compreshensive view of payment APIs, checkout Stripe's API documentation [5]

\subsection{The data model for payment service}

We need two tables for the payment service: payment event and payment order. WHen we select a storage solution for a payment system, performance is usually not the most important factor. Instead, we focus on the following:

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Proven stability.
\item
Whether the storage system has been used by other big financial firms for many years (for example more than 5 years) with positive feedback
\item
The richness of supporting tools, such as monitoring and investigation tools
\item
Maturity of the database administrator (DBA) job marktet. Whether we can recruit experienced DBAs is a very important factor to consider
\item
Usually, we prefer a traditional relational database with ACID transaction support over NoSQL/NewSQL
\end{itemize}

The payment event table contains detailed payment event information. This is what it looks like:

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|Name| & \lstinline|Type| \\
\hline
\lstinline|checkout| & string PK \\
\hline
\lstinline|buyer_info| & string \\
\hline
\lstinline|seller_info| & string \\
\hline
\lstinline|credit_card_info| & depends on the card provider \\
\hline
\lstinline|is_payment_done| & boolean \\
\end{tabular}

Table 11.3: Payment event

The payment order table stores the xecution status of each payment order. This is what it looks like:

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|Name| & \lstinline|Type| \\
\hline
\lstinline|payment_order_id| & \lstinline|String| PK \\
\hline
\lstinline|buyer_account| & \lstinline|string| \\
\hline
\lstinline|amount| & \lstinline|string| \\
\hline
\lstinline|currency| & \lstinline|string| \\
\hline
\lstinline|checkout_id| & \lstinline|string| FK \\
\hline
\lstinline|payment_order_status| & \lstinline|string| \\
\hline
\lstinline|ledger_updated| & \lstinline|boolean| \\
\hline
\lstinline|wallet_updated| & \lstinline|boolean| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 11.4: Payment order
\end{enumerate}

Before we deive into the tables, let's take a look at some background information.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The \lstinline|checkout_id| is the foreign key. A single checkout creates a payment event that may contain several payment orders
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
When we call a third-party PSP to deduct money from the buyer's credit card, the money is not directly transferred to the seller
\item
TInstead, the money is transferred to the e-commerce website's bank account
\item
This process is called pay-in
\item
When the payout condition is satisfied, such as when the products are delivered, the seller initiates a pay-out
\item
Only then is the money transferred from the e-commerce website's bank account to the seller's bank account
\item
Therefore, during the pay-in flow, we only need the buyer's card information, not the seller's bank account information
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

I nt eh appyment order table (Table 11.4), \lstinline|payment_order_status| is an enumerated type (enum) that keeps the execution status of the payment order.

Execution status includes \lstinline|NOT_STARTED|, \lstinline|EXECUTING|, \lstinline|SUCCESS|, \lstinline|FAILED|.

The update logic is:

\begin{enumerate}
\item
The initial status of \lstinline|payment_order_status| is \lstinline|NOT_STARTED|
\item
When the payment esrvice sends the payment order to the payment executor, the \lstinline|payment_order_status| is \lstinline|EXECUTING|
\item
THe payment service updates the \lstinline|payment_order_status| to \lstinline|SUCCESS| or \lstinline|FAILED| depending on the response of the payment executor
\end{enumerate}

Once the \lstinline|payment_order_status| is \lstinline|SUCCESS|, the payment service calls the wallet service to update the seller balance and update the \lstinline|wallet_updated| field to \lstinline|TRUE|

\begin{itemize}
\item
Here we simplify the design by assuming wallet updates always succeed.
\item
Once it is done, the enxt step for the payment service is to call the ledger service to update the ledger database by updating the \lstinline|ledger_updated| field to \lstinline|TRUE|
\item
When all payment orders under the same \lstinline|checkout_id| are processed successfully, the payment service updates the \lstinline|is_payment_done| to \lstinline|TRUE| in the payment event table.
\item
A scheduled job usually runs at a fixed interval to monitor the status of the in-flight payment orders
\item
It sends an alert when a payment order does not fnish within a threshold so that enineers can investigate it.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Double-entry leedger system}

There is a very important design principle in the ledger system: the double-entry principle (also called double-entry accounting/bookkeeping [6]).

\begin{itemize}
\item
Double-entry system is fundamental to any payment system and is key to accurate bookkeeping
\item
It records every payment transaction into two separate ledger accounts with the same amount
\item
One account is debited and the other is credited with the same amount (Table 11.5)
\end{itemize}

\begin{tabular}{|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|}
\hline
Account & Debit & Credit  \\
\hline
buyer & \$1 &   \\
\hline
seller & & \$1  \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

The double entry system states that the sum of all the transaction entires must be 0.

\begin{itemize}
\item
