One cent lost means someone else gains a cent
\item
It provides end-to-end traceability and ensures consistency throughout the payment cycle
\item
To find out more about implemnting the double-entry system, see Square's engineering blog about immutable double-entry accounting database service [7]
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Hosted payments page}

Most companies prefer not to store credit card information internally because if they do, they have to deal with complex regulartions such as Payment Card Industry Data Security Standard (PCI DSS) [8] in the United States.

\begin{itemize}
\item
To avoid handling credit card information, copmnaies use hosted cred card pages provided by PSPs.
\item
For websites, it is a widget or an iframe, while for mbile applications, it may be a pre-built page from the payment SDK.
\item
Figure 11.3 illustrates an example of the checkout experience with PayPal integration
\item
The key point here is that the PSP provides a hosted payment page that captures the customer card information directly, rather than relying on our payment service.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_11_3}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Pay-out flow}

The components of the pay-out flow are very similar to the pay-in flow.

\begin{itemize}
\item
One difference is that instead of using PSP to move money from the byer's credit card to the e-commerce website's bank account, the pay-out flow uses a third-party pay-out provider to move money from the e-commerce website's bank account to the sellers bank account
\item
Usually the payment system uses third-party account payable providers like Tipalti [9] to handle pay-outs
\item
There are a lot of bookkeeping and regulatory requirements with pay-outs as well
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 3: Design Deep Dive}

In this section, we focus on making the system faster, more robust, and secure

\begin{itemize}
\item
In a distributed system, errors and failures are not only inevitable but common
\item
For example, what happens if a customer pressed the ``pay'' button multiple times?
\item
Will they be charged multiple times?
\item
How do we handle payment failures caused by poor network connections?
\item
How do we handle payment failures caused by poor network connections?
\end{itemize}

In this section, we dive deep into several key topics.

\begin{itemize}
\item
PSP integration
\item
Reconciliation
\item
Handling payment processing delays
\item
Communication among internal services
\item
Handling failed payments
\item
Exact-once delivery
\item
Consistency
\item
Security
\end{itemize}

\subsection{PSP integration}

If the payment system can direclty connect to banks or card schemes such as Bisa or MasterCard, payment can be made without PSP.

These direction necttions are uncommon and highly specialized.

THey are usually reserved for really large compnaies that can justify such an investment.

For most compnaies, the paymenbt system integrates with a PSP instead, in one of two ways:

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
If a company can safely store sensitive payment information and chooses to do so, PSP can be integrated using API
\item
The companis responsible for developing the payment web pages, collecting and storign sensitive payment information
\item
PSP is responsible for connecting to banks or card schemes
\end{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
If a company chooses not to store sensitive payment information due to complex regulations and security concerns, PSP provides a hosted payment page to collect card payment details and securely store them in PSP
\item
This is the approach most companies take
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

We use Figure 11.4 to explain how the hosted payment page works in detail.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_11_4}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

We omitted the payment executor, ledger, and wallet in Figure 11.4 for simplicity. The payment service orchestrates the whole payment process.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
The user clicks the ``checkout'' button in the client browser. The client calls the payment service with the payment order information.

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
AFter receiving the payment order information, the payment service sends a payment registration request to the PSP
\item
This registration request contains payment information, such as the amount, currency, expriation data of the payment request, and the redirect URL
\item
Because a payment order should be registered only once, there is a UUID field to ensure the exactly-once registration
\item
This UUID is also called nonce [10]
\item
Usually, this UUID is the ID of the payment order
\end{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The PSP returns a token back to the payment service
\item
A token is a UUID on the PSP side that uniquely identifies the payment
\end{itemize}

\item
The payment service stores the token in the database before calling the PSP-hosted payment page

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Once the token is persisted, the client displays a PSP-hosted payment page
\item
Mobile applications usually use the PSP's SDK integration for this functionality
\item
Here we use Stripe's web integration as an example (Figure 11.5)
\item
STripe provides a JavaScript library that displays the payment UI, collects sensitive payment information, and calls the PSP directly to complete the payment
\item
Snesitive payment information is collected by STripe
\item
It never reaches our payment system
\item
The hosted payment page usually needs two pieces of information:

\begin{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The token we received in step 4
\item
The PSP's javascript code uses the token to retrieve detailed information about the payment request from the PSP's backend
\item
One important piece of information is how much money to collect
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Another important piece of information is the redirect URL
\item
This is the web page URL that is called when the payment is complete
\item
When the PSP's JavScript finishes the payment, it redirects the browser to the redirect URL
\item
Usually, the redirect URL is an e-commerce web page that shows the status of the checkout
\item
Note that the redirect URL is different from the webhook [11] URL in step 9
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The user fills in the payment details on the PSP's web page, such as the credit card number, holder's name, expiration date, etc, then clicks the pay button
\item
The PSP starts the payment processing
\end{itemize}

\item
The PSP returns the payment status
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The web page is now redirected to the redirect URL
\item
THe payment status that is received in step 7 is typically appended to the URL
\item
For example, the full redirect URL could be [12]: \lstinline|https://your-compnay.com/?tokenID-JIOUIQ123NSF&payResult=X32FSa|
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Asynchronously, the PSP calls the payment service witht eh paymentstatus via a webhook
\item
The webhook is an URL on the payment system side that was registered with the PSP during the initla setup with the PSP
\item
When the pyament system receives payment events throught ht webhook,it extracts the payment status and updates the \lstinline|payment_order_status| field in the Pyament Order database table
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

So far, we explained the happy path of the hosted payment page.

In reality, the network connection could be unrealible and all 9 steps above could fail.

Is there any systematic way to handle failure cases? The answer is reconciliation.

\subsubsection{Reconciliation}

When system components communicate aysnchronously, there is no guarantee that a message will be dlivered or a response will be returned.

\begin{itemize}
\item
This is very common in the payment business, which often uses asynchronous communication to increase system performance
\item
External systems, such as PSPs or banks, prefer asynchronous communication as well
\item
So how can we esnure correctness in this case?
\item
The answer is reconciliation
\item
This is a practice that perioidcally compares the states among related services in order
\item
It is usually the last line of defense in the payment system
\item
Every night the PSP or banks send a settlement file to their clients
\item
The settlement file contains the balance of the bank account, together with all the transactions that took place on this bank account during the day
\item
The reconciliation system parses the settlement file and compares the details with the ledger system
\item
Figure 11.6 below shows where the reconciliation process fits in the system.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_11_6}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Reconciliation is also used to verify that the payment system is internally consistent.

\begin{itemize}
\item
For example, the states in the ledger and wallet might diverge and we could use the reconciliatoin system to detect any discrepancy
\item
This fix mismatches found during reconciliation, we usually rely on the finance team to perform manual adjustments
\end{itemize}

The mismatches and adjustments are usually classified onto three categories:

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The mismatch is classifiable and the adjustment can be automated
\item
In this case, we know the cause of the mismatch, how to fix it, and it is cost-effective to write a program to automate the adjustment
\item
Engineers can automate both the mismatch classification and adjustment
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
This mis match is classfiable, but we are unable to automate the adjustment
\item
In this case, we know the cause of the mismatch and how to fix it, but the cost of writing an auto adjustment program is too high
\item
The mismatch is put into a job queue and the finance team fixes the mismatch manually
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The mismatch is unclassifiable
\item
In this case, we do not know how the mismatch happens
\item
The mismatch is put into a special job queue
\item
The finance team investigates it manually
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Handling payment processing delays}

As discussed previously, an end-to end payment request flows through many components and involes both internal and external parties.

While in most cases , a payment request would complete in seconds there are siutaitons where a pyamnent request would stall and somtimes take hours or days before it is completed or rejected.

Here are some examples where a payment request could take longer than usual:

\begin{itemize}
\item
The PSP deems a payment request high risk and requires a human to review it
\item
A credit card requires extra protection like 3D Secure Authentication [13] which requires extra details from a card holder to verify a purchase
\end{itemize}

The payment service must be able to handle these payment requests that take a long time to process.

If the buy page is hosted by an external PSP, which is quite common these days, the PSP would hanel these long-running payment requests in the following ways:

\begin{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
THe PSP would return a pending status to our client
\item
OUr client would display that to the user
\item
Our client would also provide a page for the customer to check the current payment status
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
The PSP tracks the pending payment on our behalf, and notifies the payment service of any status update via the webhook the payment service registered with the PSP
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

When the payment request is finally completed, the PSP calls the registered webhook mentioned above.

The payment service updates its internal system and completes the shipment to the customer.

Alternatively, instead of updating the payment service via a webhook, some PSP would put the burdern on the payment service to poll the PSP for status updates on any pending payment requests.

\subsection{Communication among internal services}

There are two types of communication patterns that internal services use to communicate: synchronous vs asynchronous. Both are explained below:

\subsection{Synchronous communication}

Synchronous communication like HTTP works well for small-scale systems, but its shortcomings become obvious as the scale increases.

It creates a long request and response cycle that depends on many services.

The drawbacks of this approach are:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Low performance. If any one of the services in the chian doesn't perform well, the whole system is impacted
\item
Por failure isolation. If PSPs or any other services fail, the client will no longer receive a response
\item
Tight coupling. THe request sender needs to know the recipient
\item
Hard to scale. Without using a queue to act as a buffer, it's not easy to scale the system to support a sudden increase in traffic
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Asynchronous communication}

Asynchronous communication can be divided into two categories:

\begin{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Single receiver: each request (message) is processed by one receiver or service

\item
The message queue can have multiple subscribers, but once a message is processed, it gets removed form the queue
\item
Let's take a look at a concreate example
\item
In Figure 11.7, service A and service B both subscribe to a shared message queue
\item
When m1 and m2 are consumed by service A and service B respectively, both messages are  removed from the queue as shown in Figure 11.8.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_11_8}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Multiple receivers: each request (message) is processed by multiple receivers or services
\item
Kafka works well here
\item
When consumers receive messages, they are not removed from Kafka
\item
THis model maps well to the payment system, as the same request might trigger multiple side effects such as sending push notifications, updating financial reporting analytics, etc.
\item
An exmpale is illustrated in Figure 11.9
\item
Payment events are published to Kafka and consumed by different services such as the payment system, analytics service, and billing service.
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_11_9}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Generally speaking, synchronous communication is simpler in design, but it doesn't alllow services to be autonomous.

\begin{itemize}
\item
As the dependency grah grows, the overall performance suffers
\item
Aysnchronous communication trades design simplicity and consistency for scalability and afilure resilience
\item
For a large-scale payment system with complex business logic and a large number of third-party dependencies, asynchronous commnication is a better choice
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Handling failed payments}

Every payment system has to handle failed transactions. Reliability and fault tolerance are key requirements. We review some of teh techniques for taling those challenges.

\subsection{Tracking payment state}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Having a definittive payment state any any state of the payment cycle is crucial
\item
Whenever a fialure happens, we can determine the current state of a payment transaction and decide whether a retry or refund is needed
\item
The payment state can be persisted in an append only database table
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Retry queue and dead letter queue}

To gracefuly handle failures, we utilize the retry queue and dead letter queue, as shown in Figure 11.10.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Retry queue: retryable error such as transient errors are routed to a retry queue
\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
Dead letter queue [14]: if a message fails repeatedly, it eventually lands in the dead letter queue
\item
A dead letter queue is useful for debugging and isolatin problematic messages for inspection to determine why they were not processed successfully
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_11_10}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{enumerate}
\item
Check whether the failure is retryable
\begin{itemize}
\item
Retryable failures are routed to a retry queue
\item
FOr non-retryable failures such as invalid input, errors are stored in a database
\end{itemize}

\item
The payment system consumes events fromt eh retry queue and retries failed payment transactions
\item
If the payment transaction fails again:

\begin{itemize}
\item
If the retry count doesn't execeed the threshold, the event is routed to the retry queue
\item
If the retry coutn exceeds the threshold, the event is put in the dead letter queue. THose failed events might need to be investigated.
\end{itemize}

\item
The payment system consumes events from the retry queue and retries failed payment transactions
\item
If the payment transaction fails again:

\begin{itemize}
\item
If the retry count doesn't exceed the threshold, the vent is routed to the retry queue
\item
If the retry count exceeds the threshold, the event is put in the dead letter queue. Those failed events might need to be investigated
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

If you are interested in a real-world example of using those queues, take a look at Uber's payment system that utilizes Kafka to meet the reliability and fault-tolerance requirements [15].

\subsubsection{Exacty-once delivery}

One of the most serious problems a payment system can have is to double charge a customer.

It is important to guarantee in our design that the payment system executes a payment order exactly-once [16].

A first glance, exactly-once delivery seems very hard to tackle, but if we divide the problem into two parts, it is much easier to solve.

Mathematically, an operation is executed exactly-once if:

\begin{enumerate}
\item
It is executed at-least-once
\item
At the same time, it is executed at-most-once
\end{enumerate}

We will explain how to implement at-least-once using retry,m and at-most-once using idempotency check.

\subsection{Retry}

Occasionally, we need to retry a payment transaction due to network errors or timeout.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Retry provides the at-least-once guarantee
\item
For example, as shown in Figure 11.11, where the client tries to make a \$10 payment, but the payment request keeps failing due to a poor network conneciton
\item
In this example, the network eventually recovered and the request succeeded at the fourth attempt.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_11_11}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Deciding the appropriate time intervals betwen retries is important. Here are a few common retry stategies.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Immediate retry: client immediately resends a request
\item
Fixed intervals: wait a fixed amount of time between the time of the failed payment and a new retry attempt
\item
Incremental intervals: client wais tfor a short time for the first retry, adn the icnrementally cinreases the time for subsequent writes
\item
Exponential backoff [17]:

\begin{itemize}
\item
double the waiting time between retries after each failed retry
\item
For example, when a request fails for the first time, we retry after 1 second; if it fails a second time, we wait 2 seconds before the enxt retry; if it fails a third tiem, we wait 4 secodjns before another retry
\end{itemize}
\item
Cancel: the client can cancel the request. This is a common practice when the failure is permanent or repeated requests are unilkey to be successful
\end{itemize}

Determining the appropriate retry strategy is difficult.

\begin{itemize}
\item
There is no ``one size fits all'' solution
\item
As a general guideline, use expotentnial backoff if the network issue is unlikely to be resolved in a short amount of time
\item
Overly aggressive retry strategies waste computing resources and can cause service overload
\item
A good practice is to provide an error code with a \lstinline|Retry-After| header.
\item
A potential problem of retrying is double pyament. Let us take a look at two scenarios.
\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}
\item
\textbf{Scenario 1}: The payment system integrates with PSP using a hosted payment page, and the client cliks the pay button twice.

\textbf{Scenario 2}: The payment is successfully processed by the PSP, but the response fails to reach our payment system due to network errors. THe user clicks the ``pay'' button again or the client retries the payment.

In order to avoid double payment, the payment has to be executed at-most-once.

This at-most-once guarantee is also called idempotency.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Idempotency}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Idempotency is key to ensuring the at-most-once guarantee
\item
According to Wikpedia, ``idempotence is the property of certain operations in mathematics and computer science where by they can be applied multiple times without chaning the result beyond the initial application'' [18].
\item
From an API standpoint, idempotency means clients can make the same call repeatedly and produce the same result.
\item
For communication between clients (web and mobile applications) and servers, an idempotency key is usually a unique value that is generated by the client and expires after a certain period of time.
\item
A UUID is commonly used as an idempotency key and it is recommended by many tech companies such as Stripe [19] and Paypal [20].
\item
To perform an idempotent payment request, an idempotency key is added ot the HTTP header: \lstinline|<idempotency-key: key_value>|
\item
Now that we undertsand the basics of idempotency, let's take a look at how it helps to solve the double payment issues mentioned above
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Scenario 1: what if a customer cliks the ``pay'' button quickly twice?}

In Figure 11.12, when a user clicks ``pay'', an idempotency key is sent to the pyament system as part of the HTTP request.

\begin{itemize}
\item
In an e-commerce website, the idempotency key is usually the ID of the shopping cart right before the checkout
\item
For the second request, it's treated as a retry because the payment has already seen the idempotency key
\item
When we include a previously specified idempotency key in the request header, the payment system returns the latest status of the previous request
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_11_12}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

If multiple concurrent requests are detected with the same idempotency key, only one request is processed and the others receive the \lstinline|429 Too Many Requests| status code.

TO support idempotency, we can use the database's unique key constraint. For example, the primary key of the databvase is served as the idempotency key. Here is how it works:

\begin{enumerate}
\item
When the payment system receives a payment, it tries to insert a row into the database table
\item
A successful insertion means we have not seen this payment request before
\item
If the insertion fails because the same primary key already exists, it means we have seen this payment request before. The second request will not bbe processed
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Scenario 2: The payment is successfully processed by the PSP, but the response fails to reach our payment system  due to network errors. THen the user clicks the ``pay'' button again}

\begin{itemize}
\item
As shown in Figure 11.4 (step 2 and step 3), the payment service sends the PSP a nonce and the PSP returns a correspondign token
\item
The nonce uniuqley represents the payment order, and the token uniquely maps to the nonce
\item
Therefore, the token uniquely maps to the payment order
\item
When the user clicks the ``pay'' button again, the payment order is the same, so the token sent to the PSP is the same
\item
Because the token is used as the idempotency key on the PSP side, it is able to identify the double payment and return the status of the previous execution
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Consistency}

\begin{enumerate}
\item
The payment service keeps payment-related data such as nonce, token, payment order, execution status, etc.
\item
The ledger keeps all acounting data
\item
THe wallet keeps the account balance of the merchant
\item
THe PSP keeps the payment execution status
\item
Data might be replicated among different database replicas to increase reliability
\end{enumerate}

In a distributed environment, the communication between any two services can fail, causing data inconsistency, Let's take a look at some tehcnique to resolve data inconsitency in a payment system.

To maintain data consistency between internal services, ensuring exactly-once processing is very important.

\begin{itemize}
\item
To maintain data consistency between the interal service and external service (PSP), we usually rely on idemptoency and reconciliation
\item
If the external serfvice supports idempotency, we should use the same idempotency key for payment rety operations
\item
Even if an external service supports idempotent APIs, reconciliation si stil needed because we shouldn't assume the external system is always right
\end{itemize}

If data is replicated, replication lag could cause inconsisten t data between the primary database and the replicas, There are generally two options to solve this:

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Serve both reads and writes from the primary database only
\item
This approach is easy to setup, but the obvious drawback is scalability
\item
Replicas are used to ensure data reliability, but they don't serve any traffic, which wastes resources
\end{itemize}

\item
\begin{itemize}
\item
Ensure all replicas are always in-sync
\item
We could use consensus algorithms such as Paxos [21] and Raft [22], or use consesnsus-based distributed databases such as YugabyteDB [23] or CockroachDB [24]
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Payment security}

Payment security is very important. In the final part of this system design, we briefly cover a few techniques for combating cyber attacks and card thefts.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{|p{0.32\textwidth}|X|}
\hline
\textbf{Problem} & \textbf{Solution} \\
\hline
Request/response eavesdropping & Use HTTPS \\
\hline
Data tampering & Enforce encryption and integrity monitoring \\
\hline
Man-in-the-middle attack & Use SSL with certificate pinning \\
\hline
Data loss & Database replication across multiple regions and take snapshots of data \\
\hline
Distributed denial-of-service attack (DDoS) & Rate limiting and firewall [25] \\
\hline
Card theft & Tokenization. Instead of using real card numbers, tokens are stored and used for payment \\
\hline
PCI compliance & PCI DSS is an information security standard for organizations that handle branded credit cards \\
\hline
Fraud & Address verification, card verification value (CVV), user behavior analysis, etc. [26] [27] \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\caption{Payment security}
\label{tab:payment-security}
\end{table}

We went into great deptabout retry, idempotency, and consistency

\item
A payment system is extrenely complex
\item
Event hough we have covered many topics there are still more worth mentioning
\end{itemize}

The following is a representative but not an exhaustive list of relevant topics:

\begin{itemize}

Monitoring:
\begin{itemize}
\item
Monitoring key metrics is a critical part of any modern application
\item
With extensive monitoring, we can answer questions like ``What is the average acceptance rate for a specific payment method?'', `What is the CPU usage of our servers?`, etc.
\item
We can create and display those metrics on a dashboard.
\end{itemize}

\item
Alerting. When something abnormal occurs, it is important to alert on-calll developers so they respond promptly
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Debugging tools. ``Why does a payment fail?" is a common question
\item
To make debugging easier for engineers and customer support, it is important to develop tools that allow staff to review the transaction status, processing server hisotry, PSP records, etc. of a payment transaction.
\item
Currency exchange. Currency exchange is an importanta consideration when designing a payment system for an international user base
\item
Geography. Different regions might have completely different sets of payment methods
\item
Cash payment. Cash payment is very common in India, Brazil, and some other ocuntires. Uber [28] and Airbnb [29] wrote detailed engineering blogs about how they handled cash-based payment
\item
Google/Apple pay integration. PLease read [30] if interested
