\chapter*{Digital Wallet}

Payment platforms usually provide a digital wallet service to clients. so they can store money in the wallet and spend it later.

For example, you can add money to your digital wallet from your bank card and when you buy products online, you are given the option to pay using the money in your wallet

Figure 12.1 shows this process.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_1}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Spending money is not the only feature that the digital wallet provides.

\begin{itemize}
\item
For a payment platform like PayPal, we can directly transfer money to somebody ele's wallet on the same payment platform
\item
Compared with the bank-to-bank transfer, direct transfer between digital wallets is fater, and most imporantly, it usually odes not charge an extra fee
\item
Figure 12.2 shows a cross-wallet balance transfer operation
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_2}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Suppose we are asked to design the backend of a digital wallet application that supports.

At the beginning of the interview, we will ask clarification questions to nail down the requirements.

Suppose we are asked to design the backend of a digital wallet application that supports the cross-wallet balance transfer operation. At the beginning ot he interview, we will ask clarification questions to nail down the requirements.

\subsection{Step 1: Understand the Problem and Establish Design Scope}

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\textbf{Candidate}: Should we only focus on balance transfer operations between two digital wallets? DO we need to worry about other features?

\textbf{Interviewer}: Let's focus on balance transfer operations only.
\item
\textbf{Candidate}: How many transactions per second (TPS) does the system need to support?

\textbf{Interviewer}: Let's assume 1,000,000 TPS.
\item
\textbf{Candidate}: A digital wallet has strict requirements for correctness. Can we assume transactional guarantees [1] are sufficient?

\textbf{Interviewer}: That sounds good.

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Do we need to prove correctness?

\textbf{Interviewer}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
This is a good question.
\item
Correctness is uslaly only verifiable after a transaction is complete
\item
One way to verify is to compare our internal records with statements from banks
\item
The limitation of reconciliation is that it only shows discrepanices and cannot tell how a difference was generated
\item
Therefore, we woud like to design a system with reproducibility, meaning we could always reconstruct historical balance by replaying the data from the very beginning
\end{itemize}

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Can we assume the availability requirement is 99.99\%.

\textbf{Interviewer}: Sounds good.
\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Do we need to take foreign exchange into consideration?

\textbf{Interviewer}: No, it's out of scope.
\end{enumerate}

In summary, our digital wallet needs to support the following:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Support balance transfer operation between two digital wallets
\item
Support 1,000,000 TPS
\item
Reliability is at least 99.99\%
\item
Support transactions
\item
Support reproducibility
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Back-of-the-envelope estimation}

When we talk about TPS, we imply a transactional datbase will be used.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Today, a relational database running on a typical data center node can support a few thousand transactions per second
\item
For example, reference [2] contains the performance benchmark of some of the popular transactional database servers
\item
Let's assume a datbase node can support 1,000 TPS
\item
In order to reach 1 million TPS, we need 1000 database nodes
\end{itemize}

However, this calculation is slightly inaccurate.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Each transfer command requires two operations: deducting moeny from one account and despositing money to the other account
\item
To support 1 million transfers per second, the system actually needs to handle up to 2 million TPS, which means we need 2000 nodes
\end{itemize}

Table 12.1 shows the total number of nodes required when the ``per-node TPS'' (the TPS a single node can handle) changes.

Assuming hardware remains the same, the mroe transactions a single node can handle per second., the lower the total number of nodes required, indicating lower hardware cost

So one of our design goals is to increase the number of transactions a single node can handle.

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|Per-node TPS| & \lstinline|Node Number| \\
\hline
\lstinline|100| & \lstinline|20,000| \\
\hline
\lstinline|10,000| & \lstinline|200| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 12.1: Mapping between pre-node TPS and node number

\subsection{Step 2: Propose High-level DEsign and Get Buy-in}

In this section, we will discuss the following:

\begin{itemize}
\item
API design
\item
Three high-level designs

\begin{enumerate}
\item
Simple in-memory solution
\item
Database-based distributed transaction solution
\item
Event sourcing solution with reproducibility
\end{enumerate}
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{API design}

We will use the RESTful API convention. For this interview, we only need to support one API:

\begin{tabular}{|p{6cm}|p{6cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|POST /v1/wallet/balance_transfer| & Transfer balance from one wallet to another \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Request parameters are:

\begin{tabular}{|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|}
\hline
\lstinline|Field| & \lstinline|Description|  & \lstinline|Type| \\
\hline
\lstinline|from_account| & The debit account  & string \\
\hline
\lstinline|to_account| & The credit account  & string \\
\hline
\lstinline|amount| & The amount of money  & string \\
\hline
\lstinline|currency| & The currency type & string (ISO 4217 [3]) \\
\hline
\lstinline|transaction_id| & ID used for deduplication & uuid \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Sample response body:

\begin{lstlisting}
{
  "Status": "success",
  "transaction_id": "81589980-2664-11ec-9621-8242ac130002"
}
\end{lstlisting}

One thing worth mentioning is that the data type of the ``amount'' field is ``string'' rather than ``double''.

We explained the reasoning in Chapter 11 Payment System on page 320.

In practice, many people still choose float or doubl erepresentation of numbers because it is supported by almost every programming language and database.

It is a proper choice as long as we understand the potential risk of losing precision.

\subsection{In-memory sharding solution}

THe wallet application maintains an account balance for every user account.

\begin{itemize}
\item
A good data structure to represent this \lstinline|<user, balance>| relationship is a map, which is also called a hash table (map) or key-value store
\item
For in-memory stores, on e popular choice is Redis
\item
One Redis node is not enough to handle 1 million TPS
\item
We need to setup a cluster of Redis nodes and evenly distribute user accounts among them
\item
This process is called partitioning or sharding
\item
To distribute the key-value data among \lstinline|n| partitions, we could calculate the hash value of the key, and divide it by \lstinline|n|
\item
The remainder is the desitnation of the partition
\end{itemize}

The pseudocode below shows the sharding process:

\begin{lstlisting}
String accountID = "A";
Int partition = 7;
Int myPartition = accountID.hashCOde() % partitionNUmber;
\end{lstlisting}

The number of partitions nad addresses of all Redis nodes can be stored in a centralized place.

We could use ZooKeeper [4] as a highly-avaiable configuration stroage solution.

The final component of this solution is a service tha handles the transfer commands. We call ti the wallet service and it has several key responsibilites.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
Receives the transfer command
\item
Validates the transfer command
\item
If the command is valid, it updates the account balances for the two users invovled in the transfer. In a cluster, the account balances are likely to be in different Redis nodes
\end{enumerate}

The waaet service is statless. It is easy to scale horizontally. FIgure 12.3 shows the in-memory solution.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_3}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

In this example, we have 3 Redis nodes.

\begin{itemize}
\item
There are three clients, A, B, and C.
\item
Thie raccount balances are evenly spread across these three Redis nodes
\item
There are two wallet services nodes in this example that handle the balance transfer requests
\item
When one of the wallet services nodes receives the transfer command which is to move \$1 from client A to client B, it issues two commands to two Redis nodes
\item
For the Redis node that contains client A's account, the wallet services deducts \$ from the account
\item
For client B, the wallet service adds \$1 to the account
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Candidate}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
In this design, account balances are spread across multiple Redis nodes
\item
ZooKepper is used to maintain the sharding information
\item
The stateless wallet services uses the sharding information to locate the Redis nodes for the clients and updates the account balances accordingly

\textbf{Interviewer}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
This design works, but it does not meet our correcness requirement
\item
THe wallet services updates two Redis nodes for each transfer
\item
There is no guarantee that both updates would succeed
\item
If, for example, the wallet service node crashes after the first update has gone through but before the second update is done, it would result in an incomplete transfer
\item
THe two updates need to be in a single atomic transaction
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Distributed transactions}

\subsubsection{Database sharding}

How do we make the updates to two different storage nodes atomic?

\begin{itemize}
\item
The first step is to replace each Redis node with a transactional relational database node
\item
The first step is to replace each Redis node with a transactional relational database node
\item
Figure 12.4 shows the architecture
\item
This time, clients A, B, and C are partitioned into 3 relational databases, other than in 3 Redis nodes
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_4}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Using transactional databases only solves part of the proble.

\begin{itemize}
\item
As mentioned in the last section, it is very liekly that one transfer command will need to update two accoutns in two different databses
\item
THere is no guarantee that two update operations will be handled at exactly the same time
\item
I fthe wallet service restarted right after it updated the first account balance, how can we make sure the second account will be updated as well?
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Distributed transaction: Two-phase commit}

In a distributed system, a trnascation may involve multiple processes on multiple nodes.

\begin{itemize}
\item
To make a transaction atomic, the distributed transaction might be the answer
\item
There are two ways to implement a distributed trnasaction: a low-level solution and a high-level solution
\item
We will examine each of them
\item
The low-level solution relies on the databse itself
\item
The most commonly used algorithm is called two-phase commit (2PC)
\item
As the name implies, it has two phases as in Figure 12.3
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_5}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{enumerate}
\item
The coordinator, which in our case is the wallet service, performs read and write operations on multiple databses as normal. As shown in Figure 12.5, both databases A and C are locked
\item
When the application is about to commit the transaction, the coordinator asks all databases to prepare the transaction
\item
In the second phase, the coordinator collects replies from all databases and performs the following:

\begin{itemize}
\item
If all the databses reply with a \lstinline|yes|, the coordinator asks all databses to commit the transaction they have received
\item
If any database replies with a \lstinline|no|, the coordinator asks all databses to abort the transaction
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

It is a low-level solution becaues the prepare step requires a special modification to the database transacaiton

\begin{itemize}
\item
For example, there is an X/Open XA [6] standard that coordinates heterogeneous databses to achieve 2PC.
\item
The biggest problem with 2PC is that it's not performant, as lcoks can be hled for a very long time while waiting for a message form the other ndoes
\item
Another issue with 2PC is that the coordinator can be a single point of failure, as shown in Figure 12.6.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_6}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Distributed transaction: Try-Confirmm/Cancel (TC/C)}

TC/C is a type of compensating transaction [7] that has two steps:

\begin{itemize}
\item
In the first phase, the coordinator asks all databses to reserve reousrces for the transaction
\item
In the second phase, the coordinator collects replies from all databses:

\begin{itemize}
\item
If all databases reply with \lstinline|yes|, the coordinator asks all databases to confirm the operation, which is the Try-Confirm process
\item
If any databse replies with \lstinline|no|, the coordinator asks all databses to cancel the operation, which is the Try-Cancel process
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

It's important to note that the two phases in 2PC are wrapped in the same transaction but in TC/C each phase is a separate transaction.

\subsection{TC/C example}

It would be much easier to explain how TC/C works with a real-world example. Suppose we weant to transfer \$1 from account A to account C.

Table 12.2 gives a summary of how TC/C eis executed in each phase.

\begin{tabular}{|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|}
\hline
Phase & Operation & A & C \\
\hline
1 & Try & Balance Change: -\$1 & Do nothing \\
\hline
2 & Confirm & Do nothing & Balance Change: +\$1 \\
\hline
2 & Cancel & Balance Change: +\$1 & Do nothing \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 12.2: TC/C example

Let's assume the wallet service is the coordinator of the TC/C. A the beginning of the distributed transaction, account A has \$1 in its balance, and account C has \$0.

\subsubsection{First phase: Try}

In the Try phase, the wallet service, which acts as the coordnator, sends two transaction commands to two databases:

\begin{itemize}
\item
For the database that contains account A, the coordinator starts a local transaction that reduces the balance of A by \$1
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
For the database that contains account C, the coordinator gives it a \lstinline|NOP| (no operation)
\item
To make the example adaptable for other scenarios, let's assume the coordinator sends to this database a \lstinline|NOP| command
\item
The database does nothing for \lstinline|NOP| commands and always replies to the coordinator with a success message
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

The Try phase is shown in Figure 12.7. The thick line indicates that a lock is held by the transaction.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_7}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Second phase: Confirm}

If both databases reply \lstinline|yes|, the wallet service starts the next Confirm phase.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Account A's balance has already been updatedin the first phase.
\item
However, account C has not yet received its\$1 from account A in the first phase
\item
In the Confirm phase, the waleet service has to aadd \$1 to account C's balance
\end{itemize}

The Confirm process is shown in Figure 12.8.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_8}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Second phase: Cancel}

What if the first Try phase fails?

\begin{itemize}
\item
IN the example above we assumed the \lstinline|NOP| operation on account C always succeeds, although in practice it may fail
\item
For example, accoutn C might be an illegal acocunt, adhte regulator has mandated that no money can flow into our out of this account
\item
In this case, the distributed transaction must be acanceled and we have to clean up
\item
Because the balance of account A has already been updated in the transaction in the Try phase, it is impossible for the wallet service to cancel a completed transaction
\item
What it can do is to start another transaciton that reverts the effect of the transaction in the Try phase, which is to add \$1 back to account A
\end{itemize}

Because account C was not updated in the Try phase, the wallet service just needs to send a \lstinline|NOP| operation to account C's database.

The Cancel process is shown in Figure 12.9.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_9}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Comparison between 2PC and TC/C}

Table 12.3 shows that there are many similarities between 2PC and TC/C, but there are also differences.

In 2PC, all local transactions are not done (still locked) when the second phase starts, while in TC/C, all location transactions are done (unlocked) when the second phase starts

\begin{itemize}
\item
In other words, the second phase of 2PC is about completing an unfinished transaction, such as an abort or commit, while in TC/C, the second phase is about using a reverse operation to offset the previous transaction result when an error occurs
\end{itemize}

The following table summariezes their differences.

\begin{tabular}{|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|p{3.5cm}|}
\hline
 & First Phase & Second Phase: success & Second Phase: Fail\\
\hline
\lstinline|2PC| & Local transactions are not done yet & Commit all location transactions & Cancel all local transactions\\
\hline
  \lstinline|TC/C| & Execute new local transactions if needed & Revers4e the side effect of the already committed transaction or called ``undo''\\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 12.3: 2PC v.s. TC/C

TC/C is also called a distributed trnasaction by compensation.

\begin{itemize}
\item
It is a high-level solution because the compensation, also called the ``undo'' is implementedin the business logic.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The advantage of this approach is that it is database-agnostic.
\item
As long as a database supports transactions, TC/C will work
\item
The disadvantage is that we have to manage the details and handle the complexity of the distributed transactions in the business logic at the application layer.
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Phase status table}

We still have not yet answered the question asked earlier, what if the wallet service restats in the middle of TC/C? WHen it restarts all preivous operation hisotry might be lost, and the system may not know how to recover.

THe solution is simple. We can store the progress of a TC/C/ as phase status in a transactional database. THe phase status includes at least the following information.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The ID and content of a distributed transaction
\item
THe status of the TRy phase for each database. THe status could be \lstinline|not sent yet|, and has \lstinline|been sent|, and \lstinline|response received|
\item
THe name of the second phase. It coudl be \lstinline|Confirm| or \lstinline|Cancel|. It could be calculated using the result of the TRy phase
\item
THe status of the second phase
\item
AN out-of-order flag (explained soon in the section ``out-of-order Execution'').

Where should we put the phase status table? Usually we store the phase status in the database that contains the wallet account from which money is deducted. THe update architecture diagram is shon in Figure 12.10.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_10}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Unbalanced state}

Have you noticed that by the end of the Try phase, \$1 is missing (Figure 12.11)?

Assuming everything does well, by the end of the TRy phase, \$1 is deducted from account A and account C remains unchanged.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The sum of account balances in A and C will be \$0, which is less than at the beginning of the TC/C
\item
It violates a fundamental rule of accounting that the sum should reamin the same after a transaction
\end{itemize}

The good news is that the transactional guarantee is still maintained by TC/C.

\begin{itemize}
\item
TC/C comprises several independent local transactions
\item
Because TC/C/ si sdriven by application,t he application tiself is able to see the intermediate result between these local trnasactions
\item
On the other hand, the database transaction or 2PC version of the distributed transaction was maintained by databases that are invisible to high-level applications
\end{itemize}

There are wlasy data discrepencies during the execution of distributed trnasactions.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The discrepenacies might be trnasparent to us because lower-level systems such as databses already fixed the discrepancies
\item
If not, we hav eto handle it ourselves (for example, TC/C)
\end{itemize}

The unbalanced state is shown in Figure 12.11.

\subsubsection{Valid operation orders}

There are three choice for the Try phase:

\begin{tabular}{|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|}
\hline
Try phase orders & Account A & Account C \\
\hline
Choice 1 & \lstinline|-$1| & \lstinline|NOP| \\
\hline
Choice 2 & \lstinline|NOP| & \lstinline|+$1| \\
\hline
Choice 3 & \lstinline|-$1| & \lstinline|+$1| \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

ALl three choices look plausible, but some are not valid.

For choice 2, if the Try phase on account C is successful, but has failed on account A (\lstinline|NOP|), the wallet service needs to enter the Cancel phase.

\begin{itemize}
\item
There is a chance that somebody else may jump in and move the \$1 away from account C.
\item
Later when the wallet service tries to deudct \$ from account C, it find nothing is left, which violates the transactional guarantee of a distributed transaction
\item
For choice 3, if \$1 is deducted from account A and added to account C concurrently, it introduces lots of complications
\item
For example, \$1 is added to account C, but it fails to deduct the money from account A
\item
Therefore, choice 2 and choice 3 are flawed choices and only choice 1 is valid
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Out-of-order execution}

One side effect of TC/C is the out-of-order execution. It will be much easier to explain using an example.

\begin{itemize}
\item
We reuse the above example which transfers \$1 from account A to account C
\item
As Figure 12.12 shows, in the Try phase, the operation against account A fails and it returns a failure to the wallet service, which then enters the Cancel phase and sends the cancel operation to both account A and account C
\item
Let's assume that the databse that hnadles account C has some network issues and it receives  the Cnacel instruction before the Try instruction
\item
In this case, there is nothing to cancel
\item
The out-of-order execution is shon in Figure 12.12
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_12}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}


To handle out-of-order operations, each node is allowed to Cancel a TC/C without receiving a Try instruction, by enchaing the existiing logic witht he following updates:

\begin{itemize}
\item
The out-of-order Cance operation leaves a flag in the databse indication that it has seen Aa Cacenl operation, bu ti thas not seen a Try operation yet
\item
The Try operation is enhanced so it always checks whether there is an out-of-order flag, and it retuns a failure if there is
\end{itemize}

This is why we added an out-of-order flag to the phase status table in the ``Phase Status Table'' section.

To handle out-of-order operations, each node is allowed to Cancel a TC/C without receiving a Try instruction, by enhancing the existing logic with the following updates:

\begin{itemize}
\item
The out-of-order Cacnel operation leaves a flag in the database indicating that it has seen a Cancel oepration, but it has not seen a Try operation yet
\item
The Try operation is enhanced so it always chks whether there is an out-of-order flag, and it returns a failure if there is
\end{itemize}

This is why we added an out-of-order flag to the phase status table in the ``Phase Status Table''

\subsection{Distributed transaction: Saga}

\subsubsection{Linear order execution}

There is another popular distributed transaction solution called Saga [8]. Saga is the de-facto standard in a microservice architecture. The idea of Saga is simple:

\begin{itemize}
\item
All operations are ordered in a sequence. Each operation is an independent transaction on its own database
\item
Operations are executed from the first to the last. WHen one operation has finished, the next operation is triggered
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
When an operation has failed , the entire process starts to roll back from the current operation to the first operation in reverse order, using compensating transactions
\item
So if a distributed trnasaction has \lstinline|n| operations, we need to prepare \lstinline|2n| operations, \lstinline|n| operations for the normal case and another \lstinline|n| for the compensating transaction during rollback
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

It is easier to understand this by using an example. Figure 12.13 shows the Saga work flow to transfer \$1 from account A to account C.

The top horizontal line shows the normal order of execution.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The two vertical lines show what the system should do when there is an error
\item
When it encounters an error, the transfer operations are rolled back and the client receives an error message
\item
As we metnioned in the ``Validation operation orders'' section on page 352, we have to put the deduction operation before the addition operation
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_13}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

HOw do we coordinate the operations? There are two ways to do it:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Choreograpy.
\begin{itemize}
\item
In a microservice architecture, all the services involved in the Saga distributed trnasaction do their jobs by subscribing ot other services' events.
\item
So it is fully decentralized coordination
\end{itemize}
\item
Orchestration. A single coordinator instructs all services to do their jobs in the correct order
\end{itemize}

The choice of which coordination model is to use is determined by the business needs and goals.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The challenge of the choreography solution is that services communicate in a fully asynchronous way, so each service has to maintain an internal state machine in order to undertsand what to do when other services emit an event
\item
It can become hard to manage when there are many services
\item
The orchestration soution handles complexity well, so it is usually the preferred solution in a digital wallet system
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Comparison between TC/C and Saga}

TC/C and Saga are both applicatoin-level distributed trnasactions. Table 12.5 summarizes their similarities and differences.

\begin{tabular}{|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|p{4cm}|}
\hline
 & TC/C & Saga \\
\hline
Compensation action & In Cancel phase & In rollback phase \\
\hline
Central coordination & Yes & Yes (orchestration mode) \\
\hline
Operation execution order & any & linear \\
\hline
Parallel executino possibility & Yes & No (linear execution) \\
\hline
Could see the partial inconsistent status & Yes & Yes \\
\hline
Application or database logic & Application & Application \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

Table 12.5: TC/C vs Saga

WHich one should we use in practice? The answer depends on the latency requirement.

As Table 12.5 shows, operations in Saga have to be executed in linear order, but it is possible to execute them in parallel in TC/C. So the decision depends on a few factors:

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
If there is no latency requirement, or there are very few services, such as our money transfer example, we can choose either of them
\item
If we want to go with the trend in mircroservice architecture, choose Saga
\end{itemize}
\item
If the system is latency-sensitive and contains many services/operations, TC/C might be a better option
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Candidate}: To make the balance transfer transactional, we replace Redis witha relational database, and use TC/C or Saga to implement distributed transactions

\textbf{Interviewer}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Great work! The distributed transaction solution works, but there might be aes where it doesn't work well.
\item
For example, users might enter the wrong operations at the application level
\item
In this case, the money we specified might be incorrect
\item
We need a way to trace back the root cause of the issue and audit all account operations
\item
How can we do this?
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Event sourcing}

\subsubsection{Background}

In real life, a digital wallet provider may be audited. These external auditors might ask some challenging questions, for example:

\begin{enumerate}
\item
Do we know the account balance at any given tiem?
\item
How do we know the historical and current account balances are correct?
\item
How do we prove that the system logic is correct after a code change?
\end{enumerate}

One design philosophy that systematically answers those questions is event sourcing, which is a technique developed in Domain-Driven Design (DDD) [9].

\subsubsection{Definition}

There are four important terms in event sourcing.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
Command
\item
Event
\item
State
\item
State machine
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Command}

A command is the intended from the outside world.

\begin{itemize}
\item
For example, if we want to transfer \$1 from client A to client C, this money transfer request is a command
\item
In event sourcing, it is very important that everythin has an order
\item
So commands are usually put into a FIFO (first in, first out) queue
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Event}

Command is an intention and not a fact because some commands may be invalid and cannot be fulfilled.

\begin{itemize}
\item
For example, the transfer operation will fail if the acocunt balance becomes negative after the transfer
\item
A command must be validated before we do anything about it
\item
Once the command passes the validation, it is valid and must be fulfilled
\item
The result of the fulfillment is called an event
\end{itemize}

There are two major differences between command and event.

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Events must be executed because they represent a validated fact
\item
In practice, we usually use the past tense for an event
\item
If the command is ``transfer \$1 from A to C'' the corresponding event would be ``transferred \$1 from A to C''
\end{itemize}
\item
Commands may contain randomness or I/O, but events must be deterministic. Event represent historical facts.
\end{enumerate}

There are two important properties of the event generation process.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
One command may generate any number of events. It could generate zero or more events.
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Event generation may contain randomness, meaning it is not guaranteed that a command always generates the same event(s)
\item
The event generation may contain external I/O or random numbers
\item
We will revist this property in more detail near the end of the chapter
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

The order of events must follow the order of commands. SO events are stored in a FIFO queue, as well.

\subsection{State}

State is what will be changed when an event is applied.

\begin{itemize}
\item
In the wallet system, stat is the balances of all client accounts, which can be represented with a map data structure
\item
The key is the account name or OID, and the value is the account balance
\item
Key-value stores are usually used to store the map data structure
\item
The relational database can also be viewed as a key-value store, where keys are primary keys and values are table rows
\end{itemize}

\subsection{State machine}

A state machine drives the event sourcing process. It has two major functions.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
Validate commands and generate events
\item
Apply event to update state
\end{enumerate}

Event sourcing requires the behavior of the state machine to be deterministic.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Therefore, the state machine itself should never contain any randomness
\item
For example, it should never read anything random from the outside using I/O, or use any random numbers
\item
WHen it applies an event to a state, it should always generate the same result
\item
Figure 12.14 shows the status view of event sourcing architecture
\item
The state machine is resposible for converting the command to an event and for applying the event
\item
Because state machine has two primary functions, we usually draw two state machines, one for validating command sand the other for applying events
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_14}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

If we add the time dimension, Figure 12.15 shows the dynamic view of event sourcing.

The system keeps receigin commands and processing them, one by one.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_15}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Wallet service example}

For the wallet service, the comands are balance transfer requrests. These commands are put into a FIFO queue. The command queue is shown in Figure 12.16.

Let us assume the state (the account balance) is stored in a relational database.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The state machine examines each command one by one in FIFO order
\item
For each command, it checks whether the account has a sufficient balance
\item
If yes, the state machine generates an event for each account
\item
For example, if the command is ``\lstinline|A -> $1 -> C|'', the state machine generates two events: ``\lstinline|A: -$1|'' and ``\lstinline|C: + $1|''
\end{itemize}

Figure 12.17 shows how the state machine works in 5 steps.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
Read commands from the command queue
\item
Read balance state from the database
\item
Validate the command. If it is valid, generate two events for each of the accounts
\item
Read the next event
\item
Apply the event by updating the balance in the database
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_17}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Reproducibility}

The most important advantage that event sourcing has over other architectures is reproducibility.

\begin{itemize}
\item
In the distributed transaction solutions mentioned earlier, a wallet service saves the updated account balance (the state) into the databse
\item
It is difficult to know why the account balance was changed
\item
Meanwhile, historical balance information is lost during the update operation
\item
In the event sourcing design, all changes are saved first as immutable history
\item
The database is only used as an updated view of what balance looks like at any given point in time
\item
We could always reconstruct historical balance states by replaying th events from the very beginning
\item
Because the event list is immutable and the state machine logic is deterministic, it is guaranteed that the historical states generated from each replay are the same
\item
Figure 12.18 shows how to reproduce the tates of the wallet service by replaying the events
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_18}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Reproducibility helps us answer the difficult questions that the auditors ask at the beginning of the section. We repeat the questions here.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
Do we know the account balance at any given time?
\item
How do we know the historical and current account balances are correct?
\item
How do we prove the system logic is correct after a code change?
\end{enumerate}

For the first question, we could answer it by replaying events from the start, up to the point in time where we would like to know the account balance.

\begin{itemize}
\item
For the second question, we could verify the correctness of the account balance by reclaculating it from the event list
\item
For the third question, we can run different versions of the code against the events and verify that their results are identical
\item
Because of the audit capability, event sourcing is often chosen as the de facto solution for the wallet service
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Command-query responsibility (CQRS)}

So far, we have designed the wallet esrvice to move money from one account to another efficiently.

\begin{itemize}
\item
However, the client still does not know what the account balance is
\item
There needs to be a way to publich state (balance information) so the client, which is outside of the event sourcing framework, can know what the state is
\item
Intuitively, we can create a read-only copy of the database (historical state) and share it with the outside world
\item
Event sourcing answers this question in a slighty different way
\item
Rather than publishing the state (balance information), event sourcing publishes all the events
\item
The external world could rebuild any customrized state itself
\item
This design philosophy is called CQRS [11]
\item
In CQRS, there is one state machine responsible for the write part of the state, but there can be many read-only state machines, which are responsible for building views of the states
\item
Those views could be used for queries
\item
THese readon-only state machines can derive different state representations from the event queue
\item
For example, clients may want to know their balances and a read-only state machine could save state in a datbase to serve the balance query
\item
Another state machine could build state for a specific time period to hlep investigate issues like possible double charges
\item
The state infomration is an audit trail that could helpt ot reconcile the financial recrods
\item
The read-only state machines lag behind to some extent, but will always catch up
\item
The architecture design is eventually consistent
\item
Figure 12.19 shows a classic CQRS architecture
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_19}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\textbf{Candidate}:
\begin{itemize}
\item
In this design, we use event sourcing architecture to make the whole system reproducible
\item
All valid business records are saved in an immutable event queue which could be used for correctness verification
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Interviewer}

\begin{itemize}
\item
That's great
\item
But the event sourcing architecture you proposed only handles one event at a time and ti needs to communicate with several external ssytems
\item
Can we make it faster?
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 3: Design Deep Dive}

In this section, we delve deep into techniques for achieving high performance, reliability, and scalability.

\subsubsection{High-performance event sourcing}

In the earlier example, we used Kafka as the command and event store, and the databse as a state store. Let's explore some optimizations.

\subsubsection{File-based command and event list}

The first optimization is to save commands and events to a local disk, rather than to a remote store like Kafka.

\begin{itemize}
\item
This avoid transite time across the network
\item
The event list uses an append-only data structure
\item
Appending is a sequential write operation, which is generally very fast
\item
It works well even for magnetic hard drives because the operating system is heavily optimized for sequential reads and wrties
\item
According to this artcile [12], sequential disk access can be faster than raondom memory access in some cases
\end{itemize}

The second optimization is to cache recent commands and events in memory.

\begin{itemize}
\item
As we explained bofre, we process commands and events right after they are persisted
\item
We may acache them in memory to save the time of loading them back form the local disk
\item
We are going to explore some implementation details
\item
A technique called mmap [13] is great for implementing the optimizations metnioned previously
\item
Mmap can write to a local disk and cache recent content in memory at the same time
\item
It maps a disk file to memory as an array
\item
The operating system caches certain sections of the file in memory to accelerate the read and write operations
\item
For append-only file operations, it is almost guaranteed that all data are saved in memory, which is very fast
\item
Figure 12.20 shows the file-based command and event storage
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_20}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{File-based state}

In the previous design, state (balance information) is stored in a relational database.

\begin{itemize}
\item
In a production environment, a datbase usually runs in a stand-alone server that can only be accessed through networks
\item
Similar to the optimizations we did fro command and event, state information can be saved to the local disk, as well
\item
More specifically, we can use SQLite [14], which is a file-based local relational database or use RocksDB [15], which is a local file-based key-value store
\item
RocksDB is chosen because it uses a log-structured merge-tree (LSM), which is optimized for writer operations
\item
To improve read performance, the most recent data is cached
\item
Figure 12.21 shows the file-based solution for command, event, and take
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_21}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Snapshot}

Once everything is file-based, let us consider how to accelerate the preoducibility process.

\begin{itemize}
\item
When we first introduced reproducibility, the state machine had to process events from the very beginning, every time
\item
What we could optimize is to periodically stop the state machine and save the current state into a file
\item
This is called a snapshot
\end{itemize}

A snapshot is an immutable view of a historical state.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Once a snapshot is saved, the state machine does not have to restart form the very geninning anymore
\item
It can read data from a snapshot,v erify where it left off, and resume processing from there
\end{itemize}

For financial application such as wallet service, the finance team often requires a snapshot to be taken at 00:00 so they can verify all transactions that happened during that day.

\begin{itemize}
\item
When we first introduced CQRS of event sourcing, the solution was to set up a read-only state machine that reads from the beginning until sthe specified tiem is met
\item
With snapshots, a read-only state machine only needs to load one snapshot that contains the data
\item
A snapshot is a ginait binary file and a common solution si to save it in an object storage solution, such as HDFS [16]
\item
Figure 12.22 shows the file-based event sourcing architecture
\item
When everything is file-based, the system can fully utilize the maximum I/O throughput of the computer hardware
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_22}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\textbf{Candidate}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
We could refactor the design of event sourcing so the command list, event list, state, and snapshot are all saved in files
\item
Event sourcin garchitecture processes the event list in a linear mannger, which fits well into the design on hard disks and operating system cache
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Interviewer}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
The performance of the local file-based solution si better than the system that requires accessing data from remote Kafka and datbases
\item
However, there is another problem: because data is saved on a local disk, a server is now stateful and becomes a single point of failure
\item
How do we imporve the reliability of the system?
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Reliable high-performance event sourcing}

Before we explain the solution, let's examine the parts of the system that need the reliability guarantee.

\subsubsection{Reliability analysis}

Conceptually, everything a node does is around two concept; data and computation.

\begin{itemize}
\item
As long as data is durable, it's easy to recover the computational result by running the same code on another node
\item
This means we only need to worry about the reliability of data because if data is lost, it is lost forver
\item
The reliability of the system is mostly about the reliability of the data
\end{itemize}

There are four types of data in our system.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
File-based command
\item
File-based event
\item
File-based state
\item
State snapshot
\end{enumerate}

Let us take a close lok at how to esnure the realibility of each type of data.

STate and snapshot can always abe refenerated by replaying the event list.

\begin{itemize}
\item
To improve the reliability of state and snapshot, we just need to ensure the event list has strong reliability
\item
Now let us examine command
\item
On the face of it, event is generated from command
\item
We might think providing a strong reliability guarantee for command should be sufficient
\item
This seems to be correct at first glance, but it misses something important
\item
Event generation is not guaranteed to be deterministic, and also it may contain raondom factors such as random numbers, external I/O, etc
\item
So command cannot generate reproducibility of events
\end{itemize}

Now it's time to take a close look at event.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Event represents historical facts to introduce changes ot the state (account balance)
\item
Event is immutable and can be used to reubild the state
\item
From this analysis, we conlucde that event data is the only one that requires a high-reliabilty guarantee
\item
We will explain how to achieve this in the next section.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Consensus}

To provide high reliability, we need to replicate the event list across multiple nodes. During the replication proces, we have to guarantee the following properties.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
No data loss
\item
The relative order of data within a log file remains the same across nodes
\end{enumerate}

To achieve those guarantees, consensus-based replication is a good fit.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The consensus algorithm makes sure that multiple nodes reach a consensus on what the event list is
\item
Let's use the Raft [17] consensus algorithm as an example
\item
The Raft algorithm guarantees that as long as more than half of the nodes are online, the append-only lists on them have the same data
\end{itemize}

For example, if we have 5 noes and use the Raft algorithm to synchronize their data, as long as at least 3 (more than $\frac{1}{2}$) of the nodes are up as Figure 12.23 shows, the system can still work properly as a whole:

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_23}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

A node can have three different roles in the Raft algorithm:
\begin{enumerate}
\item
Leader
\item
Candidate
\item
Follower
\end{enumerate}

We can find the implementation fo the Raft algorithm in the Raft Paper.

\begin{itemize}
\item
We will only cover the high level concepts here and not go into detail
\item
In Raft, at most one node is the leader of the cluster and the remaining nodes are followers
\item
The leader is responsible for receiving external commands and replicating data reliability across nodes in the cluster
\item
WIth the Raft Algorithm, the system is reliabile as long as the majority of the nodes are operational
\item
For example, if there are 3 nodes in the cluster, it could tolerate the failure of 1 node, and if there are 5 nodes, it can tolerate the failure of 2 nodes
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Reliable solution}

With replication, there won't be a single point of failure in our file-based event sourcing architecture.

Let's take a look at the implementation details.

Figure 12.24 shows the event sourcing architecture with the reliability guarantee.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_24}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

In Figure 12.24, we set 3 event sourcing nodes. These nodes use the Raft algorithm to synchronize the event list reliably.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The leader takes incoming command requests from external users, converts them into events, and appends events into the local event list
\item
The Raft algorithm replicates newly added events to the followers
\item
All nodes, including the followers, process the event list and update the state
\item
The Raft algorithm esnures the leader and followers have the same event lists, while event sourcing guarantees all states are the same, as long as the event lists are the same
\item
All nodes including the followers, process the event list an dupdate the state
\item
The Raft algorithm esnures the leader and followers have the same event lists, while event sourcing uarantees all states are the same, as long as the event lists are the same
\item
A reliable system needs to handle failures gracefully, so let's explore how node crashes are handled
\item
If the leader crashes, the Raft algorithm automatically selects a new leader from the remaining healthy nodes
\item
This newly elected leader takes responsibility for accepting commands from external users
\item
It is guaranteed that the cluster as a whole can provide continued service when a node goes down
\end{itemize}

When a leader crashes, it is possible that the crash happens before the command list is converted to events
\begin{itemize}
\item
In this case, the client would notice the issue weither by a timeout or by receign an error response
\item
The client needs to resned the same command to the newly elected leader
\item
In constrast, follower crashes are much easier to handle
\item
If a follower crashes, requests sent to it will fail
\item
Raft handles failures by returing indefinitely until the crashed node is restarted or a new one replaces it
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Candidate}: In this design, we use the Raft consesnsus aglrotihm to replicate the event list across multiple nodes. The leader receives commands and replicates events to other nodes.

\textbf{Interviewer}: Yes, the system is more reliabile and fault-toleratnt. However, in order to handle 1 million TPS, one server is not enough. How can we make the system more scalable?

\subsection{Distributed event sourcing}

In the previous section, we explained how to implement a reliable high-performance event sourcing architecture. It solves the reliability issue, but it has two limitations.

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
When a dgitial wallet is updated, we want to receive the updated result immediately.
\item
But in the CQRS design, the request/response can be slow
\item
This is because a client doesn't know exactly when a dgitial wallet is updated and the client may need to rely  on periodic polling
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

Let's take a look at how those two problems are solved.

\subsection{Pull vs push}

\begin{itemize}
\item
In the pull model, an external user peridiocally polls  execution status from the read-only state machine.
\item
This model is not real-time and may overload the wallet service if the polling frequency is set too high.
\item
Figure 12.25 shows the pulling model
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_25}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The naive pull model can be improved by adding a reverse proxy [18] between the external user and the event sourcing node.

\begin{itemize}
\item
In this design, the external user sends a command to the reserve proxy, which forwards the command to event sourcing nodes and periodically polls the execution status
\item
This  design simplifies the client logic, but the communication is still not real-time
\item
Figure 12.26 shows the pull model with a reverse proxy added
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_26}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Once we have the reverse proxy, we could make the response faster by modifying the read-only state machine.

\begin{itemize}
\item
As we mentioned earlier, the read-only state machine could have its own behavior.
\item
For example, one behavior could be that the read-only state machine pushes execution status back to the reverse proxy, as soon as it receives the event
\item
This will give the user a feeling of real-time response
\end{itemize}

Figure 12.27 shows the push-based model.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_27}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Distributed transaction}

Once synchronous execution is adpoted for every event sourcing node group, we can resuse the distributed transaction solution, TC/C or Sage.

Assume we partition the data by dividing the has value of keys by 2.

Figure 12.28 shows the updated design.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_28}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Let's take a look at how the money transfer works inthe final distributed event sourcing architecture.

\begin{itemize}
\item
To make it easier to undetsnad, we use the Saga distributed transaction model and only explain the happy path without any rollback
\item
THe momney trnasfer operation contains 2 distributed operations: \lstinline|A: - \$1| and \lstinline|C:+\$1|
\item
The Saga coordinator coordinates the execution as shown in Figure 12.29
\end{itemize}

\begin{enumerate}
\item
User A sends a distributed transaction to the Saga coordinator. It contains two operations: \lstinline|A: -$1| and \lstinline|C: +$1|
\item
Saga coordinator creates a record in the phase status table to trace the status of a transaction
\item
Saga coordinator examines the order of operations and determines that it needs to handle \lstinline|A: -$1| first. The coordinator sends \lstinline|A: -$1| as a command to Partition 1, which contains account A's information
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Partition 1's Raft leader receives the \lstinline|A-$1| command and stores it in the command list
\item
It then validates the command
\item
If it is valid, it is converted into an event
\item
The Raft consensus algorithm is used to synchronize data across different nodes
\item
The event (deducting \$1 from A's account balance) is executed after synchronization is complete
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
After the event is synchronized, the event sourcing framework of Parittion 1 synchronizes the data to the read path using CQRS
\item
The read path reconstructs the state and the status of execution
\end{itemize}
\item
The read path of Partition 1 pushes the status back to the caller of the event sourcing rramework, which is the Saga coordinator
\item
Saga coordinator receives the success status from Partition 1
\item
The Saga coordinator creates a record, indicating the operation in Partition 1 is successful, in the phase status table
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Because the first operation succeeds, the Safa coordinator executes the second operation, which is \lstinline|C: +$1|
\item
The coordinator sends \lstinline|C:$1| as a command in Partition 2 which contains account C's information
\end{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Partition 2's Raft leader receives the \lstinline|C: +$1| command and saves it to the command list
\item
If it is valid, it is converted into an event
\item
The Raft consensus algorithm is used to synchronize data across different nodes
\item
The event (add \$1 to C's account) is executed after syncrhonization is complete
\end{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
After the event is synrhonized, the event sourcing fraemwork of Parittion 2 synchronizes the data to the read path using CQRS
\item
The read path reoncstructs the state and the status of execution
\end{itemize}
\item
THe read path of Partition 2 pushes the status back to the caller of the event sourcing framework, which is the Saga coordinator
\item
The Saga cooridnator receives the success status from Partition 2
\item
The Saga coordinator creates a record, indicating the operation in Partion 2 is successful in the phase status table
\item
At this time, all operations succeed and the distributed transaction is completed. The Saga coordinator responds to its caller with the result
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_12_29}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Step 4: Wrap Up}

In this cahpter, we designed a wallet service that is capable of processing over 1 million payment commands per second.
\begin{itemize}
\item
After a back-of-the-envelope estimation, we conlcuded that a few thousand nodes are required to support such a load
\item
In the first design, a solution using in-memory key-value stores like Redis is proposed
\item
The problem with this design is that data isn't durable
\item
In the second design, the in-memory cache is replaced by transactional databses
\item
TO support multiple nodes, different transactional protocols such as 2PC, TC/C and Saga as proposed
\item
The main issue with trnasaction-based solutions is that we cannot conduct a data audit easily.
\item
Next, event sourcing is introduced
\item
We first implemented event sourcing using an external database and queue, but it's not performant
\item
We improved performance by storing command, event, and state in a local node
\item
A single node means a single point of failure
\item
TO increase the system reliability, we use the Raft consensus algorithm to replicate the event list onto multiple nodes
\item
The last enhancement we made was to adpot the CQRS feature of event sourcing
\item
We added a reverse proxy to change the aysnchronous event sourcing framework to a synchronous one for external users
\item
The TC/C or Sage protocol is used to coordinate COmmand executions across multiple node groups
\end{itemize}
