    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{chapter_12_summary}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\chapter*{Chapter 13: Stock Exchange}

In this chapter, we design an electronic stock exchange system

The basic function of an exchange is to facilitate the matching of buyers and sellers efficiently.

\begin{itemize}
\item
This fundamental function has not change4d over time
\item
Before the rise of computing, people exchanged tangible goods by bartering and shouting at each other to get match
\item
Today, orders are processed siliently by supercomputers, and people trade not only for the exchange of products, but also for speculation and arbitrage
\item
Technology has greatly changed the landscape of trading and exponentially boosted electronic market trading volume
\item
When it comes to stock exchanges, most popel think about major market players like The New York Stock exchange (NYSE) or Nasdaq, which have existed for over fifty years
\item
In fact, there are many other types of exchange
\item
SOme focus on vertical segmentation of the financial industry and place special focus on technology [1], while others have an emphasis on fairness [2]
\item
Before diving into the design, it is imporrtant to check with the interviewer about the scale and the important charactersitics of the exchange in question
\item
Some focus on vertical segmentation of the financial industry and place special focus on technology [1], while others have an emphasis on fairnesss [2]
\item
Before diving into the design, it important to check with the interviewer about the scale and the important characteristics of the exchange in question
\item
Just to get a taste of the kind of problem we are dealing with; NYSE is trading billions of matches per day [3], and HKEX about 200 billion shares per day [4]
\item
Figure 13.1 shows the big exchanges in the ``trillion-dollar club'' by market capitalization
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_13_1}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Step 1: Understand the Problem and Establish Design Scope}

A modern exchange is a complicated system with stringent requirements on latency, throughput, and robustness. Before we start, let's ask the interviewer a few questions to clarify the requirements.

\begin{itemize}
\item

\textbf{Candidate}: WHich securities are we going to trade? Stocks, options or ftuures?

\textbf{Interviewer}: For simplicity, only stocks
\item
\textbf{Candidate}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Which types of order operations are supported: placing a new order, canceling an order, or replacing an order?
\item
Do we need to support limit order, market order, or conditional order?
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Interviewer}:

We need to support the following: placing a new order and canceling an order. For the order type, we only need to consider the limit order.

\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Does the system need to support after-hours trading?

\textbf{Interviewer}: No we just need to support the nromal tarding hours.
\item
\textbf{Candidate}: Could you describe the basic functions of the exchange? And the cale of the exchange, usch as how many users, how many symbols and how many orders?

\textbf{Interviewer}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
A client can place new limit orders or cancel them, and receive matches trades in real-time
\item
A client can view the real-time order book (the list of buy and sell orders.)
\item
The exchange needs to support at least tens of thousands of users trading at the same time, and ti needs to support at least 100 symbols
\item
For the trading volume, we should support billions of orders per day
\item
Also the exchange is a regularted facility, so we need to make sure it runs risk checks
\end{itemize}
\item
\textbf{Candidate}: COuld you please elaborate on risk checks?

\textbf{Interviewer}: Let's just do simple risk checks. FOr example, a user can only trade a maximum of 1 million shares of Apple stock in one day.

\item

\textbf{Candidate}: I noticed you didn't mention user wallet management. Is it something we also need to consider?

\textbf{Interviewer}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Good catch!
\item
We need to make sure users have suffcient funds whey they place orders.
\item
If an order is waiting in the order book to be filled, the funds required for the order need to be withheld to prevent overspending.
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Non-functional requirements}

After checking with the interviewer for the functional requirements, we should determine the non-functional requirements
\begin{itemize}
\item
In fact, requirements like ``at least 100 symbols'' and ``tens of thousands of users'' tell us that the interviewer wants us to design a small-to-medium scale exchange
\item
On top of this, we should make sure the design can be extended to support more symbols and users
\item
Many interviewers focus on extensibility as an area for follow-up questions.
\end{itemize}

Here is a list of non-functional requirements:

\begin{itemize}
\item
\textbf{Availability.}: At least 99.99\%. Avaialblity is crucial for excahnges. DOwntime, event seconds, can harm reptuation
\item
\textbf{Fault tolerance.}: Fulat tolerance and a fast recovery mechinaism are needed to limit the pact of a production incident
\item
\textbf{Latency.}

\begin{itemize}
\item
The round trip latency should be at the mmillisecond level, with a partiulcar focus on the 99th percentile latency
\item
THe round trip latency is measured from the moment a market order enters the exchange to the point where the market order returns as a filled execution
\item
A persistently high 99th percentile latency causes a terrible user experience for a small number of users.
\end{itemize}

\item
\textbf{Security}:
\begin{itemize}
\item
The exchange should have an account management system
\item
For legal and compliace, the exchange performs a KYC (Know YOur CLient) check to verify a user's identity  before a new account is opened
\item
For public resources, such as web pages containing market data, we should prevent distributed denial-of-serivce (DDoS) [6] attacks
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Back-of-the-envelope estimation}

Let's do some simple back-of-the-envelope calculations to understand the scale of the system:

\begin{itemize}
\item
100 symbols
\item
1 billion orders per day
\item
NYSE Stock exchange is open Monday through Friday from 9:30 am to 4:00 pm Eastern Time. That's 6.5 hours in total
\item
QPS: $\frac{1 \text{ billion}}{6.5 \times 3,600} = \sim 43,000$
\item
Peak QPS: $5 \times \text{ QPS } = 215,000$. The trading volume is significantly higher when the market first opens in the morning and before it closes in the afternoon.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Step 2: Propose High-Level Design and Get Buy-In}

Before we dive into the high-level design, let's briefly discuss some basic concepts and terminology that are helpful for designing an exchange.

\subsubsection{Business Knowledge 101}

\subsubsection{Broker}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Most retail clients ttrad with an exchange via a broker.
\item
Some brokers whom you might be familiar with include Charles Schwab, Robinhood, E*Trade, Fidelity, etc.
\item
These brokers provide a friendly user interface for retail users to place trades and view market data
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Institutional client}

\begin{itemize}
\item
Institutional clients trade in large volumes using specizlied trading software
\item
Different instittutional clients operate with different rquirements
\item
For example. pension  funds aim for a stable income
\item
They trade infrequenlty, but when they do trade, the volume is large
\item
They need features like order splitting to minimize the market impact [7] of their sizable orders
\item
SOme hedge funds specialize in market making and earn income via commission rebates
\item
They need low latency trading abilities, so obviously they cannot simply view market data on a web page or a mobile app, as retail clients do
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Limit order}

A limit order is a buy or sell order with a fixed price. It might not find a match immediately, or it might just be partially matched.

\subsubsection{Market order}

\begin{itemize}
\item
A market order doesn't specify a price.
\item
It is executed at the prevailing karet price immediately
\item
A market order sacrifices cost in order to guarantee execution
\item
It is useful in certain fast-moving market conditions
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Market data levels}

The US stock market has three tiers of price quotes: L1 (level 1,), L2, and L3.

\begin{itemize}
\item
L1 market data contains the best bid price, ask price, and quantities (Figure 13.2).
\item
Bid price refers to the highest price a buyer is willing to pay for a stock.
\item
Ask price refers to the lowest price a sller is willing to sell the stock
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_13_2}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_13_3}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

L3 shows price levgels and the queued quantity at each price level (Figure 13.4).

\subsubsection{Candlestick chart}

A candlestick chart represent the stock price for a certain period of time.

\begin{itemize}
\item
A typical candlestick looks like this (Figure 13.5)
\item
A candlestick shows the market's open, close, high, and low price for a time interval
\item
The common time intervals are one-minute, five-minute, one-hour, and one-month
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_13_5}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{FIX}

FIX protocol [8], which stands for Financial INformation exchange protocol, was created in 1991. It is a vendor-netural communications protocol for exchanging securities information.

See below for an example of a securities transaction encoded in FIX [8].

\begin{lstlisting}
8=FIX.4.2.| 9=176 | 35=8 | 49=PHLX | 56=PERS | 52=20071123-05:30:00.000 | 11=ATOMNOCCC9990900 | 20=3 | 150=e | 39=e | 55=MSFT | 167=CS | 54=1 | 38=15 | 40=2 | 44=15 | 58=PHLX EQUITY TESTING | 59=0 | 47=C | 32=0 | 31=0 | 151=15 | 14=0 | 6=0 | 10=128 |
\end{lstlisting}

\subsubsection{High-level design}

Now that we have some basic understanding of the key concepts, let's take a look at the high-level design, as shown in Figure 13.6.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_13_6}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Let's trace the life of an order through various components in the diagram to see hwo the pieces fit together.

First, we follow the order through the \textbf{trading flow}. THis is the critical path with strict latency requirements. Evetyhing has to happen fast in the flow:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Step 1: A client places an order via the broker's web or mobile app
\item
Step 2: The broker sends the order to the exchange.
\item
Step 3:

\begin{itemize}
\item
The order enters the exchane through the client gateway
\item
The client gateway performs basic gatekeeping functions such as input validation, rate limiting, authentication, normalization et.c
\item
The client gateway then forwards the order to the order manager
\end{itemize}
\item
Step 4 $\sim$ Step 5: The order manager performs risk checks based on rules set by the risk manager.
\item
Step 6: AFter passing risk checks, the order manager verifies there are sufficient funds in the wallet for the order
\item
Step 7 $\sim$ 9:

\begin{itemize}
\item
The order is sent to the matching engine
\item
When a match is found, the matching engine emits two executions (also called fills), with one each for the buy and sell sides
\item
To guarantee that matching results are deterministic when replayed, both orders and executions are sequenced in the sequencer (more on the sequencer later).
\end{itemize}
\item
Step 10 $\sim$ 14: The executions are returned to the client
\end{itemize}

Next, we follow the \textbf{market data flow} and trace the order executions from the matching engine to the broker via the data service.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Step M1: THe matching engine generates a stream of executions (fills) as matches are made. The stream is sent ot the market data publisher.
\item
Step M2:
\begin{itemize}
\item
The market data publisher constructs the cnalde stick charts and the order books as market data from the stream of executions and orders
\item
It then sends market data to the data service
\end{itemize}
\item
Step M3:

\begin{itemize}
\item
The market data is saved to specialized storage for real-time analystics.
\item
Brokers connect to the data service to obtain timely market data
\item
Brokers relay market data to their clients
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

Lastly, we examine the \textbf{reporting flow}.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Step R1 $\sim$ R2 (reporting flow): The reporter collects all the necessary reporting fields (e.g. \lstinline|client_id|, \lstinline|price|, \lstinline|quantity|, \lstinline|filled_quantity|, \lstinline|remaining_quantity|) from orders and executions, and writes the consolidated records to the database
\item
Note that the trading flow (steps 1 to 14) is on the critical path, while the market data flow and reporting flow are not
\item
They have different latency requirements
\item
Now let's examine each of the three flows in more detail
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Trading flow}

The trading flow is on the critical path of the exchange. Everything must happen fast. The heart of the trading flow is the matching engine. Let's go over that first.

\subsection{Matching engine}

The matching engine is also called the cross engine. Here are the primary responsibilites of th matching engine:

\begin{enumerate}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Maintain the order book for each symbol
\item
An order book is a list of buy and sell orders for a symbol
\item
We explain the construction of an order book in the Data models section later
\end{itemize}

\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
Match buy and sell orders
\item
A match results in two executions (one from the buy side and the other from the sell side)
\item
The matching function must be fast and accurate
\end{itemize}
\item
Distribute the execution stream as market data
\end{enumerate}

A highly available matching engine implementation must be able to produce matches in a deterministic order.

\begin{itemize}
\item
That is, given a known sequence of orders as an input, the matching engine must prdouce the same sequence of executions (fills) as an output when the sequence is replayed
\item
This determinism is a foundation of high availability which we will discuss at length in the deep dive section
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Sequencer}

The sequencer is the key component that makes the matching engine deterministic.

It stamps every pair of executions (fills) completed by the matching engine with sequence IDs

\begin{itemize}
\item
In other word, the sequencr has an inbound and an outbound instance with each maintaining its own sequences
\item
The sequence generated by each sequencer must be sequential numbers so that any missing numbers can be easily detected.
\item
See Figure 13.7 for details
\end{itemize}


\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_13_7}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The incoming orders and outgoing executions are stamped with sequence IDs for these reasons:

\begin{enumerate}
\item
Timeliness and fairness
\item
Fast recovery / replay
\item
Exactly-once guarantee
\end{enumerate}

The sequencer does not only generate sequence IDs.

\begin{itemize}
\item
It also functions as a message queue
\item
THere is one to send messages (incoming orders) to the matching engine, and another one to send messages (executions) back to the order manager
\item
It is also an event store for the orders and executions
\item
It is similar to having two Kafka event streams connected to the matching engine, one for incoming orders and the other for outgoing executions
\item
In fact, we could have used Kafka if it slatency was lower and more predictable
\item
We discuss how the sequenceer is implemetned in a low-latency xchange environment in the deep dive section
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Order manager}

THe order manager receives orders on one end and receives executions on the other. I tmanagers the order's states. Let's look at it closely.

THe order maanger receives inbound orders from the client gateway and performs the following:

\begin{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
It sends the order for risk checks.
\item
Our requireemtns for risk checking are simple.
\item
For example, we verify that a user's trade volume is below \$1M a day.
\end{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
It checks the order against the user's wallet and verifies that there are sufficient funds to cover the trade
\item
The wallet was discussed at length in the ``Digital Wallet'' chapter on page 341
\item
Refer to that chapter for an implementation that would work in the exchange
\end{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
It sends the order to eh sequencer where the order is stamped with a sequence ID
\item
The sequenced order is tehn processed by the matching engine
\item
There are many attributesi in a new order, but there is no need to send all the attributes to the matching engine
\item
To reduce the size of the message in data transmission, the order manager only sends the necessary attributes
\end{itemize}

On the other end, the order manager receives executions form the matching engine via the sequencer

\begin{itemize}
\item
The order manager returns the executions for the fileed orders to the brokers via the client gateway
\item
THe order manager should be fast, efficient, and accurate
\item
It maintains the current states for the orders
\item
IN fact, the channlege of managing the various tate transitions is the major source of complexity for the order manager
\item
There can be tends of thousands of cases involved in a real exchange system
\item
Event sourcing [9] is perfrect for the design of an order manager
\item
We discuss an event sourcing desing in the deep dive section
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Client gateway}

The client gateway is the gatekeeper for the exchange. IT receives orders placed by clients and routes them to the order manager.

The gateway provides the following functions as shown in Figure 13.8.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_13_8}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

The client gateway is on the critical path and is latency-sensitive.

\begin{itemize}
\item
It should stay lightweight
\item
It passes orders to the correct desitnations as quickly as possible
\item
The functions above, while critical, must be completed as quickly as possible
\item
It is a design trade-off to decide what functionality to put int he client gateway, and what to leave out
\item
As a general guide line, we should leave complicated functions to the matching engine and risk check
\end{itemize}

There are different types of client gateways for retail and institutational clients

\begin{itemize}
\item
The main considerations are latency, transaction volume , and security requirements
\item
For instance, institutions are like the market makers provide a large portion of liquidity for the exchange
\item
They require very low latency
\item
Figure 13.9 shows different client gateway connections to an exchange
\item
An extreme example is the colocation (colo) engine
\item
It is the tranding engine software running on some servers rented by the broker in the exchange's data center
\item
The latency is literally the time it takes for light to travel from the colocated server to the exchange server [10]
\end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_13_9}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Market data flow}

The market data publisher (MDP) receives executions from the matching eingine and builds the order books and candle stick charts from the stream of executions.

The order books and candle stick charts, which we discuss in the Data Models section later, are collectively called market data.

The market data is sent to the data service where they are made available to subscribers.

Figure 13.10 shows an implementation of MDP and how ti fits with the other coponents in the market data flow.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_13_10}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Reporting flow}

One essential part of the exchange is reporting.

\begin{itemize}
\item
The reporter is not on the trading citical path, but it is a critical part of the system
\item
It provides trading history, tax reporting, complicance reporting, settlements, etc.
\item
Efficiency and latency are critical for the trading flow, but the reporter is less sensitive to latency
\item
Accuracy and complicance are key factors for the reporter
\item
It is common practice to piece attributes together from both incoming orders and outgoing executions
\item
An incoming new order contains order details, and outgoing execution usually only contains order ID, price, quantity, and execution status
\item
The reporter merges the attributes from both sources for the reports
\item
Figure 13.11 shows how the components in the reporting flow fit together.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_13_11}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

A sharp reader might notice that the section order of ``Step 2: Propose High Level Design and Get Buy-In'' looks a little different from other chapters

\begin{itemize}
\item
In this chapter, the API design and data models sections come after the high-level design
\item
The sections are arranged this way because tse other sections require some concepts that were introduced in the high-level design
\end{itemize}

\subsection{API Design}

NOw that we undertsnad the high-level design, let's take a look at the API design.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Clients interact with thte stock exchange via the brokers to place orders, view executions, view market data, download historical data for analysis, etc
\item
We use the RESTful conventions for the API below to specify the itnerface between the brokers and the client gateway
\item
Refer to the ``Data models'' section on page 393 for the resources mentioned below
\item
Note that the RESTful API might not satisfy the latency requirements of insitituional clients like hedge funds
\item
The specialized software built fo these insititutions liekly uses a different protocol, ,but no matter what it is, the basic functionality mentioned below needs to be supported
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Order}

\lstinline|POST /v1/order|

This endpoint places an order. It requires authentication.

\begin{lstlisting}
Parameters
  symbol: the stock symbol. String
  side: buy or sell. String
  price: the price of the limit order. Long
  orderType: limit or market(note we only support limit orders in our design) String
\end{lstlisting}

Response

Body:

\begin{lstlisting}
  id: the ID of the order. Long
  creationTime: the system creation time of the order. Long
  filledQuantity: the quanitty that has been successfully executed. Long
  remainingQuantity: the quantity still to be executed. Long
  status: new/canceled/filled. String
  rest of the attributes are the same as the input paremeters
\end{lstlisting}

Code:

\begin{lstlisting}
200: successful
40x: parameter error/access denied/unauthorized
500: server error
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Execution}

\lstinline|GET /v1/execution?symbol={:symbol}&orderId={:orderId}&startTime={:startTime}&endTime={:endTime}|

This endpoint queries execution info. It requires authentication.

Parameters:

\begin{lstlisting}
  symbol: the stock symbol. String
  orderId: the ID of the order. Optional. String
  startTime: query start time in epoch [11]. Long
  endTime: query end time in epoch. Long
\end{lstlisting}

Response

Body:

\begin{lstlisting}
executions : array with each execution in scope (see attributes below): Array

  id: the ID of the execution. Long
  orderId: the ID of the order. Long
  symbol: the stock symbol. String
  side: buy or sell. String
  price: the price of the execution. Long
  orderType: limit or market. String
  quantity: the filled quantity: Long
\end{lstlisting}

Code:

\begin{lstlisting}
200: successful
40x: parameter error/not found/access denied/unauthorized
500: server error
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Order book}

\lstinline|GET /v1/marketdata/orderBook/L2?symbol={:symbol}&depth={:depth}|

This endpoint queries L2 order book information for a symbol with designated depth.

\begin{lstlisting}
Parameters
  symbol: the stock symbol. String
  depth: order book depth per side. Int
  startTime: query start time in epoch. Long
  endTime: query end time in epoch. Long
\end{lstlisting}

Response

Body:

\begin{lstlisting}
  bids: array with price and size. Array
  asks: array with price and size. Array
\end{lstlisting}

Code:

\begin{lstlisting}
Code:
  200: successful
  40x: parameter error/not found/access denied/unauthorized
  500: server error
\end{lstlisting}

\subsubsection{Historical prices (candlestick charts)}

\lstinline|GET /v1/marketdata/candles?symbol={:symbol}&resolution={:resolution}&startTime{:startTime}&endTime{:endTime}|

THis endpoint queries candle stick chart data (see cnalde stick chart in data models section) for a symbol given a time range and solution.

Parameters

\begin{lstlisting}
symbol: the stock symbol. String
resolution: window lenght of the candlestick chart in seconds. Long
startTime: start time of the window in epoch. Long
endTime: end time of the window in epoch. Long
\end{lstlisting}

Response

Body:

\begin{lstlisting}
candles: array with each candlestick data (attributes listed below). Array
open: open price of each candlestick. Double
close: close price of each candlestick. Double
high: high price of each candlestick. Double
low: low price of each candlestick. Double
\end{lstlisting}

Code:

\begin{lstlisting}
200: successful
40x: parameter error/not found/access denied/unauthorized
500: server error
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Data models}

There are three main types of data in the stock exchange. Let's explore them one by one.

\begin{itemize}
\item
Product, order and execution
\item
Order book
\item
Candle stick chart
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Product, order, execution}

A product describes the attributes of a traded symbol, like product type, trading symbol, UI display symbol, setttlement currency, lot size, tick size, etc.

\begin{itemize}
\item
This data doesn't change frequently
\item
It is primarly used for UI display
\item
The data can be stored in any database and is highly cacheable
\item
An order represents the inbound instruction for a buy or sell order
\item
An execution represent the outbound matched result
\item
An execution is also called a fill
\item
Not every odrder has an execution
\item
The output of the m athcing engine contains two executions, representing the buy and seel sides of a matched order.
\item
See Figure 13.12 for the lgoical model diagram that shows the relationships between the three entitites
\item
Note that it is not a database schema
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_13_12}
    \caption{}
  \end{center}
\end{figure}

Orders and executions are the most importat data in the exchange. We encounter them in all three flows mentionedin the high-level design, in slightly different forms.

\begin{itemize}
\item

\begin{itemize}
\item
In the critical trading path, orders and executions are not stored in a database
\item
To achieve high performance, this path executes trades in memory and leverages hard disk or shared memory to persist and share orders and executions
\item
SPecifically, orders and executions are stored in the sequencer for fast recovery, and data is archived after the market closes
\item
We discuss an efficient implementation of the sequencer in the deep dive section
\end{itemize}
\item
The reporter writes orders and executions to the databsae for reporting use cases like reconciliation and tax reporting
\item
Executions are forwarded to the market data processor to reconstruct the order book and candle stick chart data
\item
We discuss these data types next.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Order book}

An order book is a list of buy and sell orders for a specific security or financial instrucment, organized by price level [12] [13].

It is a key data structure in the matching eingine for fast order matching.

An efficient data structure for an order book must satisfy these requirements:

\begin{itemize}
\item
Constant lookup time. Operation includes: getting volume at a price level or between price levels
\item
Fast add/cancel/execute operations, perfrably \lstinline|O(1)| time complexity. Operations include placing anew order, canceling an order, and matching an order
\item
Fast update. Operation: replacing an order
\item
Query best bid/ask
\item
Iterate through price levels.
\end{itemize}

Let's walk through an example order execution against an order book, as illustrated in Figure 13.13.

  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{figure_13_13}
    \caption{}
